# rock-solid design

Terms such as primary, placement group, epoch or fencing are explained in the [glossary](glossary.md).

## Goals

Build a distributed storage system in Go ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) whose headline feature is **ReadWriteMany (RWX) shared filesystems** for a 3 to 10 node homelab Kubernetes cluster: many pods on many nodes mounting one directory, with no NFS gateway in the path, caching that stays correct when files are shared, and Longhorn-level ease of install. Block volumes come from the same core, at near-raw NVMe latency.

**Target user:** homelab and small-cluster Kubernetes operators. They have several NVMe or SATA solid-state drives per node, 10 to 25 Gigabit Ethernet (GbE), and some nodes with Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) capable cards. For RWX today they choose between a Network File System (NFS) server (single server, fragile), Longhorn RWX (an NFS server pod in front of each volume), JuiceFS (needs an external metadata database and object store) and CephFS (heavy on RAM, CPU and operator knowledge).

**Product goals, in priority order**

1. **RWX filesystem** (`volumeMode: Filesystem`, `ReadWriteMany`): a Filesystem in Userspace (FUSE) client on every node, talking straight to the storage nodes, with close-to-open consistency first and automatic per-file cache coherence next. macOS and Windows clients reach the same volumes over Server Message Block (SMB), through a Samba gateway on the FUSE mount ([What is not free](#what-is-not-free)).
2. **Block volumes**: ReadWriteOnce (RWO) Persistent Volume Claims (PVCs), plus RWX block for KubeVirt live migration.
3. **S3** later, on the same core.
4. **Easy to run:** a single binary (one daemon per node, which also serves as the FUSE client) plus a Container Storage Interface (CSI) driver. No hugepages, pinned cores or external database to get started.
5. **Low CPU per operation**, so storage does not eat the cluster.
6. **Permissive license** (Apache 2.0).

**Performance targets.** Stated as round trips, because absolute numbers depend on the network and drives.

| Operation | Target cost |
| --- | --- |
| Read of a cached file (one writer, or readers only) | Page-cache speed: no FUSE or network trip (0.3 µs per 4K measured) |
| Read or write of a file being written from two or more nodes | One network round trip to the chunk primary, plus about 5 to 20 µs of FUSE overhead |
| `stat`, `lookup` within the cache timeout | Local |
| `stat`, `lookup` after a cache miss | One round trip to the MetaStore shard leader (lease read, no disk write) |
| `create`, `unlink`, same-directory `rename` | One Raft commit on one shard |
| `open` of a file | One round trip to the inode's owner (memory only, no disk write) |
| Block 4K write at queue depth 1 | Around 0.1 ms (Vitastor class) on drives with power-loss protection; Ceph is around 1 ms. Consumer drives without it floor at about 1 ms per durable write ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)) |

**Learning goals:** Go for systems code, the Storage Performance Development Kit (SPDK), the Data Plane Development Kit (DPDK) and RDMA, extended Berkeley Packet Filter (eBPF) tracing, plus distributed-systems correctness testing.

**Non-goals for v1:** SMB leases on files that pods on other nodes also write, several SMB gateways per volume, GPU-cluster scale, shared-writable `mmap` across nodes, cross-directory hard links, a custom kernel module, hard disk drives (HDDs), which can come later as a capacity tier behind flash ([Addresses belong to the log](#on-disk-format)), and authentication or encryption between daemons. v1 trusts the cluster network: any host that can reach a daemon's port can read and write any volume.

## Landscape and positioning

The open gap is **RWX without a gateway**: every client reads and writes the storage nodes directly, caches aggressively while it is safe, and stops caching automatically when it is not. It installs like Longhorn and is licensed like SeaweedFS.

| System | What it covers | Why it doesn't fill the gap |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Longhorn | Easy Kubernetes block; RWX through a share-manager pod running NFS-Ganesha | All RWX traffic goes through one pod per volume: a bottleneck and a failover pause |
| [JuiceFS](https://github.com/juicedata/juicefs) | FUSE file system, CSI driver, Apache 2.0 | Needs an external metadata engine (Redis, TiKV, SQL) and an object store; close-to-open consistency only |
| CephFS | POSIX file system with full client capabilities | Around 1 ms writes, heavy CPU and RAM, steep operations learning curve |
| [SeaweedFS](https://github.com/seaweedfs/seaweedfs) | Single binary, S3, FUSE mount, CSI driver | Built around objects; no cache-coherence protocol for shared writes |
| [Vitastor](https://vitastor.io) | Ceph-like block, file, S3 at around 0.1 ms | Solo project; server under a GPL-based network-copyleft license that deters adopters |
| [OpenEBS Mayastor](https://github.com/openebs/mayastor) | Rust + SPDK + NVMe over Fabrics (NVMe-oF) block | Block only; requires hugepages and dedicated cores |
| [Weka](https://docs.weka.io/weka-system-overview/weka-client-and-mount-modes), DAOS, [DeepSeek 3FS](https://github.com/deepseek-ai/3fs) | AI-cluster file systems with kernel bypass | Built for GPU clusters; commercial or heavy to deploy |
| [MinIO](https://github.com/minio/minio), [RustFS](https://github.com/rustfs/rustfs), Garage | S3 | Object storage only |

**Takeaways**

- Homelab RWX today means NFS somewhere in the path. Removing it is the visible win.
- Weka shows that per-file cache-mode switching gives most of the benefit of full client capabilities with a much smaller protocol. Our spike shows it can be done on FUSE, without a kernel module (see [FUSE coherence spike](#appendix-fuse-coherence-spike)).
- Vitastor proves one person can get a 10x latency win over Ceph without DPDK, by shortening the code path. The same lean core serves block.
- Trust is the adoption gate. Storage people wait years before trusting a new system, so correctness work is the product.

## Strategy

Win on a short code path, correct caching and low CPU per operation. Kernel bypass is an opt-in fast path, never a requirement.

On homelab hardware the network caps throughput before the kernel does:

```
one NVMe Gen4 drive:  ~7 GB/s sequential, 4K write in ~0.04 ms (into its write cache)
10 GbE link:          ~1.2 GB/s
25 GbE link:          ~3 GB/s
kernel TCP stack:     saturates 10-25 GbE with one or two cores
```

So DPDK buys almost no throughput here. The latency gap between the disk and Ceph is almost all software path, which is what we attack. One caveat: a *durable* write is bounded by the drive's flush latency. On the consumer NVMe drive we measured, a 4K write plus flush takes 0.95 ms at p50, 98% of it inside the drive, and about 2 ms after the drive has idled for 100 ms ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)), so the 0.1 ms block target applies to drives with power-loss protection.

**Principles**

1. **RWX first:** the file frontend and its coherence protocol are the first usable product. Block and S3 reuse the same core.
2. **Lean data path:** client → chunk primary in one hop → log append in parallel with sends to replicas → acknowledge. Every frontend uses it.
3. **Cache where safe, never where unsafe:** a file is cached on a node only while no other node writes it. Correctness never depends on timeouts alone.
4. **Defaults that need no tuning:** `io_uring` for disk, kernel TCP for network, FUSE for the file client. No hugepages, pinned cores or kernel modules just to try it.
5. **Opt-in fast paths:** SPDK for dedicated drives, RDMA for nodes with capable cards. DPDK stays a learning experiment.
6. **Correctness before features:** deterministic simulation from day one, including the coherence protocol.
7. **Apache 2.0** to stand apart from Vitastor's network-copyleft license.

## Architecture overview

The system is a two-part core (ChunkStore for data, MetaStore for metadata) under three protocol frontends, over pluggable input/output drivers.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    subgraph FE[" "]
        direction LR
        FILE["<b>File: RWX (first)</b><br/>FUSE client on every node<br/>MetaStore: namespace, opens, cache modes"]
        BLK["<b>Block volumes</b><br/>ublk + CSI; NVMe-oF/TCP remote<br/>MetaStore: attach only"]
        S3["<b>S3 gateway (later)</b><br/>PUT writes, then seals chunks<br/>MetaStore: commit, LIST"]
    end
    subgraph CORE["Core: sans-IO state machines (events in, actions out)"]
        direction LR
        CS["<b>ChunkStore (data)</b><br/>Versioned chunks, deterministic chunk IDs<br/>Redundancy per pool:<br/>1 to 3 copies or erasure coding"]
        MS["<b>MetaStore (metadata)</b><br/>Ordered transactional key-value, multi-Raft<br/>Control group: pools, placement table + PG intervals<br/>Inodes, leases, S3 index"]
    end
    subgraph DRV["Drivers: pluggable, chosen by flags"]
        direction LR
        DISK["<b>Disk</b><br/>Default: io_uring<br/>Opt-in: SPDK on dedicated NVMe"]
        NET["<b>Network</b><br/>Default: kernel TCP<br/>Opt-in: RDMA or Soft-RoCE"]
        SIM["<b>Simulator (tests)</b><br/>Simulated disk, network, time<br/>One seed replays a whole run"]
    end
    FILE --> CORE
    BLK --> CORE
    S3 --> CORE
    CORE -- "actions out, events in" --> DRV
```

*Three frontends share one core; they differ only in how much metadata they need.*

The same daemon runs on every node. On storage nodes it serves chunks and MetaStore shards; on every node, including compute-only ones, it is also the FUSE client for RWX mounts. The core never touches real I/O, so the simulator can stand in for every driver. Every daemon also reports what it holds, from its own memory and disks, on an admin port that the `rock-solid` command line and web UI read from off the cluster, so an operator can see the cluster's state when the cluster cannot report it ([Operating the cluster](#operating-the-cluster)).

## Data layer: ChunkStore

The ChunkStore is dumb, fast and versioned. It knows nothing about files, buckets or volumes.

```go
type ChunkStore interface {
	Write(c ChunkID, off uint32, data []byte, expectVer *uint64) (uint64, error)
	Punch(c ChunkID, off, n uint32, expectVer *uint64) (uint64, error) // zero a range; the whole chunk deletes it
	Read(c ChunkID, off, n uint32, atVer *uint64) ([]byte, error)
	Seal(c ChunkID) error     // now immutable -> eligible for erasure coding
	Flush(cs []ChunkID) error // durability barrier
}
```

Three sans-IO machines in `internal/chunkstore` carry it: `Store` runs one disk, `Replica` runs a disk's store as a member of every PG the table puts on it, and `Client` is a client daemon's side. The protocol they follow between them is `models/epoch/epoch.tla` ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).

**Deterministic chunk IDs.** `chunk_id = (volume_id, inode_id, offset / CHUNK_SIZE)` is computed, never looked up, so the hot path needs no metadata; a block volume uses inode 0. It packs into 128 bits, a 32-bit volume, a 64-bit inode and a 32-bit chunk index, which allows files up to 16 PiB. Volume and inode IDs are never reused, and inode IDs are scoped to their volume, so a [restored or cloned](#snapshots-and-clones) volume keeps every inode number and differs from its source only in `volume_id`. `CHUNK_SIZE` is 4 MiB, Ceph RBD's default object size; the nightly benchmarks keep it honest.

**Versions.** Every write and punch returns a new 64-bit version: the high 32 bits are the number of the chunk's PG [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) in which its primary ordered it, the low 32 bits count the chunk's writes in that interval. Each interval has one primary and a PG's interval numbers only grow (a split PG's halves start one above it), so no two primaries can give one version to different data, and a member returning with a write its new primary never saw is told apart by the version alone, as with Ceph's `eversion_t` and Vitastor's per-PG epochs; a plain counter would take that stale write for the current one. Version 0 is the absent chunk, and a punch of a chunk with no version logs nothing and returns 0. A chunk whose counter runs out, after 4.3 billion writes in one interval, asks for a new interval of its PG alone. The high half counts the PG's own intervals rather than [table epochs](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) because table epochs follow the whole cluster: in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike), one node flapping every 10 seconds would use up 32 bits of table epochs in 207 days, while the busiest PG's intervals would take 272 years. The layout is disk format: versions sit in every record entry, index key and extent ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)), and 128-bit versions would add 8 bytes to each ([PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)).

`expect_ver` gives compare-and-set on data. It is compared with the chunk's newest *ordered* version, pending writes included, since a write ordered behind a pending one it never saw would otherwise overwrite it unseen; `expect_ver` 0 creates a chunk only if it is absent. A read at a frozen version (`at_ver`) needs no ordering against writes, so any member holding that version serves it without waiting for a writer. A primary keeps an old version only while a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) pins it.

**Reads see only acknowledged writes.** A version *commits*, and its write is acknowledged, once every live member of the chunk's PG holds it. A read without `atVer` returns the newest committed version, never one still being replicated, since a primary crash could lose that one: the new primary may be a member that never received it. So a primary maps a write into its [chunk index](#on-disk-format) only when it commits, and until then a read finds the previous version's blocks without waiting. A member that is not the primary maps writes as they become durable, since it serves no live reads, and a restarted primary takes a new interval of each PG it leads and [peers](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) before it serves.

**Single order per chunk, and every write applied once.** Each chunk has one primary that applies writes in one order, and every member holds them in that order, so RWX block and uncached shared files need no client-side protocol. A write takes effect once, however often its client sends it. A client resends whenever a first attempt may have committed (an acknowledgment lost with a connection or a primary, a refusal peering may still reverse), and applying it again under a newer version would let another node's write land between the two, so a reader would see X, Y, then X again, and a resent `expect_ver` write would fail although it succeeded.

So every client write and punch carries its *identity*, the session, incarnation and a per-chunk-write request ID kept across resends, and the oldest request ID its session still has outstanding. Every disk keeps a *resend table* in its chunk index, identity → (chunk, version) for the versions it logs, and a primary looks a write up once it would order it: a version it holds answers the write with that version (OK if mapped, the outcome if pending), and no entry means the write is ordered. The contract that keeps the table small and sound:

- An entry lives only while its disk holds the version. Every path that drops a version on a disk (a settle, a decision's marker, replay's gating, a failed log write, a give-up) removes that chunk's entries of every earlier interval. Erring toward removal is safe: the disk then lists the chunk and answers no write of it until recovery brings the source's entries back.
- The table holds only writes a client may still send. Each update names its session's oldest outstanding ID, every ID below which has a final answer and is never sent again; each disk it reaches removes the session's entries in the PG below that ID and raises the session's *watermark* there. A write at or below the watermark is answered `Stale`, which the client takes as final. Without the watermark a trimmed write waiting at an unpeered primary would be ordered again; NFSv4.1 does the same per slot with `NFS4ERR_SEQ_MISORDERED`.
- A client assigns no ID more than `RequestWindow` (4,096) past its oldest outstanding one, and a member refuses one that does, so one unanswered write cannot pile up a hot chunk's entries past what its recovery carries.
- A fenced session's entries and watermarks go in one range removal per PG, applied again after a replay rather than logged, since a fenced session's writes are refused before any lookup.
- A recovery write carries the source's watermarks for the PG and its entries for the chunk, those of overwritten versions too, since the target may lead the PG next. Copying only the live map's versions would forget X once Y overwrote it; copying the source's whole range would bring entries of versions the target never holds, and answer a resend OK for a version no disk holds.

Ceph answers resends the same way from its PG log and `dups` (`PrimaryLogPG::do_op`), but keeps a count of request IDs per PG (3,000 by default); trimming by the client's oldest outstanding ID means a busy PG cannot push out a write still unanswered. The MetaStore's [replay records](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) do the same for metadata. A block daemon's reissue after a crash is the one resend that is a new write: the [incarnation fence](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) orders the old copy before it or refuses it, so at worst the same bytes land twice. The cost is a 65-byte `writer` entry per version in the header its first data shares, about 60 bytes of index per write until the session's next update passes it, a watermark key per session and PG, and, in a recovery write, 48 bytes per carried record: at most `RequestWindow` entries per session per chunk, so the 16 MiB frame bounds a chunk at some 60 sessions with full windows. The code is `resend.go`; the model checks the rule as `AppliedOnce` and `StaleOnlyHeard` in `models/epoch/epoch.tla`.

**Byte ranges become whole blocks at the primary.** The API takes byte ranges; records, checksums, the chunk index and snapshots hold whole 4 KiB blocks. For each block an operation covers in part, the primary *merges*: it overlays the operation's bytes on the newest version of that block it has ordered (a pending write's merged block, else its live map, else zeros) and sends and logs only whole blocks, so members need no base and hold the same bytes under every version. The base must be the newest *ordered* version: in the [sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)'s model, merging against the committed version lost bytes on every seed as soon as two writes to one block were in flight. Merging on the client lost bytes without compare-and-set and retried 0.64 to 1.28 times per write with it (VitastorFS merges that way); merging on every member, as BlueStore does, costs a read per member and needs identical histories ([sub-block writes study](../studies/sub-block-writes/README.md)).

What follows from merging against pending versions:

- A write merged against a pending version is acknowledged only after that version commits, and refused if the base is, so no byte of a write that never committed survives in a later one. Across intervals [peering](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) keeps that dependency: it drops only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions, and a new primary merges only once it holds every kept version.
- A base read from disk has its CRC32C checked, and one that fails is read from another member at the same version, so a merge never carries rot into a new version; a member serves such a read only while its live map holds that block at that version. The chunk's later operations wait behind the base read, so they keep their order, as Ceph's erasure-coded extent cache does.
- Each loop keeps a small cache of recently merged blocks (64, 256 KiB), valid only while the index still maps the block at the cached version, so nothing invalidates it. With the [client](#client-design) sending whole blocks wherever it holds the bytes, base reads fall on write-shared files and 512-byte [block](#block-frontend) writes.

**Punch.** `punch` zeroes a byte range as a new version: whole blocks inside the range get a punch entry, and a block it covers in part is merged with zeros like a write. Punched blocks are freed once no snapshot needs them. Punching a whole chunk deletes it and leaves a tombstone. File unlink and truncate, `fallocate` hole punches, block discard and volume deletion all free space this way ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)).

**The primary's write path.** The primary orders a client's write or punch in its store, which merges it and gives it the next version, then sends the merged blocks to every up member while its own log takes them, so members log in parallel with it. A member in `maintenance` counts toward `min_live` but is sent nothing, and a PG with fewer than `min_live` live members refuses writes. The version commits once the primary's record is durable and every member it was sent to has acknowledged; the store maps a chunk's committed versions in version order and replies after mapping, since a reply on commit alone would let a following read miss the write while an older version waited. One copy of the merged blocks goes to every member, as the [driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers) allows. A member logs a version only from the PG's current interval in its own table and while it is an up member there, and answers once it is durable, with its disk's fill.

**A failed version ends the interval.** A version a member refuses, or that runs out of space or fails in the primary's own log, ends the PG's interval: neither it nor any later pending version of its chunk can commit, since each merged version holds the bytes of the one below and members may differ in which they logged. The primary orders nothing more in the interval, refuses the writes still waiting in its store, holds new ones, and asks the control plane for the next interval, in which it peers and leaves each pending version to [Which versions survive](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). Its clients are refused, which tells them to resend, not that the write failed; should peering keep the first attempt, the resend table answers the second. A failed version keeps its number, since members may hold it. Superseding the failed versions within the interval would save a table change and a peering, but its writes can fail in turn ([Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered)).

The contract peering hands back through the store (`Store.Settle`, chunk by chunk, before activation and only once every up member has persisted its missing list, so no client hears of a decision that is not durable):

- Pending versions the kept one rests on are mapped, the rest dropped. A version rests on the kept one if it is at or below it in the kept version's interval, or below such a version; one from an earlier interval with none of the kept interval above it is dropped though it is lower by number, since an interval this primary missed may have dropped it.
- A kept version's client hears OK only if the decision proves at least `min_live` copies: the up members it leaves off the list and the members in `maintenance`. A member that lists the chunk is no copy, even if it holds the version, and a primary that keeps leading could otherwise answer OK for a write it alone logged. With fewer copies the clients are refused and the versions stay kept; the primary holds the resends until recovery gives the chunk `min_live` copies ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).
- A refused version is never dropped, since only the primary that ordered it knows it was never acknowledged. So if its one holder dies the chunk is unfound, with the acknowledged versions below it, though they are whole on other disks; no operator path gives those back ([Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered)).
- Peering decides a chunk only once none of its writes waits to be ordered and every record of it on the primary is durable or failed, and the primary names the chunks only it logged (`Store.Unsettled`) and refuses to activate while one is left.

A primary that loses its lead refuses its pending versions' clients and maps them as a member would. A rebuild of the store's index ends the interval of every PG the disk leads, as a restart does. A primary whose store failed refuses everything and takes no more, leaving its versions to another primary's peering. A member that fails to log a version refuses the chunk's later versions in that interval, so it never holds one without the versions below it; telling apart only the versions merged on the failed one would track a dependency per block for a case that means the member is full or gone.

**Admission against `write_limit`.** Before it orders a write, the primary checks every disk holding one of the PG's members, down ones at their last fill, against [Write limit](#volume-size-and-capacity). It learns each member's live blocks from every acknowledgment and the leader's heartbeat answer, its own disk's at most every 100 ms, and each disk's size, log reserve and the pool's outstanding grants from the leader's answer to its node's report ([The control-plane host](#metadata-layer-metastore)). A refused write gets `NoSpace` unless it is a flush under a space grant, which the client flags beside `expect_ver`, or an overwrite, looked up only once a member is at its limit. Punches and recovery copies are never checked. In phase 3 the primary trusts the grant flag; phase 4's control-plane group supplies the grants to check it against.

**Sends, reconnects and held writes.** A primary sends each member its writes in the order it ordered them over one connection per loop pair, and after a `PeerLost` waits `ResendAfter` (10 ms) and sends again, in order, every write of the current interval the member has not acknowledged; a write ordered meanwhile waits behind that resend. So a member never logs k.(n + 1) before k.n of a chunk. A member answers a resent version with its first copy's outcome, or OK if its index holds the version whole in the current interval (each version's last entry is flagged, [Which versions survive](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), and otherwise refuses, since a multi-record version may be durable only in part. So an acknowledgment lost with a connection costs a resend, not an interval change. Until a primary has peered in an interval it holds the PG's client writes; afterwards it orders them only if it led every interval since it held them, and otherwise refuses them.

**The chunk client.** A client daemon reaches chunks through one `chunkstore.Client` per session incarnation, shared by the FUSE daemon and the block frontend. Exactly one per incarnation, because request IDs and every disk's trim by the oldest outstanding one are the incarnation's: a second client naming its own oldest would raise the watermark past the first's unanswered writes. The client places each chunk by the hash below over its node's table, sends each request to the primary that table names after a hello naming the session and incarnation, and keeps a write's bytes in a pool buffer until a final answer. Its retry rules:

- A refusal naming a newer table waits for that table and goes to its primary; one naming no newer table, or an unfound write, goes again after 100 ms doubling to 3.2 s. No reachable primary retries after 100 ms, no answer after 2 s, a lost connection after `ResendAfter`.
- A write to an unfound chunk stays outstanding until a candidate returns or an operator gives the chunk up; an unfound read completes with `EIO`, since a read is idempotent.
- `NoSpace` is final and returns `ENOSPC`. A fenced session's writes are refused and resent until the daemon replaces the client with one of its new session.
- IDs are taken in order, so a write past the window waits in the client's queue, never in the network.

**Tables reach a primary as deltas.** A replica applies each table as the delta from the one it holds and checks the CRC, and asks for the whole table after a gap or a mismatch; a message tagged with a newer interval from a newer table waits for that table.

**Operations on one disk.** `chunkstore.Store` is the ChunkStore of one disk, driven by its owner loop with requests and disk completions. A write's bytes come in one pool buffer laid out from the start of its first block, so whole blocks need no copy, and each write or punch carries its volume's snapshot epoch, which is how the store learns a cut ([Snapshots and clones](#snapshots-and-clones)). Its contract:

- A range lies inside one chunk and is at least a byte long, or it is refused with `ErrRange` and no disk action; callers split at a chunk's end.
- A write is answered once every record holding it is durable and the chunk's earlier writes are mapped, so `Flush(cs)` adds no disk action: it completes once every write and punch to `cs` submitted before it is answered. Until a write is mapped the store keeps its buffers, and later writes to the same blocks merge against them in memory.
- Every read checks each block's CRC32C against its record header. A write whose base fails or cannot be read fails (`ErrBadChecksum` or the disk's error) rather than carry rot forward; on one node the block stays unreadable until overwritten.
- A write larger than one record goes to the log as several items of one version, freeze first, and a crash or `NoSpace` can keep some of them. Live state must be what recovery produces, so the store keeps the durable items mapped: an unacknowledged large write may be partly visible, as on a local disk, and a failed one's error carries its version for compare-and-set. All or nothing would need a commit record per write, which [chunk slots](#on-disk-format) make unnecessary for whole chunks.
- Items in flight when the disk fails report `ErrInDoubt`, and the store refuses writes (`ErrUnavailable`) until it is opened again and recovery decides.
- On one node the version's interval is the store's own: 1 at each open, never below the interval of the version it increments, raised when a counter runs out. A restart may reuse the version of a write lost at the crash, which was never durable or acknowledged.
- `Seal` returns `ErrSeal` until [encoding](#when-chunks-are-encoded) exists.

**Fencing.** Each write carries the client's session ID. When a client session expires ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)), the control plane publishes it as fenced in its session table and every storage node rejects its writes. The session's locks and cache rights are released only after every storage node has applied the fence or been marked down, so a paused or partitioned client cannot write after they are gone.

**Integrity.** Each 4K block, and each 4K block of an erasure-coded shard, carries a CRC32C in its record's header in the [on-disk format](#on-disk-format), which catches bit rot, torn writes and misdirected writes. Go's `hash/crc32` interleaves three SSE4.2 streams on x86-64: 167 ns per 4 KiB block, 7× faster than a table-driven implementation ([Go CPU spike](#appendix-go-cpu-per-operation-spike), C0); on arm64 it takes 359 ns on an Apple M4 Pro.

**Placement groups.** Chunks are not placed one by one. Each chunk hashes into one of a fixed number of placement groups (PGs) in its volume's [pool](#pools-and-redundancy), as in Ceph, and placement is decided per PG:

```
chunk_id = (volume_id, inode_id, offset / CHUNK_SIZE)        // computed
pool     = volume record → pool                              // learned once, at attach or mount
h        = xxh3_64(chunk_id as 16 big-endian bytes, seed 0)  // computed
pg       = (pool, h >> (64 - log2(pool.pg_count)))           // top bits of the hash
members  = placement_table[pg]                               // in-memory; [primary, r1, r2] in rep3
```

The hash spreads a volume's consecutive chunks over many PGs, and so over every disk of its pool, so a volume is not bounded by any one drive ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)). The PG is also the unit of recovery and rebalancing: adding a disk moves whole PGs onto it, never single chunks. The hash function and bit layout are disk format and never change.

Each pool's `pg_count` is a power of two, sized so that each disk holds around 100 PG members summed over all pools (`pg_count × width ÷ disks`); unset, it is the smallest power of two giving each disk at least 100 members of the pool alone, 2048 for a 3-replica pool on 60 disks. It grows only by doubling, since a halving would merge PGs: a PG is one contiguous range of the hash's top bits, so doubling splits PG `p` into `2p` and `2p + 1` without rewriting the per-disk index.

**Placement table.** The table maps each PG to its members in order, primary first, and to its interval state: the current and last activated interval, the members and `min_live` of intervals since, a pin flag, whether its primary reported the current interval recovered, and which members are *leaving* during a move or refill. Each pool's part carries its split epoch, and the table a *table epoch*, its version. Its size follows the pools' PG counts and widths, not the data: 2048 `rep3` PGs take 52 KiB and a 4096-PG `ec16-2` pool about 404 KiB, whether the cluster holds 1 GB or 100 TB. So it is **not sharded**: it lives in the single control-plane Raft group, every node caches a full copy, and it changes on pool, disk and node events and on primaries' activation and recovered reports, never on the I/O path. Every change raises the table epoch, but only a change to a PG's members, their order, live or leaving marks, its pool's `replicas` or `min_live`, a split, or its primary's request starts a new interval of that PG ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), and a member rejects only requests from an earlier interval of their PG.

The control-plane leader computes each new table and commits it as one Raft entry holding only the PG entries it changes and the CRC32C of the table it produces; nodes never compute placement themselves. A node applies the change and checks the CRC, and fetches the whole table after a gap or mismatch, as Ceph's daemons do with incremental maps. Each entry carries its interval number, so a node that skips tables loses nothing, where Ceph's daemons walk every map to work out each PG's interval; deltas only save bytes. In the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike), a homelab year with moves staged one per table pushed 2.25 GB of whole tables to 10 nodes against 105 MB of changes; activation and recovered reports change an entry at most once per interval each, at most tripling that.

The computation, `chunkstore.Next` in `placement.go`, is a pure function of the previous table and the cluster's state, deterministic (ties go to the lowest disk ID, then PG number, and no map order enters it), in two steps:

1. **Refill** every missing member. A member is missing once its disk has left the pool or lost its log, once it has been down `refill_after` (or `degraded_refill_after` in a PG below `min_live`) unless peering waits for it anyway ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), or once its PG is `Stuck` and it sits on a disk at its log reserve lacking copies ([A member on a full disk moves before the report](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). An up leaving member takes a missing member's place if it can; otherwise a new member goes onto the eligible disk furthest below its target, replacing a member whose disk has left the pool and standing beside one merely down or full, which may hold a chunk's last copy and so stays as a leaving member ([A refilled member stays a source](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). Refills are never held back.
2. **Balance**: while a disk is outside its fill margin, move one member of an unpinned PG from the disk furthest above its target to the eligible disk furthest below, without leaving the destination higher than the source. Each move lowers a well-founded measure, so the loop ends.

Lowering `replicas` narrows a PG through a move's removal, one member per recovered report, never a deletion: a refill member may still be recovering and the table cannot tell, so deleting a live member could delete the only complete copy. Only a member whose disk has left the pool is deleted at once. The leader allows `max_moves` (1) balance moves in flight across the cluster, as Ceph's `osd_max_backfills` of 1 does per OSD, since a move copies a whole PG while it serves; the count is cluster-wide because pools share disks and choose moves pool by pool. A move that cannot end, because another member of its PG is down, holds that budget until the member returns or its disk is declared lost: it slows moves, never refills or data.

**A move adds the new member before it removes the old one.** A move, from balance or off a draining disk, adds the destination and marks the member it replaces *leaving*, in one table that starts an interval. It starts only on a PG whose entry records a recovered report for its current interval with its members unchanged since, so every member is complete; the one exception is refill's park of a member on a full disk. Until the move ends the PG is one member wider: the leaving member acknowledges every write and is a recovery source, the new one counts toward `min_live` while it recovers, balance counts the leaving member as gone, and the failure-domain rule ignores it, so a move between two disks of one node works on 3 nodes. The first table after the current interval's primary reports it recovered removes the leaving members, unless that table marks another member down or in `maintenance`. Any change that lets a member miss writes starts an interval and so voids an older report; a late report would otherwise remove the leaving member while a member that missed writes still recovered.

A swap in one table would leave the chunks written before the move with one copy fewer on every routine move, since recovery copies only from the current interval's members and past intervals' lists are dropped at activation. Ceph keeps its old acting set as `pg_temp` until backfill ends, and CockroachDB adds a learner before removing the old replica, for the same reason. A leaving member stays while it is down, however long, and goes without a report only once its disk leaves the pool, so a move never loses a copy the PG would have kept without it. The cost is a second interval per moved PG and a PG one member wider while it copies. The model checks this as `Add`, `Remove`, `Replace`, `Drop`, `Stay` and `Refill` in `models/epoch/epoch.tla`, under the properties `RemovalKeepsCopies` and `NoStranded`; it does not cover a holder that truly dies (`LoneCopy`).

**Pins and drains.** An operator pins a PG with `rock-solid pg pin <pool> <pg> [<disk,...>]`, clears the pin with `rock-solid pg unpin <pool> <pg>`, and reorders it unpinned with `rock-solid pg edit <pool> <pg> <disk,...>`. An edit only reorders the PG's current members: one that changed the disk set would drop a member with no leaving mark to keep it until its replacement recovered, and could lose a last copy, so moving a PG off a disk is a drain's work. An edit naming more disks than the pool's width, a disk twice, or more per failure domain than `max_per_domain` allows is ignored, pin and all. Balance never moves a pinned PG's members, but refill still replaces a missing one, since a pin must not leave data on a lost disk.

A drain is a mark on the disk in the membership record (`rock-solid disk drain <disk>`, `rock-solid disk undrain <disk>`): the disk's target becomes 0, so balance moves its members off while they serve and refill puts nothing on it. The leader refuses a drain while placement finds no destination for some member of the disk, as on three nodes under `rep3`, since a drain that cannot finish would only stop refills onto the disk. It finishes once no PG names the disk, a leaving member or a past interval included; a pinned PG holds it until an operator edits or unpins that PG. Removing the drained disk from the pool is refused until then, and the refusal says how many PGs still name it, which is how an operator follows a drain. Removal is final: the drive returns only formatted again, under a new disk ID.

**Targets and margins.** A disk's target is its [capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) for the pool, water-filled over failure domains so that no domain is asked for more than `max_per_domain` of a PG's width, and split within a domain by capacity. Capacity counts the pool's own data as held, so a finished move does not shift the targets; targets by free room shifted with every move, and after a disk was added settling took 1.5 to 2 times the moves and 3 to 5 computations, against 2 by capacity ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). A target proportional to size alone cannot be met when failure domains differ (under `rep2`, nodes of 0.7 and 2 GB each hold a copy of every PG), and in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike) a loop aiming at it never converged, while capped targets reached the pool's [balanced capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) within 1%.

A disk's *fill margin* is max(5% of its target, 1 member). The 5% is Ceph's balancer deviation of 5 PGs at about 100 per disk and CockroachDB's rebalance threshold. The floor is needed because a 0.5 TB disk beside 8 TB disks has a target of 10 to 20 members, which no single move brings within 5%: without it the loop stopped outside the margin in 15 to 18 of 200 random tables. Balance takes its source from the disk furthest above target even inside its margin, as Ceph's balancer does once only underfull disks remain: that left available + held a median 0.8 to 1.2% under balanced after a disk addition, against 4.3 to 4.6% taking sources only from disks above their margins, for 11 to 17% more moves. Once converged, available + held ≥ balanced ÷ (1 + the pool's largest margin). With shards and `max_per_domain` above 1 (phase 10), two shards of a PG never share a disk and a PG's first `replicas` members sit in distinct domains.

A computed table gives exact balance on small clusters with mismatched disks, where a placement function every node evaluates (Ceph CRUSH, Weka's algorithmic ownership) leaves disks unbalanced; Ceph patches that with `pg-upmap` exception tables written by a balancer, so in practice it runs a table too, and warns that on mismatched devices a tight deviation can leave it "shuffling data forever". At thousands of disks the table and its recomputation would grow enough to revisit computed placement; that is outside the target.

What does grow with data lives elsewhere. Each disk keeps its own [index](#on-disk-format) of the chunks and shards it holds, and file-to-inode metadata lives in the sharded MetaStore.

### Epoch changes and failure detection

A new table reaches nodes at different times, so for a while a PG's old primary may act on an older table than its new primary. What keeps that window safe is the PG's *interval*: a run of tables in which the PG's ordered members and their live marks stay the same, so the PG has one primary and one set of members that must acknowledge its writes. The leader starts a new interval of a PG, its number plus one, in a table that changes the PG's members or their order, a member's live mark (down, up, `maintenance`), its pool's `replicas` or `min_live`, or splits it (both halves take the parent's number plus one), and when the PG's primary asks because a chunk's counter ran out or it restarted. Every other change raises the table epoch and leaves the interval alone. Ceph defines its intervals the same way (`PastIntervals::is_new_interval`). Three different numbers are called epochs, and none of them is in a version: the *table epoch* versions the placement table; a *support epoch* numbers one unbroken run of a loop-pair lease (below); a volume's *snapshot epoch* counts its [snapshots](#snapshots-and-clones). A PG's interval numbers, which are in versions, are not epochs. The protocol is the epoch change model, `models/epoch/epoch.tla`; the code is in `internal/chunkstore` (peering in `replica_peer.go` and `keep.go`, recovery in `replica_recover.go` and `recover.go`, the give-up in `giveup.go`, disk identity in `disks.go`, the detector in `detector.go`) and the lease arithmetic in `internal/lease`.

**Requests are checked against their PG's interval.** Every message about a PG carries the sender's table epoch and the PG's interval number in that table. A member accepts it only if that number is the PG's interval in its own table, whichever table is newer; it refuses a lower number, answering with its own table epoch and number, and fetches a table at least as new as the sender's before it judges a higher one. Comparing the PG's members in the two tables instead would not do: a member removed and added back leaves them equal, though it missed the writes in between. A split renumbers PGs, and new PG `p`, a half of PG `p ÷ 2`, can start the very interval old PG `p` was in, so the table keeps each pool's *split epoch*, that of the table that last doubled it: a member refuses any message about the pool's PGs tagged with an older table epoch, and a member whose table predates the split fetches the split table before it judges a message whose chunk is not in the PG it names under its own `pg_count`, or a peering message tagged with a newer table epoch. Neither wait comes in steady state.

A primary takes a client's write while its interval number is at least the one in which its own term began, since the write carries no state that depends on the PG's replicas. Writes it held when an interval ended under it it orders again once it has re-peered, but only if no interval since had another primary; otherwise it refuses them and the client retries, since a held write could land after writes another primary acknowledged, such as a dead daemon's write after its successor's reissue ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). So a table change makes only the writes of the PGs it changed retry: in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)'s homelab year on 10 nodes, a table changed 22% of PGs on average, or 2 to 4% with moves staged one per table, 4.4 to 46 times fewer disturbed writes than rejecting every older table epoch. On 3 nodes, where every PG has a member on every node, a node event still changes every PG. Ceph checks the same way (`can_discard_replica_op`, `same_primary_since`; [PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)).

**Writes: the new primary peers before it accepts writes.** A write tagged with interval `j` completes only when every member live in `j` acknowledges it, and a member whose table has moved the PG past `j` refuses it. So before it accepts writes in interval `k`, the new primary *peers*: for every interval since the PG's last *activated* one (the last whose primary finished peering), it first gets at least one member live in that interval to adopt a table with interval `k`. From then on a write of an earlier interval completes only if `k`'s peering keeps it, so a client may hear of such a write late, but never of one `k` dropped. One interval back is not enough, since a PG can change interval twice before the first new primary peers. An interval whose live members, counting `maintenance`, numbered fewer than the `min_live` it ran under, or were all in `maintenance`, accepted no writes and is skipped, as Ceph skips intervals below `min_size`; the table keeps each past interval's `min_live`, since judged by the current value, raising it from 1 to 2 would skip an interval that took writes on one member. If no member of an interval to stop can be reached, the PG waits, as in Ceph's `down` state, until one returns or an operator [gives it up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). Ceph also records each activation in the map first (`up_thru`) so peering can skip intervals that never activated, at a map change per peering; at 3 to 10 nodes waiting is enough.

**Which versions survive.** The primary collects, from each member it reaches, the newest version of every chunk and its *missing list*. That exchange grows with the chunks the PG holds, not with the writes a member missed, which RADOS's per-PG log of recent writes avoids for short outages (Weil et al., "RADOS: A Scalable, Reliable Storage Service for Petabyte-scale Storage Clusters", §3.4.1); whether it fits within a peering's pause is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions). Before the primary activates, every member persists, with the interval number, for each chunk it lacks or holds a dropped version of, the kept version it must receive, as Ceph's members keep a missing set. A member never serves a chunk on its list, and only recovery removes an entry: a later peering may raise it but never clears it, because a write to another block can land on a dropped version's bytes before recovery arrives, and clearing the entry would make those torn bytes a recovery source.

The keep rule (`keep` in `keep.go`):

- The primary starts from the newest decision any reached member persisted: at least the last activated interval, possibly a later one not yet recorded or never activated. Versions below it stay as that decision kept them; deciding them by their own interval's members would drop what a later interval kept.
- A version assigned in that interval or later is kept unless a reached member live in the version's interval neither holds nor lists it, which proves it was never acknowledged. Keeping a write never acknowledged costs nothing.
- The proof gives way where acting on it would leave no source: a member that lists the chunk or holds a later version in part has no say when an up member of the current interval holds the version clean, and a member outside the current interval has no say when every up member of it holds the version. Otherwise a drain, a restart during recovery or a primary moved while holding part of a version could leave a chunk unreadable with every disk up. It is safe because a primary orders a write only while its own store lists nothing for the chunk ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), so every version was first logged by a clean copy.
- A member holds a version only if every record of it is durable; each version's last entry is flagged and durability is a log prefix ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)). A member holding a version in part vetoes it like one lacking it, and is listed whatever peering keeps.
- Every version a kept one rests on is kept, and a chunk whose kept version no up member of the interval can serve is reported unfound, never served older.

The versions peering drops are always the newest of their chunk: a primary sends a chunk's writes to each member in version order, again after a reconnect, and a member refuses the chunk's later versions in an interval once it fails to log one or is sent one again. A write that [merges a partial block](#data-layer-chunkstore) carries the bytes of the version below it, so peering never keeps a version whose base it drops.

**Recovery.** A member that lacks a chunk's kept version, or logged a version peering dropped, the new primary included, is recovered by copying the authoritative image onto it, as Ceph recovers divergent objects; the copy is a later record covering the dropped write's blocks, so neither replay nor an index rebuild brings it back. Until a chunk's kept versions are on the new primary, a partial-block write to it waits for its base, as Ceph's primary holds writes to a missing object: while the primary's index maps a version other than the kept one, or its store listed the chunk before the decision. Whole-block writes go ahead, and a punch of a chunk the primary holds nothing of is ordered, not answered done, since its members may hold the chunk. A primary holds at most 64 client writes per PG and past that refuses a partial-block write to a chunk whose kept version it lacks; a write to an unfound chunk is answered unfound at once.

A primary that dropped only versions of its own it never mapped still holds the kept image, so it is its own source and merges at once, and it carries that image from one decision to the next while every interval since had its disk as primary or as a member not up and peering keeps the same version. To outlast a restart or a lost lead, a store keeps versions *gated*, unmapped, until a decision its disk persisted settles them or the PG leaves the disk; replay gates the versions of any interval at or past the PG's newest persisted decision, and the primary answers kept versions' clients only at activation. The log cannot tell replay why a chunk is listed, so the primary persists the entry with an *image* flag when it lists the chunk only for its own dropped versions; every later persist of the list rewrites the flag. Without gating and the flag, a restarted primary would map its dropped versions and be no source, and losing the other member before recovery would leave the chunk unfound for good though the primary's log held the kept bytes. The cost is a flag per missing-list entry and gated versions held across a restart. Only the primary can be a source this way; whether another listed member could be is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

Recovery is a write the primary orders like any other. It carries only the blocks no earlier write of the current interval covers, chosen when it is ordered, not when the source is read, so a client write ordered in between is never overwritten with older bytes. It takes no version of its own: it goes only to the members that list the chunk, each logs it under the newest version the interval ordered on the chunk before it, or the kept version, then ends it with an entry that sets the chunk's newest version and removes the missing-list entry. A later peering keeps or drops the recovered bytes with that version, and the chunk index keeps the version each chunk's last recovery was logged under, so a decision that keeps a version below it gives the primary no image. Rejected: a recovery write under the chunk's next version, sent only to the members it recovers, would be dropped by the next peering, so a move would copy the PG twice; sent to every member, a dropped one would list clean sources too, and two overlapping faults would leave chunks with no source. The costs: a recovery write sent again after it applied is refused and ends the interval, and a recovered block carries the recovery's version on the copy, so fetching it as a base, which only a checksum failure on the primary calls for, fails partial-block writes to it until a whole-block write or a recovery rewrites it.

The primary reads the image from its own store when its index holds the kept version whole, else from the first up member that does not list the chunk; a listed member is never a source, and a store whose image failed its checksum is none for the rest of the interval. A chunk with no source stays listed and unfound until a candidate returns or an operator [gives it up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection); kept version 0 needs none, its recovery write a map of holes. A failed recovery write ends the interval, and an index rebuild ends the recoveries under way. The image is the chunk's live map; a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones)'s frozen maps are not copied before phase 11. Each disk recovers at most 4 chunks at once across the PGs it leads: first a chunk a client write waits on, then the most degraded PG's (most members not up or listing, the lower PG among equals), and only while every target disk keeps the room outstanding [space grants](#client-design) were promised.

Once its members have persisted their lists, the primary activates and reports it on its next heartbeat, and the leader records the PG's last activated interval, dropping earlier intervals' member lists, in a later table that starts no interval; only activation guarantees every live member persisted the decision. A primary reports an interval *recovered* only once activated, since before activation a new member may not have persisted its missing list yet, so an empty one proves nothing; once its members, but a leaving one, are all up with empty lists it reports the interval recovered on each heartbeat until a table records it, which lets the leader start or end a [move](#data-layer-chunkstore). Every report, and the per-PG state a give-up reads, carries its pool's `pg_count` in the reporter's table, since a split renumbers PGs and a report for PG `p` from before it would land on the new PG `p`, a half of PG `p ÷ 2`; the leader drops one from before a split, ignores reports, edits and give-ups in the table that splits the pool, and drops an ask for a new interval tagged with a table older than the split.

**Restarts.** A node whose store was opened at start, rather than formatted, asks for a new interval of each PG it is a member of in its first table, every second until a table moves the PG on, and as primary peers in the new interval before it serves. Peering again in its old interval is not safe: a write its previous run sent could still sit in a member's socket buffer and land after that member answered peering, under a version the restarted primary would give to other data. A restarted member asks too, since replay leaves its current interval's versions gated until a decision settles them. Its own disk answers its peering, so the restart wait ([Reads](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) applies: a primary restarting at t = 100 s activates at about 104 s. Its first table is one fetched after it started, never older than its previous run's, which could hand it back its old interval with no ask pending. The cost is one table change and one peering per PG of the disk, as Ceph peers every PG of a restarted OSD.

A PG can leave a disk while its node is down, as when a move completes with its leaving member down. The store drops the PG's gated versions once a table names the disk in no interval of the PG since the last activated one, as member or in a listed past interval; nothing can read them after that. Dropping them sooner would let the disk's peering reply veto an acknowledged version through a past interval; keeping them for good would pin the replay point, so the cleaner never frees later segments and a gated slot write keeps its slot. The drop logs nothing, so a restart replays them gated and drops them again under its first table, and an idle left disk keeps the slot lane's open segment until it takes writes. Pools are never deleted and PG counts only double, so every chunk's PG stays in the table; deleting a pool, if ever added, must drop its PGs' gated versions on every disk.

**A disk is its log.** Peering believes what a member says about the intervals it was live in, so a disk formatted again or replaced, answering from an empty log under the old disk ID, would drop acknowledged writes and stop intervals whose writes it never saw. So the control plane records, beside each disk ID, the disk's log ID, drawn at random at format ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)), and the drive's stable identity, such as its WWN, and every node reports both for each disk it opens (`disks.go`). A device's slot is no identity, since a restart may renumber slots. A node refuses a drive with no unique stable identity (virtio disks, some USB bridges, cloned VM images that share a serial), and the control plane refuses a report's disks that share a device.

- An unknown log ID is a new disk with a new disk ID, from a counter that never reuses one. It retires only the disk last reported on the same device of the same node, a drive formatted again, whose ID stays down and whose members refill at once, without `refill_after`. A new drive in place of a failed one has a new identity and replaces nothing.
- A known log ID is taken only on its recorded device, and on another node only once its disk is down (a moved disk); under another identity it can only be a copy, such as a cloned drive, whose stale answers would prove acknowledged writes unacknowledged. The cost: a disk moved into an enclosure that reports its own identity is refused, and its members refill.
- An operator's declaration that a down disk is lost does the same for a disk that never reports again. The leader refuses it for an unknown disk, an up one, or one a PG's current interval holds without marking it down (the model's precondition for `Lost`); afterwards the record refuses that log, so the disk cannot rejoin under its old ID. What only it held stays unfound.
- A member answers peering only for its own disk ID, so a stale table placing the old ID at the new log's loop only delays peering, and serves a recovery read only while its disk ID is in the PG's current interval, or a new disk would copy zeros over acknowledged blocks.

A disk that leaves either way ends no peering: peering still needs an answer from a member of the last activated interval and of each later one that could have taken writes, so a PG with none of an interval's members left, as `rep2` when both go down together, stays stopped and is reported [`Blocked`](#volume-size-and-capacity) ([Blocked and unfound reports](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore)) until an operator's give-up. Ceph's `osd lost` frees the PG at once, so a declaration made in error loses data with no report; here it costs availability until the operator gives up writes knowingly. A lost log's shard replicas are lost with it, and the disk's new replicas join under new replica IDs, as etcd re-adds a member that lost its data directory; Ceph's monitors likewise ignore an OSD whose fsid changed (`OSDMonitor::preprocess_boot`). A drive that loses flushed writes but keeps its superblock keeps its log ID, which this cannot catch ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).

**An operator's give-up.** An unfound chunk or a stopped PG stays so until a candidate returns or an operator gives up what is lost, the one step that lets an acknowledged write go. `rock-solid pg give-up <pool> <pg>` ([The operator's commands](#metadata-layer-metastore)) gives up the PG's current interval k, read from the leader first, and every chunk k left unfound. The leader commits it as interval k+1 with the same members, marks and activation, in a table that changes nothing else of the PG; if a step changes the PG first, the leader refuses it with `ErrIntervalMoved` and the operator runs it again. The entry records k, so peering skips every interval up to k, and the chunks it may lose: every chunk if k is stopped, else those k's activated peering left unfound, which a primary reports `MaxReportUnfound` (64) at a time, so a PG with more takes several give-ups.

- The leader refuses it while the PG has a candidate, a member down or in `maintenance` whose disk is not gone ([Pool conditions](#volume-size-and-capacity)), as Ceph refuses `mark_unfound_lost` until every disk that might hold the object is probed or lost; and unless k is stopped or left chunks unfound. A give-up of an interval already given up is answered with that record and one of any other old interval is refused, so a resent command never gives up a newer interval.
- A node adopting a table with a new give-up drops the entry and bytes of every recorded unfound chunk, by a log entry replay applies too, before it answers peering, leaving any chunk it holds a version of from after k. Those chunks then read as zeros, a hole the filesystem above can repair or rewrite.

A stopped PG drops nothing up front: peering of k+1 keeps what the members it reaches hold, possibly older versions of acknowledged writes, and a chunk listed and held by none is unfound. The [epoch change model](#testing-strategy) lets an acknowledged version be lost only on a chunk a give-up records, and never lets a read return a version that was not written. The give-up deletes, as Ceph's `mark_unfound_lost delete`, rather than reverting: a store keeps one image per chunk, and a listed member's older bytes may lie under a later write, so there is no older image to trust, while an empty chunk is one every member agrees on.

**Reads: the old primary's read lease runs out first.** Reads need no acknowledgment from other members, so the write rule does not stop an old primary serving stale reads after the new one has accepted writes. A primary therefore serves reads only while it holds an unexpired read lease from each of the PG's other live members, and only while its interval has at least `min_live` live members, counting `maintenance`, as Ceph's PGs below `min_size` serve nothing; alone in such an interval it would need no lease and could serve after the PG moved on.

- A grantor joins no peering of an interval that moves the primary off its holder until the grant expires, or until the holder has adopted that interval, as its next lease request shows, so planned changes wait at most a heartbeat.
- A grantor grants or renews, fresh grants included, only a holder that reported a table at least as new as its own, so an old primary that reaches its members but not the control plane loses its leases within one `primary_lease`.
- Peering across several intervals adds no wait, since only the primary of the last activated interval, or of later ones that kept it, can have served reads. An interval that keeps the primary needs no wait: it serves committed versions and holds only writes until it has re-peered.
- A grant carries a *support epoch* the grantor keeps per holder within its run; a holder tells grants apart by the grantor's run ID and support epoch. Once a grant lapses on the grantor's clocks, the grantor bumps the epoch and never renews the old one. Primary leases need only the table check, but a Raft [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) counts a follower's expiry only in the support epoch it was fortified under, so without the bump a partitioned old leader could serve lease reads beside a new one.
- A restarted node has forgotten what it granted, so until both its clocks show one `primary_lease` since it started it grants no vote, joins no peering and asks for no lease, so its old grants lapse and Raft groups it led fail over; its first reads come about `rate_gap` after the wait. A new boot's clocks may run faster than holders measured the old ones, ending the wait up to `(1 − 1/ρ)·primary_lease` early (154 ms at 4% fast), which the reboot's own seconds cover.

Leases are granted per loop pair, covering every PG and Raft group with members on both loops, and renewed by heartbeats that already flow, so their cost grows with loop pairs, not PGs. A lease per node pair would cost less on a node of many loops, but its state would be shared by loops that own theirs outright ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)), or kept by one loop at a cross-core hop per read. Raft [leader leases](#metadata-layer-metastore) and client [sessions](#sessions-and-fencing) count by the same rules. Ceph added PG read leases for the same reason. `primary_lease` defaults to 4 s, shorter than failure detection, so a crash adds no lease wait unless a time daemon is slowing a grantor's clock.

**Lease time: two clocks, each side taking the safe one.** The holder counts a lease from when it asked and the grantor from when it granted, each on its own clocks, so leases need clock rates that nearly agree, not synchronized clocks. No single Linux clock promises that. `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` runs at the rate the time daemon sets: `adjtimex` allows ±10% of tick, ±500 ppm of frequency and 0.5 s of phase slew, 22.6% fast or slow in all, and chrony slews up to 8.3% by default. `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` ignores the daemon but runs at the TSC rate the kernel calibrated at boot, which nothing bounds: the kernel trusts an Intel CPU's reported crystal frequency, a wrong table entry ran Skylake-X 4% slow until December 2017, and VMs take their host's figure ([clock slew spike](#appendix-clock-slew-spike), [lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md)). So the holder ends a lease when either clock shows the lease, less its margin and `ResumeThreshold` (1 s, below), since it asked, and the grantor keeps it until both show the whole lease since it granted. A slew then only shortens a holder's lease or lengthens a grantor's wait, by at most 22.6%, so a crash can wait up to 5.2 s for a lease.

A node's two clocks can still err the same way, since ntpd and timesyncd correct at most 500 ppm and step for the rest, so each holder bounds how fast each grantor's raw clock runs against its own. A grant carries the grantor's raw reading `g`; with the holder's raw send time `s` and arrival time `r`, between grants `a` and `b` the grantor's clock ran at most `(g_b − g_a) / (s_b − r_a)` times as fast, a bound no delayed or asymmetric path can make too low; with a bound `B ≥ ρ`, a margin of `1 − 1/B` suffices whatever either daemon does. The margin is `lease_drift` (default 1%) plus the bound's excess over `1 + lease_drift / 2`, reported when nonzero, and a margin of `max_margin` (50%) gives no lease. That leaves at least `lease_drift / 2` (20 ms of a 4 s lease) of headroom for a rate that rises after measurement: a warming oscillator, a clocksource switch, a migrated VM. Naming the miscalibrated node of a reported pair by majority, as CockroachDB judges clock offsets, is not adopted: safety is per pair and each pair's bound suffices; whether to alert from it stays open ([clock skew study](../studies/clock-skew/README.md), recommendation 2).

- A bound across a stop of the grantor's clock, a suspend or VM pause, comes out too low, so the holder keeps its window, last grant and bound per grantor node and *run*: every grant and heartbeat carries a run ID the grantor's loop draws at start and at every `Resumed`. A new run ID or a raw reading below the last ends the old run's lease and starts a new window, as do a detected pause and the last grant aging past `rate_window` (default 10 s).
- On a window's first grant the holder asks again `rate_gap` (100 ms) later, and takes no lease before it has a bound. It pairs the shortest round trip of the window's first third with that of its last, so only a third of all-slow round trips loosens the bound: a full window overshoots about 600 ppm at 1 ms round trips, against 5,000 ppm of headroom, and the first lease runs 2.90 s instead of 2.96 s. A holder so pays about 100 ms without reads on a grantor when either starts, reboots or resumes, and after each detection.

A holder cannot see how long it slept: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` stops in suspend, and QEMU and Firecracker restore snapshots and migrations with clocks as saved, QEMU setting kvmclock back at a `stop` ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)). A holder cut off from its grantor would overrun the grant by the pause. So:

- A holder ends every lease on `Resumed`, which the [event loop](#runtime-in-go) delivers when `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` gains `ResumeThreshold` on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (only added sleep time changes that difference), or when the hypervisor's VMClock disruption marker changes, which QEMU 10.0+ (`-device vmclock`) and Firecracker 1.15+ do on every restore and migration, read from a mapped page at 3 ns a pass.
- The *step check*: every heartbeat carries the grantor's raw reading, and the holder ends its leases if `g − g_a > (r − s_a) / (1 − m)`, `a` the last grant and `m` its lease's margin. Tied to the margin, it never trips on a grantor whose rate the margin covers, as a fixed threshold would on any grantor 0.5% fast, and it runs only against a grant that gave a lease. It also catches a grantor stopped in place, which keeps its run ID.

The holder handles a pass's peer messages before its reads, so heartbeats queued during a pause end the old leases before a request that waited behind them, and after either detection it asks every grantor again and restarts its window. A grantor needs none of this, since a stopped clock only makes it wait longer. `ResumeThreshold` is 1 s: above the clock reads' ε, and within what the kernel's whole-second rounding leaves of any suspend of 1 s or more. A shorter suspend may deliver nothing, so the holder ends every lease `ResumeThreshold` early: a renewing lease runs 2.96 s from its ask, riding out one lost renewal, not two, and no failover lengthens; a longer `primary_lease` would win the second back only by lengthening every failover. Go's monotonic clock is `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, so the loop reads `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` with raw syscalls, about 140 ns each, between two reads of `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). Wall-clock steps move none of these clocks. What remains uncovered (a pause whose queued messages were dropped, a grantor stopped in place or speeding up while its heartbeats are lost, two sub-second suspends of a cut-off holder within one lease) is in the [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

**Failure detection.** Only the control plane changes membership, and its leader runs the failure detector, a sans-IO machine (`chunkstore.Detector`) the control-plane group hosts. Each disk's owner loop heartbeats every second, naming its disk by log ID, so one rule covers a crashed node and a stopped loop: a disk not heard from for `node_down_after` (default 5 s) is marked down, and up again at its next heartbeat that shows nothing wrong. A down mark starts a new interval of every PG with a member on the disk, so a crash pauses those PGs' writes for up to `node_down_after`, less when a primary's probe goes unanswered (below). The count runs on the leader's `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` alone: a mark changes liveness, never safety, which leases and peering carry, and a node's measurements, such as its oldest I/O, travel as ages, never as times of its clock.

A new leader starts the count from its own leader lease, so a crashed control-plane leader's PGs wait for the [failover](#metadata-layer-metastore) plus `node_down_after`. It takes each disk's mark from the disk record under [`ctl/`](#metadata-layer-metastore), where a down mark records its age, rounded down to a minute and rewritten each minute, so a failover neither starts new intervals of a dead disk's PGs nor restarts its `refill_after` by more than a minute plus the failover. A commit answered with an error restarts the detector from the state read back, losing only the flap history; that delays marks and never makes a wrong one.

Beside heartbeats, each node reports its release, whether it runs `-role meta`, its table epoch, the disks it opened by device and log ID, the devices it left failed at start, and its replicas [halted](#metadata-layer-metastore) at an undecodable entry. The leader keeps these in memory only, since a new leader hears every node within a second, and returns the failed devices, with each device whose log the record refuses, from `Control.FailedDevices`. Until it hears a node's report it drops that node's heartbeats, so no disk is marked up on an unknown release. A node's disks under `-role meta` stay in their pools as draining, so their members move off through balance moves, rather than leaving in one table as a lost disk's do, since the disk may hold a PG's only live copy. When an event comes more than two heartbeats after the detector's last, which only a stop of its own node causes, or on `Resumed`, every disk gets one more heartbeat before it counts as silent.

**Down is not gone.** A member marked down stays in its PG, marked down, and is refilled onto another disk only once it has been down for `refill_after` (default 10 minutes). Until then its PGs write to their live members, degraded, and a member that returns is marked up in a new interval, peers, and receives only the versions on its missing list, so a reboot copies what was written meanwhile, never the member's whole data; refilling at once would copy a rebooting node's members away and back. RADOS keeps a down OSD *in* the mapping until it is marked *out* for the same reason (Weil et al., §2.3), and Ceph marks it out after 10 minutes (`mon_osd_down_out_interval`). On 3 nodes under `rep3` no other node can take a member, so `refill_after` delays only refills within a node.

Two cases refill sooner. A disk whose log is gone, replaced or declared lost ([A disk is its log](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), leaves nothing to wait for, so its members refill at once. A PG below `min_live` accepts no writes, so its down members refill after `degraded_refill_after` (default 1 minute): long enough that a 5 s network hiccup or 1 s disk stall moves nothing, short enough that a stopped PG serves again within about a minute. A `rep2` pool, whose `min_live` of 2 puts every PG with a member down below it, so copies a rebooting node's PGs away and back unless the operator marks the node `maintenance` first; a member in `maintenance` is not refilled while its mark lasts ([Writes and `min_live`](#pools-and-redundancy)). An operator can also start a node's refills at once or hold them past `refill_after` until a time ([Actions](#actions)). Neither rule refills a member peering waits for, the only member live in some interval since the last activated one, since a new member would only wait beside it.

**A refilled member stays a source.** A refill member is no copy until its PG's recovered report, and the table cannot tell what a down member holds, so the down member may be a chunk's last source; deleted, it would leave the chunk [unfound](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) even after it returned whole. So refill keeps it as a *leaving* member, one member wider, with no stall of writes, removed only by the recovered report or its disk leaving the pool, unless an operator's edit or pin leaves it out. Ceph gets the same from past intervals, which keep an OSD in a PG's probe set until the PG is clean. Peering lists an unfound chunk on every up member, so a dead disk holds its PG wider only while it is a chunk's last source. Balance leaves a PG with a leaving member alone, a pin does not stop a refill, and the failure-domain rule ignores leaving members. A member that returns after its refill began still leaves, unless it takes a missing member's place, and does not lead again while another member is up. A PG can have several leaving members, since the table cannot tell which of two down members holds what, up to 8 members, the widest a primary fans out to; a leaving member whose disk leaves the pool goes alone. Two members that go down together, before a later interval activates, leave a `rep2` PG stopped until one returns, as Ceph's `down` state does.

**A member on a full disk moves before the report.** A disk is at its [log reserve](#on-disk-format) once its live blocks, its reserve and one chunk more would pass its size (the [recovery check](#client-design) with no grant out). A member there takes no more copies, so its PG never reports recovered and stays `Stuck`, and a move off it, which waits for that report, never starts. So refill counts as missing an up member on a disk at its reserve that the primary [reports](#volume-size-and-capacity) lacking copies once its PG is `Stuck`: it adds a member on the eligible disk furthest below its target and *parks* the full member as leaving in the same table, up to 8 members. Parked, it is a leaving member like any, a source for what it holds whole until the recovered report.
