It is parked rather than swapped out, because it holds writes acknowledged since it joined that its replacement lacks until its copy, and an up member leaves only beside a member the table adds; the model checks that a swap, or a park with no member added, loses data. If each replacement fills too, the PG stays `Stuck` until a disk is added or an operator edits it, and writes stall while any live member sits at its reserve. The leader judges fullness from live blocks only, until disks report the live blocks their log can hold ([Log room in the grant cap](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore)); a member short only of the grants' room is not parked, since cutting grants frees that ([Grants against a stuck refill](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore)).

**A refill member counts at once.** A member a refill or a move places is live, and counts toward `min_live`, from the table that adds it, while it still recovers. Every later write lands on it and the PG's other live members, and the chunks written before are on its missing list, persisted before activation and never served until recovery copies them. So a PG stopped below `min_live` serves again once the refill's interval activates, not once the copy ends, and `Stuck` reports a copy that stops ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)).

**A write to a chunk with too few clean copies.** A member that lists a chunk logs and acknowledges its new writes, but its blocks below may miss a kept version, so it serves nothing of the chunk until recovery clears its entry, and a write committed on it and one clean copy is lost with that copy. With `rep3` and `min_live` 2: W1 lands on disks 1 and 2; disk 2 goes down and disk 3 returns listing the chunk; W2 answered OK on disks 1 and 3 would be unreadable once disk 1 is lost, though acknowledged on two disks of a pool meant to survive one loss. So the primary holds a client write to a chunk with fewer than `min_live` clean copies (up members that list nothing of it, and members in `maintenance`), and any write while its own store lists the chunk, since the primary's is the one copy a write surely lands on. The write waits unordered until recovery, which takes such a chunk first, clears enough lists, as Ceph's `wait_for_degraded_object` does. Holding the order rather than the OK costs least: nothing is logged or sent while it waits, and peering has no extra version to decide. A held write counts toward the 64 a primary holds per PG; a write to a chunk no member lists pays nothing. The [epoch change model](#testing-strategy) checks that an OK rests on `min_live` such copies and that a held write is ordered in the end.

**Flap damping.** A node or disk marked down `flap_count` times (default 5) within `flap_window` (default 10 minutes) stays down until its heartbeats, and for a disk its I/O ages, have been normal for a whole `flap_window`, as Ceph stops an OSD marked down 5 times in 10 minutes (`osd_max_markdown_count`). Undamped, a node whose heartbeats stop for 5 s at a time starts two intervals of each of its PGs per flap; interval numbers would last centuries even so ([PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)), so damping is for the PGs each flap pauses. A damped member is down, for `refill_after`, from the mark that damped it. It is released at its first normal heartbeat when a PG it belongs to cannot serve without it (below `min_live`, or awaited by peering) and placement's own refill rule would not refill it, as on `rep2` with two storage nodes; its recent marks still count.

**Hung disks.** A node's heartbeats say nothing about its disks: a drive whose I/O never completes leaves its node and loop running, since an io_uring submission does not wait for the drive, while every PG on the disk waits. So each disk's owner loop keeps the age of the disk's *oldest unfinished I/O*, a read, write or flush, on its own clocks; discards are left out, since some drives take long over a burst of them, and a stall they cause shows in the I/O behind. A disk *stalls* when that age passes `member_timeout` (default 1 s), or when its open log has failed, which is final until recovery runs again ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)). Every heartbeat carries the age, so a stalled disk is marked down within about a second even when no primary waits on it.

A primary that waits more than `member_timeout` for a member's acknowledgment reports it, once per `member_timeout` while it waits, and the control plane sends a *direct probe* to the member disk's owner loop, which answers from memory, never with disk I/O. An answer showing the disk stalled or naming another log marks it down at once as stalled; no answer within `member_timeout` marks it down as silent, and it rejoins at its next normal heartbeat, so a short network cut keeps it down only as long as the cut. Either way a hung disk stops its PGs for 1 to 2 s plus a table. A stalled disk counts as failed, not slow: it is marked down even below `min_live`, since a PG waiting on it accepts no writes anyway. It rejoins as a fail-slow disk does, after a whole `slow_disk_window` normal or at its first normal heartbeat while a PG cannot serve without it, since holding it would reach `degraded_refill_after` and, on `rep2`, copy a whole disk away and back for one 1 s stall. A probe that answers healthy leaves the member up, right when it is only busy, but leaves the PG waiting when only the link between primary and member is down ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).

**Fail-slow.** A disk that slows down without failing would stall every write of its PGs, since writes wait for all live members. On each heartbeat a primary reports, per member disk, the 99th percentile of the acknowledgments it took since the last, from a histogram of two buckets per octave of microseconds, so counting allocates nothing and the figure is at most a third low, fine against a tenfold bar; acknowledgments of versions resent after a reconnect are left out. A disk is slow while its latest figure exceeds `slow_disk_factor` (default 10×) the median of the latest fresh figures of the other up disks of a pool it is in, its own figure left out so a slow disk cannot raise its own bar; only members acknowledge, so a disk is judged by the PGs it does not lead. A disk slow for a whole `slow_disk_window` (default 1 minute) without a gap in its figures is marked down as if failed, but never when that would leave a PG below `min_live`, counting the detector's own marks since the last table, so two disks slow at once are not both marked; those PGs stay slow and the pool reports the disk (`Detector.Slow`). A disk marked down as slow or stalled takes no writes, so its heartbeats judge it, and it rejoins only after a whole `slow_disk_window` with no stall and a working log, unless a PG cannot serve without it; flap damping holds it after `flap_count` marks.
### Wire messages

Client daemons, primaries, members and the control plane talk in messages the chunk store encodes by hand (`internal/chunkstore/wire.go`, [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)); each kind's fields are its type there. A message is a [driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers) `Msg`: a *frame* of at most 256 bytes inline, and optionally one pool buffer.

```
frame:  version (1) | kind (1) | request ID (8) | the kind's fields
PG fields: table epoch (8) | pool (4) | PG (4) | interval (4)
data:   the message's pool buffer, block i at byte i × 4096
```

- **The frame.** Integers are little-endian, as in the [disk log](#on-disk-format), and a chunk ID is its 16 big-endian bytes. A reply names its request's ID. Every message about a PG carries its PG fields, the sender's table epoch and the PG's interval in that table ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); interval 0 does not exist. A version packs interval k over counter n, so 1.1 is `0x0000000100000001`, and 0 alone is the absent chunk. An optional version (`expect_ver`, `at_ver`) is a flag byte and 8 bytes, zero when absent; the flag byte also marks a client's flush under a [space grant](#client-design). Every kind has one fixed layout except the three that carry lists (the heartbeat to the authority, the peering reply and the missing list), so the hot path sends only fixed-size frames.
- **Data is its own buffer.** A write's blocks and a read reply's travel as the message's pool buffer, never inside the frame, and the receiving driver reads them straight into a fresh pool buffer: every block lands block-aligned with no copy, and the decoder reads only the frame and the buffer's length. Padding the frame to a whole block ahead of its data would keep one buffer per message at 4 KiB of padding each; the driver contract already carries a frame and a buffer apart. Every request that carries data has a reply that can refuse it, so its sender always learns when to free the buffer, and a reply that carries a buffer is a `NetSend` marked `Reply`, freed once sent. The connection's own framing carries both lengths and a CRC32C over frame and data, as Ceph's msgr2 does in crc mode; a mismatch ends the connection as a `PeerLost` ([network driver](#runtime-in-go)).
- **Longer frames** (a report, peering reply or missing list with long lists) travel alone in a pool buffer, zero-padded to whole blocks. None is on the write path.
- **Whole blocks on the wire.** A client's write carries the blocks its byte range touches, with the range's bytes at their offset in the first, which is the layout the [store](#data-layer-chunkstore) takes, so the primary merges the edge blocks in place in the buffer it received. A sub-block write therefore costs a whole block on the wire (4 KiB instead of 512 bytes, 3.3 µs instead of 0.4 at 10 GbE); sending only the range's bytes would give every driver a second, unaligned layout. A member's write carries the merged whole blocks, and a member's punch carries the at most two blocks its range covers in part, merged with zeros, so a member never reads a base. A range is at least a byte long and lies inside its chunk; a client answers a zero-length write itself.
- **Fields for later phases travel from the start**, so none of them takes a new wire version: each write and punch carries its volume's snapshot epoch ([snapshots](#snapshots-and-clones), phase 11), and the client's session ID and incarnation, request ID and oldest outstanding request ID for the [resend table](#data-layer-chunkstore); a member's update carries the same identity. The phase 3 primary checks the incarnation ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)); the session ID is checked from phase 4 against the [session table](#sessions-and-fencing). A purger's punch names its shard and the shard's Raft term ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)); any other update carrying either is malformed.
- **Leases and peering** carry what [Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) needs and nothing more: a lease request only the holder's table epoch; a grant its support epoch and the grantor's run ID and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` reading, which every heartbeat of the loop pair carries too, but no table epoch, since the request reported the holder's. A peering request carries only its PG fields; the reply, the interval of the newest decision the member persisted for the PG (0 for none) and, for each chunk of the PG it logged or lists, its newest version held whole, its newest version logged and the kept version it lists ([Which versions survive](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). A member acknowledges its missing list once durable, or refuses it with its own table epoch and interval; a request for an older interval goes unanswered, and the primary learns of the newer table from heartbeats.
- **Errors are data.** A frame of another version is refused as such; any other mismatch (a truncated or overlong frame, a data length other than the block count times 4,096, a bad version, range or interval, a status its reply kind does not take) is malformed. Neither panics: a bad message is an event. Only the sender's own mistake, such as a long frame given a data buffer, is a broken invariant. A read reply does not repeat its range, so the requester checks the data's length against its own read. The frames of every kind are frozen in `internal/chunkstore/testdata/wire_golden.txt`; after the first release a change to them takes a new version byte.
### On-disk format

Each disk holds one log for everything stored on it: chunk data, the Raft logs of the [MetaStore](#metadata-layer-metastore) shard replicas placed on it, and the indexes over both. The daemon embeds no key-value engine and a disk needs no filesystem. The log belongs to one event loop, the disk's *owner*, which also owns its chunk index and serves its chunk writes; shard replicas on other loops hand it their records through its [inbox](#runtime-in-go). The disk is a sequence of fixed-size segments, written only through the disk driver's actions, so SPDK can own the whole drive and the simulator sees every durable byte. A disk is a raw block device or a file; a file's segments are written in full once when it is created, because a write into never-written file extents makes `fdatasync` commit the filesystem journal as well (2.5 ms instead of 0.95 ms in the [NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)). The log is `internal/disklog`, the tree under every index `internal/lsm`, the chunk index `internal/chunkstore`, and the round driver that feeds a log from other loops `internal/loop`. Every write is appended:

```
disk = segment 0:     superblock in its first and last block (log id, segment size and count)
       segments 1..n: segment header (log id, stream, lane, open sequence, newest manifest) + records

each round (what arrived from every producer during the last round, up to max_group_commit_bytes):
  record = header blocks: log id, segment sequence, offset, LSN, durable LSN,
                          entries [write | punch (pool, chunk_id, version, block range, last)
                                   | copy (pool, chunk_id, version, block range, source)
                                   | freeze (pool, chunk_id, version, snapshot epoch)
                                   | missing (pool, chunk_id, kept version, interval, image)
                                   | decision (pool, PG, PG count, interval)
                                   | writer (pool, chunk_id, version, session, incarnation,
                                             request, oldest outstanding)
                                   | blob (kind, owner, length)],
                          crc32c per data block, header checksum
         + data blocks:   written chunk blocks, then blob payloads (Raft entries and state,
                          sorted runs, manifests)
  append it to one lane's open segment in one write (after the segment header if the round
  opens a segment), flush once, acknowledge every item
```

- **One flush per round.** A round's records share one flush, however many shards' commits it carries: one combined commit for 100 shards took 1.2 ms, and 100 separate ones 114 ms ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). The owner holds its log for a whole pass, so the chunk writes and shard commits of one pass go out in one round. The header goes in the same write and flush as its data, and its own checksum plus the per-block CRCs make a torn record detectable on recovery. A round's write, segment header included, is capped at `max_group_commit_bytes` (default 1 MiB), which bounds how long a large chunk write delays a metadata commit: on the measured drive a commit costs 0.95 ms from 4 KiB to 64 KiB and 2.3 ms at 1 MiB. The cap is the only bound on a round: a smaller one costs throughput, since a round left behind waits a whole flush (256 KiB lost a third), and a bound on a round's collection time changed nothing ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). Each round writes one record to one lane of one stream (see Three streams), taking the lanes that have work in turn. At most `commit_depth` rounds are in flight, 1 by default, and a round's flush is submitted only once its own write and every earlier round's write have completed, so **durability is a prefix of the log**: when a record is durable, so is every record before it, and at a crash only the last `commit_depth` records can be torn or missing. A depth of 2 does not pay on a consumer drive, because a flush drains the drive's whole write cache: a hot 4K commit beside a stream of 1 MiB cold records took 2.3 ms with two rounds in flight, against 2.35 ms with both records in one round ([stream overlap spike](#appendix-stream-overlap-spike)). Whether a round should carry one record per stream under one flush is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
- **One owner loop per disk, not a log per loop.** A shard replica may run on any loop of its node. Once per pass, a loop hands each disk's owner one inbox message holding its shards' `Ready` records for that disk, and the owner hands back one message per round naming what became durable: about two inbox wakeups per loop per round, not per operation, and none for a shard on the owner's own loop. An owner fed by 8 loops wrote 39 items per flush where a log per loop wrote 8, at the same CPU per item, a wakeup costing 13.6 µs and a batched item 0.07 µs ([log per core spike](#appendix-log-per-core-spike)). A log per loop would give a disk as many flush streams as its node has loops, 4 to 16, past the two a consumer drive overlaps ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike), N7), split each round into smaller records with a header each, and give the format several LSN sequences. TiKV hands every region's writes to one store writer and raft-engine merges them into one sync; Pebble and RocksDB group every committing thread under the running sync, and CockroachDB runs every range of a store through one Pebble WAL. ScyllaDB keeps a commitlog per core, but acknowledges before a periodic sync, so it pays no flush per write. The round driver (`internal/loop`) keeps these contracts:
  - **A round asks only the shards that changed.** A loop collects the `Ready` of its marked shards in marking order up to `max_group_commit_bytes`, and the leftovers stay marked, first in line, so a shard waits behind marked shards, never idle ones, and a pass costs the same with 1,000 idle shards as with 10.
  - **One batch out per loop until the owner takes it**, meaning until the batch's first record is in a round the log started, and while fewer than `commit_depth` + 1 of its batches await durability. Loop and owner share a batch without a lock, through counts the owner stores once per pass; a loop whose batch is not yet taken sets a *waiting* flag so the owner wakes it on taking, and its next batch does not miss a round. With 8 producer loops, commit p99 stays within one round of one loop's.
  - **A record from another loop is copied into the owner's pool**, since a buffer handle names a slot in its own loop's slab ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). Only Raft records cross loops, as chunk writes are made on the owner, so the copies are bounded by loops × (`commit_depth` + 1) × (cap + one shard's `Ready`), about 16 MiB with 8 loops at the defaults. A batch that does not fit waits, untaken, for a later pass (a `PoolWait` in the trace), before the owner's own shards take pool room, and no proposal fails for want of buffers. Moving chunk writes off the owner would make these copies the bulk of a disk's bytes.
  - **The owner holds a pass's rounds until the pass ends**, so the batches it takes and its machine's own records share a round. A durable notice carries each record's place (segment, block, LSN, entry), which a Raft shard needs to report its blobs dead later.
  - **The owner's machine shares the loop with its logs.** Each log is attached with its own range of IO IDs, and a completion goes to the log whose range holds it, any other to the machine; a machine that calls into its logs (a chunk store's reads, checkpoints, the cleaner) implements `LogMachine` and gets their events after the driver has counted its tickets. A log is served (`Log.Serve`) only once recovered, and until then, or while a rebuild recovers it, other loops' batches and dead-blob reports wait. An event no one submitted is a broken invariant and panics.
  - **Recovery, checkpoints and the cleaner stay on the owner** and reach shards on other loops by mail on the hand-off (`MailRun`, `MailReplayed`, `MailRecovered`, `MailFlush`, `MailPiece`, `MailCheckpointed`, `MailFlushFailed`, `MailShare`, `MailRewrite`, with their `Reply…` answers). Mail is off the data path, under a mutex with its bytes on the Go heap; dead-blob reports, at the write rate, allocate nothing. A loop holds mail for a `ShardID` it has not added yet, since the owner must know a shard before its first blob ([Shards on a disk's chunk store](#metadata-layer-metastore)), and answers a piece ask for a shard it has removed as finished, so a checkpoint never waits on a shard that is gone.
  - **A removed shard is told nothing more.** `RemoveShard` retires its ID for good, so nothing addressed to it reaches a new replica of the same group. Its handed-off batches are still written, the loop freeing their buffers and reporting their Raft blobs dead, and its removed reply waits for them.
- **Write, then flush.** The driver makes a record durable with a write followed by a flush command (`IORING_FSYNC_DATASYNC` through io_uring, an NVMe Flush under SPDK), never with Force Unit Access (FUA) writes or `O_DSYNC`, which took 2.2 ms per 4K write on the measured drive. When every earlier round's write has completed, the log emits a round's write and flush together and the io_uring driver links them (`IOSQE_IO_LINK`), one `io_uring_enter` and no wakeup between the two. The write gathers the round's buffers in place, so chunk blocks are never copied: a header buffer the log fills, each producer's blocks in the buffer it submitted, and one buffer into which the log packs the round's blobs. The log takes each round slot's header and blob buffers from the pool once, at start, so producers that fill the pool cannot stop the round that would free their buffers. A disk error fails the log: queued items fail, and the items of rounds in flight are in doubt, since their writes may still land, so recovery decides. Any flush error fails the log, even one reported late for a round a later flush made durable, because the kernel may report a failed writeback to only one caller. Drives with power-loss protection may do better with FUA, so `durability` is a per-disk setting, `flush` (default) or `fua`.
- **Three streams, in lanes.** Records go to one of three streams, so that a segment holds data of one kind, which tends to die together: the *hot* stream takes new chunk copies and the Raft entries of the same round, the *cold* stream blocks the cleaner moves and encoded shards, the *metadata* stream sorted runs, compaction output and manifests, which are written whole and die whole. A stream fills its segments in *lanes*, each a sequence of segments with at most one open at a time, so LSNs rise along a lane, which is what recovery reads it by. Lane 0 of each stream is shared. Hot lane 1, the *slot lane*, takes each [chunk slot](#on-disk-format) write's record alone, so slot headers, which live as long as their chunks, share segments only with each other; cold lane 1 takes the cleaner's copies, so an ordinary item never appends to a segment opened from the reserve; metadata lane 0 takes checkpoints, and lane *w* merge worker *w*'s output, which continues one segment across merges so no tail is stranded behind run pieces the cleaner cannot move. `merge_workers` (default 1, at most 255) is one setting the log and the merge scheduler share; a lane left past it by a restart with fewer takes no more records. The two hot lanes take items in submit order between them, since replay applies a chunk's writes and freezes in LSN order, so a slot write costs a round of its own under mixed load (an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)), and a stall fails both hot queues whole with NoSpace, so no item of a chunk becomes durable after an earlier one failed. Mixed with the cleaner's copies, runs raised block-volume write amplification by 25 to 54% at 80 to 90% full, and in a stream of their own by 3 to 15% ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). Raft entries stay in the hot stream because a Raft stream would split a commit into two rounds and two flushes, to save 6%. More streams by temperature are not worth it yet: splitting the cleaner's copies by age, as SepBIT does, cut write amplification by up to 30% on hot/cold overwrites but raised it by up to 63% on uniform ones, and perfect hot/cold separation is only 1.1 to 1.6× below two streams once headers count. The stream tag and lane are a byte each, so a later stream needs only a new format version.
- **Chunk slots.** A chunk written whole, all 4 MiB in one write-back, goes to a *chunk slot* instead of the log: a slot segment holds as many slots of exactly one chunk as fit (16 in a 64 MiB segment), and a disk whose segments are smaller than a chunk has none. The round's record goes alone to the slot lane, its write entry naming the slot and its CRCs covering it; the slot's data goes out in a write of its own from the producer's buffer, and the round's flush follows both. A round carries at most one slot write, outside `max_group_commit_bytes`, so it still delays the commits behind it by at most a chunk. A whole chunk takes the lowest free slot of the fullest slot segment, or a newly opened segment, never one of the reserve, and goes to the log when there is none. A slot goes back once the index no longer refers to it after a durable rewrite or delete, no read in flight uses it and no unmapped slot write names it, and the log holds it until a round begun after its release is durable, so recovery never checks a slot against a newer chunk's data. A slot segment has no segment header: its usage-table row, in state *slot*, holds a bitmap of its slots, replay marks it from the records that write its slots, never from its first block, which is chunk data, and it reads as free only by a first block of zeros, which the driver writes behind its discard. Recovery checks a tail record's slots as it checks data in the log, but not those of records already named durable, which would read about 32 GiB per restart at the default `max_replay_bytes`; a damaged slot there is found by the read that reaches it, which checks each block. Whole chunks deleted at random then cost no cleaning, a write amplification of 1.004 at any fill against 4.9 at 90% full in the log, and a pool with half its writes whole writes 2.75 times what it is sent at 90% full against 6.1 ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). The record headers stay in the log, 0.4% of the chunk, in a lane of their own: among small writes each kept its neighbours' dead space from being reclaimed, and the cleaner, which never takes a segment holding a live slot's header, ran out of victims at 50% full. Rejected: copying headers forward, to win back the 0.9 to 1.2% of the disk the slot lane holds; a slot carrying its own headers, which caps a pool of whole chunks near 93% full; a stream of their own for whole chunks, which die as randomly as small writes across placement groups; and smaller segments for the whole disk (1.5 at 90% full, and 16 times the usage table). Partial chunks, such as file tails and small files, stay in the log.
- **Every header names its place.** Segment and record headers repeat the log id drawn at format time, the segment's open sequence number and their own position, and the log sequence number (LSN) orders records across the streams. A record header also names the newest LSN that was durable when its round was built, as Pebble writes the offset its write-ahead log (WAL) had synced into every chunk header, so recovery can tell a missing record that was durable from one still in flight. A block left from an earlier use of the segment or an earlier format of the disk, or written to the wrong address, therefore never decodes as a record; since the log id is random, neither does a user's data block crafted to look like a header.
- **Addresses belong to the log, not the device.** Every address the format stores (an extent's location, a record header's address, a copy's source, a chunk slot's segment) is a block of the log's own address space; today that is one device, block for block. That lets hard disk drives (HDDs) come later without rewriting an index entry. A log on an HDD alone would run every MetaStore commit on the disk at HDD speed, a seek per turn between lanes. The fix is Ceph BlueStore's DB device and ZFS's special vdev: one log over two devices, segment numbers running on from a flash partition onto the HDD, the hot and metadata streams on flash and the cold stream and chunk slots on the HDD, so small writes reach the HDD only as the cleaner's sequential copies. Each device's superblock then names its place in the space, a new format version, and both carry the one log ID, so the pair is one disk to placement and to [disk identity](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). The tier needs more than the split: reading every segment header of a 16 TiB HDD takes about 20 minutes, a deep HDD queue holds I/Os past the 1 s `member_timeout`, and drive-managed shingled (SMR) HDDs must be refused at format. Until then a disk is one solid-state drive, NVMe or SATA.
- **The chunk index maps extents, and the live map never overlaps.** A chunk's keys start with `(pool, h, chunk_id)`, big-endian, so a PG's chunks are one key range. That prefix alone holds the chunk's entry: its current version, the snapshot epoch of its last freeze, the newest version the disk holds whole, its [missing-list](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) entry if any, and the version its last recovery was logged under. Then comes its *live map*, `(…, 0, first block) → (version, location, block count, record header)`, one entry per run of consecutive blocks one record wrote. Applying a write trims or splits the entries it covers, as BlueStore's extent map and Btrfs do, so the map holds at most one entry per block however many versions wrote it and a live read costs one lookup per block; one entry per version resolved at read time would make a read of a fragmented chunk scan every version that touched it. A write covering two or more whole extents removes their keys with one range removal, and a whole-chunk punch keeps the chunk's entry as its tombstone and removes the live map with one, so a lookup never steps over dead keys. A read takes one *reader* per chunk, an iterator that probes the run filters once with the chunk's prefix and serves ascending blocks by stepping forward. A version a snapshot freezes keeps its map under `(…, frozen version, first block)`, behind `(…, frozen version)` holding its freeze epoch, outside the live map's range, so a punch never touches what a snapshot reads. Three reserved pools hold the rest: 2^32 − 1, per record header, the blocks the maps still refer to in it (a block a live and a frozen map share counting twice), so a record is dead to the cleaner at zero; 2^32 − 2, per PG, the interval of its newest persisted peering decision; 2^32 − 3, the [resend table](#data-layer-chunkstore). Decisions and resend entries written before a PG split hold for both halves, so a lookup walks the PG's ancestors, at most 32 keys. A missing-list entry takes effect only once its PG's decision entry, logged after it, is applied, so a disk persists a decision whole or not at all. A cleaner move changes every map, live or frozen, that still stores the copied blocks where the cleaner read them. Trimmed entries are gone, so a primary maps a write only once it [commits](#data-layer-chunkstore), and a chunk's writes in version order: out of order, an older write would overwrite a newer one and a freeze would copy writes made after it. A checkpoint's replay point never passes a pending write's record, so a restart applies again records its runs already hold, which changes nothing: every block ends at the last record in LSN order that wrote it ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike), R1, R2). Replay and rebuild hold each write of an interval at or past its PG's newest persisted decision unmapped until a decision entry settles it, so a write peering dropped stays out of the map ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), R3). The checksums stay in the record headers, and a read fetches its extent's header block beside the data. A chunk overwritten at random 4K holds one extent per write and the cleaner does not regroup it, so a block volume can push a 1 TB disk toward 270 million entries, more than 10 GB, whose sequential reads are random 4K reads, which NVMe handles well and SATA SSDs and HDDs less so.
- **The log is the source of truth.** The chunk index and each MetaStore shard's state are log-structured merge (LSM) trees inside the log. A memtable holds what was applied since the last flush; a flush writes it to the log as an immutable sorted run, in pieces of at most one record each, and compaction merges runs. A *checkpoint* flushes the memtable of every index on the disk that holds anything, then writes a manifest: a *snapshot*, naming each index's runs, replay LSN and merge bounds, the whole [segment usage table](#on-disk-format) and `NextSeq` (see Recovery), or an *edit* holding only what changed since the manifest before it, as Pebble and RocksDB keep their MANIFEST. A checkpoint writes a snapshot once the edits since the last one would exceed the smaller of that snapshot and the next, Pebble's rule (`record/rotation.go`), so recovery reads at most twice a snapshot; RocksDB's default writes a third less but lets recovery read six. Edits keep manifests small on large disks: on a 16 TiB disk at 90% full under 4 KiB overwrites, a full table in every manifest costs 6.6% of the log, edits about 0.16% ([durability study](#appendix-lsm-study-durability-spike)). A manifest larger than a record is written in pieces, the last listing the others, and segment headers point at that last piece. A checkpoint is due once `max_replay_bytes` (default 64 MiB) of records follow the last manifest, which bounds replay and the Raft entries a shard keeps in memory. If checkpoints fall behind and other producers' records after the newest manifest reach twice `max_replay_bytes`, group commit takes items only from checkpoints, merges and the cleaner until a manifest is durable, as Pebble and RocksDB stop writes at two full memtables, so that bound is hard. Checkpoints and merges write only to the metadata stream and do not count toward this hold, which a large snapshot or merge step would otherwise keep closed, and their queue never waits behind held items, which is safe because a checkpoint covers only records durable when it began. A flush costs what changed since the last one: a full snapshot at every checkpoint would write a 16 GiB shard 256 times over for each 64 MiB of entries. Replay time adds to restart time (fjall needed 2.8 s to replay a 64 MB journal, [KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). If the chunk index is lost, a scan of the record headers rebuilds it; a [punch](#data-layer-chunkstore) or a `freeze` is recorded like a write, so a rebuild cannot bring punched blocks back, and knows which versions a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) pinned.
- **Recovery.** The superblock gives the geometry. It is kept twice, in the first and last block of segment 0, so one bad block cannot make the disk unreadable, and a disk keeps the size it was formatted with; which copy wins, and when recovery refuses to write, is [Superblock repair](#superblock-repair). Recovery reads every segment header, many at a time since a disk has 4,096 to 262,144 of them, with the block after each, which holds the record written with the header. It starts from the newest manifest a header of this log naming its own segment points at, applies its snapshot and edits, has the index owners read their runs, and replays the records after the oldest replay LSN in LSN order, each index taking its own entries. Each **lane** is read from the last of its segments whose first record is at or before the replay point: LSNs rise along a lane, so that rule misses nothing, where the same rule per stream missed a record past the replay point in 74 to 99% of a model's crashes, since a merge's segment opens before, and keeps filling after, shared metadata segments opened later ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)). F2FS's checkpoint records where each of its logs stood in the same way. Merge lanes are read for their LSNs, which the tail rule needs, but their entries go to no index: a step's pieces count once a manifest names them. A segment header names the manifest newest when it opened, so replay can read up to a segment per lane past `max_replay_bytes`, plus what was logged while the checkpoint's runs streamed, plus, on a disk holding replicated primaries, what was logged while its oldest pending write waited, at most `node_down_after` of writes. Rebuild mode replays every record from LSN 1 and takes from the manifest only its usage table; it works only on a log the cleaner never cleaned ([The LSM tree](#on-disk-format) says why).

  Replay merges the lanes by LSN and stops at the first LSN it does not find. By the *tail rule*, that missing LSN ends the log only if no record header found past it names a durable LSN at or past it, every record header found past it, whole or torn, lies within the next `commit_depth` LSNs, and no segment header names a manifest at or past it: those were rounds in flight, and are dropped. Otherwise the log is corrupt at that LSN, and recovery reports it rather than cutting the log short, as [Protocol-Aware Recovery](https://www.usenix.org/conference/fast18/presentation/alagappan) argues: a replica that silently lost acknowledged writes must not rejoin as intact, so recovery never truncates on its own. Pebble confirms WAL corruption by the same durable offset; RocksDB's default, point-in-time recovery, opens with the prefix before any corruption, and its guard against a flushed table past the corruption is the manifest condition here. A scan that finds no valid record at a block reads the rest of its segment for later headers, so a damaged header cannot hide the records behind it. A prelude claiming more header blocks than `max_group_commit_bytes` holds is not a header, so one flipped bit cannot make recovery read a whole segment at once. Two valid records with one LSN are corruption. A dropped round's record may have landed whole and its LSN is reused, so before it reports the log recovered, recovery zeroes the first block of every record header it found past the end of the log and flushes, which also makes durable what a restarted process left in a volatile cache. A read error, or a failed write or flush, fails recovery, and a recovery that ends corrupt writes nothing.

  Which segments are free comes from the [segment usage table](#on-disk-format) of the loaded manifest, which lists every segment not free at its base LSN; a segment it quarantines stays quarantined and is not read again. A freed segment keeps its header until it is reused, so a header does not say by itself whether its segment was opened after the manifest or freed before it. Every manifest therefore records `NextSeq`, the open sequence number the first segment opened after its base takes. A header at `NextSeq` or more is a segment opened after the base: used, its row taken from replay. Below it, the table decides: listed, it keeps its row; left out, it is free and its header stale. That holds in every crash order of freeing, reusing and TRIM ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/#2-nextseq-in-the-manifest)). `NextSeq` is one past the newest segment a *durable* round had opened at the base, not the next sequence when the checkpoint begins: a round in flight may already have opened a segment, and a crash that keeps it would leave durable records in a segment below `NextSeq` the table does not list. Pebble and RocksDB judge their files by the next file number each manifest records in the same way, as F2FS judges node blocks by checkpoint version and NILFS2 and BSD LFS segment summaries by a serial.

  A segment left out of the table whose header reads as zeros or as another log's is free. Its block 1 may still hold the record of a round in flight that opened it again and whose segment header never landed, one at `NextSeq` or more; recovery erases that record and opens later segments past its sequence. Under a header of zeros, such a record may instead follow a header damaged to zeros, so the segment is read in full and its records count for the tail rule. A round appends to a segment only once the round that opened it is durable, which keeps block 1 the only place such a record can be. A segment is *damaged* if its header is otherwise unreadable or names another segment, or if the table lists it and its header reads as zeros or another log's; a segment header fits in one sector, so a torn write leaves it whole or as it was, and damage is not a crash. Recovery reads a damaged segment in full: a record header of this log past the replay point makes the log corrupt there; otherwise the segment is quarantined if the table lists it or it holds a record of this log, never reused and reported for repair from a replica, as NILFS2 never allocates a segment marked erroneous, and free if not. Format trims every segment but segment 0 and flushes before it writes the superblock and its copy, so what a device's earlier use left never makes a start read a segment in full; before its first trim it zeroes the copy and then block 0, each flushed, so a crash mid-format leaves an unformatted disk, never an old superblock over trimmed segments. A disk that is a file is never trimmed and needs neither. Nothing on disk records `commit_depth`, so the tail window uses the configured one: lowering it across a crash can report a torn tail as corruption.

  At start a storage node recovers each device and acts on how recovery ended (`chunkstore.StartDevice`): a log that serves is opened; a device whose block 0 and copy both read as zeros (`NotFormatted`) is formatted under a new log ID and opened, or reported failed if the format fails; anything else (`Unrecognized`, `CopyOnly`, a read error, a corrupt log) is never formatted, since it may hold the only copy of something, and is reported failed for the operator ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), as is a device the node cannot open, while its other devices serve. The only writes before that report are recovery's own repairs: a superblock rewritten from its valid copy, or zeros over a record of a round that never became durable. A log ID is 64 bits from the random source the node passes `StartDevice`, seeded from the operating system at each process start, so a format cut short and run again draws another ID; over n formats two share an ID with odds of about n²/2⁶⁵. The rule lives in `chunkstore`, beside the disk record that judges the log IDs nodes report, so the store's tests and `storetest` start a device as a node does.
- **The LSM tree** is a memtable, immutable sorted runs and compaction, behind one interface (`internal/lsm`). The memtable is a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages that refer to each other by `uint32` page number and sit in slabs outside the Go heap, so the garbage collector neither scans nor counts them ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)): a tree of one heap allocation per entry cost a quarter to a third of the CPU in collection at 10 to 20 million files, the page tree under 3 ms per collection with pauses under 1 ms ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike)). A removal is an entry that hides the key in older runs. A *range removal* hides every key from one key up to another, as RocksDB's and Pebble's `DeleteRange` do; a memtable keeps its range removals as non-overlapping fragments in a second page tree, as both engines keep theirs in a second skiplist, and removing an empty range (`lo ≥ hi`) is an error. Without sequence numbers, a fragment hides every older layer and none of its own layer's entries, Pebble's rule for an ingested table. A get stops at the first layer holding the key or a fragment over it; a floor moves every older layer below a covering fragment in one search; an iterator's floors inside one prefix step a layer forward from its last candidate instead of searching it again, unless it is more than 16 entries ahead (Pebble's `TrySeekUsingNext`). Without range removals, a chunk rewritten whole over 1,024 one-block extents cost 200 to 730 µs per lookup until a merge reached the old extents' run, which size tiering had not done after 192 GiB of further writes; with them, about two searches ([LSM runs spike](#appendix-lsm-runs-spike)). A full leaf hands entries to a neighbour before it splits, since pages filled in create order are otherwise 63% full; with it, leaves filled in random order are 80% full. A split needs each entry within half a page, so a key is at most 1,032 bytes (`MaxKey`, an S3 key's 1,024 bytes plus its 5-byte prefix, rounded to 8) and a value 1,000 (`MaxValue`); the MetaStore refuses a range bound over 1,000 bytes and stores a larger value as [pieces](#metadata-layer-metastore). A write that cuts its key out of a fragment can leave a bound one byte too long; the memtable then keeps the fragment whole and lets its own entries win over its fragments from then on, as a run's do. An entry that would not fit a run piece alone is refused when written.

  A flush freezes the memtable, starts an empty one and streams the frozen one into the log as a sorted run, one piece at a time, each built at its final size and handed over once the one before is durable, so its peak is the frozen tree, one piece and the run. A piece is one block in LevelDB's format, which Pebble keeps (prefix-compressed entries, a restart every 16), then a trailer; the run's last piece, its *root*, lists the others, so a manifest names a run by its root, and a run holds up to about 57 GiB. A run's range removals follow its entries in blocks of their own, and its own entries win over its fragments. A checkpoint names again the runs already durable and writes only the frozen memtable and new merge output; one that fails keeps the frozen memtable for the next. **Run checks** are always on, as RocksDB's `flush_verify_memtable_count` and `compaction_verify_record_count` are: a flush's run and drops must hold as many entries and fragments as the frozen memtable, and a merge's output and drops must add up to its inputs. Runs are never read back from disk, so each piece in memory keeps its CRC32C, verified before a merge reads it and as a flush writes it, so a flipped bit never reaches the log under a fresh checksum, for 4 bytes a piece and 12% of a merge's CPU on arm64. A failed check fails the flush or merge, and the index is rebuilt from the pieces on disk, checksummed when written, and the log after them, while the disk's other indexes serve; a piece that fails its CRC32C fails the rebuild with `Corrupt` for repair from a replica, and a check that fails again before a checkpoint completes stops the store, since the fault is then not in the log. Rebuilding by replaying the whole log stops working once the cleaner frees segments, which takes away LSNs, punches and freezes surviving writes still need, so the cleaner frees only segments whose records all precede the replay points of the oldest manifest recovery may load. Until a disk holds a second index, the chunk store rebuilds by refusing operations and recovering its log again (a [tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-an-index-rebuild-restarts-the-whole-log)). A MetaStore shard replica's log is truncated behind its flushes, so it rebuilds by catching up from a Raft snapshot.

  Runs stay in memory, pointer-free, as the blocks of their pieces, each block's first and restart keys its fence pointers, so a search never touches the disk, where a cold lookup costs 0.15 to 1.4 ms ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). A 16 GiB shard keeps up to about 16 runs, so, as in every RocksDB and Pebble table, each run carries a Bloom filter that rules out most runs that cannot hold a key; a lookup probes them newest first and searches only the runs a filter lets through whose bounds bracket the key. A filter never holds fragments, and a run's bounds include its fragments', so a lookup checks each run's fragments whatever its filter says: RocksDB twice returned keys a `DeleteRange` had removed because a prefix filter skipped the table holding it. The filter is RocksDB's full-filter format, probes in one 64-byte cache line, ported from Pebble's `bloom` package with its tests, about 1.2% false positives at 10 bits per key, and partitioned as RocksDB's partitioned filters are: one partition per merge step, fenced by the step's bound and holding the fence's prefix so a prefix straddling a fence is found, or one for a flush. A restore loads partitions rather than rehashing keys, and refuses a filter holding other keys than its index's configuration names. Each index names what its filter holds, as RocksDB's prefix extractor does, and its prefix must be closed (every key that starts with a prefix has it), or a prefix lookup would skip runs holding its keys. The chunk index's filter holds each chunk's 28-byte prefix, which its floors probe, as RocksDB's `SeekForPrev` does in prefix mode; the MetaStore's holds whole keys and each inode prefix `fs/{volume}/{inode}`, for gets and one inode's scans such as `readdir`. Over 24 runs, a MetaStore lookup of an absent name then cost 1.9 µs instead of 13 µs and a chunk-index read of a chunk written whole 5 µs instead of 32 µs, for filters of 0.8% and 2.9% of the run bytes ([LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike)); fewer, larger runs would not do as well, since six unfiltered runs already cost 4.3 µs for the miss and capping runs needs leveled merges that rewrite entries several times as often. Filters stay on the oldest run too, unlike RocksDB's `optimize_filters_for_hits`, because they are in memory and misses (a `create`'s name check, a read of unwritten blocks) are common. A scan merges its layers with a min-heap by key then age, as Pebble's and RocksDB's merging iterators do.

  A run takes 133 B per file of the spike's file model, against 199 B in the memtable ([MetaStore memory spike](#appendix-metastore-memory-spike), F4), plus about 3.5 B of filter, so a 16 GiB budget holds roughly 120 million file replicas, 40 million distinct files per node at three replicas, plus about 125 B per xattr.

  Compaction follows RocksDB's universal compaction, whose sorted runs are this tree's: each spans the whole key range, they are ordered newest first, and a merge takes only runs adjacent in age and puts its output in their place, so entries carry no sequence number; a run's sequence number orders the runs a tree holds at once, and a manifest names a run by its root, never by its number. Once an index holds four runs, counting those a running merge holds, the picker tries three rules in order, as RocksDB's `UniversalCompactionBuilder::PickCompaction` does:

  - **Size amplification** merges every run once the runs newer than the oldest hold 200% of its bytes.
  - **Size ratio** walks from the newest run, takes the next one while it is at most 1% larger than the runs taken so far, and merges the first span of two or more.
  - **Run limit.** If neither rule applies and the index holds more runs than its limit, the newest runs beyond the limit merge whatever their sizes.

  Each rule considers only runs no merge holds, and a rule whose span would make a run larger than a root can list picks nothing, so the picker tries the next rule; that limit leaves room for each step's filter partition and short pieces.

  The limit is RocksDB's auto-tuned `max_read_amp`, the runs a size-ratio tree needs from 1 MiB up to the largest run, about 2 + log2(largest run ÷ 1 MiB): 8 for a 44 MB chunk index, 13 for a 1.6 GB shard, 16 for 16 GiB. A fixed limit costs bytes rewritten: four runs cost 100 times the flushed bytes on a 1.6 GB index under steady flushes, since the second-oldest run absorbs every flush. Cassandra's size-tiered buckets merged only while flushes were steady, and left one run per flush when checkpoint flushes alternated, came in bursts or spread over sizes; universal compaction kept 2.3 to 6.7 runs on average under all of these, rewrote 2.3 to 6.3 times the flushed bytes, and its runs took 1.3 to 1.7 times the live index ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)).

  A flush or merge drops a removal or fragment unless a run older than what it writes holds a key it hides, checking older runs with cursors that only move forward, since removals arrive in key order. RocksDB keeps every removal through a flush and Pebble drops one only where no deeper file overlaps it; these runs span the whole key space, so only an exact check drops anything before the oldest run is merged. A range removal over a large range, such as a dropped volume's `fs/{volume}/` keys, frees nothing until a merge reaches the runs holding them. Pebble's delete-only compactions were weighed: dropping the blocks a fragment covers whole freed 22 of a dropped volume's 28 MB at the first checkpoint ([LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)), but pays only if a root could name pieces shared with the run it replaces, and no piece is ever named by two roots (see Cleaning), so a range removal frees its keys through ordinary merges.

  A merge reads only its immutable inputs, so it runs on a worker goroutine like the cleaner, and the owner installs its output. One scheduler per disk runs its indexes' merges, `merge_workers` at once (default 1), the index whose free runs most exceed its limit first, as RocksDB scores universal compaction. A merge installs its output in *steps*, as ScyllaDB's incremental compaction does: a step merges up to `compaction_step_bytes` (default 64 MiB), the owner appends it to the output and raises each input's *bound* to the step's last key, and the next checkpoint writes the step's pieces to the worker's lane (`MergePiece`), a new root and a manifest holding the bounds; when no checkpoint is due the owner writes one for the step alone, which leaves the replay floor where it was, so the records to replay still count toward the next checkpoint. Flushing the memtable at every step would add a small run per step for merges to take again, and restarting the count at a manifest that flushed nothing would let the memtable and the records to replay outgrow `max_replay_bytes`. A run holds only its keys above its bound, so output and inputs never hold one key; input pieces wholly at or below the bound die once that manifest is durable, and the piece across it stays whole, as a Pebble virtual sstable keeps its backing file. A merge's next step runs only once the manifest naming its previous one is durable (see Free space), so its space and memory peaks are a step. Installed whole, a merge held all its output until the next manifest, up to the whole index, which ran the [cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)'s disk out of its 8-segment reserve; in steps, 2 segments, for at most 0.6% more manifest bytes ([LSM space spike](#appendix-lsm-space-spike)). Pebble and RocksDB free a compaction's inputs only when it ends and bound its size instead, which suits levels cut into files but would leave whole-key-space runs unmerged. A restart resumes a merge past its bound. A worker's own lane lets later steps free whole segments (in the shared metadata segment, 30 to 57 stayed stranded instead of 8 to 10), and puts one merge's tail and the next one's head in one segment, which is not yet measured (an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). A reader or a Raft snapshot takes a point-in-time view, the memtable's root page plus the current runs, read on the owner's loop; while it is open, a write copies each page it changes that the view can reach.

  A block cache that reads runs from the log instead of memory comes when an index outgrows memory: a block volume's fragmented index does, so the cache lands before the block frontend at the latest, and a MetaStore shard does past about 40 million distinct files per 16 GiB at three replicas. Until then every index lives in memory whole, each node reports its indexes' memory against its limit, and `rock-solid node status` warns from 80%. Those runs will cut each piece into data blocks of 4 to 32 KiB, so a miss reads one block, with fence pointers and filters kept in memory outside the cache, as RocksDB does by default (`cache_index_and_filter_blocks = false`). The cache is one CLOCK cache over off-heap buffers owned by the index's loop, so it needs neither Pebble's sharded Clock-PRO nor RocksDB's lock-free HyperClockCache, which exist to serve many threads.
- **Versions share blocks.** An overwrite appends only the blocks it writes. When a write carries a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) epoch newer than that of the chunk's newest write the primary has accepted, pending writes included, the primary logs a `freeze` entry for the chunk's current version just before the write's entries, which durability as a prefix keeps ahead of the write, and every member copies the live map under that version's key before it applies the write. The frozen version so shares every block the write did not touch with the live version, without a reference count per block, for one entry per live extent, once per snapshot per written chunk. A chunk with no version yet freezes version 0, the *absent* chunk: its frozen map is empty, so a snapshot reads the chunk as never written (a miss, not zeros), and its epoch tells later writes in that snapshot epoch not to freeze again. Record entries carry no epoch, so without it a chunk first written after a cut would look written before it, and that snapshot and every older one would read it ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike), E1 to E3). Ceph keeps the same fact in each object's `SnapSet`. The absent freeze costs one 37-byte entry per chunk created in a volume that has had a snapshot, where an epoch in every write entry would cost 8 bytes on each. A punched chunk's tombstone keeps its version, so the first write after a cut freezes that version, and the snapshot reads zeros. The absent version holds no keys, so releasing it removes none, and its freeze replayed after the release changes nothing. A read at a snapshot picks the frozen version whose freeze epoch is the smallest at or above the snapshot's, or the live map when there is none. A version can be frozen twice, when a write lost in a crash after its freeze leaves the chunk at the frozen version and the next write, at a later cut, freezes it again: the frozen map then takes the later epoch, as does the chunk's entry, since both snapshots read the one map, and a freeze replayed at an epoch no newer than the map's changes nothing.
- **Cleaning.** A block is live while the live version, a snapshot or an unflattened clone still maps to it. A Raft round's blobs are dead once every index the round holds is compacted after the memtable that applied them was flushed, and a later round of its shard holds a newer hard state ([Raft shard](#metadata-layer-metastore)); a sorted run once compaction has replaced it. A manifest, what its last piece lists, the runs it names and every record after its replay points stay live until a segment header naming a newer manifest is durable. A freed segment may be reused at once, since recovery tells its new header from the old by `NextSeq` (see Recovery), provided the cleaner frees nothing the newest manifest named by a durable segment header still needs and no index's replay LSN passes a copy record whose moves its runs do not hold; F2FS's *prefree* state, held until the next checkpoint, would still need `NextSeq` and add a wait in allocation. The cleaner never copies a manifest piece (a checkpoint writes the next snapshot early when the cleaner asks for its segment) nor a piece of a live run: it marks the run for a *rewrite*, a merge of the run alone in steps, so no piece is ever named by two roots and the usage table counts each piece once. Copying the piece and naming the copy in every root that lists it would need those roots rewritten in one checkpoint and a count per piece while a view or an older manifest still holds an old root; a rewrite reuses merge steps, so the reserve still holds one step per worker. It may take a used segment no lane has open, holding no record header of a live chunk slot, whose records all precede the replay points of the oldest manifest recovery may load. It picks up to 4 victims a pass: first segments with nothing live, since nothing ages while writes wait and a metadata segment its merges just emptied would otherwise score 0 while the cleaner copied nearly full victims until the reserve drained; then by cost-benefit, (1 − u) × age ÷ (1 + u) for live share u, the original log-structured file system's rule, a segment's age being the ordinary blocks written since its youngest, with copies keeping their source's age and age capped at one disk capacity of writes, so old, nearly full segments such as snapshot data stop looking attractive. Live contents are copied grouped by chunk in key order, which wrote 6 to 7% less than log order under hot/cold overwrites, and a victim is freed once its copies are durable and no read in flight reads from it, since a reused segment could hand such a read another record's bytes. A chunk overwritten at random is not regrouped: merging moved extents with their neighbours removed at most 0.7% of the index's entries ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). Net-gain guards keep the cleaner from livelocking, as the [cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike) saw without them: a victim must free more than copying it costs, records, headers, the tails rolled-over copies leave and an estimated 134 bytes of index run per moved block included; its copies must fit the room the cleaner's lane can reach, reserve included; and a pass that frees nothing starts no other until the durable LSN moves. On skewed overwrites, cost-benefit with two streams writes 36 to 45% less than greedy cleaning, on uniform ones 3 to 14% more; no separation policy simulated beats two streams on every workload, and none of WiscKey, BlobDB, Titan or Pebble separates its cleaner's output by temperature ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). TRIMs are paced at `trim_rate` (default 128 MiB/s, CockroachDB's file deletion rate), faster only to clear a backlog older than 5 minutes within 5 more, as Pebble's delete pacer does, since some SSDs stall on bursts of discards; a TRIM frees no log space, so low space never speeds it up, and a lost or failed one costs only the discard. A freed segment's TRIM is queued only once a header naming the freeing manifest is durable, a segment allocated again drops a queued TRIM, and allocation passes over one in flight, as F2FS does, so a late discard cannot erase new records. The driver writes zeros over the header behind every discard, so it reads as zeros whatever the drive does ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)); a disk that is a file ignores TRIM.
- **The cleaner runs beside the writer, not on it.** Its copying runs on a worker goroutine, never on the event loop that owns the disk's index and serves its writes: inline, cleaning cost that loop 5.9 to 11 µs per write at 90 to 95% full and a write p99 of 250 to 540 µs, with a worker 1.6 to 1.8 µs and about 6 µs ([parallel cleaner spike](#appendix-parallel-cleaner-spike)). The index is single-writer, so the owner lists a victim's live blocks and reads them into pool buffers that become the copy items' buffers, nothing copied in memory, with at most 16 reads in flight a pass. The worker verifies each block's CRC32C against its record header and sorts the runs into key order; the owner hands them to the log as `copy` entries carrying those checksums, so no checksum of a copied block is computed on the owner's loop, and applies each move when its record is durable, only where a map, live or frozen, still points at the source, so a write that lands during cleaning wins in every order (Titan's garbage collector guards its write-back the same way). A victim holding a block that fails its checksum is quarantined, as a damaged segment is: nothing of it is copied, and reads of the block keep failing, since a single node holds no other copy. Applying costs the owner one index entry per moved block, about as much as applying a write. Pebble instead remaps a rewritten blob file behind a stable ID, and RocksDB moves values only inside compactions; neither pays with an in-memory index: a remap per record wrote 19 to 33× fewer index entries but sent 63 to 96% of reads through an extra lookup and forced copies into log order, and relocating in compaction would rewrite the whole index ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). A disk gets one worker, and a second once free segments outside the reserve fall two below `clean_free`, to 14 by default, as RocksDB and Pebble add compaction threads under a backlog; an idle extra worker costs CPU and speeds nothing up.
- **Writers slow down before the log runs out.** Free segments outside the reserve play the part of RocksDB's and Pebble's level 0. The cleaner keeps `clean_free` of them (default 16); at RocksDB's level-0 proportions a second cleaner worker joins at 14, chunk writes slow at 8, and at none every ordinary item waits. A slowed disk admits chunk writes through a token bucket counted in blocks, as CockroachDB's admission control hands out tokens from the bytes compacted out of level 0; RocksDB instead slows to a fixed rate it scales by 0.8 or 1.25, and Pebble only stops. The bucket refills at the rate the cleaner freed space over one-second windows, net of everything it spent (copies, checkpoints, merges, record and segment headers and lane tails, `BackgroundBlocks`), so the rate is the room left for ordinary items; net of copied blocks alone, it would admit writers into the room its headers, index runs and manifests take. Each window's rate is averaged with the last, as CockroachDB smooths at the same weight, because near full a window often frees nothing while a victim's copies outweigh it; a window that spent more than it freed carries the difference forward. The rate scales from 1 at 8 free segments to 0.5 at none, absorbing what writes cost beyond their blocks, and the bucket holds one window's worth. A write goes out while the bucket holds a token and is charged all its blocks, so a whole chunk still goes out when the cleaner frees less than 4 MiB a window. The bucket starts with no rate and charges nothing, as CockroachDB starts with unlimited tokens, and charges only while the cleaner has a pass to run: with no victim at the current durable LSN, the log's wait and `NoSpace` decide. Raft entries, punches and freezes are not charged, but an item behind a held write of its chunk waits with it. A refused chunk write stays with the store in order, and while one is held the loop stops reading the connections that send chunk writes, so clients see TCP backpressure ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). A disk the cleaner cannot free gets `NoSpace`: no victim passes the net-gain guard and fits the room its lane can reach, or the cleaner's own copies wait with no segment free at all. An index over its run limit only costs lookups, so run count never stops writes, unlike level 0.
- **Free space is recorded, and some of it is reserved.** The manifests carry a segment usage table as of their base LSN: one row per segment that is not free, with its state (used, slot, or quarantined), its live blocks or a slot segment's bitmap, and its newest record's LSN, 17 bytes a row (under 136 KiB below 256 GiB, 272 KiB per TiB above); an edit holds the rows that changed, a freed segment as state free. The log counts a durable record's data blocks live (header blocks are dead to the cleaner), and the owners lower the counts as data dies: the chunk index reports every change to a record's count (a trim, a punch, a released version, a move, and a freeze, which adds), so a chunk segment counts what the index counts, and an index reports a run piece dead once a checkpoint drops it, which the log keeps live until a segment header names that manifest. The counts a manifest holds are advice: recovery decides free or used by state, and at every open the counts are set again from the index and the loaded manifest's pieces, since counts carried across a crash drift as replay applies again records they reflect. Counting a record live until it is wholly dead would overstate partly dead records under 4K overwrites, and counting distinct blocks would cost a lookup per frozen version on every trim of a snapshotted chunk. A segment is free because the table says so, never because its header is unreadable, as in F2FS and the original log-structured file system. A *reserve* of segments is kept for the work that frees space (checkpoints, the cleaner, merge steps), each of which frees at least what it writes once its manifest is durable; the tree picks a merge's next step only after the checkpoint naming the previous one, and a merge keeps its worker and lane until a manifest names its last step, so merges hold at most one step per worker beyond the manifests. Why not lane priorities instead is in [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered). The reserve is ⌈(2 × `max_replay_bytes` + workers × `compaction_step_bytes`) ÷ segment⌉ + victims per pass (4, covering both cleaner workers), and at least 8, the reserve the cleaner spikes ran with: 8 on a disk of 64 MiB segments with one merge worker, 772 of 4,096 on a 1 GiB disk. The log works it out at format and at every open from its settings and the superblock's segment size; a disk whose reserve would take every segment is refused with a bad-settings error rather than run with less, and only tests run with none. An ordinary item never opens a segment while free segments number the reserve or fewer, and checkpoints and merges write to a stream ordinary items never share, so the reserve goes only to work that frees space. RocksDB's `SstFileManager` reserves the same three things, as ZFS's slop space and Btrfs's global reserve keep room for freeing space. A checkpoint that finds no segment free at all waits like a write and fails with `NoSpace` on the no-victim report (`CheckpointFailed`): quarantined segments or settings below the formula can shrink the reserve, so this is an event the owner handles, not a broken invariant.
- **Segment count matters, segment size does not.** At a fixed number of segments, write amplification is the same from 1 to 256 MiB per segment. What drives it near full is the share of the disk the cleaner keeps free: a reserve of 8 segments costs nothing on 4,096 segments, but on 256 it raised write amplification at 95% full from 17 to 70. So a segment is min(64 MiB, disk size ÷ 4096), rounded down to a power of two: 64 MiB on disks of 256 GiB and up, with 0.5 MiB of segment metadata per TiB. Large segments, written sequentially and trimmed whole, also suit the drive's own garbage collection.
- **Headers are capacity.** Header blocks are dead to the cleaner, so near full they cost far more than their bytes: at 95% full, the headers of 64 KiB records with 32-byte entries raise write amplification from 11.4 to 17.3, and at 90% full, queue-depth-1 4K writes, one header block per data block, double it. The header encoding is part of the format and must stay compact: a write or punch entry takes 33 bytes, 8 of them the [version](#data-layer-chunkstore) (its PG's 32-bit interval number over a 32-bit counter per chunk), a freeze 37, the 8 after the version being the snapshot epoch, a write to a chunk slot 6 more for its slot, and a cleaner's copy 8 more for its source, plus 4 bytes of CRC per data block. How full a disk may get is the pool's [`write_limit`](#volume-size-and-capacity).
- **Whole blocks are the floor.** A record's chunk data is whole blocks, written by the primary's [merge](#data-layer-chunkstore) where a write covers a block in part, so a file of 1 to 4 KiB takes one block per copy, as in XFS, BlueStore since Pacific and Vitastor: 12,399 raw bytes in `rep3` with its entry and CRC, 8.1 times a 1.5 KiB file, and as much in an erasure-coded pool, where such a file [stays as copies](#when-chunks-are-encoded). Per copy it also costs about 108 bytes of chunk index beside the inode's 133 bytes in the MetaStore. A fsync'd append of 100 bytes rewrites its tail block: alone on the disk, 8.3 KiB per copy; with 8 appenders sharing each round, about 2 blocks per append; among overwriters, about the log's write amplification ([sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)), about what a local filesystem writes per fsync. No layout below a block pays for itself yet: inline data in the inode would put file bytes in the MetaStore's in-memory runs, 12.5 times the per-file budget for a 1.5 KiB file; byte-granular patch entries help only when their bytes ride in header blocks, which would then hold live data; and storing a file's last block short saves 2.6 times on a 1.5 KiB file for a stored length in every write entry and extent and liveness counted in bytes. The last two are [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions); old write entries stay valid under either, so neither needs a migration.
- **Upgrades.** Segment headers, record headers, sorted runs and manifests carry a format version. Until the first release no disk holds data worth keeping, so the format is version 1 and changes in place, its golden fixtures rewritten; from the first release on, every change takes a new version. A new form of log entry takes an entry kind of its own rather than changing an entry's size, so old records still decode. The chunk index's values make no such promise: a chunk entry that does not decode fails the open that loads its run, and the node reports the device failed, as for a corrupt log. A daemon reads the current and the previous format, but writes the new one only after the operator raises the cluster's format level (a `ctl/` key) once every node runs the new release; the control-plane leader refuses a level that a node heard from within `node_down_after` runs below, or before it has heard from every node with a disk up, both when the operator sets it and again when it commits. A node on an older release that heartbeats after the raise is not marked up, and a condition names it, as Ceph's monitor ignores the boot of an OSD below its minimum release. Until the level is raised, rolling back is safe. The cleaner rewrites old segments over time.

**Why one log.** For chunk data the alternative is a journal plus in-place data (Vitastor, BlueStore's deferred writes): a small write is journaled, acknowledged and copied into place later, big writes must skip the journal or media bandwidth doubles, and sharing blocks with snapshots needs a per-chunk extent map with reference counts, BlueStore's most complex part. For indexes, shards and Raft logs the usual choice is an embedded key-value engine, which opens and flushes files through the kernel itself: it cannot run on a drive SPDK owns, the simulator cannot crash it, and its flushes, one per Raft group if each keeps its own log, come on top of the chunk log's. One log gives one sequential write and one flush per durable write, shared by every shard on the disk, and snapshot block sharing, checksums and torn-write detection come with the format. This is value separation as WiscKey introduced it and RocksDB's BlobDB, TiKV's Titan and Pebble ship it: the index holds locations, the data stays in a log, and a cleaner copies live data and updates the index ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). It also rules out Raft libraries that do their own storage I/O. The price is the cleaner and the LSM tree, and the cleaner's write amplification, which grows as a disk fills.

#### Superblock repair

A block 0 that does not decode is not always damage. A disk can be reused without the copy at the end of segment 0 being cleared: a partition table (`parted`, `sgdisk`), a new filesystem, an LVM or BlueStore label, or a partial wipe with `wipefs` or `dd` all write at the start of the disk, and the copy sits up to 64 MiB in. Device names such as `/dev/nvme1n1` can also change across reboots, so a node can be pointed at another disk that once held a log. Writing the copy back over such a block 0 destroys the new owner's data. The failures the copy is for are a block 0 the drive cannot read (NVMe reports uncorrectable media as an error, not as flipped bits) and a block 0 written torn or rotted in place, which keeps this format's magic and fails its checksum. So what block 0 holds decides:

| Block 0 | Recovery |
| --- | --- |
| A valid superblock | Uses it, and rewrites the copy if the copy differs |
| Unreadable, or this format's magic and version with a bad checksum or inconsistent contents | Uses the copy if it is valid and sits where its own geometry puts it, and rewrites block 0; else reports the read error or `Unrecognized` |
| Zeros, another magic or another format version | Writes nothing. Reports `CopyOnly` when the copy is valid, `NotFormatted` when block 0 and the copy are both zeros, else `Unrecognized` |

A repair is flushed before recovery reads any segment header. Restoring the copy over a block 0 of the last row, or over a bit flip inside the magic or version, is an operator's decision, through a repair command. Other systems split the same way. ext4, XFS and Btrfs mount from the primary superblock only and leave their copies to offline tools (`e2fsck -b`, `xfs_repair`, `btrfs rescue super-recover`, which asks before it writes). The Linux kernel reads the backup GPT header only when a protective MBR in block 0 still marks the disk as GPT, and never writes it ([`block/partitions/efi.c`](https://android.googlesource.com/kernel/common/+/e6cfc1098ef0/block/partitions/efi.c)). Ceph BlueStore refuses to start an OSD whose label copies disagree and repairs them only through `ceph-bluestore-tool repair`; stale copies left by `sgdisk --zap-all` and `wipefs` have kept reused disks from starting ([IBM note](https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/node/7242545)). ZFS rewrites its labels automatically, and has rewritten them over a disk reformatted with UFS ([FreeBSD bug 204622](https://bugs.freebsd.org/bugzilla/show_bug.cgi?id=204622)). mdadm's superblocks near the end of the device are called dangerous with auto-detection by the [kernel RAID wiki](https://raid.wiki.kernel.org/index.php/RAID_superblock_formats).

### Pools and redundancy

Redundancy is set per **pool**: a named set of placement groups that share one redundancy scheme, one failure-domain rule and, optionally, one class of disk. Every block volume and RWX filesystem belongs to one pool, named by its StorageClass:

```yaml
kind: StorageClass
metadata: { name: footage }
parameters: { pool: ec4-2 }
```

Pools are cluster objects (a `Pool` custom resource, stored under `ctl/pool/` in the control-plane group); settings, defaults and creation rules are `internal/chunkstore/pool.go`.
