Installation creates `rep3` as the default pool. Every pool setting is the user's choice; the defaults suit a small team that wants its data protected but watches cost. They never lower how many failures data survives, and otherwise pick the option that uses less space.

| Setting | Default | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `replicas` | 3; `parity + 1` in an erasure-coded pool | Copies of a mutable chunk, 1 to 3 |
| `sealed` | `replicated` | Layout of a chunk that is no longer being written: copies, or `ec` with `data` and `parity` shards |
| `encode_on_write` | On | Encode whole new chunks as they are written, skipping the copies stage |
| `seal_after` | 1 hour for files, 24 hours for block | Idle time before a chunk held as copies is encoded |
| `min_live` | `min(replicas, 2)` | Live PG members needed to serve reads and writes, 1 to `replicas` |
| `failure_domain` | `node` | `node` or `disk` |
| `max_per_domain` | 1 | Shards of one PG allowed in one failure domain |
| `locality` | `node` with one copy, `spread` otherwise | Keep each volume on one node, or spread it over every disk |
| `overcommit` | On | Let volume sizes add up to more than the pool holds |
| `write_limit` | Per disk by segment size: 90% on disks of 128 GiB and up, 85% from 32 GiB, 75% from 16 GiB, lower below | Disk fill past which writes that add data are refused; see [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) |
| `disk_class` | Any | Only disks of one class, such as NVMe. A disk's class is the one its node reports when the disk joins the [disk record](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), committed with its entry |
| `pg_count` | Sized from the disk count | Power of two; see [placement groups](#data-layer-chunkstore) |

**Overwrites only ever hit copies.** A 4K overwrite in a 4 data + 2 parity stripe reads the old block and both parity shards, then writes the block and two new parity shards, which must land together on three nodes or leave a write hole after a crash: 3 reads and 3 cross-node writes, where three copies cost 3 writes and no read. So a chunk in an erasure-coded pool takes one of two forms, `replicas` copies or `data + parity` shards, and is only overwritten as copies. It is encoded when it is written whole or once it goes idle, as [Windows Azure Storage](https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/atc12/atc12-final181_0.pdf) does with its extents ([When chunks are encoded](#when-chunks-are-encoded)).

**Presets.** Names follow `rep<copies>` and `ec<data>-<parity>`; any combination within the rules below is allowed.

| Pool | Copies | Shards | Survives | Failure domains needed | Usable / raw, encoded | Suits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `none` | 1 | None | Nothing | 1 | 1 | Caches, CI scratch, apps that replicate themselves |
| `rep2` | 2 | None | 1 failure | 2 | 1/2 | Two storage nodes plus a [metadata-only node](#one-copy-pools-and-locality); re-creatable data |
| `rep3` (default) | 3 | None | 2 failures | 3 | 1/3 | Block volumes, databases |
| `ec2-1` | 2 | 2 + 1 | 1 failure | 3 | 2/3 | Media, photos and backups on a 3-node cluster |
| `ec4-2` | 3 | 4 + 2 | 2 failures | 6, or 3 at 2 per node (then 1 node) | 2/3 | Media, backups and S3 |
| `ec16-2` | 3 | 16 + 2 | 2 failures | 18, or 9 at 2 per node (then 1 node) | 8/9 | Archives on a large cluster |

An erasure-coded pool keeps `parity + 1` copies of a chunk by default, so a chunk survives the same number of failures in either form. "Usable / raw" counts encoded data; data held as copies (being edited, or idle for less than `seal_after`) costs `replicas` times its size.

**Rules checked when a pool is created.** A refused setting is named in the error.

- **Width fits.** Copies always sit in distinct failure domains. Shards may share one up to `max_per_domain`, but never a disk, so a domain takes at most min(`max_per_domain`, its disks) shards of a PG, and the sum over the domains must reach `data + parity`, counting only disks of the pool's class.
- **Node tolerance is printed.** With `max_per_domain` above 1, one node failure takes out several shards of a PG, so an encoded chunk survives `⌊parity ÷ max_per_domain⌋` node failures: `ec4-2` at 2 per node fits on 3 nodes and survives one (as Ceph's `crush-osds-per-failure-domain` does), `ec16-2` at 3 per node fits on 6 and survives none. The CLI and `rock-solid pool status` state, for every pool, how many node failures its copies and its shards survive (none under `failure_domain: disk`, which keeps no PG's members on distinct nodes), and name each single loss after which the survivors cannot hold a PG's width, such as any node of `rep3` on three nodes: "losing node A leaves its PGs degraded until it returns". Such a loss is a fact of the layout, not a [condition](#volume-size-and-capacity).
- **Settings in range.** `replicas` 1 to 3; `min_live` 1 to `replicas`, as Ceph bounds `min_size` by `size`; `pg_count` a non-zero power of two, which later only doubles; `write_limit` above 10%, so that `NearFull` has a range below it, and at most 100%. Until erasure coding arrives in phase 10, `sealed: ec`, `data`, `parity` and `encode_on_write` are refused.
- **Shape is fixed, the rest is not.** `data` and `parity` are fixed at creation, as in Ceph; to change them, [move the volumes](#moving-a-volume-to-another-pool) to another pool. Every other setting can change later; a change to `replicas` or `min_live` starts a new [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) of each of the pool's PGs.

**Writes and `min_live`.** A write is acknowledged once every live member of the chunk's PG holds it ([The primary's write path](#data-layer-chunkstore)). A PG with fewer than `min_live` live members rejects writes rather than accept them onto too few copies, and reads too, since peering skips such an interval and a primary left alone in it could read on after the PG moved on ([Reads](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). A member that joined after a write's interval holds no copy of it until recovery reaches the chunk, so a write to a chunk with fewer than `min_live` copies that can serve it waits for that recovery ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). A stopped PG waits for a member to return or a refill to add one, which counts as live while it still recovers; its down members are refilled after `degraded_refill_after` (1 minute) rather than `refill_after` ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). So under `rep2`'s default of 2 a PG serves nothing while either of its disks is down, for up to a minute plus a peering.

A planned reboot avoids that with `rock-solid node maintenance <node> --for <duration>` ([Actions](#actions)): the mark takes the node's members out of the live set, so writes stop waiting for them, but they still count toward `min_live`, so the node's PGs accept writes on one copy until it returns and resyncs. The operator chose that exposure until the mark's deadline, and [peering](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) counts members in `maintenance` the same way, so it never skips an interval that accepted writes under the mark.

The mark ends at `--end` or at its deadline, whichever comes first, and each member is then marked up if its disk heartbeats and down otherwise, from which `refill_after` counts: a mark that never ended would keep a lost node's PGs on fewer copies with no refill. The deadline is kept in the disk record as the time left, rounded down to a minute and rewritten each minute, as a down mark's age is ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), so a control-plane failover moves it by at most a minute plus the failover. The epoch change model allows a mark from up to `maintenance`, from there back to up, and to down as an expired mark on a node that does not return. Only an up member enters maintenance, which is all the model allows: a down member has already missed writes, so the mark would save it no copying, while counting it toward `min_live` would reopen a stopped PG on fewer copies, a choice `min_live` exists to make, and would hold off its refill. Setting `min_live: 1` permanently takes the same exposure for unplanned failures, the setting Ceph users most often report losing data with.

**Encoded chunks need no write quorum.** An encoding commits only once all `data + parity` shards are durable, and only then are any copies dropped; a PG with a member down writes copies and defers encoding until recovery. An encoded chunk stays readable while any `data` of its shards survive. Ceph recommends `min_size = k + 1` for erasure-coded pools because it accepts writes to a stripe while only `min_size` shards are live; an encoding here waits for every shard, so the rule does not arise.

#### When chunks are encoded

In an erasure-coded pool, a chunk's copies sit on the first `replicas` members of its PG and its shards on all `data + parity` members, so changing form moves no data between PGs. Shard `i` is the chunk's `i`-th contiguous byte range, so a 4K read of an encoded chunk touches one disk; a read whose shard is lost fetches `data` other shards and decodes. A chunk is encoded only if its shards, each rounded up to a 4 KiB block, take less space than its copies, so a small file's only chunk stays as copies: in `ec4-2` every file up to 8 KiB (a 1.5 KiB file costs 8.1 times its size, 5.4 times what the pool promises), in `ec16-2` every file up to 24 KiB. Encoding is Reed-Solomon (`klauspost/reedsolomon`, pure Go with SIMD assembly).

1. **On write** (`encode_on_write`). The daemon's [write-back buffer](#client-design) collects a file's writes chunk by chunk. A chunk that held no data when the file was opened is encoded by the daemon once it fills, each shard sent straight to its member, and the primary commits the encoded version once every member holds its shard. In `ec4-2` the data then crosses the network once, at 1.5× its size, where copies-then-encode costs 3× and then 1.5× more. Chunk 0 waits in the buffer until `close`, because many writers patch the file's header last (the `mdat` size in MP4 and MOV, WAV sizes, zip local headers), and a patch to an encoded chunk would send it back to copies; at `close` it and the file's last partial chunk are encoded the same way. S3 objects take this path too. Chunk 0 flushed early by buffer pressure is written as copies and encoded once idle. This is the path for write-once data such as a studio's footage and photos.
2. **When idle** (`seal_after`). Everything else is written as copies: overwrites, files several nodes are writing, chunks flushed early by `fsync` or a full buffer, and block volumes. Each primary encodes the chunks not written for `seal_after`, every [frozen version](#snapshots-and-clones) of a chunk along with its live one, and commits the encoded version once every shard is durable; each member then replaces its copy with its shard.
3. **Back to copies on overwrite.** A write to an encoded chunk first turns it back into copies: the primary reads `data` shards, writes the copies, then applies the write, and the chunk is encoded again once idle. That costs a full chunk read and a replicated chunk write. Editors write new files rather than patch footage, so media rarely pays it; a database on a block volume pays it on the first write to each idle chunk, which is why block chunks wait 24 hours before encoding.

#### One-copy pools and locality

`none` keeps one copy of everything. With `locality: node`, its default, each node has its own PGs, and a volume is placed on one node (the node of its first pod, through CSI's `WaitForFirstConsumer`) with its chunks hashed over that node's PGs only. Losing a disk then loses part of that node's volumes only; reads of the lost chunks return `EIO`, and the pool lists the volumes affected. Pods on other nodes still reach the volume over the network. With `locality: spread`, a volume uses every disk, and any disk loss takes part of every volume in the pool.

**Metadata is not pooled.** Pools set the redundancy of data only. MetaStore shards and the control plane are always 3-voter Raft groups, whatever pool their files use: metadata is small, and losing a directory loses every file under it. A cluster therefore needs three nodes that hold metadata. Every node holds metadata, and a node can hold metadata without data disks (`rock-solid node -role meta`, which its heartbeat reports, so the leader drains its disks for as long as it does and places no member on them). A two-node cluster adds a small third machine, such as a single-board computer with an SSD, and runs `rep2` or `none` pools on the other two.

#### Moving a volume to another pool

A pool's `data` and `parity` are fixed, so changing them means moving each volume to another pool. Snapshot and restore could do that, but the result is a new PVC, and Kubernetes does not let a PVC change its StorageClass, so the application would have to stop and rebind its claim. Instead a volume moves in place, keeping its `volume_id`, PVC and inode numbers, by reusing the [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) machinery: a cut at snapshot epoch E pauses every node using the volume, one transaction points the volume record at the new pool with the old pool at E as its source, writes then go to the new pool's PGs while a read of a block not yet written there falls through to the old pool at E, a background copy finishes the rest as a flatten does, and the source pointer is dropped and the old pool swept.

Chunk IDs are the same in both pools; only the PG changes, so a chunk's [versions](#data-layer-chunkstore) in the new pool follow its new PG's intervals and are never compared with the old pool's: reads fall through by snapshot epoch, and an `expect_ver` from before the move fails. Snapshots taken before the move stay in the old pool, since each snapshot record names its pool, and keep their space there until deleted. Until the copy finishes, the volume uses space in both pools, and reads of uncopied blocks take an extra hop. Moves reuse the cut, read-through and flatten code and its simulation coverage, so they ship with snapshots.

In Kubernetes, a move starts when a `VolumeAttributesClass` with a different `pool` parameter is applied to the PVC: the driver advertises the CSI `MODIFY_VOLUME` capability and starts the move in `ControllerModifyVolume`. The CLI can start one too.
### Volume size and capacity

A volume, block or file, can be larger than any single drive. Its chunks are spread across every disk of its pool through placement groups, so the limit is the pool's capacity, and a pool is full when its first disk is: every volume hashes over every PG, so once one disk refuses writes, some of every volume's writes are refused.

**What a pool reports.** A disk's *fill* is its live blocks, from its segment usage table, over its size; a pool's *data* on it is the blocks of the pool's live and frozen maps, which the chunk index counts by pool. A disk's *capacity* for a pool is what `write_limit` leaves of it after other pools' data, and its *room* that capacity less the pool's own data, never below 0. A disk's *share* of the pool's new data is its member slots over the PG count, a shard counting `1 ÷ data`. A pool's *held* data is what applications stored: each chunk's raw bytes over the overhead of its form. From the fills and per-pool data that heartbeats report, the control plane computes per pool, and per node for `locality: node` (`chunkstore.Pool.Capacity`):

| Figure | Definition | Used for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Available | The smallest room ÷ share over the pool's disks, less the [space grants](#client-design) outstanding on the pool | `statfs`, CSI capacity, the CLI |
| Balanced | The most the pool could hold in all under its failure-domain rule: min over j of S₍>ⱼ₎ ÷ (b − j·a) | Admitting volumes with `overcommit` off, `Unbalanced` |
| Stranded fraction | Σᵢ max(0, cᵢ − a × balanced) ÷ Σᵢ cᵢ, and 0 when Σᵢ cᵢ is 0 | `Stranded`, and the confirmation when a pool or its disks change |
| Reprotectable | The least balanced over the survivors of any single loss listed below | `CannotReprotect` |

Here cᵢ is failure domain i's capacity; b is the pool's overhead (`replicas`, or `(data + parity) ÷ data`); a is the most of each byte one domain may hold, 1 for copies and `max_per_domain ÷ data` for shards. Domains are sorted largest first, S₍>ⱼ₎ is the capacity of all but the j largest, and j runs while j·a < b. Balanced is the largest D with Σᵢ min(cᵢ, a·D) ≥ b·D: no domain holds more than its capacity, nor more than `max_per_domain` members of a chunk, and McNaughton's wrap-around rule reaches it, so placement can approach it to within PG granularity. Capacity ÷ b, its j = 0 term, is what Ceph's `MAX AVAIL` and MinIO's usable capacity report, and it holds only while every domain can take a share proportional to its capacity: an empty `rep2` pool on nodes of 0.7 and 2 GB has 1.215 GB by that measure, but every chunk needs a copy on the small disk, so balanced is 0.63 GB. With shards and `max_per_domain` above 1 (phase 10), a domain also holds at most one member of a PG per disk, and balanced is found by bisection. Available is what Vitastor reports: in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike), uniformly hashed fills reached it within 0.3% in every layout ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). Available and balanced assume new data is encoded; data held as copies counts at its size. Pools sharing disks draw on the same capacity, so their figures overlap. The CLI lists each disk's fill, capacity, room, share and room ÷ share, which names the disk that sets available.

Everything but available is measured against capacity, not room, because room shrinks with every byte the pool writes even when nothing is wrong, while available + held, balanced and the stranded fraction stay put as the pool fills. `rep3` on nodes of 4, 4, 2 and 1 TB (the spike's C2) strands 18.2% of its capacity at any fill, but against room 26% at 1 TB held and 75% at 2.5 TB, so a condition on room would rise on every healthy pool near full ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). A domain's *stranded room*, max(0, cᵢ − a × balanced), is what it leaves unused at balance.

Reprotectable says whether the survivors of a likely loss could restore full redundancy. The losses it considers:

- **With a spare domain**, one whose loss still leaves enough domains for a PG's width: the domain with the largest capacity, since balanced never falls when a domain grows and losing a domain is worse than losing one of its disks. By capacity, not size, so a disk another pool filled counts as smaller.
- **Without a spare domain**, as `rep3` on three nodes: each disk that shares its domain with another, its capacity taken out of the domain, since the disk is the likely failure and recovery refills within the node.
- **Shards with `max_per_domain` above 1** (phase 10): each domain, then each disk, since with a member per disk the largest domain was not the worst loss in 367 of 3,000 random layouts.

A loss whose survivors cannot hold a PG's width is a fact of the layout, stated when the pool is created and in `rock-solid pool status` ([Pools and redundancy](#pools-and-redundancy)), and not counted here. A one-copy pool has nothing to re-protect.

The figures cover every disk with a share in the current table, a down disk at its last reported fill, so a node reboot changes none of them; a disk leaves them only when it is removed or marked out. Fill comparisons are in whole 4 KiB blocks: a disk's limit is ⌊its size in blocks × `write_limit`⌋, and balanced is rounded down to whole blocks.

**Conditions.** A pool raises conditions on its `Pool` resource, as events and as metrics (`chunkstore.Conditions`):

| Condition | Raised when | Cleared when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Full` | A member disk's live blocks plus ⌈grants × its share⌉ reach its limit: the primary's admission predicate ("Write limit", below), so `Full`, an available figure of 0 and the first refused write coincide | Every member disk is 1 point below its limit, grants counted |
| `NearFull` | Not `Full`, and a member disk's live blocks plus ⌈grants × its share⌉ reach `write_limit` less 10 points | Every member disk is 1 point below that |
| `Stranded` | The stranded fraction exceeds 10%, and the confirmed fraction (below) by more than 5 points. It names each domain with stranded room, and how much | The fraction falls 1 point below the threshold that raised it, or the operator confirms the current layout |
| `Unbalanced` | Evaluated only when placement proposes no move for the pool and every PG of it records a recovered report for its current interval; it keeps its value otherwise. Raised when available + held < 0.9 × balanced, available taken before grants. It names the disk that sets available, with its share and target | The same evaluation finds available + held ≥ 0.91 × balanced |
| `CannotReprotect` | Pools of 2 or more copies only: held > reprotectable. It names the domain or disk whose loss it is | Held ≤ reprotectable |
| `Stuck` | A PG of the pool needs copying and has copied nothing for `stuck_after` (60 s) on `ctx.Now`. A PG needs copying while it has a leaving member, a member down for `stuck_after`, or, with no member in `maintenance`, no recovered report for its current interval or copies its primary reports lacking. Maintenance is planned, so neither a member in it nor the copying it causes counts. It names the PGs | The PG copies again, or needs no copying |
| `Blocked` | A PG is `Stuck`, or its primary's last peering left chunks unfound. It names the PG, how many chunks, and its candidates: the members down, in `maintenance` or leaving, which [refill keeps](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) so that any surviving copy is on one. They are candidates, not holders, since peering cannot ask a down disk what it holds. A stopped PG, whose current interval no active primary reports, has an unknown count, not 0 | Neither holds any more |

What sets these boundaries:

- **`Full` and `NearFull` count grants and use `≥`**, where Ceph's per-disk states compare strictly and ignore pending writes, because the [block frontend](#block-frontend) reports a pool holding writes as `Full`, and a window in which admission refuses but the pool is not `Full` would hide it. The 1-point clearing gap keeps a full pool holding block writes from emitting an event pair at every discard.
- **`Stranded` waits for stranding nobody accepted.** On a mismatched layout stranding is permanent (`rep3` on nodes of 2, 2 and 4 TB strands 25% from the first day) and only new hardware clears it, so a standing condition would teach operators to ignore conditions. The prompt at creation is where it is accepted; the condition reports what changes later, such as C2 confirmed at 18.2% rising to 40% when its 1 TB disk is marked out.
- **`Unbalanced` has no timer.** A move lasts as long as its data takes to copy, and the leader knows when placement is done, so the condition is a state predicate, as Ceph's balancer gates on states. Its 10% is twice the fill margin's 5%: in the study's model, 400 random disk additions to converged tables never raised it, the worst leaving 9.4%. So it reports placement stopped well short: pinned PGs, a drain, a `pg_count` too small for a disk.
- **`Stuck` is the one timer.** A copy blocked by a full disk, or by the room [space grants](#client-design) hold, keeps its PG's leaving member, so `Unbalanced` is never evaluated and no state shows that copying stopped. `stuck_after` is Ceph's `mon_pg_stuck_threshold`.
- **`Stuck` and `Blocked` come from the primaries' heartbeats.** Each names, per PG the node leads, the interval, whether it leads it active, the chunks its last peering left unfound, and a bit per member lacking copies, which tells placement which member to [park off a full disk](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). The leader keeps when each PG last copied in memory only, so after a failover `Stuck` can show up to `stuck_after` late, which is cheaper than committing a timestamp per PG per copy through Raft.
- **`CannotReprotect` fires only when data is the problem.** Counting losses whose survivors cannot hold the width would raise it from the first byte on the default `rep3` with three nodes. One-copy pools have no redundancy to restore, which Ceph reports as a separate warning, `POOL_NO_REDUNDANCY`.

No system read warns about stranded space or predicts whether losing a domain leaves room to recover: Vitastor reports space efficiency, stranding and imbalance together with no threshold, and Ceph leaves both to planning and reports the failure when recovery runs out of room. The 10%, 5 points, 1 point and 60 s are judgments checked against the capacity conditions study's model, not against operators.

**Confirmation.** Placement never refuses a layout, because another pool on the same disks, such as `none` with `locality: node`, can use the room one pool strands. `rock-solid pool create` and disk changes print, per domain, its capacity, its load at balance and its stranded room, ask for confirmation when a domain would strand more than half its capacity, and record the stranded fraction as the pool's *confirmed fraction*. `rock-solid pool confirm` does the same for the current layout, for a `Pool` created without the CLI among others, whose confirmed fraction is 0 until then. `rock-solid pool status` shows the stranded fraction, its domains, the confirmed fraction and the losses the layout cannot recover from.

- **Thin provisioning.** A chunk exists only after its first write, so creating a volume allocates nothing. With `overcommit` on, volume sizes may add up to more than the pool holds. With it off, the pool keeps a reservation, the sum of its volumes' sizes, at the copies' overhead for block volumes in an erasure-coded pool since they stay copies for at least `seal_after`; `CreateVolume` and `ControllerExpandVolume` succeed only while reservations plus what snapshots hold stay within balanced, and otherwise fail with `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`. Balanced, not available, because a reservation outlives every rebalance; over capacity, not room, because reservations count data already written (C2 holding 2 TB with 2.5 TB reserved admits a 0.1 TB volume within its 2.7 TB, where balanced over room, 0.7 TB, would refuse it).
- **Write limit.** A write that adds live data is refused once a disk holding one of its PG's members reaches the pool's `write_limit`: its live blocks plus the pool's outstanding grants times its share, rounded up, reach ⌊size in blocks × `write_limit`⌋, the predicate `Full` uses, judged by the primary from members' reported fills and a grant total at most one report (1 s) old. Three kinds of write pass, so a filesystem's journal and preallocated database files keep working on a full pool: a flush under a space grant, promised when the daemon acknowledged it; an overwrite of blocks the chunk holds live as copies since its latest freeze, which kills as many blocks as it writes, as dm-thin keeps writing provisioned, unshared blocks; and punches, as Ceph lets every operation that adds no bytes through. The limit is per disk, not a pool average, because one full disk stalls every PG on it; CockroachDB likewise stops placing replicas on a store over 95% full. The log's reserve and the cleaner's free segments sit below it, so a disk at its limit still cleans, checkpoints and merges ([Free space](#on-disk-format)), and pools sharing a disk each check their own limit against its total fill. Chunks written whole go to [chunk slots](#on-disk-format) and cost no cleaning, so a pool of large files, such as backups, can raise its limit to 95% on disks of 256 GiB and up.
- **Small disks fill less.** A pool that leaves `write_limit` unset gives each disk the default for its [segment size](#on-disk-format), which follows the disk's size. The cleaner's [net-gain guard](#on-disk-format) charges a victim's copies in whole 1 MiB records plus a record's tail, a large share of a small segment, so it caps a victim's live share lower there, and once every segment is fuller than the cap writes fail with `NoSpace` below `write_limit`. Each default is the highest fill, in 5-point steps, at which a simulated disk under that cap ran a disk capacity of uniform 4 KiB overwrites with no `NoSpace` (`TestDefaultWriteLimitBySegmentSize`):

  | Segment size (disk size) | Guard's cap on a victim's live share | Default `write_limit` |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 32 to 64 MiB (128 GiB and up) | 92.7 to 94.2% | 90% |
  | 8 to 16 MiB (32 to 127 GiB) | 83.6 to 89.7% | 85% |
  | 4 MiB (16 to 31 GiB) | 71.6% | 75% |
  | 512 KiB to 2 MiB (2 to 15 GiB) | 47.2 to 48.3% | 55% |
  | 128 to 256 KiB (512 MiB to 2 GiB) | 41.9 to 46.0% | 50% |
  | 64 KiB (256 to 511 MiB) | 33.3% | 45% |

  Disks under 256 MiB take 45% too, which no run has checked ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). Each default keeps a margin, since a real disk has more segments than the simulation's. A cheaper guard would let through victims that cost as much as they free; smaller records would raise the cap but cost group commit a third of its throughput at 256 KiB ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)).
- **Fill sets drive wear.** A block volume's random overwrites cost each disk its cleaner's write amplification, which climbs with fill. Under uniform overwrites the [cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike), which writes no index, gives 2 at 66% full and 3 at 77%; the store, with its index's runs, reserve and admission pacing, writes about 4.5 at 80% and 11.4 at 90% on 64 MiB segments, estimated from its simulation (`TestCleanerWriteAmplification`), and about 30 at 95% in the [cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike). Skewed overwrites cost less (4.2 at 90% for Zipf), and file data deleted in write order 1.0 at any fill. The drive's own garbage collection is not measured yet ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). Over five years, a drive's rated endurance allows this many bytes a day of overwrites landing on one disk held at each fill:

  | Fill (write amplification) | 1 TB TLC, 600 TBW (Samsung 990 PRO) | 1 TB QLC, 220 TBW (Crucial P3) | 960 GB, 1,700 TBW (Micron 7450 PRO) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 66% (2) | 164 GB | 60 GB | 465 GB |
  | 77% (3) | 110 GB | 40 GB | 310 GB |
  | 80% (4.5) | 73 GB | 27 GB | 207 GB |
  | 90% (11.4) | 29 GB | 11 GB | 82 GB |

  Each overwrite lands on `replicas` disks, so `rep3` on three single-disk nodes puts every byte on every disk: 50 GB of database overwrites a day held at 90% reaches the TLC drives' rating in 2.9 years and the QLC drives' in 1.1. The fill that matters is the one a disk sits at, not the limit. `write_limit` still defaults to 90%, since pool defaults pick the option that uses less space ([Pools and redundancy](#pools-and-redundancy)); 80% cuts writes by three fifths for a ninth of the pool, and is what a pool of randomly overwritten block volumes on consumer drives should set, QLC lower still. Each disk exports its write amplification as two counters and, on NVMe, the SMART log's Percentage Used and Data Units Written, and `rock-solid pool status` shows Percentage Used and writes per day over the last week.
- **Snapshots use space.** Versions pinned by [snapshots](#snapshots-and-clones) count toward the pool, and an overwrite of a block a snapshot shares needs new space, so past `write_limit` it is refused like a new write.
- **Wider blast radius.** A volume spread over every disk is hit by any failure, anywhere in its pool, that takes out more members of one PG than the pool survives. Recovery must restore a degraded PG before a second failure arrives, so it starts with the PGs that have lost the most members.
- **A filesystem volume's size is a quota.** The PVC size caps the sum of its files' sizes. Each inode slot keeps a byte counter (`u`, in the [key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)), changed by an `Update(u, Add, delta)` in the transaction that changes a file's size, which never conflicts with other writers. `statfs` reports the PVC size as total, the counters' sum as used, and as available the smaller of the rest and the pool's available figure. The daemon caches both for `statfs_cache` (default 5 s) and fails writes extending files past the PVC size with `ENOSPC` before it looks at its grant, so writers can overshoot by one cache period's writes.
- **What Kubernetes sees.** `GetCapacity` answers for the class's pool and the node's topology segment, per node for `locality: node` pools. With `overcommit` off, `available_capacity` and `maximum_volume_size` are balanced minus reservations. With it on, `available_capacity` is available, and `maximum_volume_size` is 0 when available is 0 and otherwise the largest volume the pool accepts, so the scheduler keeps `WaitForFirstConsumer` pods off a node whose node-local pool is full but still places thin volumes larger than what is free. The check is advisory: a refused `CreateVolume` returns `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, and Kubernetes reschedules.
- **Largest file.** A file can't grow past the volume setting `max_file_size` (default 16 TiB, ext4's limit), which bounds a [purge](#freeing-space) to 4 million chunk IDs.

**How a full pool reaches a writer.** From the top:

| Check | Where | What the writer sees |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PVC size (file volumes) | Daemon, at `write()` | `ENOSPC` |
| Space grant (buffered file writes) | Daemon, at `write()` | The write goes through to the primary instead of the buffer |
| `write_limit`, for writes that add data | Primary | `ENOSPC` from `write()` on a file volume; on a block volume the write is held until the pool has room ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) |
| Cleaner behind | Disk log | Slower writes, then waiting ([Writers slow down](#on-disk-format)) |
| Only the reserve free, nothing to clean | Disk log | `NoSpace`, which reaches a file's writer late, at its next `write()`, `fsync` or `close` ([Client design](#client-design)) |

### Freeing space

Chunk IDs are computed, so freeing data never needs to find chunks: a purge lists the IDs and [punches](#data-layer-chunkstore) them, batched per PG. A punch to a chunk that doesn't exist succeeds, so a purge interrupted by a crash simply runs again. Punches carry the snapshot epoch like writes, so a purge never frees a block a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) still holds. The purge list and its entries are `internal/meta` (`Purge`); the protocol is `models/purge/purge.tla`, whose negative configurations leave out these rules one at a time.

- **Allocation mark.** Each inode records `alloc_end`, one past the highest chunk that may hold data. Before a daemon writes a chunk at or past it, it raises `alloc_end` by a step (1 chunk at the file's first raise, doubling up to 16), in a transaction that also commits the size already written. So other nodes see a file grow while it is written, at most 64 MiB behind, and a purge never misses a chunk written before a crash.
- **Unlink.** Removing a file's last link puts the inode on its slot's orphan list (`o/{inode}`, see [Tier 1](#cache-coherence-tiers)). Once no node has it open, its owner, in a transaction of its own checked on the attributes it read, removes the inode's whole key range, deletes the orphan entry and adds a purge entry (`p/{inode}` → `0 .. alloc_end`). The unlink cannot free the file itself: the open registry lives in the owner's memory, not the log, so an open registered between the unlink's proposal and its apply would be missed. The cost is one more transaction per unlinked file. The slot's shard leader works through the purge list in the background.
- **Truncate.** Shrinking a file commits, in one transaction, the new size, `alloc_end` lowered to the new end and a purge range from the new size to the old `alloc_end`, and `truncate` returns. The purger's first punch zeroes the rest of the new end's block, by a [merge](#data-layer-chunkstore), and of its chunk; the others delete the whole chunks after it. So the cut bytes are zeroed on disk whatever crashes, where zeroing only in memory is the bug XFS fixed in 2015 (commit `5885ebda`) and FreeBSD's fusefs kept until 2019 (`e312493b`, found by fsx). Anything that extends the file over a range with a pending purge waits for it. The truncating daemon does not send the first punch itself, since it could land after the purger finished and another node extended the file, and zero that write.
- **A truncate drains the file before its purge.** A write from another node that read the old size could otherwise land past the cut after the purge ran, where a later extension would show it. So before running a truncate's entry the purger asks the inode's [owner](#metadata-layer-metastore) to drain the file: each other node with it open flushes its buffer, sends later writes under the new size, and answers once every write it sent under an older size has returned. A silent node is evicted as for an unanswered [recall](#cache-coherence-tiers); a crashed daemon's successor answers once its new incarnation is acknowledged ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). The cost is one round trip before the purge, which an extension over the cut waits for and `truncate` does not, up to `recall_timeout` plus `session_lease` for a silent node. An extending write commits its size with a `Check` on the attributes it read, and on failure rereads, waits for any purge over its range and writes its chunk again. Zeroing the gap instead, as XFS does (`xfs_zero_eof`), does not hold across nodes: a gap punch can land after another node's extension was acknowledged.
- **One purge entry per inode, merged.** The purge list is keyed by inode, so a truncate, unlink, rename-over or an orphan's last close that finds an entry pending replaces it with the union of the pending range and its own, checked against what it read. Every pending range lies past the file's current size, so the union never covers live data. Overwriting the old range would leak chunks: a truncate then an unlink before the purger runs would leave only `0 .. alloc_end` at the lowered `alloc_end` ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md), finding 3).
- **The purger's writes are checked.** The list is not a queue: an entry for any inode can be added or merged at any time, below the purger's position too. So every purger write carries a `Check` that the entries it read are unchanged: removing a finished entry, recording progress in a long one (only once every punch before the new start has completed), and removing a consumed run in one range removal with a `CheckEmpty` over each gap ([Lists consumed in key order](#metadata-layer-metastore)). An unchecked removal, or a blind delete retried after an election, would delete a newer entry and leak chunks (finding 4).
- **The purger's punches carry its term.** A shard leader that lost its term may still have a punch in flight, which could land after its successor finished the entry and the file was written again. So each punch carries its shard and Raft term, and a primary that has seen a newer term of that shard rejects an older one. Since progress is recorded only once the punches before it completed, every chunk an older leader may still punch lies past a new leader's resume point. The primary's table of terms need not survive a primary change, for the reasons the table of incarnations need not ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)).
- **Block discard.** A discard (TRIM) from the pod's filesystem, which the CSI driver mounts with `discard` ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)), reaches the daemon through ublk and becomes a punch of the range, so a thin-provisioned block volume gives space back as soon as files in it are deleted.
- **Volume deletion.** Deleting a block volume purges chunks `0 .. size ÷ CHUNK_SIZE`. Deleting a filesystem volume removes its `fs/{volume}/` key range in one range removal, checked against the volume's record saying it is being deleted, and has every disk of the pool sweep its chunk index for the volume's ID, which is cheaper than purging millions of small files one by one. A volume that still has snapshots or clones keeps the chunks they need (see [Deletion](#snapshots-and-clones)).

## Metadata layer: MetaStore

The MetaStore is an ordered, transactional key-value store, sharded into many small Raft groups, each with its own leader. Consensus is needed per key, not globally. The API, the state machine, the router and the key encoding are `internal/meta`; the Raft shard under it is `internal/raftshard`.

```go
type MetaStore interface {
	Txn(t Txn) (Result, error)
	Scan(start, end []byte, limit int) (kvs []KV, more bool, err error)
}

type Txn struct {
	Ops    []Op
	Replay []byte // the replay record's key, fs/{volume}/{slot · 0}/r/{session}/{mount}/{request}, or nil for an internal transaction
	Oldest uint64 // the mount's oldest outstanding request; its records below it are dropped
}

// The op kinds, the whole set:
//   Put(key, value)
//   Delete(key)                          succeeds if the key is absent
//   DeleteRange(start, end)              removes every key in [start, end)
//   Update(key, off, Set|Add|Bump, n)    changes the 8-byte big-endian field at off; an absent key
//                                        or short value reads as zeros; Add wraps; Bump stores
//                                        max(n, field + 1), so the field always moves forward,
//                                        saturating at 2^64 - 1
//   Check(key, value | absent, final)    the key holds exactly value, or is absent
//   CheckEmpty(start, end, final)        no key in [start, end)

type Result struct {
	FailedCheck int      // index of the first failed check, or -1
	Actual      []byte   // that check's key's current value; nil if absent, a CheckEmpty or replayed
	Updated     []uint64 // each Update's new field value, in op order
}
```

**Why consensus is unavoidable.** Leases, atomic rename and S3 `if-not-exists` are compare-and-set (CAS) operations, and agreeing on one CAS winner among replicas that can crash is consensus. Dynamo-style quorums and conflict-free replicated data types (CRDTs) scale without a leader but cannot do CAS: two racing lock grabs would both succeed.

**How a `Txn` applies.** Every replica evaluates a `Txn` when it applies the log entry, in log order: every `Check` and `CheckEmpty` against the state before the `Txn`, then, if all pass, every write in list order, each seeing the ones before it. Nothing is ever undone, since no write can fail once the checks pass. A failed check applies nothing and returns its index and its key's current value, so the caller can retry without another read. This is etcd's rule (compares before ops) and CockroachDB's order within a batch; etcd refuses two writes to one key in one `Txn`, but here they are common (`mtime` and `ctime`, size and `alloc_end`). Evaluating at apply time costs each replica the checks' reads, about 1 µs each from the memtable, and makes the ops' meaning disk format: a new op kind or entry format needs a raised [format level](#on-disk-format), which the operator raises only once every node runs a release that decodes it, as CockroachDB gates new behavior on its cluster version. The alternative, CockroachDB's evaluation at the proposer, would make a write that depends on a key wait until the write before it on that key applied, or make the leader track every proposal in flight.

**A replica that cannot decode a committed entry halts.** It applies nothing of that entry or after it, refuses `Txn`s and reads with `ErrHalted`, answers its waiting `Txn`s as dropped, reports the entry's index and when it halted, and halts there again after a restart. Skipping would let replicas on different releases hold different states under one log; and since the entry came from the network and the disk, this is an event, never a panic. The halted replica acts as a down one so the group commits on the others: it takes no appends and restores no snapshot, since its log can never compact past its applied state, votes, campaigns and fortifies in no election, serves no lease read, and sends snapshots without data. While it leads it hands its leadership on, at most once an election timeout so a group whose every replica halted does not spin new terms; once it no longer leads it answers an ask about it as gone, so its followers stop supporting it ([voter replacement model](../models/voters/voters.tla), `neg-halted-silent`). The control plane replaces it after `refill_after`, on another node only ([Replica placement and replacement](#metadata-layer-metastore)). A `Txn` whose replay key holds a value that is not a record is different: every replica holds the same value, so each answers `ErrMalformed`, changes nothing, and none diverges.

**Why `Update`: keys many writers share.** Of the concurrent writers in one round that compare one key, only one commits and the rest retry. Some keys are written by many clients at once: a slot's counters (`n`, `u`), and a directory's attributes, whose `mtime` and `ctime` every `create`, `mkdir`, `unlink` and `rename` in it moves (POSIX, checked by `pjdfstest`), as `mkdir` and `rmdir` move its link count. With compare-and-set only, 64 parallel creators in one directory (`make -j`, a parallel copy, many pods writing one upload directory) would get one create per commit, 333 to 1,667 per second; with an `Update` that cannot fail, the shard's loop is the bound, about 60,000 proposals per second at 90% busy ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). So keys many clients write change only through `Update`, and a file's own attributes, which one node usually writes, by `Check`-and-`Put`, which keeps its size, `alloc_end` and the `u` delta consistent. A parent's times move by `Bump`, so a create from a node whose clock is behind still moves the directory's `mtime` by at least 1 ns ([Modification time](#client-design)). FoundationDB's atomic `add` and `max` at "a known offset in the value" are the precedent, CockroachDB's `Increment` the single-key form. `CheckEmpty` is etcd's `Compare` over a `range_end`: `rmdir` and rename over a directory need it for `ENOTEMPTY`, flock for "no other holder", and the purger for the gaps in what it consumes ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)).

**Limits, checked before proposing, as errors, never panics.** A `Txn` has at least one op and encodes to at most `max_txn_bytes` (256 KiB, a quarter of `max_group_commit_bytes` and three times the largest designed transaction, a 64 KiB xattr in pieces), or `max_ctl_txn_bytes` (896 KiB) when every key it names is under `ctl/`, where whole control-plane tables commit ([`ctl/` keys and commits](#metadata-layer-metastore)); both leave room for the headers in a 1 MiB log entry. Keys are at most `MaxKey` (1,032 bytes), values `MaxValue` (1,000), and each `DeleteRange` and `CheckEmpty` bound at most 1,000 bytes with its start below its end, so a range removal fits the [LSM tree](#on-disk-format)'s page. A replay key must lie under the replay tag (`ErrBadReplay`), so the range below `Oldest` that its apply removes holds that mount's records alone, and a `Txn` with one holds at most 124 `Update`s, so its record fits `MaxValue`. An op carrying a field its kind does not encode is refused, since an entry decoded differently from what its leader checked would fail on every replica. A `Txn` is never split, since splitting breaks all-or-nothing.

**`Scan`** reads `[start, end)` from one point-in-time view, at most `limit` keys and at most `max_scan_bytes` (256 KiB), and sets `more` when it stopped at either bound. A `limit` below 1 is an error, since an unbounded reply would allocate without bound. The caller resumes with `start` = last key + `0x00`, which is also S3's `start-after` and a `readdir` cookie, so a scan bound may be `MaxKey` + 1 bytes; it is never stored. A prefix scan is `Scan(p, PrefixEnd(p), n)`, and a point read such as `getattr` or `lookup` is `Scan(k, k + 0x00, 1)`. CockroachDB bounds a reply by keys and bytes the same way; etcd returns `more`.

**Internal transactions are guarded.** A client request is safe to resend because of its replay record ([Daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). A `Txn` without one, from the control-plane leader or the purger, carries a `Check` or `CheckEmpty` on every key or range it read, so a repeat after an election, or a write that raced it, fails instead of doing harm; an `Add` whose repetition would matter, such as one to `u`, rides only in a `Txn` with a replay key or a check a repeat would fail. Being idempotent is not enough: a blind delete retried after an election can remove an entry written after its first attempt applied. The exceptions remove keys nobody writes again: replay records below a mount's oldest outstanding request, and a departed session's records.

**Sharding (multi-Raft).** The key space is split into ranges, each its own 3-replica Raft group, with leaders spread across nodes. The shard map gives each group the first key of its range, which runs to the next group's, and every key a `Txn` reads or writes, its ranges and its replay key included, routes through it, so a replay record lands in the shard its request changed. A `Txn` or `Scan` that reaches two groups, the control-plane group included, is refused with `ErrCrossShard` naming them before anything is sent; a range ending at a group's first key stays in the group before it. That refusal holds until phase 13's [cross-shard transactions](#metadata-layer-metastore); a caller scans each group's range in turn.

**Routing.** A `Txn` goes to its shard's leader, from the daemon's route cache (`meta.Router`, a sans-IO machine). A follower or candidate never forwards it (etcd-raft's `DisableProposalForwarding`): it answers `NotLeader{group, leader replica and node, term}`, proposing nothing, and the router updates its cache and resends, as CockroachDB's `NotLeaseHolderError` and TiKV's `NotLeader` do. Forwarding would add a hop, leave the proposer learning the result only by applying it, and bypass the [inode owner](#metadata-layer-metastore)'s open registry at the leader. The cache keeps the newest term it has heard per group, except that the cached leader answering `NotLeader` drops it at once. With no leader known the router tries the group's voters in turn, holding the `Txn` an election timeout (1 s) after each `NotLeader` naming none, so it never resends in a loop; a held `Txn` waits out its timer even once another answer names the leader, a stall of up to 1 s. A lost connection fails the `Txn`s sent to its node, every loop of it, as dropped, since each may have applied, and forgets the node as any group's leader. A new shard map fails the held `Txn`s of a group it no longer holds (`ErrGroupGone`), to be routed again. A leader never re-proposes, so a proposal from an old term is either in the new leader's log or gone, and the only ambiguity is the caller's: a client resends under the same replay key, which returns the first final result, and an internal caller reads again, its guards making a repeat fail harmlessly. CockroachDB needs its `max_lease_index` because its leaseholder re-proposes; this shard does not.

**The router on the wire.** A `Txn`, a read and their answers travel as one message each over the [network driver](#runtime-in-go): a protocol byte, the group ID, a kind and the router's token, then the body, inline up to 256 bytes and otherwise in a pool buffer; a `Txn`'s body is its log entry encoding. The protocol byte (`core.ProtoGroup` for Raft groups, `core.ProtoChunk` for chunkstore frames) lets one connection between two loops carry every protocol, where a group message, which begins with its little-endian group ID, could otherwise be taken for a chunkstore frame; a tag set per connection would need a connection per protocol for every pair of loops.

A read (`meta.Read`) is answered from the leader's applied state only while `raftshard`'s lease read holds, which every `Txn` acknowledged before the read was sent has reached, since a `Txn` is acknowledged only once applied ([epoch model](../models/epoch/epoch.tla) and [voter model](../models/voters/voters.tla) check one lease reader at a time, and that it has every committed entry). Otherwise the server answers `NotLeader` and the router holds the read for an election timeout, so a read waits out a new leader's fortification after a failover rather than taking a ReadIndex round, which would give the server a second, rarely run path.

On each node a `meta.Client` wraps the router, routing by the shard map it reads from `ctl/` and giving every `Txn` a token unique across the node's starts, so an answer to a `Txn` sent before a restart cannot settle one sent after. A `meta.Server` beside each loop's replicas proposes under tokens of its own, which `Shard.Txn` refuses from any other proposer (`ErrServerToken`). Since the router has no timeout, a server answers every `Txn` it hears; an error with no code, and an answer its pool has no buffer for, travel as dropped, the answer that promises nothing, rather than closing the connection and failing every other `Txn` on it. The router resends only after `NotLeader`, which a replica gives only when it proposed nothing, so no answer makes it send a `Txn` that may have applied a second time. The control plane's messages to nodes and clients use the same framing, addressed to the control-plane group ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)).
**`ctl/` uses the same API.** The control-plane leader's logic (placement, session renewals in memory, fencing, space grants) is a sans-IO machine, `meta.Control`, that proposes `Txn`s on `ctl/` keys like any client. So the shard state machine, its tests and its invariants are one piece of code, and the group's state, and so its Raft snapshot, holds everything the control plane has decided. The shard map pins `[ctl/, PrefixEnd(ctl/))` to the control-plane group, which never splits, as CockroachDB's first-level addressing range never splits: it is the map's first range, running from the empty key, and `ShardMap.CheckRanges` refuses a map with a boundary or a second range inside it. A `Txn` on `ctl/` keys and data keys at once is refused as cross-shard; no designed transaction does that. While the committed map is empty, the leader commits the first one itself: the control-plane group's entry as its applied configuration holds it, and the first data group, which `PlaceVoters` fills ([Replica placement and replacement](#metadata-layer-metastore)), or a later step once an up disk can take a voter.

**The control-plane host.** Each control-plane voter runs one sans-IO machine, `meta.CtlHost`: its replica of the group and, only while that replica leads under its leader lease with an entry of its term applied, a `Control` built from the group's applied state, dropped as soon as it stops leading. The host routes what reaches the group: Raft messages and `Txn`s to a `meta.Server`; node reports to `Control.Heartbeat`, only from the node the connection's hello names (which stops a misrouted report, not a node that lies, since nothing authenticates the hello); session fetches to `Control.FetchAnswer` ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)); replicas' heartbeats, member timeouts and probe replies to `Control.OnEvent`; and replicas' asks for a new interval ([Restarts](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) to `Control.Ask`, taken only from a node heard from at the format level. It steps the `Control` every 100 ms and after each answer, handing it every recorded disk not gone at the size its node last reported and its last fill. Sizes live in the leader's memory, so after a failover a disk whose node has not reported goes in at size 0: it takes no new PG and keeps its PGs, where leaving it out would read as the disk leaving its pools.

What the host hands out, and why each is safe:

- **Placement tables.** A committed table goes as its delta to every node heard from within `node_down_after`, and whole to a node whose heartbeat reports an older epoch and to a node's fetch. A node fetches at its start, since its replicas start with no table and a replica with none refuses writes without asking, and again at each heartbeat and each move to another voter until a table arrives. A replica's ask for an interval is answered by the table's delta; it repeats the ask every second until then.
- **Session tables.** A committed fence is pushed to those nodes (`SessionPush`), and to each node a heartbeat shows behind (`SessionTables`).
- **The disk membership,** in answer to each report: per disk not gone, its log ID, its loops, its reported size and log reserve, its live blocks if heard within 2 s, and each pool's grants outstanding. From it a node learns its logs' disk IDs, and each replica its members' loops and what [admission](#data-layer-chunkstore) and [recovery's room check](#client-design) count each disk at. A disk is named only once the record holding it commits, since a leader that fails first may give its ID to another log. The membership carries no version and a node takes it from any voter: a committed disk ID never changes, so a stale list costs only liveness until the next answer.
- **Session starts,** handed to `Control.StartSession` only while the leader would answer a fetch, answered with the ID once committed. A client that hears nothing asks again, so it can get two sessions; the one it never heard of holds nothing and is fenced when its lease ends. No ID is handed out twice: each start commits the next ID, every session `Txn` checks the table's head, and a leader stages no start until a session `Txn` of its own has committed.

A node is configured with every voter's address and talks to one. A voter that does not lead answers with the leader it knows, and the node, and its replicas' reports (`SetAuthority`), move there or to the next address, at most once a second: each move makes every replica report at once, and while the driver backs off from an unreachable voter every send fails at once, which without a pause would spin the loop.

Each voter keeps the group's log on a disk log of its own through a Raft host, as any shard does (Log entries, below): an answer, a granted vote or a new term never runs ahead of the disk, and a restart restores the last checkpoint and replays the log after it. A restarted voter keeps its term, vote and acknowledged entries, which is what lets the group have several voters, and the control plane keeps every pool, table, disk record and session, so session IDs and placement epochs never start over. `-voters` sets the configuration only at the first boot, as the first round written at format (and again by a restart whose log holds no round); from then on the configuration is the log's, driven toward the group's own shard-map entry like any group's ([Replica placement and replacement](#metadata-layer-metastore)), and `-voters` gives only addresses. Checking the list against the log was rejected: it would refuse every restart after the first swap until the operator edited it, and a voter obeying a list of itself alone would elect itself and acknowledge commits no quorum holds.

The `rock-solid ctl` process runs one voter: one event loop with the TCP driver and an io_uring ring on a file of its own. `-format` formats the file at the first start by a storage device's rule ([Storage node disk startup](build-checklist.md#phase-5-default-drivers)): a file whose superblock and copy both read as zeros, as a cut-short format leaves it, is formatted again, and any other is refused. `-max-replay-bytes` (64 MiB) may be at most a quarter of the file: the reserve holds a checkpoint of up to twice it, and the records it bounds take up to twice it again. The process hosts no data replica, since it reports no disk. Only a chunk replica renews leases and the process runs none, so a group of several voters elects and commits but its leader never holds its lease and reads no `Control`: until a voter's loop renews leases with the other voters, the control plane runs one voter. A failed disk stops the host for good.

**The control-plane host's checkpoint.** The group's state is an in-memory LSM tree, checkpointed on the voter's own log as the disk owner checkpoints a disk's indexes ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)). A checkpoint begins, between rounds, once `max_replay_bytes` of records follow the last manifest, or at once when the log stalls for room, since the host runs no cleaner and frees segments only when a checkpoint ends. It first writes again what holds the replay floor back (`Host.Rewrite`), the hard state of an idle group and the uncommitted entries a cut-off leader keeps appending, then runs one merge step of `compaction_step_bytes` (the step the log's reserve holds, which a whole merge would outgrow), flushes the memtable under a manifest (`Host.Indexes`), and once that is durable compacts the Raft log to the index applied at the begin (`Host.Compact`) and frees the dead segments. A failed checkpoint stops the host for good: its causes recur, and a host that cannot checkpoint stalls at twice `max_replay_bytes`, so `rock-solid ctl` exits rather than run a group that cannot commit.

The runs carry their own mark under key `0x00` in key space 0: the applied index and the Raft log's base, the index, term and configuration of the last compaction. Written into the memtable as the checkpoint begins, it never falls out of step with the runs, and the manifest needs no field for it. A restart loads the runs, takes the base from the mark (`Host.Loaded`) and replays the log after the replay LSN; the base is the compaction before the checkpoint, not the applied index, since the rounds between stay live until the manifest is durable. A `Txn` naming key space 0 is refused (`ErrBadOp`) and no scan or snapshot reads it. Compaction makes Raft snapshots live for the group: a follower behind the compacted log gets the whole state in its round, and restores it from that round ([tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-the-control-plane-hosts-restored-snapshot-in-its-round)), where a replica on the round driver writes it as runs (below). A tree whose largest run piece passes the log's largest blob is refused (`lsm.ErrBadConfig`), since each piece is one blob.

**Shards on a disk's chunk store.** A disk's chunk store hosts Raft shards through a `raftshard.Host` on its own log (`Store.AddRaft`, `WriteRound`), so their rounds share records and flushes with the chunk writes, and their trees join its merge scheduler and checkpoints. A shard is hosted on the owner loop, or runs on another loop's round driver (`raftshard.Rounds`, `Store.AddRemote`). For both, what must hold:

- the replay floor never passes a live round of any shard, and a segment holding a live Raft blob is never cleanable; Raft blobs die only by compaction;
- a shard compacts only to what a durable manifest recorded of it, and a restart loads its runs, restores its storage's base from them and replays its rounds after its replay LSN;
- a removed shard's blobs and pieces die only once no manifest a restart may load names it, and its owner key goes to no new shard until then (`Store.RemoveRaft`, `Store.RemoveRemote`, `Store.Reusable`), or a restart would restore the old replica under that owner and replay the new one's blobs to it;
- the cleaner rewrites any tree's runs with a live piece in a metadata victim, the shards' as well as the chunk index's.

An index check that fails, or a log out of room, stops a store that has hosted a shard since it opened rather than rebuild the chunk index: a rebuild would close the log under the round driver's batches and replay to loops whose shards still hold their rounds. Rebuilding one index while the others serve is still to come ([Shard indexes in the disk owner's checkpoint](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore)).

A shard on the round driver shares no memory with the owner loop, so the two speak only by mail (`loop.Mail…`) and replies (`loop.Reply…`): the store replays the shard's blobs to it, the shard reports the replay LSN it needs (`Rounds.ReplayLSN`) and its share of twice `max_replay_bytes` filling (`Rounds.Full`), and a due checkpoint asks it for its mark and runs, which it flushes on its own loop a piece at a time for the store to write under its owner. The choices in that protocol:

- The pieces do not ride the round driver's batches, which are noticed durable whole and in order: under checkpoint backpressure a piece behind held records would wait for the manifest that waits for it.
- After a checkpoint, the shard compacts and rewrites what holds the floor back (`Rounds.Rewrite`) to the base of the due manifest before the new one: an idle shard then writes one small round every other checkpoint, where the new base would rewrite at every checkpoint, and on a small log each rewrite would make the next checkpoint due, for good.
- A merge step no manifest names makes the shard ask for a due checkpoint, not a step checkpoint, which reuses its one flush round trip at the cost of a memtable flush; merges are rare against rounds. The shard names its runs before its first piece (`Log.CheckpointRuns`): the owner predicting them would race an install, and a reply before the begin would be a second wait.
- The cleaner weighs such a shard's runs from a cost table the shard replies (`lsm.Tree.RunCosts`), since only the tree knows its runs' blocks and filters, and mails a victim's run to the shard to rewrite.

The store's own records (chunk writes, checkpoint pieces, the cleaner's copies) go to the log directly, not through a `loop.Shard`, since the places `Durable` hands a shard lack the address, header size and payload offsets its chunk index maps. On a storage node, the device loop owns the store's log (`Store.OnLog`).

**A MetaStore replica on the round driver.** `meta.RoundShard` is the `loop.Shard` of a MetaStore replica on a storage node's loop: it composes the replica (`meta.Shard`), its state machine's tree and its `raftshard.Rounds`, and answers its store's mail. It lives in `internal/meta` beside the control-plane host, the other driver of a replica, which imports `internal/loop` for it alone: it calls only the loop's shard API, starts no goroutine, reads no clock but `ctx` and does no I/O. A package of its own under `internal/loop` would put sans-IO replica code under the loop's looser rules.

A new replica writes its boot state, the voters' configuration, or an empty one for a learner that takes its state by snapshot, as its first round, and starts once a restart would restore it (below); a restored replica starts once its replay ends. It answers every ask for its pieces, even holding nothing or stopped for good, since an unanswered ask would hold every checkpoint of the disk, and backpressure then every write. While `Rounds.Full` reports it full it drops each Raft append (`raftshard.Appends`), which its leader resends, and as leader refuses a `Txn` with `meta.ErrOverloaded` before proposing it, so no committed entry is dropped; it keeps applying the commits heartbeats carry and asks for a checkpoint. The drop is the adapter's, before the message reaches the replica, so the Raft replica stays the one the models check.

A snapshot the replica restores goes to its runs, not its round, and is acknowledged only once a restart would load them: the round carries only the snapshot's index, term and configuration (`raftshard.Round.Runs`), the state restores the snapshot into its memtable, and a due checkpoint flushes it under a manifest a segment header names (`Log.NameNewest`); only then does an empty round let Raft send the acknowledgment. A restart on older runs drops the snapshot round, which acknowledged nothing, and so that it keeps its vote the replica reports no replay LSN from that round until its next round is durable. Carrying the data in the round, as the control-plane host does, was rejected: it would hold the whole state in memory through a replay and write it twice, in the round and in the next checkpoint's runs.

**MetaStore replicas on a storage node.** A storage node runs each MetaStore replica on the loop that owns its disk, beside the disk's store (`node.Device`), so the store's mail and the replica's replies stay on one loop. `Device.AddMeta` and `Device.RemoveMeta` add and remove replicas, refused before the store serves (`node.ErrNotServing`).

The disk records which replicas it holds: each manifest names every shard on the round driver with its group and replica ID (`disklog.Replica`), and recovery restores each under that owner, group and ID (`chunkstore.Config.Replicas`), with its peers, tree and boot state from the device's settings for its group (`DeviceConfig.Metas`). A disk holding a replica of a group the device has no settings for fails the start, left to the operator, rather than take the replica's blobs for dead. The control plane's shard map as the record was rejected: nothing would check that the data on the disk belongs to the replica restored, and a stale map would drop a replica with acknowledged entries. So a new replica acknowledges nothing until a segment header names a manifest holding it: it asks for a due checkpoint as it writes its boot round, that manifest opens a new segment, and once it is durable the replica writes its boot state again in a round that can be durable only after that header. A store formatted again holds no replica; each must rejoin its group under a new ID.

A removed replica answers each `Txn` it holds dropped and reports its blobs dead. Until the store takes the removal, the device takes no new replica of that group (`node.ErrHosted`), since one manifest naming two replicas of a group would fail every restart that loads it; the next checkpoint's manifest leaves it out and is named in a new segment header, which an empty round under the removed owner opens even on an idle disk (`raftshard.Host.Retry`). A crash before then restores the replica whole, and its group removes it again. Once its store serves, the device answers an ask about a replica it does not host `Gone` (`raftshard.Gone`), and about one it removed only once the manifest a restart loads leaves it out (`Store.LeftOut`): Gone tells a follower the replica has left its group for good, so it stops supporting it as leader (**Replica placement and replacement**, below), which a crash that restored the replica would make false. Before the first release the manifest format changes in place and older disks are refused, never migrated.

The device reports its disk's size as the log's formatted size (`OpenedDisk.Blocks`), since `write_limit` counts against it: space a volume gains stays unused until a reformat, and a restored replica's `MaxBlobBytes` comes from the superblock. The replicas share the loop's leases with the chunk replica, which renews them with every loop hosting a member of a running replica's configuration (`Replica.SetLeasePeers`), so a leader on a storage node is [fortified](#metadata-layer-metastore) and serves lease reads, and a `Resumed` ends the replicas' leases too. The device's `meta.Server` (`Server.ServeRound`) answers a `Txn` for a group it hosts none of `NotLeader` naming no leader, and refuses one past `Server.Held` (1,024) unanswered in its group with `meta.ErrOverloaded`, a refusal to send again, which bounds the `Txn`s a replica held by backpressure gathers. A replica whose disk failed a round answers each `Txn` it holds dropped (`raft.ErrProposalDropped`), since it may have reached a follower, and the server then answers `NotLeader`, so the router tries another replica.

**How a storage node learns its MetaStore replicas.** The control-plane leader's membership answer, sent only while its lease runs, carries the committed shard map: each group's entry with its voters, the node and disk each is placed on, and its `Boot`, the voters its first plan placed, replica IDs 1 to `Boot`. A device applies it (`Device.Plans`): it hosts each voter placed on its disk that it does not host yet, with the plan's voters as peers at the loops the membership names, hands each replica its group's plan (`SetPlan`), which a leader drives its configuration toward, and hands each replica every member's current loop (`SetPeer`), so replicas a cold start restores find each other. `meta.PlaceVoters` places all of a group's boot voters in one plan, so each boots the same configuration whichever answer it heard; a later replica joins with empty storage as a learner. A node drops the plans of a map older than one it took, and never hosts again a replica a delete removed, which a stale plan may still name: back under its old ID with empty storage it would be a voter that forgot its log. A restart forgets the newest map taken, which is why the leader answers only under its lease: every leased leader has applied the map each delete came from, while a deposed one could place the deleted replica again. The device reports the replicas it runs, and those halted at an entry they cannot decode with the halt's age (`NodeReport.Halts`), so the leader replaces a halted voter after `refill_after`.

A device starts recovering only once its node holds a membership, whose map gives the settings of every group's replicas the disk may hold, including those of a drive moved from another node. A replica of a group the map no longer names is restored closed (`Device.Unmapped`, logged): its runs and checkpoints kept, but taking no timer or message, so it never votes or leads. The wait costs only a node whose control plane does not answer, which serves nothing anyway, and no control-plane replica lives on a storage node, so it never waits on itself. Rejected: a boot count recorded with each replica in the manifest, a disk format change that also leaves a restored replica unable to reach its peers until the next membership, and the settings in a node-local file, which a moved drive would lack. The whole map rides on every answer, simple while groups are few; its ceiling is one message, 16 MiB (`core.MaxMsgData`), about 15,000 groups with the longest range starts or 300,000 with short ones, past which no device starts and the leader logs it once. Well before that, a node would take only its own groups' entries.

**The storage node process.** `rock-solid node` runs one event loop for the node's machine (`node.Node`) and one per device for its `node.Device`, each with a TCP driver of its own and each device's with an io_uring ring, as [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go) has it. Loop *k* listens on `-listen`'s port plus *k* and reaches a peer's loop *k* at its port plus *k*, so `-ctl` and `-peers` name first-loop addresses and the process needs no other address book. The node's loop takes the control plane's messages and each device's loop its replica's chunkstore frames. Neither machine reads the other's state: the process clones each output meant for the other side (`node.Output.Clone`, `node.DeviceOutput.Clone`), since tables and memberships alias buffers the next message overwrites, and posts it as mail to the other loop. Only control traffic crosses: a device reports its session version and placement epoch only when they change, so a write costs no mail. Each start seeds every machine's random source from the operating system, so a cut-short format draws a new log ID and no earlier start's fetch token is reused ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). A device that cannot open still gets its loop, its `node.Device` failed on the error and reported in `NodeReport.Failed`; only a bad flag or a loop that cannot start refuses the start. A device's stable identity is its path, hashed, so the operator names each drive by a stable link such as `/dev/disk/by-id/wwn-…` ([tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-a-devices-identity-is-its-path)).

**The operator's commands.** `rock-solid disk lost|drain|undrain|remove <disk>`, `rock-solid disk failed`, `rock-solid pool <pool> [settings]`, `rock-solid pg give-up <pool> <pg>`, `rock-solid pg edit <pool> <pg> <disk,...>`, `rock-solid pg pin <pool> <pg> [<disk,...>]`, `rock-solid pg unpin <pool> <pg>` and the node commands `rock-solid node maintenance|refill|hold|out-of-service <node>` ([Actions](#actions)) are one-shot programs off the data path, ordinary Go with the standard library's `flag` and `net`. Each speaks the voters' own framing, naming itself in the hello with a random node ID from the upper half of the 32 bits, which every daemon refuses for its own IDs, and sends one message under a random request number. Only a leader with its lease and a `Control` handles it: `Control.Lost`, `Drain`, `Remove`, `SetPool` (a new pool takes each setting's default, a change keeps each setting it leaves out), `GiveUp` for the interval the command read first ([An operator's give-up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), `Edit` for edits, pins and unpins, which refuses disks other than the PG's members or a PG with a leaving member ([Pins and drains](#data-layer-chunkstore)), and one call per node command, each with the refusals [Actions](#actions) lists. `disk failed` answers from `Control.FailedDevices`, the leader's soft state, empty right after a failover until each node reports again.

The command line never prints success for a change that has not committed. A refusal is answered at once with its reason. A command taken is answered once nothing of it is staged: committed as asked, or refused as overtaken, ignored by the placement rules, or dropped when the state read back after a failed commit no longer takes it. A pool change replaced by another change of the pool before it commits is answered as dropped even when the winner carries its settings, since a refusal for settings that may have committed is the safe one of the two errors. A leader without its lease, or that loses its `Control` while a command waits, answers nothing, so after 5 s the command line resends to the next voter under the same request. Resending is safe: a declaration, removal, drain or undrain the record already holds is accepted and changes nothing, a pool change sets only the settings it names over the pool as it then is, an edit writes the same members and pin, a give-up names the interval it read, and a node command sets the same mark or fact, its deadline counted again from the resend. Nothing authenticates a command, as nothing authenticates any daemon's port in v1, which trusts the cluster network ([Goals](#goals)).

**`rock-solid volume create`.** `rock-solid volume create -pool <pool> -size <bytes> [-case-index]` makes a filesystem volume, its record, the counter past its ID and its root directory (mode 0755, owned by root), in the one `Txn` [A volume's record and its ID](#metadata-layer-metastore) describes. It takes the membership from the control-plane leader as a node does, refuses a pool that does not exist, waits while the membership places nothing yet, and routes the `Txn` through the map to its group's voters, following `NotLeader`. A resend after a lost answer must make one volume, so the `Txn` carries a replay key, `fs/{id}/{0}/r/{0}/{0}/{request}` (session 0, which no session has, mount 0, a random request), and a lost answer or 5 s of silence resends the same `Txn`, answered from the replay record; only a failed counter check, which recorded nothing, rebuilds it for the ID the answer carries. Past `-timeout` unanswered, the volume may exist and the error says so: a second run would make another.

**`ctl/` keys and commits.** After its tag, each `ctl/` key names its record by fixed-width big-endian IDs, pools, PGs and nodes 32 bits, groups, sessions and audit entries 64:
