| Key | Value |
| --- | --- |
| `ctl/pool/{pool}` | The pool's settings |
| `ctl/placement/` | The table epoch and the table's CRC32C |
| `ctl/placement/{pool}` | The pool's `replicas`, `min_live`, PG count and split epoch as the table holds them |
| `ctl/placement/{pool}{pg}` | The PG's entry |
| `ctl/membership/` | The disk record's version |
| `ctl/membership/{disk}` | The disk's log ID, device and node, whether it is up, its log gone or it drained, its age while down, the time left of its `maintenance` mark and of a hold on its refill ([Actions](#actions)), and its class |
| `ctl/format/` | The format level |
| `ctl/session/{0}` | The session table's head: the next session ID, the table's version and its acknowledged version |
| `ctl/session/{id}` | The session's node, its newest incarnation, and the version that fenced it, 0 while it is live |
| `ctl/shardmap/` | The shard map's version |
| `ctl/shardmap/{group}` | The group's range start, the last replica ID handed out in it, and its voters |
| `ctl/node/{node}` | Present while the node is [out of service](#actions) |
| `ctl/audit/{seq}` | One operator command that committed: the time the command line read, the command, its arguments and the operator's self-declared name ([Audit log](#actions)) |

Every PG entry and group is stored as a head, as [large values](#metadata-layer-metastore) are. The leader commits what one step decides as one `Txn` (or several tables, below), one in flight at a time: the pool changes it staged, the table's head with the pool headers and PG entries that changed, the changed groups with a new shard-map version, the changed disks with a new disk-record version, and a raised format level. A step that changes nothing commits nothing, so the table epoch rises only with a change. The `Txn` opens with a `Check` that the table head, the shard-map and disk-record versions, the format level and every pool the table is computed from still hold what this leader last committed, so a deposed leader's commit fails and it reads `ctl/` back, as a new leader does; its staged pool changes are checked again there and dropped with their error if the rules refuse them. Nothing is handed out before its `Txn` commits, since a plan handed out first could be lost in a failover and its replica ID reused on another node. So a commit whose answer was lost is never handed out by the commit itself: after a failover, or an answer that is an error, the leader hands every group's committed entry to its leader once more, and the whole table once to each node whose heartbeat shows an older epoch.

The [session table](#sessions-and-fencing) commits in `Txn`s of its own, which take turns with the steps above, so a run of fences never holds up a table change, nor a run of table changes a fence. Each holds what the table owes at that moment, and only while the leader holds its lease: every session due, as one new version; the newest version as acknowledged once every node with a disk up holds it, with the sessions it fenced deleted; and the starts staged since the last. It checks the head and the disk-record version, so a deposed leader's fence or start fails, and writes at most 8,192 session keys, the rest in the next.

**A step past `max_ctl_txn_bytes` commits over several tables.** A node applies a table whole, so each table is one `Txn`, and one step can change more entries than that holds: a `rep3` PG's entry is 40 bytes as created, 61 with a past interval and 87 after a refill, so a `Txn` holds about 10,500 changed `rep3` entries, and losing every third node can change every entry, since placement tends to put a PG's members on neighbouring nodes. Measured with `chunkstore.Next`, that loss on a `rep3` pool of 16,384 PGs (the default from 246 disks) takes about 1 MB of down marks and 1.43 MB of refill, and a rack loss on a 4,096-PG `ec16-2` pool 868 KB and 1.3 MB. So the leader builds the step's other writes first and `Next` takes what fits in the bytes they leave: each pool whose header changes, whole, since a pool's entry count must match its header and a changed setting starts an interval of every PG, then the other PGs' changes in pool and PG order from where the last table stopped, wrapping round. A PG left out keeps its previous entry, so each table is valid on its own with no bookkeeping across tables, and the next table takes the left PGs first, so none starves. Where a table stopped is kept in the leader's memory only; a new leader starts from the first PG. A table that leaves changes makes no balance moves, and the leader keeps the reports and asks it may have left out.

A disk's down mark commits in the disk record only with the table that marks the disk's last PG, since the session table's acknowledgment counts a node whose disks are down in the record as holding every version ([Owners release last](#sessions-and-fencing)), and a PG still naming the disk live could order a fenced session's write; a detector restarted meanwhile starts such a disk down. This follows the models: `models/epoch/epoch.tla` commits each PG's change as its own table, so spread down marks are its `Refill` of one PG at a time, and `models/sessions/sessions.tla`'s `MarkDown`, a node's down mark with its PG's new interval in one step, is what holding the record's mark until the last PG's table keeps. A pool whose own first table, split or change of `replicas` or `min_live`, beside a record entry of every disk, does not fit one `Txn` could never commit, so `SetPool` refuses it (`ErrTooLarge`): a `rep3` pool of 16,384 PGs is taken and one of 32,768 is not; one of 8,192 can change `replicas` and `min_live` but not double; one of 16,384 can do neither, since every PG would gain a past interval. A step whose staged whole pools do not fit together drops its staged pool changes with `ErrTooLarge`, so the next step commits the rest.

**Key layout: the design goal is single-shard for almost everything.** A `create` writes a directory entry under the parent and a record for the new inode, so the two keys must fall into one shard without a global transaction service. Other systems run one global transaction across shards (3FS on FoundationDB, JuiceFS on TiKV), store inodes inside directory entries with a separate path from inode number back to the entry (CephFS, HopsFS), or order writes and garbage-collect what a crash leaves (Tectonic).

Keys are written below as paths. On disk, each `word/` is a one-byte tag, and integers are fixed-width and big-endian, so numeric order is byte order. Only the last component may have variable length (a file name, xattr name or S3 key), so keys need no escaping and a prefix scan is a byte-prefix scan. CockroachDB escapes every byte string in a key because a SQL key holds several variable-length columns; with one variable part last, escaping is unnecessary, and fixed widths keep decoding trivial and make slot boundaries plain prefixes, at about 8 bytes per key over varints, which the runs' prefix compression mostly recovers.

| Level | Tags |
| --- | --- |
| First byte | `0x00` reserved for a state's own mark ([The control-plane host's checkpoint](#metadata-layer-metastore)), `0x01` ctl, `0x02` volume, `0x03` fs, `0x04` s3 |
| `ctl/` | `0x01` pool, `0x02` placement table, `0x03` membership, `0x04` session, `0x05` format level, `0x06` shard map, `0x07` node, `0x08` audit |
| `volume/{id}/` | `0x01` attach, `0x02` snap, `0x03` record |
| An inode's keys (sequence ≥ 1) | `0x01` a, `0x02` x, `0x03` t (symlink target), `0x04` l, `0x05` d, `0x06` c, `0x07` P (a directory's parent) |
| A slot's keys (sequence 0) | `0x10` n, `0x11` u (bytes of the slot's regular files; a symlink's target counts nothing), `0x12` o, `0x13` p, `0x14` r, `0x15` f (next free slot, at the shard's lowest slot only) |
| Pieces of a split value | The value's tag with its high bit set (`0x82` for x, `0x83` for t), a 1-byte piece number from 1, then the last component |
| Lock modes in `l/{mode}/` | `0x01` s (shared), `0x02` x (exclusive) |

Volume IDs are 32 bits, inode IDs 64, session IDs 64, mount IDs 32, request IDs 64 (FUSE's `unique`), table and snapshot epochs 64, and a lock mode 8, so `fs/5/2:1/a` is `03 00000005 0000020000000001 01`. `a` and `x` are adjacent, so a file's attributes and xattrs are one range; an inode's tags sort before a slot's, which appear only at sequence 0; piece tags sort after everything an inode scan reads; the gaps leave room. Replay records have their tag to themselves, so one mount's records share a prefix no other key has, and dropping those below the oldest outstanding request is one range removal. A shard refuses, before proposing, a client `Txn` (one with a replay key) that writes or deletes a replay-tagged key (`ErrReplayTagged`), so a resend never finds a value there that is no record; an internal `Txn`, such as the leader's drop of a departed session's records, may. `meta.AppendKey` and `meta.DecodeKey` encode and decode every key, the decoder refusing any key the layout does not give, and `internal/meta/testdata/key_golden.txt` freezes the encoding. A snapshot's metadata copy at `fs/{snapshot}/` puts a snapshot ID where a volume ID goes, so snapshot IDs come from the volume ID counter.

**A volume's record and its ID.** `volume/{id}/record` holds a volume's pool (32 bits), its size in bytes (64) and a flags byte whose low bit is `case_index`. `volume/{0}/record` holds the next volume ID, and is absent before the first volume: no volume has ID 0. The counter sits beside the records it numbers rather than under `ctl/`, so a volume's creation writes the counter, the record and the root in one `Txn` of one shard; under `ctl/` it would span two groups. That `Txn` cannot raise the counter with an `Update`, since its other keys contain the new ID and no op can use an earlier op's result, so it checks that the counter holds the ID it takes and puts the next; a failed check answers with the counter, so the creator needs no read first ([`rock-solid volume create`](#metadata-layer-metastore)).

**Large values are split into pieces.** An xattr's value and a symlink target are stored as a head under their own key: the count m of the pieces after it, one byte, then the value's first 999 bytes. A longer value continues in pieces 1 … m under the piece tag, 1,000 bytes (`MaxValue`) each but the last, all written in one `Txn`, as FoundationDB advises for values over 10 KB. A split value is at most 64 KiB (`meta.MaxSplit`, the largest xattr value), so m is at most 65, and a reader refuses any other count or piece length. A 64 KiB xattr, which Samba's `vfs_streams_xattr` writes for macOS resource forks, is about 82 KiB in one `Txn`; a read is 1 + m point lookups, about 1 µs each. The frontends check sizes before they reach the MetaStore: 255 bytes for names and xattr names, 4,095 for symlink targets, 64 KiB for xattr values (Linux's `XATTR_SIZE_MAX`), 1,024 for S3 keys, whose index key, at 1,029 bytes, sets `MaxKey`.

**Inode IDs** are 64 bits: a 24-bit *slot* and a 40-bit sequence number. Every key of an inode starts with `fs/{volume}/{inode}`, so a slot is one contiguous key range, and shards split only at slot boundaries.

- A new file takes a sequence number in its parent's slot, so its entry and its inode are in one shard. A daemon reserves sequence numbers 64 at a time with a `Txn` of its own, `Update(n, Add, 64)`, and the next batch in the background when 6 are left, as JuiceFS prefetches its batches of 1,024. The reservation cannot ride in the create's `Txn`, since the create's keys contain the new number; it costs one round before a node's first create in a slot, and a repeat after an election only skips numbers.
- A new directory takes a free slot from the same shard's range, so `mkdir` is single-shard too, and a later split can move whole subtrees. Daemons reserve slots 16 at a time from the shard's `f` key, which they read once per shard first, since an `Update` would create it; a shard whose `f` is absent counts as used up. The directory is sequence 1 of its fresh slot, and its `mkdir` sets the slot's `n` to 66, so the creating daemon owns sequences 2 … 65 and "`mkdir`, then create in it" needs no reservation round. The `mkdir` checks, not final, that the slot has no `n` yet, so a slot handed out twice fails the second `mkdir`, which takes another. With no free slot, a directory shares its parent's slot; when a slot's 2^40 IDs run out, new files go to a fresh slot. A split will write the new shard's `f` past every slot the old shard handed out (phase 13).
- IDs are never reused. The root directory is inode 1, and sequence 0 of each slot holds the slot's own keys. A volume starts with one `Txn` that writes the root's attributes, slot 0's `n` = 2 and its `f` = 1, with a final check that `n` is absent, so a repeat fails.

| Data | Key | Shard rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pools, placement table, membership, sessions, format level, shard map | `ctl/...` | The control-plane Raft group: the shard map pins the `ctl/` range to it and it never splits; small because it maps placement groups, not chunks |
| Inode attributes: nine fixed 8-byte big-endian fields, `mode`, `nlink`, `uid`, `gid`, `size`, `alloc_end`, `atime`, `mtime`, `ctime`, 72 bytes | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/a` | The inode's slot. The volume prefix makes a filesystem one contiguous key range, which a snapshot copies and a deletion drops |
| Directory entries | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/d/{name}` → inode, type (the mode's type bits, as `readdir`'s `d_type`) | The directory's slot, usually shared with its files, so `readdirplus` is one request to one shard |
| A directory's parent | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/P` → parent | Every directory's but the root's, since a rename's loop check reads it on each ancestor; its own key, so creates in an ancestor never fail that check |
| Extended attributes | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/x/{name}` | Next to the attributes, so one request returns both; the daemon caches small ones with the attributes |
| Symlink target | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/t` | Next to the attributes |
| Case-folded names | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/c/{folded name}` → name | Only with the volume setting `case_index` (off by default) for SMB-shared volumes; turning it on later builds it in the background |
| flock and whole-file locks | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/l/{mode}/{session}` | The inode's slot; the mode in the key lets one `CheckEmpty` find an exclusive holder |
| Slot counters (next sequence number, bytes used), orphans (unlinked while open), purge list | `fs/{volume}/{slot · 0}/n`, `…/u`, `…/o/{inode}`, `…/p/{inode}` | The slot itself; see [Freeing space](#freeing-space) |
| Replay records of mutating client requests | `fs/{volume}/{slot · 0}/r/{session}/{mount}/{request}` → result | The slot the request changed; see [Daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) |
| Volume records, the volume ID counter, attach and mounts, snapshot records | `volume/{id}/record`, `volume/{0}/record`, `volume/{id}/attach`, `volume/{id}/snap/{epoch}` | Any shard; touched only at create, attach, detach, snapshot and move. A create writes the record and the counter with the volume's root, one shard while the first data group holds every data key, and two-phase once phase 13's splits leave `volume/` and the root in different shards |
| S3 index | `s3/{volume}/{key}` → object ID, size, version | Range sharding, never hash, so `LIST prefix` is a sequential scan. A bucket is a volume, and object IDs come from a slot each shard reserves, as inode numbers are, so a `PUT` stays single-shard |

**The transactions.** Each is one `Txn` in one shard, except a cross-directory rename after phase 13. "The parent's times" is `Update(parent a.mtime, Bump, t)` and `Update(parent a.ctime, Bump, t)`; a check marked final is one whose failure the daemon returns as an errno.

| Operation | Ops |
| --- | --- |
| `create` | `Check(d/name absent, final)`, `Check(parent P = what was read)` unless the parent is the root, `Put(d/name → inode)`, `Put(child a)`, the parent's times; with `case_index`, `Check(c/folded absent, final)` and `Put(c/folded → name)` |
| `mkdir` | As `create`, plus `Update(parent a.nlink, Add, 1)` and `Put(child P → parent)`; in a fresh slot s, the child is s:1, with `Check(s:0 n absent)` and `Put(s:0 n = 66)` |
| `link` | As `create` for the entry, then `Check(child a)` and `Put(child a)` with `nlink` + 1 and `ctime` |
| `symlink` | As `create`, plus `Put(child t)`, in pieces past 1,000 bytes |
| `unlink` | `Check(d/name, final)`, `Delete(d/name)`, `Check(child a)` and `Put(child a)` with `nlink` − 1 and `ctime`, the parent's times; for the last link, `Put(o/inode)`; with `case_index`, here and in `rmdir`, `Delete(c/folded)` |
| An orphan's free, the owner's | `Check(child a = what was read)`, `DeleteRange` over the child's keys, `Delete(o/inode)`, its purge entry merged ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)), for a regular file `Update(u, Add, −size)` |
| `rmdir` | `Check(d/name = inode, final)`, `CheckEmpty(child's d/, final)`, `Delete(d/name)`, `DeleteRange` over the child's keys, `Update(parent a.nlink, Add, −1)`, the parent's times |
| `rename` in one directory | `Check(src entry, final)`, `Check(dst entry absent, or = what was read)`, `Delete(src)`, `Put(dst)`, the parent's times; over a directory also `CheckEmpty(its d/, final)`, its keys removed and `Update(parent a.nlink, Add, −1)`; a replaced inode as in `unlink`; with `case_index`, the source's `c/` key moves to the destination's, checked absent (final) when no entry was replaced, or `= src name` (final) when the two names fold alike, as in `readme.txt` to `README.txt`. When both entries name one inode, only `Check(src entry, final)` and `Check(dst entry = what was read)`: POSIX says such a rename changes nothing |
| `rename` across directories | As above, plus both parents' times and `Check(new parent P = what was read)` unless it is the root; for a directory, `Update(a.nlink, Add, ±1)` on both parents, `Put(its P → new parent)`, and `Check(P = what was read)` on each ancestor of the new parent up to the root, so a directory never moves under itself |
| `setattr` on a directory | `Check(P = what was read)` unless it is the root, `Update(field, Set, v)` and `Update(a.ctime, Bump, now)`; never a `Check` on a directory's attributes, which every create in it changes |
| `alloc_end` raise, `close` or `fsync`, a size change, O_APPEND | `Check(a = what was read)`, `Put(a)` with size, `alloc_end` and `mtime`, `Update(u, Add, new size − old size)`; a failed `Check(a)` is not final, so the daemon reads again and retries |
| Truncate | `Check(a)`, `Put(a)`, `Update(u, Add, −delta)`, the purge entry merged |
| flock `LOCK_EX` | Scan `l/`; for each key of a session gone from the session table, or of the requesting session itself (a conversion between modes), `Check` it and `Delete` it; any live holder fails the request; then `CheckEmpty` over each gap between those keys, and `Put(l/x/{session})` |
| flock `LOCK_SH` | The same over `l/x/` only, then `Put(l/s/{session})` |
| xattr set | `Put(x/name)`, in pieces past 1,000 bytes, and `Delete` of the old value's pieces past the new count, which the daemon read with the head; `removexattr` deletes the head and every piece; either checks, not final, that the inode is still there: a file's `a = what was read`, a directory's `P` as `setattr` does |
| S3 `PUT` | `Check(s3/v/key absent, or = version, final)`, `Put(s3/v/key)` |
| `ctl/`: a table or shard map | `Check` on the table head, the shard-map version and each pool the table is computed from; `Put` of the new head and version and what changed ([`ctl/` keys](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| `ctl/`: a session-table version | `Check(version = e)`, `Put(e + 1)` and its contents |
| `ctl/`: a session starts | `Check(counter = k)`, `Put(counter = k + 1)`, `Put(session/k)`; sessions start rarely, so compare-and-set is enough |

Why the transactions take these shapes (`meta.FS` builds each one):

- **A last unlink checks the attributes it read.** A `link` racing it adds an entry to the inode; without `Check(child a)` the unlink's `DeleteRange` would remove the inode and leave that entry naming nothing. The check is not final, so the daemon reads again and finds the second link; a rename over a last link carries it too.
- **An entry is added only under a live directory.** `rmdir` deletes the directory's `P` with its other keys, and nothing else deletes it, so every op that adds an entry, a directory's `setattr` and a directory's xattr carry `Check(P = what was read)`, not final; the root, never removed, has none. Without it, a `create` built before an `rmdir` that commits first would put its entry under the removed directory, and its `Update`s on the parent's times would bring back the directory's `a`. Creates never write `P`, so creates in one directory never conflict. Every directory has `P`, not only one in a fresh slot, since a rename's loop check reads it on each ancestor.
- **Times are nanoseconds since 1970 plus 2^63**, so `Bump`, which compares unsigned fields, orders a time before 1970 below every later one.
- **A case fold is not a lower-casing.** The Kelvin sign K, the long s ſ and the final sigma ς each match a letter under Unicode's simple case folding (as SMB clients match names) whose lower case they are not. So `case_index` replaces each rune of a name by the least rune of its `unicode.SimpleFold` orbit, and for valid UTF-8 two names collide exactly when `strings.EqualFold` matches them; bytes that are not UTF-8 compare as bytes. The fold comes from Go's Unicode tables, so a Go release that changed one would change stored `c/` keys; `TestCaseFoldIsPinned` fails on any such change ([tech debts](tech-debts.md)).
- **An xattr set deletes the old value's surplus pieces** given the old count the daemon read with the head. A daemon that did not read it passes 0: the old pieces stay, unread since the head counts its own, until the inode's keys go.
- **A flock clears dead holders in the transaction that grants it,** with a check per gap, which saves the round a separate clean-up would cost after every fence; clearing the requesting session's own key too makes a conversion between modes one `Txn`. A dead session is one `Control.Holding` reports false: an ID below the table's next one that has left the table. An ID at or above the next one counts as live, since a session holds a key only once its start commits and a new leader can read the table before the old leader's last start applies; an owner reading a lagging copy of the table counts a session it has not seen as live for the same reason.

**What the layout costs.** `create`, `mkdir`, `unlink` and `readdirplus` touch one shard, and a split never cuts a directory away from its files. In exchange:

- A cross-directory `rename` keeps the inode in its old slot, so locality decays slowly over time; correctness does not depend on it.
- A directory and all its files live in one slot, so one huge, busy directory is bounded by one shard, as in CephFS without directory fragmentation.
- A slot holds 2^40 IDs, about 3.5 years of creates at 10,000 per second.
- 64-bit `st_ino` values can overflow 32-bit programs built without large-file support.
- A volume shared over SMB needs `case_index` on, which costs one extra key in each create's transaction; small xattrs cached with the attributes answer a gateway's per-entry `user.DOSATTRIB` reads ([Samba spike](#appendix-samba-gateway-spike)).
- The hash function and bit layout of chunk IDs and PGs are disk format: changing either moves every chunk.
**Inode owner.** The leader of an inode's shard is that inode's *owner*. Besides serving its keys, it keeps soft, in-memory state for open files: who has the file open, and from phase 7 who it knows has written (a node in Exclusive says so only when a second open recalls it) and the current cache mode ([Cache coherence](#cache-coherence-tiers)). None of it goes through the Raft log, so an `open`, and a mount's last close of a file, cost one round trip and no disk write: each is a lease read of the file's attributes that carries the session and mount (`meta.Read`'s `Reg`). The contract:

- The owner registers an open only while the attributes show a link, so an orphan takes no new open, and refuses an open from a session its table shows not live. A create registers once its transaction applies.
- The mount orders a file's registrations: an open or create holds the inode until its registration is answered, and the last release until its deregistration is, since the kernel's release can race another `open(2)` and a release answered after an open would deregister it. A re-registration (below) skips a file released before its turn, and a release of the file whose re-registration is in flight waits for that answer, or the file would stay registered after its release and its space never be freed.
- A session's opens close at its fence once a session table acknowledges a version at or above the fence, when every storage node refuses its writes, never at the fence's commit; a handle opened under a session the mount has since replaced fails with `EIO`.
- Every new leadership term of a group starts a *grace period*, as in NFS version 4: the registry is in memory, so a new owner, after a crash or a planned transfer alike, knows no open. Meanwhile it takes re-registrations, refuses new opens with `ErrGrace`, which the router holds for its election wait and sends again, and frees no orphan, while I/O on open handles carries on at the chunk primaries. Handing the registry over on a planned transfer, so opens there don't pause, is phase 7's ([Roadmap](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).
- The new owner learns whom to wait on from a [session table](#sessions-and-fencing) its node fetches after the period began, which lists each live session's node: a table read before could miss a session that registered at the old owner after it. It waits on every session live there, and on each the table shows fenced until a table acknowledges that fence, since the old owner may hold such a session's opens and the session cannot re-register them. Every 100 ms (`ReclaimGap`) it asks each session it waits on to re-register, a message to loop 0 of its node, where the FUSE daemon runs. The owner pushes rather than sessions polling each group, so a session's cost stays one heartbeat whatever the number of shards.
- The mount re-registers each file it has open under its session in the owner's group, then ends with the owner's term; an end for an older owner's term does not count. It starts over when asked again, so a new term never counts re-registrations an older owner answered. An open or create whose registration is in flight at the ask registers again once answered, as the owner that answered may have failed, and hands out its handle only after the second answer. One end per session is right while a daemon hosts one mount; a daemon of several mounts would end once all of them have re-registered. Only a node that mounts holds a session, so a client-role node started without a mount, which nothing would re-register for, costs no grace period a lease.
- The period ends once every session it waits on has re-registered (an empty re-registration when nothing is open) or left the table, acknowledged; one still waited on after a session lease the owner asks the control plane to evict, again each lease since an ask that reaches a voter not leading is dropped. So the worst case is about 2 × `session_lease` plus a fence round, plus up to `dead_wait` after a control-plane failover. A fixed-time end would let the new owner free an orphan a node still reads, and give a file to one node while another still caches it ([purge model](../models/purge/purge.tla), [recall model](../models/recall/recall.tla)). At its end the owner scans its group's orphan list (`o/`), since the orphans whose opens closed under the old owner are listed nowhere else; the scan runs to the end of the group's keys in one loop event, which on a large shard stalls the loop it shares with the chunk store (**Orphan scan in bounded steps**, [build checklist](build-checklist.md#phase-6-file-frontend)).
- A re-registration does not yet say whether the node has written the file. Nothing reads that before phase 7's cache modes, which add it to re-registrations: without it a new owner could compute ReadShared for a file being written (the recall model's `neg-rereg-no-wrote`).

**Cross-shard transactions (CockroachDB style, no global timestamp service):**

1. Coordinator writes a transaction record `{id: T, status: PENDING}` in shard X.
2. Each touched shard gets an "intent": a provisional value tagged T, written in parallel.
3. Flip T's record to COMMITTED. This single key is the atomic commit point.
4. Intents are resolved into real values asynchronously. A reader that meets an intent checks T's record, so anyone can finish or abort T after a coordinator crash.

Cross-directory `rename` is the main file operation that takes this path. Every operation stays in one shard until range splitting makes a second data shard in phase 13: the control-plane group is its own Raft group from phase 4, but no designed operation writes it and a data shard in one `Txn`, and every volume's keys, `volume/{id}/` included, sit on the one data shard, so the [snapshot cut](#snapshots-and-clones) and a volume's deletion are one-shard transactions until then. The protocol is finished in phase 13, with a TLA+ model as its first precise form, before its code: the four steps leave out what other systems' bugs came from (what a reader or writer that meets a PENDING intent does and how the coordinator learns it lost, when a record may be removed, how a cross-directory rename refuses to make a directory its own ancestor across shards, and how the snapshot cut's markers commit with the snapshot record), and models of CockroachDB's parallel commit and MongoDB's two-shard transactions found exactly such bugs ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)). Every transaction gets a commit timestamp from a hybrid logical clock on the coordinator, as in CockroachDB, rather than a central oracle, and intents and committed values carry it in their keys, so the [transactional key-value and SQL layer](#roadmap-and-open-questions) can later read at a timestamp without a second commit protocol or key format; filesystem operations read only the newest version. Until then a `Txn` whose keys span two Raft groups is refused with `ErrCrossShard{groups}` before anything is proposed, so nothing comes to depend on an unmodelled path.

**Lists consumed in key order.** A list worked through in key order, such as a purge list, removes each consumed run with one `DeleteRange`, not one removal per entry: removals flushed before they were consumed pile up at the list's head, and in the [LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study) a seek to a queue's head stepped over 10,820 point removals against one range removal. RocksDB documents the same pile-up. The purge list is keyed by inode, not a queue, so entries can be added inside a run while it is consumed: the `DeleteRange(K₁, Kₘ + 0x00)` carries a `Check` on each entry it consumed and a `CheckEmpty` over each gap between them ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)).

**Storage.** Each shard replica lives on one disk of its node. Its Raft log and its state, an LSM tree, are part of that disk's [log](#on-disk-format), so the daemon embeds no key-value engine and SPDK drives hold metadata too.

**Raft shard.** The Raft shard (`internal/raftshard`) is etcd-raft itself (`go.etcd.io/raft/v3`, pinned to v3.6.0), which already has the sans-IO shape: the shard wraps its `RawNode` in `OnEvent`, the driver feeds it ticks and messages, and it returns a `Ready` of entries to persist, messages to send and entries to apply. Ticks, messages and persistence all belong to the driver, so it shares flushes, batches messages per connection and quiesces shards across every group on the node. etcd-raft's `Node` wrapper and hashicorp/raft run their own goroutines or own their storage, which rules both out, and a Raft written from scratch would have to find again the edge cases etcd-raft has fixed over years in etcd and CockroachDB. It is v3.6.0, not v3.7.0: v3.7.0's `google.golang.org/protobuf` messages make 16 allocations per message against 2.7, spend 1.64× the CPU per proposal, and its `Storage` returns `[]*pb.Entry`, which would allocate on every read of entries kept off-heap ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). A mature library is no guarantee of safety, so etcd-raft's own tests, the simulator and the [voter replacement model](../models/voters/voters.tla) all cover the shard.

- **Synchronous `Ready` and `Advance`.** A `Ready` that must be durable (etcd-raft's `MustSync`: entries, a new term, a vote or a snapshot) becomes the shard's *round*: the driver flushes it, and only once it is durable does the shard write it to its `raft.Storage`, send the messages it held, hand over committed entries and call `Advance`. A `Ready` that only moves the commit index needs no flush, as in etcd, since Raft's safety does not rest on a durable commit index, with two exceptions: every round writes the newest hard state etcd-raft handed out (it hands one out only where it changed), and a `Ready` that commits a configuration change, or whose committed entries stop short of the commit index its messages carry (entries come in pages of up to 1 MiB), is a round that sends nothing before its flush. Without them a replica can restart in a configuration older than one it knew committed and help elect two leaders of one term ([voter replacement model](../models/voters/voters.tla), `neg-hard-state-old`, `neg-conf-commit-not-durable`). etcd-raft drops a transfer's `MsgTimeoutNow` while a committed configuration change is unapplied, so the shard keeps one that arrives mid-round and steps it after. Asynchronous storage writes stay off: with them a restored snapshot can overtake committed configuration changes queued for apply, which panics upstream ([study](../studies/etcd-raft-async-snapshot-apply/README.md)); turning them on needs two guards, dropping apply work a restored snapshot covers and never compacting past the durable commit index.
- **Ticks and messages.** The shard ticks once per 10 ms of `ctx.Now` and credits at most one heartbeat of ticks per event, since a longer gap means the loop stood still with the leader's messages possibly waiting behind the timer; dropping time only delays elections, as nothing counts ticks for safety (`CheckQuorum` is off and leases count on the clocks). A message is the group's ID and the encoded `raftpb.Message`, inline up to 256 bytes and otherwise in a pool buffer. One that does not decode, carries a snapshot the state machine refuses, or names another group or replica is dropped and counted, never answered; an append the pool has no buffer for is dropped as lost, for Raft to resend; a lost connection (`PeerLost`) reports the peer's replicas unreachable, so the leader probes them.
- **A pinned fork.** Upstream draws election timeouts from `crypto/rand` (`globalRand`), which the simulator cannot replay, so a `replace` builds `go.etcd.io/raft/v3` from a fork of v3.6.0 in `third_party/raft` whose `Config.Rand` takes a seeded `math/rand/v2` source per replica. Its only other change is v3.7.0's ReadIndex retry fix (etcd-io/raft#397), without which late acknowledgments of a retried ReadIndex can confirm a read at a deposed leader's stale commit index. `scripts/raft-tests.sh` runs etcd-raft's own tests on the fork in CI and before every tag update; the patches, and what removes the pin and the fork, are in [tech debts](tech-debts.md#workaround-etcd-raft-fork-in-third_partyraft).
- **Log entries.** A round's entries, hard state and any restored snapshot join the next record of the [disk log](#on-disk-format) through the disk's *host*, which carries every shard's rounds on the disk as blobs of the shard's owner (`BlobRaftEntries`, the last a `BlobRaftState`) and reports a round durable only once its last blob is, so a torn round never reached the shard and replay drops it whole. A restart replays the shard's rounds in log order: a restored snapshot drops every entry before it, a later round's entries replace an earlier one's from their first index, and the latest hard state wins. A leader's appends go out in the same round as its own write, as etcd's server sends them; every other message waits for the round's flush, since an acknowledged entry, a granted vote and a new term must survive a crash (Figure 2 of the Raft paper), except pre-votes and their answers, which change no hard state. etcd-raft reads entries synchronously while it builds appends, so the shard keeps every entry since its last compaction in memory, in pointer-free chunks that serve any range with no allocation per entry and are all freed by a compaction that leaves no payload; they belong in the loop's off-heap slab ([tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-lsm-runs-and-retained-raft-entries-on-the-go-heap)). The shard compacts once the memtable that applied the entries has flushed.
- **Log room.** A round's blobs stay live until every index it holds is compacted and a later durable round holds a newer hard state, or a durable full rewrite holds its entries again; a deleted replica's die at once, and a disk gives a blob owner to no new shard while its blobs can replay. A shard's replay LSN is the older of its oldest live round and its memtable's start. So that an idle shard's hard state, or a stuck follower's uncommittable entries, cannot hold the replay floor back for good, a checkpoint writes them again once the oldest live round falls behind the last manifest's base, as etcd's write-ahead log carries its hard state into each new file. A shard takes no new entries once it retains its share of twice `max_replay_bytes` (`Store.RaftFull`): a follower drops appends, which Raft takes as lost, and a leader refuses proposals as overloaded, and the disk takes one checkpoint for a full shard per commit it applied. That bounds retained entries by twice `max_replay_bytes` per disk, so one stuck group never stalls a disk's chunk writes. The costs: a stuck shard about doubles its disk's writes while stuck, and a busy shard among idle ones checkpoints once per share of its own writes, which caps its throughput.
- **Snapshots.** A snapshot is built when the leader needs one, at its applied index, with the configuration there and the state machine's [point-in-time view](#on-disk-format) as its data: one kept from the last compaction could predate a learner added since, which would refuse it. It goes out whole in one message and is reported finished a heartbeat later, so a lost message does not leave the leader in `StateSnapshot` for good and a refusing follower is not resent one every round. A follower on the round driver writes a restored snapshot's data as sorted runs of its tree ([A MetaStore replica on the round driver](#metadata-layer-metastore)); the control-plane host still carries it in the round ([tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-the-control-plane-hosts-restored-snapshot-in-its-round)). The shard hands restored snapshots and committed entries to its state machine within the call that takes them (`Config.Apply`), since a snapshot can be built later in that same call (`Config.SnapshotData`).
- **Cost.** A group may cost at most 3 allocations per Raft message and 4 KiB of heap while idle, written or not, which `TestCostPerGroup` holds at 1,000 groups per node on every `go test`; the first bar is the etcd-raft [version pin](tech-debts.md#version-pin-goetcdioraftv3-v360)'s exit condition. Nearly all of a proposal's allocations are etcd-raft's own, so the driver neither recycles messages nor encodes from entries until a profile says otherwise.

**Shard count.** A shard splits, at a slot boundary, once it holds more than `shard_split_size` (default 512 MiB, as CockroachDB's ranges), so a 3 to 10 node cluster runs tens of groups per node. Splitting by load comes with leader balancing. The Raft shard is built to stay cheap at about 1,000 groups per node, so per-group cost never limits splitting:

- **Batching per connection.** The driver sends every group's messages for one peer loop as one batch over their [connection](#runtime-in-go). In the spike, 80,000 Raft messages per second fit in 240 batches.
- **Quiescence.** A group with no writes stops ticking and heartbeating. The [multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike) cut the idle cost of 3,000 replicas from 13% of a core at the design's timing, 36% at raft-rs's default 50 ms heartbeat, to 0.07%. Its leader keeps its [lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) and its followers stay loyal through the loop-pair leases, so a quiescent group sends nothing until it writes again or a loop-pair lease lapses, which wakes every group led from that node. TiKV's hibernate regions and CockroachDB's leader leases on store liveness work the same way.
- **Timing.** `raft_heartbeat` defaults to 100 ms and `raft_election_timeout` to a random 1 to 2 s, on a 10 ms election tick: with only a few distinct timeout values, followers time out together and split the vote. A crashed leader's followers stay fortified until its node's grants lapse, `primary_lease` (4 s, up to 5.2 s under a slewed clock), and a leader's node restarted in place asks for no lease during its restart wait, so its groups wait the same. The lapse shows the follower its leader's node is gone, so it campaigns, pre-vote first, after a random `raft_lapse_campaign` (150 to 300 ms, the range the Raft paper measured, §9.3) rather than a whole election timeout; a failed pre-vote, because another voter's grant has not lapsed on its own clocks, retries at its election timeout. So the failover budget is about 3.2 to 4.3 s from crash to new leader (a grant lapses one `primary_lease` after its last renewal, up to a heartbeat before the crash), up to 5.5 s under a slewed clock, and 1 to 2 s more for a group whose campaigns collide. Over many groups that budget is reasoned, not measured: no run has measured many groups campaigning on a lapse ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).
- **Control-plane failover and client leases.** The control-plane group fails over the same way, and meanwhile a client keeps the lease of its last answered renewal. By the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) a client ends that lease `lease_drift` and `ResumeThreshold` (1 s) early, about 7.26 s after the renewal on a clock a time daemon runs 22.6% fast, so a control-plane failover longer than about 6.3 s pauses that client's writes until the new leader answers, and one longer than about 7.9 s pauses every client's ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). The budget stays within about 6.3 s except where a slewed clock and colliding campaigns meet. `session_lease` stays at 10 s: the pause fences nobody, and a longer lease would hold a dead client's locks longer. A crashed control-plane leader's own PGs also wait for the failover plus `node_down_after` before they are marked down ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).

**Replica placement and replacement.** The control-plane leader places every data group's three voters, one per node on nodes that hold metadata, each on the up disk with the fewest voters of any group (the lower disk ID on a tie), and records them in the shard map under `ctl/`. Placement is a pure function of the shard map, the disk record, the halted and stuck replicas and the time (`meta.PlaceVoters`), counting each voter on the node its disk now reports from. A new voter always takes a replica ID its group never handed out, so a replica whose disk lost its log rejoins under a new ID; `PlaceVoters` never reuses an ID or shrinks a group, and `SetShardMap` refuses a map that would. A group obeys its own configuration, in its Raft log; the shard map is the plan its leader drives it toward (`Shard.SetPlan`). A voter is replaced:

- when its disk has been down for `refill_after`, as a PG member is refilled ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), or at once when its disk's log is gone, on the up disk with the fewest voters among other nodes holding none of its group and its own node's other disks; with none it stays, so on three metadata nodes a group with a node down runs on two voters and survives no further failure;
- when it [halted](#metadata-layer-metastore), as if its disk went down at the halt, but only on another node, since its own node's disks run the same release; each heartbeat reports halted replicas with each halt's index and age;
- when, planned and on an up disk, it has not held its plan for `refill_after` while a majority of the plan's voters do (a learner that never catches up, a replica never started, one cut off from the leader), on another node. A voter holds its plan once its own report shows a configuration that has it as a voter, at or past the plan's leave once any replica reported it. The majority keeps a gap in the reports, such as a release that sends none, from replacing every voter at once. The count lives in the leader's memory, so a control-plane failover restarts it, and since a report carries no log progress, a group whose snapshot takes longer than `refill_after` to reach a learner needs a larger `refill_after`;
- when its disk reports its log from another node, as after a drive is moved: counting it there could put two voters of a group on one node, so it is placed again under a new ID and swapped out like a dead one.

The control-plane group's entry is left as the map has it: its voters are the `rock-solid ctl` processes, so only a staged map changes them. A plan is never taken back when a dead voter returns, as a refilled PG member is not restored, so a flapping node does not start and drop a snapshot transfer each time.

**The swap.** A replacement never joins as an empty voter: with one voter of three down, an empty fourth would raise the quorum to three, and every commit would wait for it to catch up from a snapshot of up to `shard_split_size` (Ongaro and Ousterhout, §6). So the leader adds it as an etcd-raft *learner*, its node in the change's context, and once it has caught up proposes one `ConfChangeV2` that removes the dead voter and promotes the learner through a joint configuration, which etcd-raft leaves on its own. The two survivors are a majority of both halves, so the swap commits without the dead voter or the newcomer, and the [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) needs a quorum in each half throughout. Caught up means holding every entry the leader had committed a heartbeat earlier: under steady writes a learner behind a slower link trails the current commit index for good. The leader proposes one change at a time, only once it has applied an entry of its term, and never its own removal: a leader the plan drops hands its leadership to a caught-up planned voter whose node it holds a loop-pair lease from, since etcd-raft drops proposals while a transfer waits and a dead target would stall them.

**Learning a removal.** A replica whose removal commits knows it from its log. One that missed it hears nothing more, so after an election timeout without a leader it asks its peers whether it is still a member, and a peer whose applied configuration lacks it answers with its applied index; an answer at or past the asker's own applied index, while the asker's applied configuration holds it, proves the removal committed, and since IDs are never reused it is final. A peer also answers a Raft message from a replica outside its applied configuration with that notice, which reaches a leader cut off while the others swapped it out. A returning voter asks within its restart wait, so it campaigns in no election. A replica is deleted only once its group's committed configuration shows it removed, never on the shard map's word, which runs ahead of the group.

A dropped voter can still lead: if the leader fails while the group is joint, a voter of the outgoing half can win and commit its own removal, while its node, still up, renews its followers' grants. So a follower that has heard nothing from the leader it supports for an election timeout asks that leader's node about the leader's replica ID, and once that replica has learned its removal the node answers that it left the group; the follower drops its support and campaigns after `raft_lapse_campaign`. The ask names the replica ID, never the group, so a new replica of the group on that node is not taken for the old one; an ask naming no replica the node holds is answered by `raftshard.Gone`. A replica that has learned its removal serves no lease read, so dropping support for it is safe whenever the answer comes.

**A removed replica stays a voter until its group's plan is complete.** When the leave that drops it commits, it hands the entry over without applying the change to etcd-raft, which keeps the joint configuration, and its state machine applies nothing from the leave on, so it serves no read and compacts no further. Unlike a [halt](#metadata-layer-metastore), Raft runs on: it campaigns, votes, takes appends and fortifies under the joint configuration. Leading, it re-proposes the leave as an empty change each time it applies the last one, and hands its leadership to any caught-up voter of the plan, with no lease asked since a removed leader takes no proposals, trying the next target in ID order once a transfer times out. Its node deletes it once the plan is complete.

Two wedges force this ([voter replacement model](../models/voters/voters.tla)). Once the leave commits, a voter that holds it but applied only the joint configuration can still win, and commits nothing until a quorum of each half takes an entry of its term. If the old leader then halts or dies and the dropped voter has deleted itself, the outgoing half has one voter left, and the new leader, which never steps down with `CheckQuorum` off, holds the group for good (`neg-removed-deletes-at-once`); if the dropped voter instead applies its removal and campaigns no more, as etcd-raft has it, it can hold the outgoing half's newest entry, whose vote the others' older logs never win (`neg-removed-applies-removal`). Letting a replica take a peer's commit index would break the wedges too, but needs a patch to the [etcd-raft fork](tech-debts.md#workaround-etcd-raft-fork-in-third_partyraft), where staying a voter needs none. Staying is safe, since the replica is just a voter whose log holds the leave unapplied, and a quorum of the joint configuration holds a quorum of the new one; deleting it is safe once every voter of the new configuration has applied the leave, provided no restart takes a replica back across a change it knew committed, which the round rules of [Synchronous `Ready` and `Advance`](#metadata-layer-metastore) guarantee.

**When a plan is complete.** Each node's heartbeat reports each metadata replica it holds: group, replica ID, the index its applied configuration holds at, whether it is joint, its voters, and whether the replica has learned its removal. A plan is complete once every voter it names reports an index at or past the lowest at which any replica reports a configuration of the plan's voters alone. Fresh IDs make that sound: no earlier configuration had the plan's voters, so no stale or reordered report completes it early. A joint report never counts, since a voter that applied only the joint configuration can lead it and need the dropped voter's vote (`neg-joint-report-counts`), and a majority is not enough, since the voter left out can be the one leading. The leader that runs `Control` keeps nothing beyond each node's last report: answering a heartbeat while it holds its lease, it names each replica the node reported removed that its group's current plan no longer holds, once that plan is complete, and the node deletes it. Only the current plan counts, whichever plan dropped the replica, which is never looser than waiting for its own and lets a new leader rebuild everything from the shard map and the next heartbeats. A plan never waits for the one before it: when a planned voter is down for good, the next plan, replacing it, is what completes. Overlapping plans are safe (`ci-two-plans`), since every entry of the next plan comes from a leader that applied the earlier leave.

**The control-plane group's own voters** change the same way, driven by its own leader through its own `Control`, which runs only once that leader has applied an entry of its term, so a leader stuck in the joint configuration sends no delete. Each control-plane voter reports its replica every second (`VoterReport`), to its own `Control` or else to every other member of its configuration, and those reports count toward nothing a node's do, such as the releases the format level waits for. A delete stops the voter's replica and writes a round flagged deleted; the host then stops (`ErrDeleted`) and the process exits with status 3, as every later start does on replaying the flag, so a service manager should not restart it on that status (systemd's `RestartPreventExitStatus=3`). The log is kept, since a log holding no round would boot from `-voters` again; only a fresh `-format` brings the voter back.

**Group commit.** Each leader batches whatever arrived during the last round into one log entry and one network round: a `Txn` arriving with no round in flight is proposed at once, and those arriving during a round are proposed together as it becomes durable, as one entry of at most `max_group_commit_bytes` (1 MiB). The entry joins the next record of its disk's log, sharing one flush with every other shard and chunk write on that disk. The leader answers its `Txn`s as it applies its own entries, which it recognises by its term; a waiting entry of an earlier term than one it applies never committed, so its `Txn`s fail retryably, as do those waiting when the replica restores a snapshot, and a resend under the same replay key returns the result they had. Only the leader answers a client, once applied; a follower may apply an entry a round sooner, but no client is told of it there. The batching, not a bound on the round, keeps rounds short: with one entry per proposal, 32,000 proposals in flight on 1,000 groups per node ran at 164,000 proposals/s with a p50 of 178 ms, and with one entry per group per round at 1.6 to 2.0 million/s in rounds of 5 ms ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). The loop collects `Ready` only from the shards it stepped, proposed to or ticked since the last round, never by scanning all of them: a scan of `HasReady` over 250 shards per round took 54% of a loop's CPU once 0.1 ms flushes made rounds frequent.

**Event loop per core.** Each core's [event loop](#runtime-in-go) owns a set of shards outright, their Raft state and LSM trees, with no locks between them; their records go into their disk's log through its [owner loop](#on-disk-format). A commit waits for about three rounds (the leader's append, the follower's append and flush, the leader's apply), each behind its loop's round in flight, so commit latency follows loop utilization, not round size: with each loop under half busy, p50 3 ms and p99 5 ms on a 0.95 ms flush, while one loop holding 1,000 shards at 90% busy sees its p99 reach 10 to 50 ms ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). So a node spreads its shards over its loops and plans its shard leaders so that no loop runs above about half busy.

**Reads and leader leases.** The leader serves reads under a lease, with no round trip. The lease rides on the loop-pair leases that already back [primary read leases](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), as CockroachDB's leader leases ride on its store liveness: a loop holds a lease from every loop hosting a member of a group one of its shards is in, so loop-pair heartbeats renew every group's lease at once, a quiescent group keeps its lease with no Raft heartbeat, and the cost does not grow with the group count. The contract:

- Once elected, a leader *fortifies* its term: it asks each follower whose loop it holds a lease from for support, and again each heartbeat while that support does not count. A follower whose loop's grant to the leader's loop holds records the term and the grant's support epoch and answers with them and its loop's run ID; while that grant holds in that epoch and its term has not moved on, it neither campaigns nor grants a (pre-)vote in the group, except a transfer target's vote.
- The leader holds its lease while the configuration, or each half of a joint one, has a quorum of voters, itself included, whose support counts: its loop holds an unexpired lease from the follower's loop under the run ID and support epoch the follower answered with. Its own entry never expires, since it votes for a challenger only after stepping down. Each expiry is the holder's end of a loop-pair lease by the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), and a voter whose grant has no rate bound yet gives none, so a restarted node's groups hold no lease for their first 100 ms or so. A holder that ends its loop-pair leases, on `Resumed` or a tripped step check, ends every lease read on them at once.
- When a grant lapses on the grantor's clocks, every fortification under it ends and the groups led from that node campaign after `raft_lapse_campaign`. That is safe: a challenger needs a quorum of votes, a follower whose grant still holds drops them, and by the lease-time rule the leader's expiry from each follower passes before that follower's grant lapses. A follower also stops supporting its leader when the leader itself asks for a (pre-)vote in a later term, which it does only once it no longer leads, and when the leader's node answers that the leader's replica left the group (above).
- `LeaseRead` says whether a read may be served now from the entries handed over to apply, which a new leader may not until it is fortified and has committed an entry of its term. `ReadIndex` gives a caller that would rather wait the commit index to see applied; reads over the wire are refused instead ([the router on the wire](#metadata-layer-metastore)). Followers can read with ReadIndex later, if leaders get hot.

Readers never wait for writers, except one that meets a cross-shard intent (above). A reader gets a point-in-time view of the shard's LSM tree without blocking the writer; the view pins the memtable and runs it reads, so a view held open holds back the cleaner, and anything long-lived, such as a [snapshot's metadata](#snapshots-and-clones), is copied out instead.

etcd-raft's own lease (`CheckQuorum` with `ReadOnlyLeaseBased`) is rejected: it counts ticks, which a quiescent group does not take, and checks the quorum once per election timeout, so a leader that lost its quorum just after a check believes it leads for up to two election timeouts while followers refuse votes for one (`raft.go` at v3.6.0, `tickHeartbeat` and `inLease`). So `CheckQuorum` is off and `PreVote` on, and the shard implements fortification around `RawNode` with no change to the fork: its own fortification messages beside Raft's, dropping a supported leader's challengers' (pre-)votes, and every candidate's during the restart wait, before they reach etcd-raft, ticking no election while supporting a leader or waiting out a restart, calling `Campaign` itself after a lapse, and computing the lease itself. CockroachDB's fork of etcd-raft has the same mechanism, but its fortification and store liveness code is under the CockroachDB Software License, so we take the design from it, not the code.

**Leader transfer.** A planned transfer ends the old leader's lease, for the rest of its term, the moment it takes the `Ready` that carries `MsgTimeoutNow` to the target. etcd-raft keeps the old leader in office until it sees the new term, but it serves no lease read after that point, even if the transfer times out, since the target may still campaign on a late `MsgTimeoutNow`. The target campaigns with etcd-raft's transfer context, which followers let through even while they support the old leader. The new leader fortifies its own term before it serves lease reads. The open registry does not move with the transfer: the new owner starts a grace period as after a crash ([Inode owner](#metadata-layer-metastore)), and handing the registry over is phase 7's.

**Deferred:** leaderless consensus (Egalitarian Paxos, Accord). Leader-per-shard already removes the single-leader bottleneck.
## File frontend: RWX filesystem

This is the first product. Many pods on many nodes mount one POSIX directory: shared media, uploads, CI caches, datasets, app data. The data path is the shared ChunkStore path. The hard part is keeping each node's cache of pages, attributes and directory entries correct.

The kernel side is `/dev/fuse`, spoken by the codec in `internal/fuseproto`; the daemon's sans-IO machine for a mount is `fsclient.Mount` (`internal/fsclient`), which builds its MetaStore `Txn`s through `meta.FS` and its chunk I/O through `chunkstore.Client`; the driver that owns the device, `mount(2)` and the wall clock is `internal/frontend/fuse`.

### Client design

Every node runs the daemon, which mounts RWX volumes through FUSE. Three rules come straight from the [FUSE spike](#appendix-fuse-coherence-spike):

1. **The kernel page cache is used for reads only.** We never enable FUSE's `writeback_cache`. It is a mount-wide switch, it hides writes from the daemon until the kernel flushes them, and it disables the kernel's automatic invalidation on attribute changes. With it on, the daemon could not stop a node from caching a shared file.
2. **Write-back buffering lives in the daemon.** Every `write()` reaches the daemon as a FUSE request (about 5 µs), with exactly its offset and length (`fs/fuse/file.c`, `fuse_perform_write`), so the daemon takes any byte range and always knows when a file has been written. While the file is not shared, the daemon buffers writes under its [space grant](#client-design) and acknowledges at once. It flushes a chunk once it is full, the rest on `fsync`, `close` or a recall, and the oldest partial chunk when the buffer (`writeback_buffer`, 256 MiB per node) is full. A flush covers whole blocks wherever the daemon holds a block's other bytes or they lie past the end of file, which are zero on disk ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)) and which no other node can change while the file is not shared; the daemon keeps a file's last partial block after flushing it, until a recall, so a small new file and each append go out as whole blocks. The chunk primary [merges](#data-layer-chunkstore) every other partial block, which is what write-shared files pay.
3. **Whether a handle is cached is decided at `open` and cannot change.** The kernel fixes a handle's direct-I/O flag when the file is opened, so the daemon chooses it per open from the file's cache mode at that moment. Handles already open and cached are made coherent by other means (see Tier 2).

**Write path**

```
FUSE write(inode, off, data)
  past the PVC size (cached)  → ENOSPC
  file not shared and the grant covers it:
                   take it from the grant, buffer, acknowledge   // flushed later, in order
  otherwise:       flush the chunk's buffered writes first
                   chunk_id = (volume, inode, off / CHUNK_SIZE)
                   → ChunkStore.write via the chunk primary, which merges partial blocks
                   → acknowledge, or ENOSPC if the pool refuses it
                   → if the file is write-shared and the write extends it: MetaStore size = max(size, end)
any chunk write    → first, if its index >= alloc_end: MetaStore.txn([raise alloc_end, set size written so far])
close() / fsync()  → flush buffered chunks, widened to whole blocks where the daemon holds the bytes
                   → MetaStore.txn([set size, mtime = max(last write's time, mtime + 1 ns)])
                   → an error this handle has not seen yet, if any
```

`alloc_end` bounds the chunks a later [purge](#freeing-space) must delete, so no chunk is written at or past it. Its raise, in steps that double up to 16 chunks, also commits the size written so far, which lets other nodes watch a large file grow. Outside write-shared mode no extending write commits the size on its own, since close-to-open needs it only at `close`; until it commits, the node counts its own acknowledged writes in the size it gives its kernel and clamps reads to. The requests that change one inode's attributes on a node (a raise, a `close` or `fsync` commit, a `setattr`) run one at a time, each building its `Txn` from the attributes the one before it committed.

**Modification time.** A file's `mtime` comes from the wall clock (`CLOCK_REALTIME`) of the node that wrote it, sent with its `close` or `fsync` transaction. Wall clocks on different nodes disagree and can step back, so the transaction stores the latest of that time, the stored `mtime` plus 1 ns and, for a file that was write-shared, the largest `mtime` the node reported for it ([Leaving WriteShared](#cache-coherence-tiers)). A file's `mtime` therefore never goes backwards when another node writes it next, which `make` and `rsync` rely on. An explicit `utimensat` stores the time it is given. CockroachDB's hybrid logical clocks stay monotonic by the same rule ([clock skew study](../studies/clock-skew/README.md)).

**Space grants.** A buffered write is acknowledged before any disk takes it, so it must not fail later for lack of space. ext4, XFS and Btrfs give that guarantee by reserving blocks at `write()`, while the Linux NFS client, CephFS and JuiceFS report a full server only at a later `write()`, `fsync` or `close` ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). So the daemon buffers only against a *space grant*: blocks of a pool its node may hold unflushed, counted as physical blocks written, so a 100-byte append is charged a whole block per copy. Grants ride on the session heartbeat each client daemon sends the control-plane leader about once a second ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). The leader's side is `internal/meta/grants.go`. What must hold:

- **Ask.** Each heartbeat asks, per pool, for min(`writeback_buffer`, what the node holds buffered + one second of its writes at its rate over the last second). It counts writes, buffered or written through, not flushes: flushes are bounded by the grant, so a node held to a small grant would ask for less every heartbeat. An idle node asks for 0, so its grant comes back within a heartbeat, which is Lustre's shrink protocol for free. The heartbeat also carries, per pool, what the node holds buffered, the grant it holds and a cumulative count of blocks flushed, which the leader differences, so a lost heartbeat loses no report.
- **Divide.** The leader keeps a pool's grants at most half its [available](#volume-size-and-capacity) figure counted before them, so grants shrink as the pool fills and daemons turn to writing through before it is full, as CephFS's client syncs every write once its pool is nearly full. When the asks exceed that cap or the disk cap below, it divides them max-min fairly with what each node holds buffered as a floor: grantᵢ = min(askᵢ, max(bufferedᵢ, L)), the level L set so the grants fill the cap (asks of 256, 256, 64 and 16 MiB against 512 get 216, 216, 64 and 16). Each reply opens a new reuse window, so a reply never carries more than the room the other nodes' counts leave under the cap; a daemon whose grant is below what it holds buffered buffers nothing new until it drains below it. If the floors alone exceed the cap, every grant is 0. Only a renewed session is granted anything.
- **Reuse budget.** The daemon reuses its grant as each flush completes, for at most k = 2 grants' worth of flushes counted from the send of its last heartbeat whose reply it received; past that it writes through until the next reply. Once it has used half the budget it sends its heartbeat at once, as CephFS's client reports its size early, so the budget bounds unseen bytes, not throughput: a streaming node at 100 GbE sends about 41 heartbeats a second, each a memory update at the leader and no Raft entry. Whatever happens to heartbeats, clocks or the control plane, a node lands at most (1 + k) × its grant since its window began.
- **The disk cap.** For every up disk d holding a member of the pool, (1 + k) × Σ grantᵢ + F ≤ g_d, where F is what nodes reported flushed since the fills the leader holds, and g_d the disk's size less its log reserve less the larger of its fill and `write_limit`. A granted flush passes `write_limit`, and all of a node's buffer can land on one disk (one full buffer put up to 3.4 times its share on one disk in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike)), so this cap, with recovery leaving the grants' room (below), is what keeps a granted flush off the log reserve. Fills and each pool's data per disk come from the disks' heartbeats to the [failure detector](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), sizes and log reserves from each node's report of its opened disks; a heartbeat carries at most 256 pools, and the leader counts any other pool's data there as other pools', which errs low on that pool's figures. Below `write_limit` the gap is fixed by the disk's size, so the cap binds before half of available from about 80% full under a 90% `write_limit`. The cap counts live blocks while a log takes 1 to 2% more (headers, open segments, tails, the cleaner's guard); counting the live blocks each disk's log can hold is [owed](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore).
- **Recovery leaves the grants' room.** Recovery passes `write_limit`, and a refill onto disks near it would fill their logs to the reserve under grants already out, refusing flushes the daemon had acknowledged. So a primary starts copying a chunk only while every disk the copy goes to keeps, after it, its log reserve plus (1 + k) × the grants outstanding on the pools with a member there: live + reserve + (copies in flight to it + 1) × a chunk + (1 + k) × Σ grants ≤ size. The whole Σ grants counts on every disk, not a share, because one node's buffer can land on one disk. Otherwise the PG copies nothing until room may have come free (a lower fill, fewer grants, a copy ending, or a second passing), except that a leaving member without room is left out rather than stopping the copies to the others; once its lacking copies stop falling, the leader raises `Stuck` ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)). Grants are promises acknowledged writes rest on, and data safety outranks a refill's progress. The inputs are each member's live blocks, summed afresh for a recovery write, the grant totals the primaries hold for admission, and each disk's size, reserve and fill, which the leader passes on with its answer to each node's report; a primary copies nothing to a disk whose size it has not learned, and the leader passes on only fills it heard within 2 s on both its clocks while a primary keeps a member's last acknowledged fill for 4 s on both of its own, so a stale fill never restarts copies the check stopped under any [slew](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). A chunk a client write waits on is copied regardless, since stopping it would hold that write for good. What the rule leaves uncounted, so a granted flush can still be refused: those copies, other primaries' copies in flight, and copies started while fills or raised grants are a report behind. The leader holding a pool's grants until its table is recovered, and cutting grants while a refill target past `write_limit` is `Stuck`, are [owed](build-checklist.md#phase-4-embedded-metastore). Rejected: the leader counting a refill's incoming copies in F, since it cannot see which copies each primary will send where or when, cannot take back grants already out when a refill starts, and would still race its own grants between fills.
- **Counting.** The leader counts each node at (1 + k) × the larger of its grant and what it holds buffered, and at the larger of its old and new grant until a heartbeat echoes the new one, so a shrink whose reply was lost is never counted before the node has it. A flush counts in F until the leader holds a fill of every member disk received at least 1 s after the heartbeat that reported it. That is a timing assumption of the same kind as [`dead_wait`'s](#sessions-and-fencing): a disk sums its fill at most 100 ms before it sends its heartbeat, so no fill heartbeat may reach the leader more than about 0.9 s after a flush it leaves out. If one does, granted flushes can pass g_d into the reserve, and past it a granted flush is refused, reported once per handle.
- **Failover, crashes and lapsed sessions.** A new leader knows no held grants, so it counts every session at `writeback_buffer` per pool until a heartbeat echoes a grant of its own, and adds (1 + k) × the grant a session says it holds to F when the session first names a pool. Each answer carries its *issuer*, the leader lease that answered (the control-plane group's term and the lease's start on its leader's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`), and a daemon takes an answer only if its request is newer than the last it took and its issuer is not older than the one it holds, so an old leader's late reply never overrides a new one's. A leader that regains its lease starts its grants over the same way. It answers no heartbeat until its first step under its [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) has built each pool's state, and grants nothing until it holds a fill of every up member disk received at least 1 s after that step, so its fills include every flush an earlier leader heard; meanwhile daemons write through, about 1 ms per write instead of 5 µs. A lapsed session's grant counts until its [fence](#sessions-and-fencing) is acknowledged, since until then a primary can still apply its flushes, as Lustre discards an evicted client's grant only "once we're sure no more interaction with the client is possible". The cumulative flush count lives in the [handoff memfd](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade); a successor that cannot read it buffers nothing until its first heartbeat is answered, and the leader, seeing the count go down, keeps the old (1 + k) × grant in F until fills cover it.
- **Capacity figures.** The leader computes each pool's [capacity figures and conditions](#volume-size-and-capacity) from the same fills and sizes, with the grants it counts, so `Full` rises once a member disk reaches `write_limit` and the pool's available figure goes to 0 there, leaving nothing to grant.

Why a budget: a grant reusable for the whole `session_lease` would let a node whose heartbeats stopped reaching the leader, while its writes still reached storage, flush unseen for 10 s, 11 GB per disk on a 3-node `rep3` pool at 1.1 GB/s against a 9.87 GB gap. Reuse for one heartbeat interval with a peak rate per disk would close that too, but it depends on clocks, sends even a light writer to writing through after one late reply, and leaves a small fast disk almost no grant. A larger k buys a heavy writer less than a second more of buffering through a failover and lowers the cap near full in proportion, so k is small ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)).

A write the grant does not cover goes through: the daemon flushes that chunk's buffered writes and sends the write to the primary, as for a shared file, so a full pool returns `ENOSPC` from `write()`, at a round trip per write near full. The PVC size is checked first; it bounds a file's logical size, while the grant bounds new physical space, which an overwrite needs too. Mapped writes cannot be granted: the kernel sends them at writeback, so a refusal reaches `msync` or `fsync`.

**Errors after acknowledgment.** A flush can still fail: the log has no segment the cleaner can free, the session was fenced, or the daemon crashed ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). The daemon drops the data, which a recall would otherwise wait on forever, as CephFS's client discards its cache when a pool fills, and reports the error once to each handle that was open on the file, at that handle's next `write()`, `fsync` or `close()` (which the kernel sends as `FUSE_FLUSH`): `ENOSPC` for space, `EIO` otherwise. That is `errseq_t`'s rule for local filesystems, extended to `close()` as NFS does. A FUSE file volume can fail a write this way because no filesystem sits between the daemon and the application; a [block volume](#block-frontend) holds its writes instead.

**Read path**

```
cached handle:   kernel page cache → on a miss, FUSE read → daemon → ChunkStore.read
uncached handle: FUSE read every time → daemon → ChunkStore.read via the chunk primary
both:            the daemon clamps a read to the file's size, its own uncommitted writes counted
```

**Namespace operations** (`create`, `mkdir`, `link`, `symlink`, `unlink`, `rmdir`, `rename`, `setattr`) are MetaStore [transactions](#metadata-layer-metastore), single-shard except for cross-directory rename, each carrying a replay key ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). Each moves the parent directory's `mtime` and `ctime`, and `mkdir`, `rmdir` and a directory's rename its link count, through `Update`, so concurrent creators in one directory never conflict on it. A `create` without `O_EXCL` whose name another node created first opens that file, as `open(2)` with `O_CREAT` does, since the kernel would hand the `EEXIST` straight to the application; the kernel drops `O_TRUNC` after a `CREATE` reply, so the daemon applies it. A `CREATE` reply also makes the kernel skip the opener's permission check (`FMODE_CREATED`, `fs/namei.c` `do_open`), so a `CREATE` that opens an existing file checks the access mode, and `O_TRUNC`'s write, against the opener's uid, groups and `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE` read from `/proc/<pid>/status`, the capability counting only in the daemon's user namespace; the check errs toward refusing with `EACCES`, including when the opener's status cannot be read. If the file is gone by the time the daemon reads its entry, the daemon creates the name again, once, under a replay ID of its own, the request's `unique` with bit 0 set (request IDs step by 2, `FUSE_REQ_ID_STEP`, and only a `FUSE_INTERRUPT`, which makes no `Txn`, sets bit 0), since the first ID's replay record would answer the failed check again; a second loss answers `EEXIST`. With `case_index`, a create whose name only folds to another file's answers `EEXIST`.

### Cache coherence tiers

Coherence ships in tiers. Each tier is correct for the workloads in its row, and each one is a usable release.

| Tier | What | Correct for | Effort |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | RWX block: multi-attach, no daemon-side cache, fencing | KubeVirt live migration, cluster-aware apps | Small |
| 1 | Close-to-open consistency, short attribute and directory-entry timeouts, flock, unlink and rename of open files, fencing | One writer per file at a time: media, configs, uploads, datasets, CI caches | Medium |
| 2 | Per-file cache mode switch: cache while one node holds the file or all are readers; uncached once two or more nodes share it and one has written | Concurrent writers on a file, automatically, with no tuning | Medium |
| 3 | Byte-range locks, directory caching with pushed invalidations | SQLite-style apps that lock byte ranges; large directory trees | Large |

Tier 1 already beats Longhorn RWX: there is no NFS pod per volume, so no single-server bottleneck or single point of failure.

**Tier 1: close-to-open**
