- **On `open`:** the kernel takes a file's size only from `LOOKUP` and `GETATTR` replies and keeps it for the attribute timeout, and while a write on the node runs past the size it holds it discards every attribute reply (`FUSE_I_SIZE_UNSTABLE`, `fs/fuse/inode.c`, Linux 6.18.44, the spikes' kernel). So the daemon keeps three rules, which the [simulated FUSE clients](#testing-strategy) (`internal/sim`, `sim.Mount`) check against a model of that kernel:
  - **A regular file's directory entry has a timeout of 0,** so every open by path sends `LOOKUP`, which the daemon answers with the file's current attributes from the inode's owner; with `auto_inval_data` a changed `mtime` or size drops the node's old pages. An open is returned with `keep_cache` only when the attributes equal those of the node's previous open of the file, never at a first open. The strongest reason for the timeout of 0 is rename-over, an editor's atomic save: with the entry cached, an open on another node resolves the name to the old, unlinked inode and reads the old file until the entry expires. It also brings the size, which an open with a cached entry would miss.
  - **The daemon never writes an attribute reply whose attributes it fetched before it read another request for the same inode;** it fetches them again, as often as that takes. The kernel drops a reply to a request sent before the inode's attributes last changed (`attr_version`), so a stale reply landing after a later open's `LOOKUP` was sent would make the kernel keep its old size and drop the fresh one. A `LOOKUP` that has not yet read its entry counts as a request for every inode. Nor does the daemon keep attributes fetched before it last changed them itself (a commit, a raise of `alloc_end`, a `setattr`, an unlink or rename), since an old leader's lease read could otherwise put back the old size.
  - **A name that does not exist is answered with `ENOENT`,** which the kernel does not cache, never with an entry of node ID 0, which it keeps as a negative entry and so hides a file another node creates.

  What it costs: every open, every `stat` of a path and each name of an `ls -l` sends a `LOOKUP` and an owner round trip, so `READDIRPLUS` answers names and types with node ID 0, which the kernel takes as a name to look up, and a listing costs one scan of the directory. The node's own close moves `mtime`, so its next open drops its pages too.

  What it leaves open: a stream of requests, each read during the last one's fetch, holds a fetch as long as it lasts, and a stream of `LOOKUP`s anywhere in the mount, such as a `find`, holds every attribute fetch; waiting for such `LOOKUP`s to read their entries instead of fetching again would narrow that to one inode. An open that skips the path walk sends no `LOOKUP`: a reopen through `/proc/self/fd/N` or `/dev/stdin` (FUSE defines no weak revalidation) or `open_by_handle_at` may see the size and pages the node held for up to the attribute timeout. [Tier 2](#cache-coherence-tiers)'s recalls in phase 7 are where this can close ([What is not free](#what-is-not-free)).
- **On `close` and `fsync`:** the daemon flushes buffered writes and commits size and mtime. The next open on any node sees them.
- **Metadata:** attributes, and the entries of names other than regular files, are cached for 1 second by default. A `strict` mount option sets every timeout to 0. No timeout the daemon hands the kernel reaches past its [session lease](#sessions-and-fencing).
- **flock and whole-file `fcntl` locks:** MetaStore keys owned by the client's session.
- **Unlink and rename-over of open files:** removing an inode's last link records it in its slot's orphan list rather than freeing it ([Unlink](#freeing-space)). The owner moves an orphan to the [purge list](#freeing-space) once no node has it open, and only after any failover grace period. This replaces NFS's `.nfsXXXX` silly rename. It is in Tier 1 because temp files, log rotation and "write a temp file, then rename it over the original" are common.

**Tier 2: the per-file cache mode switch**

Weka caches file data in the page cache by default. When several servers access a file and at least one writes, it bypasses the page cache and sends all I/O to the backend. We do the same, and the inode's owner tracks the mode:

```go
// Exclusive: one node; it caches reads and the daemon buffers its writes.
// ReadShared: several nodes, none has written; everyone caches reads.
// WriteShared: several nodes, one or more has written; nobody caches.
type CacheMode struct {
	Kind    ModeKind
	Members []ClientID
}
```

The mode is computed from the open registry. A node counts as writing after its first write to the file, whatever flags it passed to `open()`. In ReadShared and WriteShared that first write waits for the owner to record it. In Exclusive it goes straight to the buffer; instead, a second node's open of an Exclusive file recalls the holder, whose acknowledgment says whether it has written. One that has written carries out the recall below, and the file becomes WriteShared; one that has not stops buffering, so its first write will register, keeps its cache, and the file becomes ReadShared. The round trip falls on the rare second open rather than on every file's first write, at the cost that a second open of an Exclusive file always waits for the holder. The owner records a writer only while it still has the file open, since an acknowledgment can arrive after its sender closed.

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
    direction LR
    Exclusive: Exclusive<br/>One node has the file open<br/>Cached reads, buffered writes
    ReadShared: ReadShared<br/>Several nodes, no writer<br/>Everyone caches reads
    WriteShared: WriteShared<br/>Several nodes, 1+ has written<br/>Nobody caches
    Exclusive --> ReadShared: 2nd node opens (recall), holder has not written
    Exclusive --> WriteShared: 2nd node opens (recall), holder has written
    ReadShared --> WriteShared: first write (recall)
    ReadShared --> Exclusive: all but one node close
    WriteShared --> Exclusive: all but one node close
```

*Caching turns off only while a file is write-shared, and turns back on when only one node still has it open.*

**Entering WriteShared.** The owner sends a recall to every other member and waits for every acknowledgment, or for that member's session to expire and be fenced; a timeout alone never counts as an answer. The write or open that caused the switch waits too, up to the recalled node's `session_lease`, or `recall_timeout` plus `session_lease` if it renews but does not answer, plus the fence ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). Each recall carries the owner's term and the owner counts an acknowledgment only in the term it names, so a delayed acknowledgment of a failed owner's recall never stands for a newer one; within a term, one recall pending per file and fenced sessions never returning make the term enough. The cache-mode recall model, `models/recall/recall.tla`, checks these rules. A recall does four things on the receiving node:

1. **Flush:** write the daemon's buffered writes for the file to the ChunkStore.
2. **Drop cached pages:** `notify_inval_inode` (a few µs).
3. **Uncache new opens:** from now on, open the file with `direct_io`.
4. **Revalidate old handles:** a one-time drop is not enough, because an already-open cached handle refills the page cache on its next read (spike test T3). So while the file is WriteShared, the daemon answers every attribute request for it with a new change counter in the mtime field, with an attribute timeout of 0: the larger of the stored `mtime` and the last it reported for the file, plus 1 ns. With `auto_inval_data` on, the kernel then drops the cached pages before each `read()` on an old handle and fetches fresh data (spike T5c, about 15 µs per read locally), with no extra network trip.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant A as Node A (writer)
    participant O as Inode owner
    participant B as Node B (reader, cached)
    A->>O: first write to file (mode ReadShared)
    O->>B: recall(inode)
    B->>B: flush buffer, drop pages, direct_io for new opens, revalidate old handles
    B-->>O: ack
    O-->>A: mode = WriteShared
    A->>A: write goes straight to the chunk primary
```

**While WriteShared:**

- **Data:** every read and write goes to the chunk primary, which orders them, at one network round trip per I/O. Write-sharing is rare in homelab workloads, so this is acceptable.
- **File size:** a write that extends the file updates the size in the MetaStore before it is acknowledged, so readers on other nodes can read past the old end of file. Writes inside the file don't touch the MetaStore.
- **Appends** (`O_APPEND`) from several nodes use a MetaStore compare-and-set on the size.
- **mmap:** FUSE does not tell the daemon when a file is mapped, and mapped pages are not revalidated on access (spike T5b). So after each write it commits, the writing node tells the other members' daemons, and each calls `notify_inval_inode` on its own kernel: mapped readers see the write within one message delay. The `mmap_sync` mount option (off by default) makes each write wait for those invalidations. Shared-writable `mmap` across nodes stays a non-goal.

**Leaving WriteShared.** When all but one node have closed the file, the owner returns it to Exclusive with no recall, since no other node is caching. The change counters ran ahead of the stored `mtime`, so a daemon never reports a lower `mtime` for a file than it last did, and each closing node's `close` transaction carries the largest `mtime` it reported ([Modification time](#client-design)): a node that sees the file afterwards sees an `mtime` at least as late as any the closed nodes showed.

### Sessions and fencing

Each client daemon holds one session, for its FUSE mounts and block devices alike, and every chunk write carries the session's ID. The session's locks, open registrations and cache rights all belong to it. The control plane's side is `internal/meta/sessions.go`, the daemon's `meta.SessionClient` (`internal/meta/sessclient.go`), and the protocol is `models/sessions/sessions.tla`.

**The control plane owns every session.** A session is a key in the control-plane group, `ctl/session/{id}` → node and state, with IDs from a counter that never reuses one. Its lease (`session_lease`, 10 s by default) is renewed by the session heartbeat (`SessionBeat`), which the client daemon sends the control-plane leader about once a second, beside the node's failure-detection report rather than inside it, since its timing comes from the lease holder and only a leader under its leader lease answers it. Renewals change only the leader's memory; the Raft log changes only when a session starts, when a crashed daemon's successor commits its new [incarnation](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade), and when a session is fenced. So renewal costs one message per daemon per second whatever the number of shards, and inode owners never decide that a session expired: they cannot disagree, and their failovers leave sessions alone. Chubby, ZooKeeper, etcd and HDFS keep sessions at their leader the same way; CephFS lets each metadata server expire its own sessions but turns the first expiry into a cluster-wide blocklist, and Lustre's servers evict clients on their own only because each holds both the locks and the data they protect ([session expiry study](../studies/session-expiry/README.md)).

**Each side counts the lease on its own clocks**, by the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection). The client is the holder and counts from the send of its last answered renewal; the leader is the grantor and counts from when it processed it. A new leader's count is the hard case: it starts on a clock the client's lease was never measured against, so a voter whose raw clock runs fast could fence a client whose lease still runs. So every leader also bounds its own raw clock's rate over each client's: each heartbeat b names the last answer a the client got, with a's arrival and b's send on the client's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, and the leader, which kept its own raw time of answering a, takes (b received − a sent) ÷ (b sent − a arrived), a bound no delay can make too low. Answers pair with sends by request ID, not arrival order, so request IDs rise with every send, a resend included, and across a daemon restart (the incarnation in their high bits). Only the last pair counts, with no rate window. The leader fences a session only once `session_lease` times the larger of 1 and that bound has passed on both its clocks since the renewal, or since the start of its own lease after a failover; a session it has not measured it fences only after `dead_wait` (2 × `session_lease`, 20 s). The old lease therefore ends before any successor's count, as long as no voter's raw clock runs twice as fast as a client's.

**A bound above 2 counts as 2**, so no measured session waits longer than `dead_wait`. The cap adds no assumption, since an unmeasured session's `dead_wait` already rests on the same one. Without it, a client whose VM stood still between an answer and its next heartbeat would measure a bound in the thousands, and if it then died it would keep its locks for hours. A client that stood still ends its own lease on `Resumed`, so the cap never shortens a lease it holds.

What the rate bound costs: a client that dies, or cannot reach the new leader, within a lease of a control-plane failover keeps its locks for `dead_wait` instead of `session_lease`; a daemon's start holds its writes about 100 ms more, until its second renewal (`rate_gap` after its first) gives it a bound. The rules that keep a leader from fencing early, all of which only delay an expiry:

- Only a leader holding its [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) answers renewals or commits a session change, a start as well as a fence. It leaves unanswered a renewal for a session at or above its table's next ID, since its applied table can lag a start the old leader committed.
- Each time its lease starts, a leader gives every live session a full lease from that start before it expires any, since renewals live only in the previous leader's memory.
- A leader whose loop fell behind, with more than 2 s between two inputs, or that resumes from a pause restarts every session's timer, as CephFS's metadata server skips expiries after it was itself slow; a `Resumed` also drops each session's last answer, since a pair across the leader's own stop would measure too low.
- A start names the client's start request, 64 random bits sent again unchanged until answered, and the session's entry keeps it, so a start resent after a lost answer or to a new leader makes one session rather than a second that never renews.

How a session ends:

- **The client limits itself.** It issues no write after its lease ends, and no attribute or directory-entry timeout it gives the kernel reaches past that instant, so a paused client's kernel stops trusting its cached pages when the lease ends and the daemon, seeing its lease lapsed, drops the file's pages first. Mapped pages are the exception, since the kernel does not revalidate them (spike T5b); whether attribute timeouts hold across a machine suspend is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions). A daemon whose lease lapsed without word of a fence holds its requests until a renewal is answered, so a control-plane failover shows as a pause, as in Chubby's grace period.
- **The control plane fences.** The leader fences a session when its lease runs out on the leader's clock. An owner that asks because the session left a recall unanswered for `recall_timeout` (default 30 s) or a grace period without re-registering, and an operator who evicts it, only stop its renewals, and the leader fences it once its lease runs out: fencing at once would break the rule every fence keeps, that the client's own lease has ended, since a client that still renews trusts its cached pages and locks until then. The leader commits every session due as one new version of the *session table*, which lists the live sessions with each one's newest incarnation and node, and those being fenced, published like the placement table; a start does not make a version, so storage nodes apply versions only to reject sessions.
- **Storage nodes reject.** Every storage node applies the version, from then on rejects every write from a fenced or removed session, queued ones included, and acknowledges through its heartbeat, which reports the oldest version its replicas hold, never one only received (`internal/node` hands each table to every replica, taking tables only from the control plane's address). A store's fence table lives in memory and starts empty, and a replica takes no session's write before it holds a table: persisting it would let a replica open with an older table while its node reports the newer one. A write from a session newer than the node's table waits, at most 64 of them, while the node fetches the table, and is refused if that table does not know the session either. The leader answers a fetch (`Control.FetchAnswer`) only under its leader lease and once a session-table `Txn` of its own has committed, since only a commit whose check of the table's head passed shows its applied table current. Session 0 marks a recovery write, which neither check applies to.
- **Owners release last.** Once every storage node has acknowledged the version, or has been marked down in a committed table in every PG that names its disks, the leader commits it as acknowledged and the fenced sessions leave the table; a version fenced again before every node held it is acknowledged with the next. A down node is marked up again only once its heartbeat reports the current version. Owners act only on that: they reject requests from sessions not live in their table, a lock key whose session has left the table is free and is deleted in the transaction that grants the lock to the next holder, the session's open registrations go, and its recalls count as answered.
- **A fenced client starts over.** It learns from its next renewal or a rejected write and starts a new session, unless its node is *out of service*: while `ctl/node/{node}` holds that fact, which an operator commits before Kubernetes moves the node's pods elsewhere ([Actions](#actions)), the leader refuses every session start from the node, and a start's `Txn` checks the key, so a start staged before the fact committed fails. The fact's `Txn` checks the session table's head, as the other session `Txn`s do, so a start that commits after the leader saw every session of the node fenced but before the fact commits fails the fact, which the leader then checks again. Pods the node still runs, because its kubelet has not yet heard that the API server deleted them, then cannot write beside their replacements. The sessions model checks these rules. Every call on a handle opened under the old one fails with `EIO`, since the owners closed the old session's opens, so applications get errors rather than silently stale data. Reads the kernel serves from its page cache or an mmap through such a handle, and a daemon that has not yet learned of its fence, miss this until [Client fencing](build-checklist.md#phase-6-file-frontend) closes them.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client daemon
    participant L as Control-plane leader
    participant P as Storage nodes
    participant O as Inode owner
    C->>L: heartbeat, session S
    L-->>C: renewed
    Note over C: paused, its lease ends lease_drift early
    Note over L: no renewal of S for session_lease
    L->>L: commit S fenced, table version v
    L->>P: session table v
    P-->>L: applied v, or the node is marked down
    L->>L: commit v acknowledged
    L->>O: session table, S gone
    O->>O: free S's locks, drop its opens, answer its recalls
    C->>P: late write, session S
    P-->>C: rejected
```

*The client's lease ends before the leader's, the fence commits after both, and owners release only once every storage node has applied it.*

This is safe because the client's lease ends first: it counts from a send that came before the leader processed the renewal, and the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) makes a holder's count end before its grantor's whatever a time daemon does to their clocks. A renewal answered by a deposed leader was answered under its leader lease, which ended before the new leader's began. So the client issues no write after the fence commits, every write it issued earlier is applied before its primary applies the fence or is rejected after, and the next holder writes only once every storage node has applied it or has been marked down. Marking a node down starts a new [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) of every PG with a member on it, after which peering stops any write tagged with an earlier one, and in every other PG each member checks the fence on every write it receives, replicated ones included. A write the fenced client got applied is therefore ordered before every write of the next holder.

A dead client's locks wait `session_lease` on the leader's clock plus one commit and one acknowledgment round, at most one heartbeat interval, or until an unreachable storage node is marked down; a control-plane failover restarts the count at `dead_wait`. When the client's own node crashed, that node is already down, since `node_down_after` is shorter than `session_lease`. Fences are batched, so a switch failure that strands many nodes costs one version and one round, and the session table is versioned apart from the placement table, so a fence never waits behind a rebalance. A control-plane outage longer than about 6.3 s can pause the writes of a client whose clock a time daemon runs fast, one longer than about 7.9 s every client's, until a new leader answers, and it fences none of them ([Raft shard timing](#metadata-layer-metastore)).

This is the same machinery for flock (Tier 1), recalls (Tier 2), snapshot cuts and RWX block (Tier 0).

### Kubernetes integration for RWX

- **CSI:** advertise `ReadWriteMany` for `volumeMode: Filesystem`. The node plugin asks the local daemon to mount the volume with `allow_other`, then bind-mounts it into each pod. The daemon's container mounts the kubelet directory with `mountPropagation: Bidirectional`, which requires a privileged container; without it the mount stays in the daemon's own mount namespace. Application pods need no propagation, since their volume is mounted before their containers start. The node plugin also holds the daemon's `/dev/fuse` descriptors ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)).
- **fsGroup:** advertise the CSI `VOLUME_MOUNT_GROUP` node capability. Kubelet then passes the pod's fsGroup to the driver instead of recursively changing ownership of every file, which would stall mounts of large volumes.
- **Compute-only nodes** run the daemon in client-only mode (`rock-solid node -role client`): no disks, no shards, just FUSE.

### Daemon restart and upgrade

When the last process holding a mount's `/dev/fuse` descriptor exits, the kernel aborts the connection, and every pod using the mount gets "Transport endpoint is not connected". The [FUSE restart spike](#appendix-fuse-restart-spike) showed that any process holding the same descriptor can serve the mount without a new handshake, and files opened under the old process keep working. One process per volume would not avoid this, since that process can crash too; JuiceFS's mount pods pass their descriptors on for the same reason. So the daemon restarts in place:

- **The CSI node plugin holds every descriptor, from mount time.** The daemon passes each `/dev/fuse` descriptor, every clone of it, and a memfd holding the negotiated `INIT` parameters, the session ID, the cumulative flush count and the handles holding unflushed data to the CSI node plugin over a Unix socket in a hostPath directory both pods mount, and a successor takes them back. The mount belongs to the connection, not to a process or mount namespace: the [FUSE handoff spike](#appendix-fuse-handoff-spike) served an `allow_other` mount from the descriptor alone after the daemon's namespace disappeared. Each side hands descriptors only to a root peer, checked with `SO_PEERCRED`, since a descriptor is the whole filesystem. A successor sets the file flags it needs, since flags such as `O_NONBLOCK` belong to the shared open file. The kernel never renegotiates `INIT`, so the successor refuses a mount whose saved parameters differ from its own; a release that changes them, or that serves an opcode its predecessor answered with an `ENOSYS` the kernel remembers (below), needs a remount.
- **Kernel-visible IDs come from durable state.** The FUSE node ID is the inode ID, and file handles name state the successor can rebuild on a miss. Lookup counts are lost in a crash, so the daemon tolerates `FORGET` for nodes it does not know.
- **An upgrade drains; a crash resends.** For an upgrade, the old process stops reading requests, finishes those in flight, flushes its write-back buffer and hands over, a stall of a few milliseconds in the spike. After a crash, the successor writes `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` at once, and the kernel requeues every request the dead process had read but not answered; without it those callers would block forever.
- **Replayed requests are idempotent.** A resent `mkdir` that the dead process had already applied would return `EEXIST`. So every mutating MetaStore request carries a replay key, `r/{session}/{mount}/{request}` in the slot it changes ([key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)). If that record exists, the shard returns the stored `Result` and applies nothing; otherwise it applies the `Txn` and stores its `Result` in the same transaction, if the result is final: every check passed, or the failed one is marked final because the daemon returns it as an errno (`EEXIST`, `ENOENT`). A lost compare-and-set race, such as `close`'s, stores nothing, and the daemon reads again and retries under the same key. The key holds the mount ID because FUSE numbers requests per connection. Each request also carries its mount's oldest outstanding request ID, below which the shard removes the mount's records in that slot; when a session leaves the session table, each shard's leader removes its `r/{session}/` records in the background. The successor resumes the dead process's session, which the memfd names.
- **A crash loses the write-back buffer, and says so.** Unflushed buffered writes die with the process, as dirty pages die in a kernel crash, but here the application keeps running, so the successor fails the next `write()`, `fsync` or `close` on each handle the memfd lists with `EIO` ([Errors after acknowledgment](#client-design)). A flush the dead process already sent can still reach its primary, and nothing resends it, so the successor's first heartbeat names its new incarnation, the control plane commits it in a new [session-table](#sessions-and-fencing) version, and every storage node rejects the older incarnation's writes from then on, as for a fence. Until that version is acknowledged, the successor closes none of its predecessor's files and answers none of their recalls or drains, so the stale flush can neither land after an unlinked file's purge and bring back a chunk (the purge model, `models/purge/purge.tla`, fails without this rule) nor overwrite another node's later write.
- **Mount settings.** The daemon negotiates `FUSE_PARALLEL_DIROPS`; without it, one unanswered `LOOKUP` blocks every lookup in its directory for the whole handoff. It never negotiates a request timeout shorter than the worst-case restart, since an expired request aborts the whole mount. It never unmounts on exit and does not rely on `auto_unmount`; unmounting belongs to CSI `NodeUnpublishVolume`. The one mount it undoes is its own that never started serving.
- **No FUSE over io_uring.** Since Linux 6.14 a daemon can take requests from per-CPU io_uring queues (`FUSE_OVER_IO_URING`). That transport loses requests in a crash, on Linux 6.18.44 and mainline alike: `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` does not requeue requests fetched from a queue, a request handed to the dying process can fail in the application with `ECANCELED`, and a connection whose ring was ready can never go back to `/dev/fuse`. So the daemon never negotiates it; revisiting that once the kernel requeues these requests is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
- **The frontend speaks `/dev/fuse` itself.** A successor must adopt a descriptor with saved `INIT` parameters, write `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` and echo the full 64-bit request ID. libfuse 3.18 does none of these in its default build; go-fuse v2.12.0 does only the echo, and its goroutine per request, its reply-from-the-call API and its per-request allocation all break the [runtime rules](#runtime-in-go). So the FUSE protocol is a hand-written, fuzzed codec in a sans-IO package (`internal/fuseproto`), a few dozen opcodes, speaking FUSE 7.45, the protocol of Linux 6.18, which has `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND`. For some opcodes the kernel remembers an `ENOSYS` answer for the connection's life: `CREATE`, `TMPFILE`, `LINK`, `STATX`, `ACCESS`, `FSYNCDIR`, `RENAME2` ([`fs/fuse/dir.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/fs/fuse/dir.c)) and the four xattr opcodes ([`xattr.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/fs/fuse/xattr.c)), which a Samba gateway calls for every entry. The codec decodes all of these, and the filesystem client answers each per call with an errno the kernel does not remember where it lacks the operation (`EINVAL` for a `rename` flag, `EOPNOTSUPP` for `O_TMPFILE`, `ENODATA` for an xattr), so a later release can serve it on a live mount. An opcode the codec does not decode is answered `ENOSYS`. The frontend's driver reads and writes non-blocking `/dev/fuse` descriptors in the event loops, as it does sockets; how one mount's requests spread across loops is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

Block volumes have the same problem: a ublk device disappears when its server exits, unless it was created with ublk's user recovery. The block frontend creates every device with `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY` and `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY_REISSUE`, as SPDK's ublk target does, plus `UBLK_F_QUIESCE` on kernels that have it (6.16 and later):

- **The kernel keeps the device.** Once the server's `/dev/ublkcN` is closed, `/dev/ublkbN` stays with its size, parameters and flags, and new requests wait in the block layer, without a timeout, until a successor recovers it ([`ublk_drv.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/drivers/block/ublk_drv.c)). `/dev/ublkcN` admits one opener, so unlike `/dev/fuse` no other process holds the descriptor. The daemon stores the volume ID in the device's `ublksrv_flags`, which the kernel keeps and returns with `GET_DEV_INFO`, so a successor finds its devices without a file of its own.
- **A crash reissues; an upgrade drains.** With `REISSUE`, every request the dead server had received is requeued for the successor; without it they would fail with `EIO`, and a failed write makes ext4 abort its journal and XFS shut down, where a reissue costs the pod a stall. For an upgrade, `QUIESCE_DEV` stops dispatch, the old server finishes what it holds and exits, and nothing is reissued; without `QUIESCE`, what the old server received but never sent is reissued, which is safe since none of it left the node.
- **A reissued write is idempotent, and the dead server's copy is fenced.** A block write carries no compare-and-set, so the same bytes applied twice give the same blocks, and the block layer completes the original only when the reissue completes. But the dead server's own copy, already queued on a socket, can still reach the primary after the process dies ([`tcp.c` `__tcp_close`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/net/ipv4/tcp.c#L3123)) and land after the reissue and the pod's next write. So each connection's hello names the client's session and *incarnation*, the daemon's start time on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, and so does every chunk write and punch, in 8 bytes. A primary that has seen a newer incarnation of a session closes the older one's connections and rejects their writes, checking when it orders a write, so a write it already holds is caught too. The incarnation rides in each write rather than only in the hello because the old connection, the new one and the chunk's loop can be three different [event loops](#runtime-in-go), and ordering hellos before writes across loops would be a rule the runtime keeps forever. The primary's table of newest incarnations need not survive a primary change, since the copy is bound to its connection and a primary orders a held write again only if no other primary led the PG since ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); the phase 2 epoch change model checks that these two rules are enough. The same rule fences the chunk writes of a dead FUSE daemon that the kernel resends; its flushes, which nothing resends, are fenced through the session table (above).
- **RWX block.** Another node's write to the same block between the original and its reissue is overwritten by the reissue. If the original committed, it takes effect twice, and a reader on a third node can see it, the other node's write, then it again ([Single order per chunk](#data-layer-chunkstore)). KubeVirt's live migration never writes one block from two nodes at once, so it never meets this.

The holder restarts and upgrades too, and its pod and the daemon's are rolled in an order that never leaves a FUSE descriptor with neither:

- **While the holder restarts, the daemon holds.** The daemon keeps its own copy of every descriptor, so its mounts keep serving while the node plugin is down. A new holder connects, the daemon sends it every descriptor, clone and memfd again, and the holder confirms once it holds them all; until then the daemon refuses a planned handoff, and a daemon crash in that window aborts the node's FUSE mounts, the one case in which a restart reaches the pods. ublk devices do not depend on the holder.
- **The holder stores, never parses.** It keeps descriptors and memfds as opaque objects behind a small protocol of its own (hold, list, give back), versioned apart from the daemon, so the memfd's contents can change in any release without a new holder. A daemon accepts the mount and unmount calls of the previous release's plugin, so an upgrade rolls the daemon first.
- **One node, one pod at a time.** Under the default `RollingUpdate` a chart upgrade could replace a node's daemon and node plugin at overlapping times and drop every descriptor. Both DaemonSets therefore use `updateStrategy: OnDelete`, and `rock-solid upgrade` walks the nodes one at a time: it deletes the daemon pod and waits until the successor has adopted every mount and device and the holder holds every descriptor, then does the same for the node plugin pod, and before the next node waits until no PG with a member on this one lacks a member or still recovers, as Ceph's upgrades check `ok-to-stop`.

### What is not free

- File metadata speed is bounded by MetaStore round trips, so small-file workloads never match a local disk. Every shared POSIX system pays this. Tier 3's pushed directory invalidations let caches live longer.
- While a file is write-shared, its reported mtime changes on every `stat` (a side effect of revalidation). Tools that compare mtimes on a file that another node is actively writing may notice.
- Tier 1's close-to-open holds for opens by path. An open that skips the path walk, a reopen through `/proc/self/fd` or `open_by_handle_at`, may see the size and pages its node held for up to the attribute timeout (1 s by default) ([Tier 1](#cache-coherence-tiers)). Samba's reopens are safe. Phase 7's recalls are where this can close.
- SQLite in WAL mode works from one node at a time only. Its WAL index is a `-shm` file that every connection maps and updates through shared memory, which no filesystem keeps coherent across nodes, and SQLite's own documentation says WAL does not work over a network filesystem. Tier 3's byte-range locks let several nodes share a database in rollback-journal mode, never in WAL mode.
- SMB for macOS and Windows clients goes through one Samba gateway per volume, an smbd pod mounting the volume through the FUSE frontend like any other pod. The [Samba spike](#appendix-samba-gateway-spike) found that Samba's leases, share modes and lock state are per gateway, and that nothing a pod on another node writes reaches them. So a share that pods also write runs with `oplocks = no`: its SMB clients cache nothing and see a pod's write on their next open, at a round trip per read. Share modes and byte-range locks hold between the gateway's own clients only, which keeps byte-range locks in Samba's lock database (`posix locking = no`). The volume is created with `case_index` on, so no pod can create a name that SMB clients could not tell from another. Leases that stay coherent with other nodes' writers wait for Tier 2, through a Samba VFS module that registers the gateway's opens with the inode owner and turns a recall into a lease break, and several gateways per volume for Clustered Trivial Database (CTDB), a MetaStore-backed cluster lock and cluster-wide FUSE locks: phase 7 or later.

## Block frontend

Block volumes use the same ChunkStore path and never touch the MetaStore on the hot path.

**Block 4K write**

```
pod write() → kernel block layer → userspace block device (ublk) in node daemon
  → chunk_id = (volume_id, 0, offset / CHUNK_SIZE) // computed
  → pg = (pool, top bits of xxh3_64(chunk_id))     // computed
  → placement_table[pg] → primary, interval        // in-memory, per-PG interval
  → primary: log append ‖ send to the other replicas → acks → done
```

**RWO:** a single-writer attach record is taken in the MetaStore at attach. It prevents two nodes from mounting one volume and corrupting it. Remote clients use NVMe over Fabrics over TCP (NVMe-oF/TCP) instead of ublk.

**Logical block size.** A block volume exposes 4 KiB logical and physical blocks, `io_min` 4 KiB and `io_opt` the chunk size, so the kernel never sends a write smaller than a block and the block path never merges. Vitastor exposes its 4 KiB `bitmap_granularity` as the sector size the same way, while krbd keeps 512 bytes and BlueStore merges each 512-byte write on every replica. KubeVirt guests that see 512-byte sectors are padded by QEMU's own read-modify-write. The StorageClass parameter `block_size: 512` exposes 512-byte logical blocks for guests and tools that need them; the primary [merges](#data-layer-chunkstore) them like a file's partial blocks, at the cost of a base read and a 4 KiB block per write. The size is fixed when the volume is created and inherited by its clones and restores, since XFS refuses a device whose logical block is larger than the sector size it was formatted with.

**RWX block (Tier 0):** several nodes attach one raw device; the homelab user is KubeVirt live migration. The changes are a multi-writer attach record and no caching in the daemon, so every I/O goes to the chunk primary. The kernel's own cache for the block device is the application's choice: KubeVirt opens shared disks with `cache=none` (direct I/O), which is what makes live migration safe.

**A full pool pauses a volume's new writes; it never fails them.** A write the pool refuses at [`write_limit`](#volume-size-and-capacity) because it needs new space is held until the pool has room again: the daemon retries it whenever the control plane reports that the pool's available figure has grown, with no time limit. The [block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike) decided this: a hold that ended when the pool grew completed every held write and left ext4 and XFS clean, while failing the writes lost their data, metadata among it, stopped both filesystems, and left a log whose replay needs the same new blocks, so failing needs the same space as holding, plus a remount, and still loses data. dm-thin's default, a 60 s hold that then fails, does the same a minute later, and ublk never times a request out for a privileged device. Reads, discards and overwrites of live unshared blocks pass `write_limit` and carry on, but held writes keep their ublk tags and ext4's journal commit waits for them, so a volume that keeps writing soon stops altogether, as a Ceph RBD image pauses while its cluster is full, and its pod cannot be killed until the writes complete. That is the trade: a hang that ends without loss once space appears, instead of an outage that needs the same space and a remount, and loses data. While writes are held, the node plugin posts a Warning event on the PVC naming the pool, the pool's condition is `Full`, and a metric counts held writes per volume. There are three ways out, in this order:

1. Free space outside the volume: delete snapshots, which are what make overwrites need new space, delete other volumes, or add a disk. The writes resume by themselves.
2. Raise the pool's `write_limit` into the room above it. The writes resume at once, and space grants and recovery have less room.
3. `rock-solid volume fail-held <volume>` completes the held writes with `ENOSPC`, for when the pod must go. Their data is lost; ext4 then refuses every write and XFS shuts down at unmount, and the filesystem is consistent again once it is mounted with room in the pool, which replays its log.

**A hold must not take the node with it.** Nothing else on the node may wait with the pod: the node plugin answers `NodeGetVolumeStats` for a block volume from the daemon's figures, never from the filesystem on the device, and the daemon warns at start when `kernel.hung_task_panic` is set, since the kernel then panics the node once a write is held past `hung_task_timeout_secs` (120 s by default). Whether kubelet keeps the node Ready while a pod's processes sleep on held writes for hours is for a real cluster to show ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)).

**Remote clients.** An NVMe over Fabrics host cannot be held forever: Linux times a command out after 30 s (`nvme_core.io_timeout`) and retries a status without Do Not Retry at most 5 times (`nvme_core.max_retries`, [`drivers/nvme/host/core.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/drivers/nvme/host/core.c#L399)). So the target holds a write at most 20 s, then answers Capacity Exceeded without Do Not Retry and with a Command Retry Delay naming the longest delay its controller advertises, 6,553.5 s, the most the field holds. A stock host so pauses about 9 hours before it fails the write, after which its filesystem stops as above; a client that must never fail raises `nvme_core.max_retries`, up to 255, about 19 days.

**Discard is on, and restart is invisible.** The device advertises discard, and the CSI node plugin formats a `volumeMode: Filesystem` block volume (ext4, or the StorageClass's `fsType`) and mounts it with `discard`, so files the pod deletes return their space to the pool at once as [punches](#freeing-space). In the [block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike), without discard a write after deleting a file still failed, because the freed space never reached the device. A scheduled `fstrim` would leave a full pool full until its next run, and a punch costs one log entry, so online discard is cheap here. A raw block volume's consumer sends its own discards. Devices are created with ublk's user recovery and reissue, so they survive a [daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) with requests in flight.

## Snapshots and clones

Volume snapshots are an optional CSI capability. Once the driver has it, backup tools such as Velero and Kasten work with no further integration: Velero's data mover creates a temporary PVC from the snapshot, mounts it and uploads it with Kopia. Without snapshots, Velero falls back to file-system backup from the live mount, which is not point-in-time.

**CSI side.**

- Advertise the `CREATE_DELETE_SNAPSHOT` controller capability (`CreateSnapshot`, `DeleteSnapshot`, `ListSnapshots`) and `CLONE_VOLUME`, and support `CreateVolume` from a snapshot. Backup needs restore too, since the data mover reads through a restored PVC.
- Ship the `csi-snapshotter` sidecar in the controller pod and a `VolumeSnapshotClass`. The snapshot CRDs and `snapshot-controller` are installed once per cluster and are missing from many distributions, so the Helm chart installs them optionally.
- `csi-sanity` already has snapshot tests.

**A snapshot is a crash-consistent cut.** It holds what the volume would hold had every node using it crashed at one instant: every write acknowledged before the cut and none issued after. Each chunk has its own primary, so no single chunk can define that instant. The cut is taken at the writers instead, with the same fan-out and fencing as a [Tier 2 recall](#cache-coherence-tiers):

```
snapshot(volume):
  E = next snapshot epoch of the volume
  to every node with the volume attached or mounted, in parallel:
    pause new writes and namespace operations on the volume
    wait until in-flight ones are acknowledged, then ack    // or its session expires and is fenced
  txn: put volume/{id}/snap/{E}, plus a marker key in each shard holding fs/{volume}/   // one shard until phase 13
  resume every node; its writes now carry snapshot epoch E
```

**A paused node resumes only once it knows the cut's outcome.** The cut is a two-phase commit: pausing prepares it and `volume/{id}/snap/{E}` commits it. A node whose coordinator crashed cannot resume on a timeout alone: if E committed and the node resumed on the old epoch, its next writes would not freeze, and the snapshot would hold a post-cut write. So it asks the MetaStore whether E exists. How the coordinator's commit is made conditional, so that a cut a node gave up on can never commit later, and how a node that attaches or mounts the volume during the cut is paused, are for the phase 11 model of the cut to settle.

Writes pause for about one round trip to every node using the volume. Tagging writes without the pause would be cheaper but wrong: a node still on the old snapshot epoch could write data derived from a post-cut write it read from another node, and the snapshot would hold an effect without its cause. A block RWO volume has one node to pause; a volume nobody is using needs no pause.

The daemon's [write-back buffer](#client-design) is not flushed by default. Unflushed data is what a crash would lose too, and closed files are always included because `close` flushes. The `VolumeSnapshotClass` parameter `flush: true` makes each node flush its buffer for the volume before it acks. Application consistency (a database's own flush, `fsfreeze`) comes from backup-tool hooks.

**Data: copy-on-write per block.** A write carrying snapshot epoch E to a chunk last written before E freezes the chunk's current version for the snapshot with a `freeze` entry in the [log](#on-disk-format), or version 0, the absent chunk, if it has never been written, then applies. The frozen and live versions share every 4K block the write does not touch, so the first write after a snapshot writes only the blocks it touches, not a 4 MiB chunk copy; the [on-disk format](#on-disk-format) shares them without reference counts. In an erasure-coded pool a frozen version keeps the form its chunk has: copies while the chunk is held as copies, and when the chunk has been idle for `seal_after`, the [seal](#when-chunks-are-encoded) encodes every version, live and frozen, each into shards of its own, since it reads the whole chunk anyway. Encoding a frozen version as soon as it froze would read and write the whole chunk on the first 4 KiB write after a snapshot, the cost per-block copy-on-write exists to avoid. Encoded versions share nothing, so each frozen version of a sealed chunk costs a full encoded chunk, 1.5× in `ec4-2`, where as copies it cost 3× only the blocks that differ. A write to an encoded chunk after a snapshot [returns](#when-chunks-are-encoded) only the live version to copies. A backup reads the snapshot with `read(at_ver)`, which [any PG member](#data-layer-chunkstore) holding the version can serve, so backups don't load the primaries.

**Metadata.** On each shard, the marker key's Raft log index is the cut. When the shard applies the marker, it opens a point-in-time view of its LSM tree, the same kind of view a Raft snapshot is built from, and copies the `fs/{volume}/` range out of it into `fs/{snapshot}/` in the background. The view is closed once the copy is done, so it never holds back space reclamation for the life of the snapshot.

**Restore and clone.** A restored volume gets a new `volume_id` and a parent pointer to `(source volume, E)`:

- **Metadata** is copied from `fs/{snapshot}/` into the new volume's range: one pass over its inodes and directory entries, no data. Orphaned inodes are dropped, since nothing has them open.
- **Data** is read through: a read of a block that chunk `(new, inode, k)` has not written falls through to `(source, inode, k)` as it was at snapshot epoch E, at the cost of an extra hop. Writes land in the new volume, block by block. Inode numbers are unchanged, so `st_ino` survives a restore.
- **Clone from a PVC** (`CLONE_VOLUME`) is a snapshot followed by a restore.
- **Flatten.** With the StorageClass setting `flatten: true` (off by default), a background task copies every chunk the new volume has not written yet, then drops the parent pointer. Off saves space and suits Velero's temporary PVC, which is read once and deleted; a long-lived restored volume can turn it on. A restore into a different pool always flattens. To change a volume's pool without a new PVC, [move it](#moving-a-volume-to-another-pool).

**Deletion.** Deleting a snapshot removes its record at once. A sweep over the volume's PGs then drops each frozen version that no remaining snapshot and no unflattened clone needs. A deleted volume that still has snapshots or clones is hidden from Kubernetes and keeps those chunks until they are gone. Snapshot space counts toward the pool (see [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)).

## S3 frontend (later)

```
stream body → fresh chunk ids → ChunkStore.write (once each; encoded on write in an erasure-coded pool) → seal
  → MetaStore.txn([put "bucket/key" → chunk list, if-not-exists or version check])
LIST → MetaStore.scan(prefix)
```

The object becomes visible atomically in that one transaction.

S3 is HTTP, so the gateway talks to ChunkStore and MetaStore directly rather than through the file frontend. Storing each object as a file would make a `PUT` a temporary file, an `fsync` and a `rename`, all metadata round trips, where here it is one transaction. A `LIST` would walk directories instead of scanning one sorted prefix, and every byte would pass through FUSE as well.

**Request handling is ordinary Go**: `net/http` and `crypto/tls`, with a goroutine per connection, not the event loops' rules ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). Those rules are written for 4 KiB requests, where fixed per-request cost sets CPU and latency: a goroutine per connection costs 1.5 to 1.7× Rust's CPU per replicated 4K write ([tuned Go spike](#appendix-tuned-go-spike)), against 1.07× for a tuned loop ([codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)). An object request spreads that cost over a body of up to several MiB, and the S3 frontend's hot cost is throughput and listing ([Frontend comparison](#frontend-comparison)). Under the loops' rules the gateway would need its own HTTP/1.1 parser and TLS driven by hand inside a loop, giving up Go's mature HTTP and TLS stack, the main reason Go suits this frontend. A handler reads the body straight into slab buffers and passes each chunk write, and the `PUT`'s commit, to the owning loop's inbox: one inbox hop per chunk, not per 4 KiB. ChunkStore, MetaStore and the loops behind them keep the data-path rules. Small objects carry the most HTTP cost per byte, but each `PUT` also commits a MetaStore transaction, which is expected to cost more; neither cost has been measured.

## Frontend comparison

|  | File (RWX) | Block | Object (S3) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Write pattern | Byte-range writes, appends | 4K overwrites in place | Whole object, written once |
| Writers | Many, coordinated per file by cache mode | One (RWO), or many with app coordination (RWX block) | Many, atomic per key |
| Metadata | Heavy: directories, rename, stat, opens | Almost none | Sorted key index |
| MetaStore on hot path | Open, close, fsync, namespace operations; extending writes while write-shared | Never (attach only) | Once per PUT commit, and on LIST |
| Redundancy, in an erasure-coded pool | Encoded as written; copies while shared or edited, encoded once idle | Copies; chunks idle for a day encoded | Encoded as written |
| Snapshot cut | Pause every node with the volume mounted | Pause the attached node (or nodes, for RWX block) | None |
| Hot cost | Metadata round trips | Latency at queue depth 1 | Throughput, listing |
## Operating the cluster

An operator needs two things on a bad night: whether the data is safe, and what to do next. Longhorn's UI and `ceph status` both answer from the cluster's own account of itself, the manager's or the monitors', and that account is missing or stale exactly when the cluster is in trouble: Ceph's monitors without a quorum answer nothing, and Longhorn's UI shows what its manager last recorded. So rock-solid never asks the cluster how it is. Every node reports what it holds in its own memory and on its own disks, with no quorum and no leader, and a collector running off the cluster, on the operator's laptop if need be, reads every node directly and sets each report beside the control plane's record. The `rock-solid` command line and the `rock-solid ui` web UI are two views of the collector's output, in the same words.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph C["The cluster"]
        N1["node-1<br/>facts endpoint"]
        N2["node-2<br/>facts endpoint"]
        V["control-plane voters<br/>facts endpoint"]
    end
    subgraph L["Off the cluster: the operator's machine"]
        COL["collector (internal/ops)<br/>cluster map beside node reports<br/>health, disagreements, rules, auditor"]
        CLI["rock-solid status / explain / facts / audit"]
        UI["rock-solid ui (localhost)"]
    end
    K["kubectl: nodes, pods, PVCs, events"]
    N1 -- "GET /facts" --> COL
    N2 -- "GET /facts" --> COL
    V -- "GET /facts" --> COL
    K --> COL
    COL --> CLI
    COL --> UI
    D["dead node's disk"] -- "rock-solid inspect, daemon stopped" --> COL
```

*Each node answers for itself; the collector does every comparison, off the cluster.*

Where it lives: the facts types, their decoder, the comparison, the health rules, the auditor, redaction and action previews are one sans-IO package, `internal/ops`, a pure function of the facts read and the time given. Everything that does I/O is in `cmd/rock-solid`: the daemon's `net/http` server and the recorder's file writer, since `internal/node` is sans-IO ([Modularity](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers)), and the collector's HTTP calls, `kubectl` and files. Each loop's part of a node's facts is answered by its machine in `internal/node`.

**One word per thing.** The screens and the command line use these words and no others for the same state, because operators read "unreachable", "away" and "silent" as three different problems:

| Word | Means |
| --- | --- |
| Cluster map | The control plane's record as the operator sees it: pools, the placement table with every member's mark, the disk record, the session table and the shard map. "Control plane" is never shown, since to a Kubernetes operator it means the API server |
| Node report | What one node says about itself on its facts endpoint |
| Down | The cluster map marks the disk or node down ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). Nothing else makes a node down |
| Can't reach | This collector got no answer from a node. Shown apart from the cluster map's mark, with the Kubernetes node status and whether the node's loop port and admin port accept a connection, so a firewalled laptop never paints a healthy cluster degraded |
| Cut off | A mount whose session the cluster map has fenced ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)) |
| Halted | A metadata replica stopped at a committed entry it cannot decode ([MetaStore](#metadata-layer-metastore)), almost always a node on an older release than the entry needs. Never a group that lost its majority |
| Writes, updates | Changes to a PG's chunks are writes; changes to a metadata group are updates |
| Rebuild | A [refill](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) and the recovery that fills the new member |

PGs, sessions and mounts are named by the volume and pod they serve on every screen, the protocol's numbers behind `--raw` and `-o json`. Units are binary (GiB, TiB) and every time is absolute, in UTC on the command line, so output pasted into an issue still means something an hour later.

### Node facts

Every daemon, storage node, client-only node and control-plane voter alike, serves `GET /facts` on its admin port (`-admin`, one port number for the whole cluster, set by the chart), plain HTTP with a JSON body. It answers from the node's own state only: it asks no other node and no leader, so a node answers while the control plane has no leader and while the node is cut off from every peer. A node reports:

- **The node:** its release, roles, run IDs, uptime, its `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` and wall-clock readings at the answer (the wall clock only to place the report on a timeline; the protocol never reads it), its flags, and the hosts it knows from `-peers` and `-ctl`, which is how one reachable node leads the collector to the rest.
- **Disks and their segments:** each device by its by-id path, its disk and log IDs, size, fill, `write_limit` and log reserve, segments by state and live share, the cleaner's write amplification counters, the age of its oldest unfinished I/O, its I/O errors and checksum failures, and on NVMe the SMART critical warning, media errors, temperature, Percentage Used and Data Units Written ([Fill sets drive wear](#volume-size-and-capacity)).
- **PG members and their logs:** per member the PG, its interval in this node's table, its role (primary, member, leaving), as primary whether it is peering, active or has reported its interval recovered, its read lease, writes it holds, the newest decision its disk persisted, its missing-list entries and unfound chunks (counts; the chunks themselves on `/facts/pg/<pool>.<pg>`, paged), recovery under way, and the 99th percentile acknowledgment time it measures per member ([Fail-slow](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).
- **Groups and their replicas:** per metadata or control-plane replica its group, replica ID, term, the leader it supports, commit and applied index, its leader lease, and a halt's index and age; a leader adds each follower's match index, which gives "catching up, N updates behind".
- **Mounts and sessions:** the session ID and incarnation, the session lease's remaining time as the client counts it, the age of its last answered renewal, and per mount its volume, the pod from the CSI publish path, open handles, buffered bytes, pending errors, request counts and a latency histogram of the fail-slow kind (two buckets per octave, so counting allocates nothing).
- **Leases:** per loop pair the grants held and given, support epochs, the margin and measured rate bound ([Lease time](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), the shortest round trip, step-check trips and `Resumed` deliveries.
- **The cluster map it holds:** the table epoch and CRC, the session-table and shard-map versions, the format level and the voter it reports to. A control-plane voter also reports its replica's committed `ctl/` state and, while it leads, the leader's soft state: each disk's last heartbeat age, marks and flap counts, the node reports it holds, what each primary's heartbeat last said of its PGs (interval, active, unfound chunks and the members lacking copies, [`Stuck` and `Blocked`](#volume-size-and-capacity)), the pools' conditions and capacity figures ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)).

Times are reported as ages on the node's own clocks, never as instants of one node's clock compared with another's, as heartbeats do ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).

**Answered by each loop, off the data path.** The admin port is ordinary Go (`net/http`, a goroutine per request), as everything off the data path may be ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). It reads no loop's state itself: it posts each loop a call through the `mail` that already carries work between loops (`cmd/rock-solid/loops_linux.go`), and the loop runs it at its next event, copying its part into the reply in one pass, so no loop's state is read while it changes. The mail's mutex is held only to queue and take calls, never while a machine runs. The reply holds counts, not lists that grow with data, and a node builds at most one answer a second, so several collectors cost a node no more than one. A loop that has not copied its part within 5 s is stuck, and the node answers without it: the answer holds the other loops' parts and names the stuck loops in `unanswered_loops`, which a collector shows as unanswered, never as a node without those disks, members or replicas. A stuck loop gets no new call until it runs the one it holds.

**A versioned schema.** Every answer names its schema, `rock-solid.io/facts/v1`. Within v1 fields are only added; renaming, removing or changing the meaning of one takes v2, and a release that introduces v2 still serves v1 on request, so a collector of either release reads a cluster in the middle of a rolling upgrade. The schema is a JSON Schema file in `internal/ops`, frozen by a golden test like the [wire frames](#wire-messages), and each string field in it is marked as a name or not, which drives [redaction](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle). The collector's own documents (`status`, `explain`, `facts --diff`) carry schemas the same way, under the same promise, since scripts read them.

Rejected: a status API on the control-plane leader, which is the self-report this design avoids and answers nothing while the group has no leader; the voters' own framing, as the [operator's commands](#metadata-layer-metastore) speak, which `curl` cannot read; gRPC, a dependency and a code generator for a read that happens once a second; Prometheus text alone, which flattens member lists and replica states into labels. Prometheus reads the collector's verdicts instead ([Alerts](#alerts-and-capacity)).

### The collector

The collector reads every node it can reach, at once, and builds one view: the cluster map, each node report beside it, the Kubernetes objects that name volumes and pods, and the disagreements between them, each classified.

**Running off the cluster.** The collector is a library, not a service: `rock-solid` and `rock-solid ui` link it and run wherever the operator is, on a node or on a laptop that can reach the nodes' addresses. Nothing it needs runs in the cluster beyond the daemons, so it works with Kubernetes down, the control plane without a leader, or half the nodes off. An in-cluster dashboard was rejected because it fails with the cluster it describes.

**Finding the nodes.** The collector starts from seeds: `--nodes`, `ROCK_SOLID_NODES`, the node list it saved after its last full read (`~/.config/rock-solid/<cluster>.json`), or, while the Kubernetes API answers, the host addresses of the daemon's pods. One node that answers names the rest, since each report lists the hosts it knows and the admin port is the same on every node. The saved list is what lets it find every node again while the Kubernetes API is down.

**Kubernetes through `kubectl`.** Node readiness, pods, persistent volumes and claims, volume attachments and events come from `kubectl get -o json` with the operator's own context, and the out-of-service taint goes through `kubectl taint`. `kubectl` already handles every credential plugin an operator's kubeconfig can name; `client-go` would add dozens of modules pinned to Kubernetes releases for the same calls. Without `kubectl`, or with the API down, Kubernetes columns read "unknown" and nothing else changes. The same binary installed as `kubectl-rock_solid` is the `kubectl rock-solid` plugin form.

**No authentication in v1.** The admin port is as open as every other daemon port, because v1 trusts the cluster network and leaves authentication between daemons out ([Goals](#goals)): anyone who can reach the admin port can already reach the chunk ports and read any volume. Mutual TLS on the admin port alone would protect nothing while the data ports stay open, and its certificates could expire, or be impossible to issue, in the outage the operator is debugging. The operator's name in the [audit log](#actions) is therefore self-declared and shown as such, and both it and `rock-solid ui`'s `--actions` gate are [tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-no-admin-authentication) until daemons authenticate; how an off-cluster operator gets a credential that works while Kubernetes is down is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions) for when authentication arrives.

**Which cluster map.** The collector takes the cluster map from the leading voter's report, labelled with that voter and its applied index; without a leader, from the voter with the highest applied index, labelled as that voter's view; with no voter reachable, from the newest table any node holds, which carries every member's mark but no session table or leader soft state.

**Disagreements.** A node report that differs from the cluster map is not news by itself: a table reaches nodes at different times, and much of the protocol is waiting for things to settle. So each disagreement gets a class, an age, a deadline, and what it means for data and applications:

- **Settling:** the protocol resolves it by itself within a deadline the design gives. Hidden until it passes its deadline, after which it is *act*.
- **Act:** nothing in the cluster will resolve it, or it outlived its deadline.
- **Never:** a fact the protocol forbids, found by the [auditor](#the-auditor). It links to a [support bundle](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle).

| Disagreement | Class | Deadline, from the design's own rules |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A node holds an older table or session-table version than the cluster map | Settling | Two heartbeats after the commit: a node heard within `node_down_after` gets every delta, and one reporting an older epoch the whole table ([The control-plane host](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| A session the cluster map fenced whose daemon reports its own lease ended but has not learned of the fence | Settling | The daemon's next answered renewal; it issues no write meanwhile, since its lease has ended |
| A session the cluster map fenced whose daemon reports its own lease still running | Never | No session is fenced while its client's own lease runs ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)); the [auditor](#the-auditor) checks it |
| A member down in the cluster map, refill pending | Settling | `refill_after` from the down mark, `degraded_refill_after` in a PG below `min_live`, or the member's return |
| A member listing chunks, recovery under way | Settling | None fixed; *act* once its pool raises `Stuck` |
| A metadata replica catching up | Settling | None in the protocol; the collector's own rule makes it *act* once its lag has not shrunk for a minute |
| A disk down in the cluster map that its node reports open and healthy | Settling | `node_down_after`, or `flap_window` while flap damping holds it ([Flap damping](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); past it the node reaches this collector but not the leader |
| A leader's soft state missing after a failover (sizes, failed devices) | Settling | Two heartbeats, by when every node has reported again |
| A halted replica | Act | The control plane replaces it after `refill_after`, but the release mix that halted it remains |
| A volume attached in Kubernetes to a node whose session is fenced | Act | Kubernetes keeps the pod off other nodes until the node returns or is marked [out of service](#actions) |

### Health states

`rock-solid status` prints one state, a plain sentence on whether the data is safe, the margin left, the next deadline and the recommended action, then the items behind it, split into **Now** (rules firing on current facts) and **Coming up** (forecasts such as wear). Its exit code is the state, so a script can act on it. Each state is defined by facts, and the highest that applies wins:

| Exit | State | Defined by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Healthy | Every PG is known to have `replicas` clean copies of everything it holds (a move's extra member aside), every metadata group has all its voters up and none halted, and no rule fires |
| 1 | Degraded | Some PG has fewer clean copies than `replicas` (a member down, in `maintenance` or listing chunks) or is unknown, some group runs short of a voter, or a warning rule fires; of the PGs known, every chunk still has two clean copies, or its pool keeps one by choice |
| 2 | At risk | One more failure can lose acknowledged writes: some chunk of a pool of two or more copies has one clean copy, or a degraded PG or group keeps a copy or voter it needs on a [failing](#alerts-and-capacity) disk or node |
| 3 | Unavailable | Some data cannot be read or written: a PG below `min_live` or stopped, unfound chunks, a group without a majority of voters up and not halted, the control-plane group with a majority up but no leader under its lease for longer than a [failover](#metadata-layer-metastore) takes, so no session renews and every mount's writes pause, or a pool `Full` |
| 4 | Safety check failed | The [auditor](#the-auditor) found a fact the protocol forbids |
| 5 | Not enough read | No control-plane voter answered and fewer than half the nodes of the newest table this collector holds did: it cannot tell a current cluster map from a stale one |

A clean copy is one a member holds with nothing of the chunk on its missing list ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); from the reports' counts, a PG's worst chunk has at least its up members that list nothing. Those counts come from the PG's primary; for a primary this collector cannot read, from what its last heartbeat told the leader, whose report carries it (the members lacking copies). A PG neither gives is *unknown*: never counted clean, so the state cannot be Healthy, and named as unknown rather than short of copies, since this collector, not the cluster, lacks the fact. A command line `status` cannot parse exits 64, as `sysexits.h`'s `EX_USAGE`, since 2 is a state; the [operator's commands](#metadata-layer-metastore) keep 0, 1 and 2. `-o json` carries the same state in its `state` field, and `--watch` redraws every 2 s with a line per change of state. A violation the auditor found is printed even when state 5 wins the exit code.

Ceph's three levels were rejected: `HEALTH_WARN` covers both a rebuilding PG and a PG one failure from loss, which is the split an operator deciding whether to get up needs. "At risk" counts correlated risk, not only copies: three copies with two on a disk that is failing are one copy waiting to happen.

### Alerts and capacity

**One rule set.** The items behind a state are rules, one table in `internal/ops`: each has an ID, the state it raises, the facts it reads, the design section that says why it matters and its recommended action. They are written once, in Go. For paging, `rock-solid exporter` runs the collector as a process, a Deployment the chart installs, and publishes its verdicts on `/metrics`: `rock_solid_state`, the health state, and `rock_solid_rule{id=...}`, 1 for each rule firing. The chart's `PrometheusRule` alerts on those series, and on their absence, since an exporter that cannot run is itself news, so a page, the UI's headline and `rock-solid status` say the same thing. The exporter runs in the cluster, unlike the command line, but it reads every node directly like any collector, so it needs no leader, and the alert on its silence covers the outage that takes it down. Writing each rule a second time in PromQL over per-node metrics was rejected: two forms of every rule drift unless a test holds them equal, and a rule over the whole cluster becomes joins across per-node series, where some rules, such as a PG's clean copies, cannot be written at all.

| Rule | Raises | Why, in the design |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A PG below `min_live` or stopped | Unavailable | It refuses reads and writes ([Writes and `min_live`](#pools-and-redundancy)) |
| Unfound chunks, `Blocked` | Unavailable | Only a candidate's return or a [give-up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) frees them |
| A group without a majority | Unavailable | No update or lease read commits ([MetaStore](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| `Full` | Unavailable | Writes that add data are refused ([Conditions](#volume-size-and-capacity)) |
| A chunk with one clean copy | At risk | A write committed there is lost with that copy ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) |
| A failing disk or node holds a copy or voter a degraded PG or group needs | At risk | Its loss drops the PG to one copy or the group below a majority |
| A member down, refill pending | Degraded | [Down is not gone](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection): the deadline is its refill |
| A node in `maintenance` | Degraded | Its PGs write to fewer copies by the operator's choice ([Writes and `min_live`](#pools-and-redundancy)) |
| A halted replica | Degraded | It serves nothing until replaced; mixed releases halt more |
| A failing disk | Degraded | See below |
| `NearFull`, `Stuck`, `CannotReprotect`, `Unbalanced`, `Stranded` | Degraded | The pool conditions, as [Conditions](#volume-size-and-capacity) defines them |
| A mount cut off | Degraded | Every call on a handle opened before the fence fails with `EIO` for good, so the application must reopen its files and retry what failed ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)) |
| Nodes on different releases outside `rock-solid upgrade` | Degraded | A replica halts at an entry its release cannot decode |
| A lossy link to a peer | Degraded | A PG waits on its slowest member, and a cut link can leave writes waiting ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)) |
| A disk's wear reaching its rating within a year at its writes per day | Coming up | [Fill sets drive wear](#volume-size-and-capacity) |

A disk is *failing* while any of these holds: its NVMe critical warning is set, its media errors grew in the last day, its store counted an I/O error or a checksum failure in the last hour, or the detector marked it stalled or slow within `flap_window`. These are facts, not scores: a healthy drive has none of them, so the first one is worth a look, and a threshold would be a judgment no measurement backs.

**No automatic drain.** rock-solid already takes a disk out by itself when it stalls or slows ([Hung disks](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); a disk that is failing but still answers gets an alert and a recommended drain, never an automatic one. A drain copies the disk's whole share while it serves, so a false alarm costs every other disk wear and hours of copying, and on three nodes under `rep3` a disk usually has nowhere to drain to. Whether to drain on SMART counts is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

**Capacity in usable terms.** The UI shows the pool's own figures ([What a pool reports](#volume-size-and-capacity)) under operators' names, never new ones: *raw* is the pool's disk sizes, *usable* is balanced, already divided by the copies' overhead, *stored* is held, *free* is available, *fits after losing the largest node* is held ≤ reprotectable (`CannotReprotect` otherwise), and the *write limit* marks where writes that add data are refused, with `NearFull` 10 points below it.

**Rebuilds.** For each PG a rebuild touches, the UI names the source recovery will read, the primary itself or the first up member that lists nothing ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), and the destination placement chose. An estimate is the bytes left divided by the recovery rate the destination disks reported over the last minute; before a rebuild starts it uses the rate of the last rebuild onto those disks, and otherwise shows none, since a guessed rate on a homelab drive misleads more than a blank. The bytes per PG need a count the chunk index does not keep yet ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)); until then a PG's share is its pool's data on the disk divided by the disk's members of the pool.

### The auditor

`rock-solid audit` checks the facts gathered against the safety properties the protocol models prove ([Protocol models](#testing-strategy)), so a bug that slipped past the models and the simulator shows on a real cluster as a named violation rather than as corrupt data later. Each check is Go in `internal/ops`, named after the spec property it stands for. A test runs every check over seeded simulator runs, reading each node at a different tick with the leader read before and after them, as the collector reads a real cluster: a check must never fire on a run the simulator's invariants pass, and must fire on the runs of the [planted bugs](#testing-strategy) whose property it checks. Evaluating the TLA+ invariants themselves with TLC was rejected: it needs Java on the operator's machine, and facts are not the spec's state, so it would need a second trace mapping.

A false "Safety check failed" teaches an operator to ignore the one alarm that means a bug, so a check fires only on what holds whatever the skew between reads:

- **Against what the leader committed.** A check compares a node with the leading voter's report, never with a follower's, which may lag. Without a leader read, the check reads *unknown*.
- **Per run.** Facts are keyed by node and run ID. A node whose run ID changed between two reads, or that holds no table yet (a store's fence table starts empty, and a restarted node fetches its table), reads *unknown* for every check that compares it across reads or with the leader.
- **Confirmed.** A check that fires reads the nodes it named again, the leader before and after them, and fails only if it fires again; otherwise it reads *unknown*. Only a confirmed violation exits 4.

The checks:

- **From one snapshot:** at most one Raft leader per group per term; no disk ID with two log IDs and no log ID up under two disk IDs; no node holding a table epoch or session-table version newer than the leader, read after the node, reports committed; no two primaries of one PG both reporting a read lease whose remaining time, shortened by the lease's own margin, overlaps on the collector's clock across the read's round trip; no storage node, read after the leader, holding an older session-table version than the leader records as acknowledged while the node is not marked down, since a version is acknowledged only once every node holds it; no session the leader, read before its daemon, reports fenced while the daemon reports its own lease running past the margin and the read's round trip, since no session is fenced while its client's own lease runs ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)).
