- **Across snapshots** (`--watch`, and the facts the [flight recorder](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle) keeps), within one run of each node: no table epoch, interval number or last activated interval goes backwards, no group's commit index or a replica's term goes down, and a halted replica's applied index stays put.
- **Not from facts:** anything about acknowledged writes and the bytes they wrote, such as no acknowledged write lost or no two members holding different data under one version. Those need the history the simulator keeps, so a field suspicion goes to [replay](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle) and to [inspect](#offline-inspect).

### Offline inspect

`rock-solid inspect /dev/disk/by-id/<id>` reads one disk with its daemon stopped: a dead node's disk moved to a working machine, or a node whose daemon will not start. It opens the device with `O_EXCL`, which the daemon also uses, so it refuses while a daemon holds the disk, and runs the disk's own recovery (superblock, replay, chunk index, persisted decisions and missing lists, Raft hard state and applied index) on a driver whose writes go to memory, so it never changes what the daemon will find. It prints the disk's part of the facts schema plus what only the disk can say: whether its last stop was clean, and per PG the newest decision it persisted and what its missing list holds. `rock-solid facts --add <inspect.json>` sets that beside the cluster map as the node's report, marked as read from its disk at a given time, which is what an operator needs before choosing between bringing the disk back on another node ([A disk is its log](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), declaring it lost, or giving a PG up.

A repair mode was rejected: every repair the design allows goes through the control plane, where its refusals are, and a tool that writes a disk behind it could make a member answer peering from a log that is no longer what it acknowledged.

### Flight recorder and support bundle

**What a node keeps.** Every loop keeps its last `trace_records` records in a ring ([Traces, not logs](#testing-the-design-decisions)), which covers seconds under load. The ring is always on: a record is a 64-byte copy into memory the ring was given at start, allocating nothing, and a recorder that must be switched on before an incident records none of it. Its cost is held by a benchmark, and the rings' memory, `trace_records` × 64 bytes per loop (4 MiB at the default), counts in the node's `-memory` budget ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). Beside it each loop copies the records whose rate follows membership and leadership rather than I/O (tables adopted, intervals asked and started, peering decisions, activations and recovered reports, declarations, Raft state and leader changes, resumes) into a small second ring of its own. Once a second the recorder's writer in `cmd/rock-solid` posts each loop a call, as the facts endpoint does, and the loop copies its second ring's new records into a buffer the writer then writes, so only the loop that owns a ring ever reads it. The writer adds changes to the node's facts and, each minute, a line pairing its `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` and wall-clock readings, which places the lines on one timeline. The files are rotated and the oldest deleted past `recorder_keep` (7 days) or `recorder_bytes` (2 GiB), whichever comes first, under the daemon's state directory. A ring overrun writes a gap line, never a silent hole. History therefore lives on the nodes, there whether or not anyone had the UI open; the two defaults are judgments to check ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). Per-write records stay in the memory ring: kept for days they would cost the disk writes the data path is built to avoid.

The lines are `internal/trace`'s text format, `tick loop kind fields...`, with the daemon's loop number, so the checker library that reads a simulator trace reads them unchanged.

**The support bundle.** `rock-solid support-bundle --since <time>` writes one `tar.gz` (the standard library's, so no compression dependency): for the window, each node's recorder files and a dump of every loop's memory ring, each node's facts, the cluster map versions, Kubernetes events and the fields of `kubectl`'s objects on an allowlist, the [audit log](#actions), and the collector's own record of which nodes it could reach. It holds no file contents, but does hold names: file paths, volume, pod, claim and namespace names, hostnames, IP addresses and device serials. So by default it replaces every field the schema marks as a name with an HMAC-SHA-256 under a key drawn for the bundle and saved beside it, not in it: a name becomes the same token everywhere in the bundle, so events still line up, nobody can recover a hostname by hashing guesses as with a plain hash, and the operator can still say which pod a token was when asked. `--no-redact` keeps names for a private report. Removing names outright was rejected because events could no longer be matched across nodes.

A Kubernetes object holds more than names: a pod's spec carries environment values and arguments, which can hold secrets, and annotations carry whatever tools put there. So the bundle keeps only the fields on an allowlist in `internal/ops`, each marked as a name or not like the facts schema's: an object's kind, name, namespace, node, phase and conditions, a pod's volumes and their claims, a claim's volume and size, a node's taints and readiness, an attachment's node and volume, an event's object, reason, time and message, the message marked as a name since it is free text that may hold any. Nothing else of an object enters the bundle, so a field Kubernetes adds later stays out until someone adds it to the list.

**For developers.** `rock-solid debug check <bundle>` runs the trace rules of `internal/trace` over each ring dump and the auditor's checks across the bundle's facts. `rock-solid debug replay <bundle>` builds a simulator scenario from it: the pools, nodes and disks of its first cluster map and the fault schedule its records show (marks, restarts, resumes, partitions read from missed heartbeats, operator commands) at their times, then runs seeds of that scenario under the simulator's invariants and the [spec conformance check](#testing-strategy). A seed that fails becomes a regression test like any other. It reproduces the incident's faults, not its interleaving: messages' contents are not recorded, and a field trace starts mid-history where the conformance check needs the spec's `Init` ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).

### Actions

The command line and the UI act at node scope, because that is how an operator thinks about a failure: "node-2 is coming back", "node-2 is gone". Each action has a confirm step showing a preview, a refusal when it is unsafe with the reason, the command it runs (shown so it can be copied), a way back where one exists, and an audit entry. `--dry-run` prints the preview alone and `--yes` skips the confirm for scripts. The commands are [operator's commands](#metadata-layer-metastore) like the disk and PG ones, sent to the control-plane leader and answered once they commit.

| Action | Command | Refused when | Way back |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Maintenance until a time | `rock-solid node maintenance <node> --for <duration>` | The node is not up, a PG of it would be left with no up member, or a metadata group with a voter on the node, the control-plane group included, would be left without a majority of up voters | `--end`; at its deadline the mark ends by itself |
| Rebuild now | `rock-solid node refill <node>` | The node is not down, or no eligible disk can take its members | None: the new members stay, and the old ones leave once the new ones recover |
| Wait longer | `rock-solid node hold <node> --for <duration>` | The node is not down | `--end` |
| Drain a disk | `rock-solid disk drain <disk>` | Placement finds no destination for some member ([Pins and drains](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | `rock-solid disk undrain <disk>` |
| Mark out of service | `rock-solid node out-of-service <node>` | Some disk of the node is not down, or some session of it is not fenced with its fence acknowledged; on the command line, Kubernetes does not report the node NotReady | `--end`, once Kubernetes reports the node Ready |

- **Maintenance** sets the `maintenance` mark, with its deadline, on every disk of the node; what the mark does and how it ends are in [Writes and `min_live`](#pools-and-redundancy). The preview lists the PGs that will write to fewer copies, those left on a single copy first, with their volumes, and the metadata groups with a voter on the node, with how many voters each keeps up. A group refusal has no override: on three nodes with one down, rebooting a second takes every group with voters on both below a majority, the control-plane group among them, and then no session renews and every mount's writes pause whatever the PGs do, so the mark would buy nothing. The voters counted are the ones each group's leader reports, not the shard map's plan, since a replacement under way gives a group voters the map does not yet list; a control-plane voter's node is its host.
- **Rebuild now** sets the leader's own count of how long each of the node's down disks has been down, the count placement reads, to `refill_after`, so its next table refills their members at once rather than after the wait ([Down is not gone](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). That table commits the age in the disk record, and the command is answered once it commits, so after a failover before then the command line resends it to the new leader. It is no declaration of loss: each old member stays as a leaving member and a recovery source until its replacement recovers, so the PGs keep every copy they have throughout, and a node that returns still serves as a source. [Declaring a disk lost](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), which is final, stays a disk-screen action behind a confirmation that names the disk. The preview runs the leader's own placement function, `chunkstore.Next`, over the cluster map the collector read: the destinations, the bytes, the sources and the estimate.
- **Wait longer** holds the refills of the node's down members past `refill_after` until a time, for a node known to be coming back. The time left is kept in the disk record, rounded down to a minute and rewritten each minute like a `maintenance` deadline, so a failover moves it by at most a minute plus the failover. It holds no `degraded_refill_after` refill, since a PG below `min_live` serves nothing while it waits, and the preview names those PGs.
- **Out of service** lets Kubernetes delete the node's pods and detach their volumes so they start elsewhere, through its `node.kubernetes.io/out-of-service` taint. Kubernetes leaves it to the operator to be sure the node no longer writes, so the command makes sure in three steps. It checks through `kubectl` that Kubernetes reports the node NotReady. It then commits the node's out-of-service fact at the leader, which refuses it unless every disk of the node is down and every session of the node is fenced with the fence acknowledged; from then on the leader refuses every session start from the node, and the fact's `Txn` checks the session table's head, so a session that starts between the leader's check and the commit fails the commit ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). Only once that commits does it apply the taint. The fence stops the node's sessions and the refused starts stop new ones, so pods the node still runs cannot write beside their replacements, even when its storage network heals before its kubelet hears that the pods were deleted. A taint that fails after the commit is reported, and running the command again applies it. `--end` is refused until Kubernetes reports the node Ready, by when its kubelet has stopped the deleted pods; it ends the fact, then removes the taint.

The leader checks each refusal of its own again when the command arrives, as it checks a drain or a give-up, so a preview computed from facts a second old can never let an unsafe change through. Kubernetes' readiness, which the leader cannot see, is checked by the command line alone. Moving one or several PGs stays with the existing `rock-solid pg` commands. A command needs the control-plane leader, so while no voter answers as leader the UI shows every action disabled and says why; reading reports, the timeline and bundles still works.

**Audit log.** Each change an operator command commits, an out-of-service fact included, writes an entry under `ctl/audit/` in the same `Txn`, with the time the command line read, the command, its arguments and the operator's self-declared name (the `kubectl` context's user, else the local user and host), so an entry exists exactly when the change committed; it fits the key-value store's `MaxValue` of 1,000 bytes, so long arguments are truncated. The leader keeps the newest 1,000 entries, deleting the oldest in the `Txn` that adds one. The taint, which commits nothing at the leader, is in Kubernetes' own record of the node; it and every refused command are logged on the operator's machine.

### The web UI and Learn

`rock-solid ui` is the collector plus a web server on `127.0.0.1`, polling the nodes every 2 s. Each screen renders one collector document, the same JSON the matching command prints with `-o json`, so the UI holds no rule of its own and cannot say something the command line does not. The screens are the overview, a PG, the metadata groups, a disk, the timeline, the volumes and the nodes and disks. The pages are plain HTML, CSS and JavaScript embedded in the binary, with no build step, since a JavaScript toolchain beside the Go module would be a second dependency tree to pin and audit.

**Only the operator's browser.** Binding to `127.0.0.1` keeps other machines out, not other web pages: any page the operator's browser opens can send a request to `127.0.0.1`, and a page whose name an attacker points at `127.0.0.1` (DNS rebinding) can read the answers. So `rock-solid ui` draws a random token at start and prints the URL with it, as Jupyter does; the page holds it from then on. Every request must carry the token, name the UI's own address in its `Host` header and, when it has an `Origin`, the UI's own origin; anything that changes state is a `POST`, never a `GET`. Binding elsewhere takes `--listen`, and then actions stay off unless `--actions` is given, since in v1 anyone on that network who obtained the URL could press them ([tech debt](tech-debts.md#workaround-no-admin-authentication)).

**Learn.** The explainers are the [posts](../blogs/README.md), linked at a section anchor from the screen that needs them: blocks and segments from the disk screen, metadata groups against placement groups from the groups screen, a placement group from the PG screen. A link opens the post in the project's repository at the release's tag, so it matches the binary's release; embedding them was rejected, since `go:embed` reaches only files under the embedding package's directory and the module keeps Go packages to `cmd/`, `internal/` and `tools/`. A test resolves every link the UI holds against the posts' headings, so editing a post cannot break one, and the screens show a one-line summary with the link rather than a copy of the text, which would drift from the post.

## Modularity: sans-IO core, pluggable drivers

All protocol and replication logic, including the coherence protocol, is written as pure state machines that take events and return actions. They never touch real I/O, so the simulator can stand in for every driver, one seed replays a whole cluster run, and every input/output (I/O) backend is swappable. The shared types live in `internal/core`; the chunk replica's entry point is:

```go
// OnEvent fills out, which the caller owns, resets and reuses, so a call allocates nothing.
func (r *Replica) OnEvent(ctx *core.Context, ev *core.Event, out *ReplicaOutput)
```

Each machine's output type holds its actions plus what it asks of its host, such as the replica's request for a newer placement table.

`Context` carries the time on both clocks (`Now` on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, `Raw` on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, 1 tick = 1 µs) and the driver's buffer pool. An `Event` is a received message, a lost peer, a disk completion, a timer or `Resumed`; an `Action` is a send, a disk read, write, flush or trim, or a timer to set. Both are tagged structs, not interfaces, so passing one never allocates.

**Rules for state machines:**

- **No allocation per call.** `OnEvent` fills an output the caller owns and reuses, and hot-path structs are filled in place rather than built as temporaries and copied: a frame literal copied into a slice cost 534 ns, filled in place 177 ns ([codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)).
- **Time comes in, waiting goes out.** A machine reads time only from `ctx.Now` and `ctx.Raw` and never blocks: it records what it waits for under an `IOID` or `TimerID`, and the matching event resumes it. `Resumed` says the node may have stood still; a machine that holds no lease ignores it, emitting nothing and keeping its deadlines and rounds in flight.
- **Deterministic.** No goroutine, channel, `select`, `sync` or `sync/atomic`; no clock, `os`, `net`, `syscall` or `unsafe`; no `range` over a map, which Go randomizes on purpose and which would shape the order of emitted actions. Randomness comes only from a seeded `math/rand/v2` source the machine owns. In the simulator each machine's source is forked from its node's, and each node's from the run seed, in creation order (`Rand.Fork`), so a draw added in one machine shifts no other machine's stream.
- **Errors are data.** A corrupt message or a failed disk is an event to handle. A panic is only for a broken invariant of the machine's own state, never for peer or disk input.
- **Protocol logic lives in the core**, even when only one frontend uses it today: who may cache, when to recall, what is fenced. A frontend only translates between FUSE, ublk or S3 requests and events and actions.
- **Tested by feeding events.** Unit tests feed events and assert on the returned actions, with no mocks. A new machine registers its invariants with the simulator's checker in the same change, and a new protocol is modeled in TLA+ before it is written ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)).

A `go vet` analyzer, `tools/sansio`, enforces the deterministic rules in every sans-IO package: everything under `internal/` except `loop`, `driver/…`, `frontend/…` and `csi` ([package layout](#package-layout)). Test files may read their `testdata`. The loop and driver packages get a narrower rule set of their own ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)).

A driver executes `Action`s and feeds back `Event`s. Chunk data, Raft logs and indexes all live in the [disk log](#on-disk-format), so the disk driver is the only code that touches storage:

| Slot | Default driver | Opt-in fast driver | Test driver |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Disk | `io_uring` on a file or raw device | SPDK on a dedicated NVMe drive | Simulated disk with fault model |
| Network | Kernel TCP | RDMA (real ConnectX or Soft-RoCE); DPDK as an experiment | Simulated network |
| Clock and timers | `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, which counts through suspend, and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, which no time daemon slews, read together each pass for [lease time](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), with the VMClock disruption marker where the VM has one; timers on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` | Same | Simulated time: per-node oscillator drift, time-daemon slews, suspends and VM pauses, which the event loop's own tests take through its [kernel seam](#runtime-in-go) |
| Frontend | FUSE client; ublk + CSI driver | NVMe-oF/TCP for remote block clients | Simulated clients with a model page cache that record histories |

**Driver contract.** The [bypass-drivers spike](#appendix-kernel-bypass-drivers-spike) showed that SPDK and RDMA fit this slot only if the first three rules hold from the first (default) driver; the [open-loop latency spike](#appendix-open-loop-latency-spike) added the fourth. Every driver passes the shared suites that check them: `internal/chunkstore/storetest` for disks and `internal/loop/nettest` for networks, each run on the simulated driver and the real one.

- **Data buffers come from the driver's pool, and the core frees them.** `IOBuf` is a handle into a pool the driver provides, never a heap allocation: SPDK can only DMA from SPDK-registered memory and RDMA only send from registered memory, one stray buffer breaks a whole RDMA connection, and registering per I/O costs as much as the I/O. The pool has an explicit size: without hugepages, vfio limits SPDK to just under 256 MB of DMA memory, so the SPDK pool defaults to 128 MB. An exhausted pool is an outcome the machine handles, typically by retrying on a timer, never a panic or a fallback to the heap. Control messages up to 256 bytes go inline and need no buffer.

  The core allocates with `Alloc` for buffers it overwrites or `AllocZeroed` for clean memory, and frees every buffer, including the data a `NetRecv` delivers. A buffer named in an action stays with the driver until the action is over:

  | Action | The driver is done with the buffer when |
  | --- | --- |
  | `DiskRead`, `DiskWrite` | its `DiskDone` arrives |
  | `NetSend` of a request | the peer's reply arrives, or `PeerLost` for that peer |
  | `NetSend` marked `Reply`: an answer, or a one-way message | it is sent, or `PeerLost` for that peer; the driver frees the buffer itself |

  A send reports no completion of its own, so a peer answers every request that carries data, even if only to reject it, and the core marks a send that gets no answer `Reply`; the driver never parses a frame to tell. The driver releases a send's buffer before it delivers the reply, and all of a peer's buffers before its `PeerLost`. A timeout gives up on the result, not the buffer. A buffer sent to several peers is freed once all are done with it. `IOBuf` is a plain value (slot and generation), so the type system tracks none of this; the pools check the generation, and the simulator's poisons `Alloc`ed memory.
- **Every driver exposes a readiness fd and defaults to event mode.** SPDK's thread interrupt fd and the RDMA completion-channel fd sit in the loop's epoll set; busy polling is a separate opt-in for dedicated cores. SPDK supports event mode on PCIe drives bound to `vfio-pci` ([SPDK NVMe interrupt spike](#appendix-spdk-nvme-interrupt-spike)), but elsewhere silently attaches in polling mode, and a loop sleeping on its fd then hangs. So the driver reads the mode back after attach and fails the disk with a clear error unless the operator gave it a busy-polled core.
- **Network connections fail as a unit and deliver in order.** A transport error becomes one `PeerLost`, every outstanding request on that connection fails, and the driver reconnects; between two `PeerLost`s a peer's messages arrive once each, in the order sent, so a replica primary may rely on that order. The core keeps its own deadlines and never relies on transport timeouts. `SetTimer` on an armed `TimerID` replaces its deadline and there is no cancel, so a `Timer` event can arrive for a deadline that no longer matters, and the core checks its own deadline against `ctx.Now` before acting.
- **Every pass of an event loop does bounded work.** A pass reads at most a fixed budget from each connection, the bytes queued for sending are capped and stop the loop reading clients while over the cap, and output buffers are rings or compacted in place rather than grown. Without the budget, both the Rust and the Go replication harnesses ran away under overload into multi-second stalls with up to 200 MB buffered and most cores idle, Go from 30,000 writes/s since growing a slice copies it; with it, neither dropped a request up to 100,000 writes/s.

**Transport negotiation.** Every pair of [event loops](#runtime-in-go) that talk connects over TCP first, then upgrades to RDMA if both ends report a usable device; RDMA needs that TCP channel anyway, to exchange queue pair numbers and addresses. The daemon loads `libibverbs` at runtime, so one binary runs on nodes with and without rdma-core, and a missing library, device or memory-lock allowance means TCP, not an error. The daemon pod always carries `IPC_LOCK`, which the io_uring driver's registered buffers already need, so RDMA's pool can be registered too.

### Runtime in Go

The daemon runs the state machines inside event loops tuned to what the Go spikes measured, on one 4-vCPU cloud VM with Go 1.27.0 against the same designs in Rust 1.99.1:

| Question | Measurement |
| --- | --- |
| CPU per replicated 4K write, same epoll design ([tuned Go spike](#appendix-tuned-go-spike)) | Go 1.21× Rust; 1.5 to 1.7× with a goroutine per connection, whatever its synchronization |
| Where that comes from ([codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)) | Not the compiler. About 61% is runtime scheduling (`sysmon` retaking the P of a loop blocked in `epoll_wait`), 16% syscall wrappers |
| After tuning the runtime (codegen spike) | **Go 1.07× Rust** with `GOMAXPROCS` above the blocking loops, raw syscalls for non-blocking I/O, and memory touched before use |
| Compute-bound code ([Go CPU spike](#appendix-go-cpu-per-operation-spike), tuned Go spike) | Go 1.1 to 1.25× Rust; PGO, `GOAMD64=v3` and bounds checks each under 3.5% |
| Rising open-loop load ([open-loop latency spike](#appendix-open-loop-latency-spike)) | Same throughput up to 100,000 writes/s; Go's p99 knee 1.2× earlier (before the runtime tuning above) |
| `GOMAXPROCS` under a CPU limit ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)) | Ps above the limit throttled in up to 6.5% of 100 ms periods; as many Ps as loops starves the collector and workers; loops = Go's default − 1 never throttled |
| Garbage collector ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike), tuned Go spike) | Pauses under 1 ms. A memtable with a pointer per entry costs 23 to 35% of CPU in collection; in a Go arena 9%; as pointer-free 4 KiB pages 0% |

The rules below keep the data path near 1.07×; without them it costs 1.5 to 1.7× the CPU, a quarter of it in collection. They apply to the data path: the sans-IO packages, the event loops, the drivers and the file and block frontends' request handling. Code off the data path (the S3 gateway's HTTP handling, which [S3 frontend](#s3-frontend-later) explains, the CSI gRPC server, metrics, the admin API, the FUSE descriptor holder) is ordinary Go with goroutines, `net/http` and `context`. Each serial owner (a hot PG primary, a disk's commit loop, a busy shard) still reaches its throughput ceiling and latency knee somewhat earlier than in Rust, so headroom is planned per owner, from latency against offered load.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph LOOP["Event loop goroutine (one per core)"]
        direction TB
        EP["epoll_wait"] --> SM["OnEvent of the owning state machine"]
        SM --> EX["execute actions: writev, io_uring submit, timer heap"]
        EX --> EP
    end
    SOCK["peer and client sockets"] --> EP
    URING["io_uring completion eventfd"] --> EP
    TFD["timerfd: earliest deadline"] --> EP
    INBOX["inbox: lock-free queue + eventfd"] --> EP
    WORK["worker goroutines: cleaner copies, erasure coding"] --> INBOX
    OTHER["other loops"] --> INBOX
```

*A loop sleeps only in `epoll_wait`; everything that can wake it is a descriptor in its set.*

**Event loops** (`internal/loop`)

- **One loop per core**, a goroutine running `epoll` through `golang.org/x/sys/unix` on raw non-blocking descriptors, owning its shards, its disks' logs and indexes and its connections outright. Never `net.Conn` or a goroutine per connection on the data path: 1.21× Rust's CPU against 1.5 to 1.7×. A disk's log belongs to one loop; a loop with shards on another loop's disk hands that loop its records through the inbox, one batch per pass, since every hop costs a wakeup, and gets one durable notice per round back ([round driver](#on-disk-format)).
- **Connections are per loop pair, and a request goes to the loop that owns its target.** Each node publishes which loop owns each of its disks and shard replicas, and a loop connects to each peer loop it needs, as Seastar's shard-aware clients do, so a chunk write touches no core but its two ends'; one connection per node pair would cost two cross-core wakeups per operation. A request that reaches the wrong loop after a shard moved is to be forwarded through the inbox, its reply naming the new owner, so the owner map is only a hint; no dispatch forwards yet ([TCP network driver](build-checklist.md#phase-5-default-drivers)).
- **One spare P, and no more Ps than the CPU limit.** With as many busy loops as Ps, `sysmon` retakes a blocked loop's P (2.9 µs per write) and the collector and workers wait for one; Ps beyond a CPU limit run more threads than the CFS quota pays for, and the kernel stalls them all until the next period ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)). So the daemon sizes from Go's own default `GOMAXPROCS`, which follows the cgroup limit (`loop.Size`): under a limit it runs one loop fewer than that default and leaves `GOMAXPROCS` to the runtime; without one, a loop per CPU and one P more. A limit equal to the CPU count reads as none, an accepted P too many. The chart asks for an integer limit of at least 3 CPUs or none. A limit lowered while the daemon runs leaves no spare P, which it reports until it restarts.
- **Raw syscalls where nothing blocks.** Reads, writes and sends on non-blocking descriptors use `unix.RawSyscall`, and `epoll_wait` and anything else that can block `unix.Syscall`: 1.4 µs less per write.
- **Never wait inside `io_uring_enter`.** The ring signals completions on its eventfd. Waiting in `io_uring_enter` instead would need a bound for the loop's timers and a way for its inbox and sockets to end the wait, and on the durable write benchmark (`TestDurableWriteBenchmark`) it gave no consistent latency gain, saving CPU only after idle gaps under the `powersave` governor. At queue depth 1 the governor matters more than the wait, so a node whose low-load latency matters runs `performance`; the daemon leaves the governor as it finds it.
- **Loops are not locked to OS threads** (35 against 29 µs per write), except where a C library requires its own thread, as SPDK's `spdk_thread` does.
- **A loop waits only on its epoll set.** A goroutine blocked in `epoll_wait` cannot also wait on a channel, so everything that can wake a loop is a descriptor: its sockets, its io_uring eventfd, one timerfd armed for the earliest deadline in the loop's timer heap, and one eventfd for its inbox. Others hand it work through a lock-free multi-producer queue and write the eventfd only when no wake is pending, so a burst costs one wakeup. No channel, `select`, `time.Timer` or `time.AfterFunc` on the data path.
- **Bounded passes** and **one `writev` per descriptor per pass**, gathering every frame ready for it: syscalls set the cost of a write, and batching halved Rust's and cut Go's by 29%. Watching is level-triggered, so a connection left unread past its budget is reported again next pass. At the queue cap (`Config.QueueCap`, 16 MiB, provisional) the loop drops its clients' read interest and reads only peers until the queue falls below it, so it sleeps rather than spins on clients it will not read.
- **Traces, not logs.** A loop records every event, action and syscall as a fixed-field trace record and never writes a free-form log line ([Testing the design decisions](#testing-the-design-decisions)). An inbox post's eventfd write is the poster's, on any goroutine, so the inbox counts it (`Inbox.Wakes`) instead.

**The kernel seam.** Everything a loop asks of the kernel that involves time or waiting goes through one interface, `loop.Kernel` (register descriptors, wait, wake, read every clock together, arm the timerfd), so its tests run many loops stepped on scripted clocks (`internal/loop/looptest`) from the simulator's scheduler ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)); the real one is `loop.Epoll`. A connection's ID carries a count of its slot's earlier holders, so an ID kept past its connection names nothing.

A pass (`Loop.Pass`) waits, then reads the clocks, delivers `Resumed`, fires due timers, delivers disk completions, handles peer messages before client reads, executes actions, makes one `io_uring_enter` and arms the timerfd. So every event is handled at a time no earlier than when it became ready, as in FoundationDB's loop (`flow/Net2.cpp`). Reading the clocks before the wait would be unsafe for [leases](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection): a pass could serve on the old lease the reads that woke it after a suspend, handle an expiry timer and a read ready together at a `Now` from before the expiry, and record a grant's arrival early, making the grantor-rate bound too low. Peers come before clients so that a holder that lost time ends its leases before it serves a read that waited behind the grantor's messages. The seam's other rules:

- **The loop decides when to block**, only when `Loop.Pending` reports no work left (I/O merely in flight does not count: its completion wakes the loop). A pass fires only the timers due when it began, so it stays bounded.
- **Timers fire from the heap, not from the timerfd's readiness**, and `Arm` is called only when the earliest deadline changed. The timer heap is a pure function in `internal/core` and fires equal deadlines in `TimerID` order.
- **One `Resumed` detector for the loop and the simulator.** `core.Resumed` reports a suspend when `CLOCK_BOOTTIME − CLOCK_MONOTONIC` grew by at least `core.ResumeThreshold` (1 s), a changed VMClock marker, or a `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` or `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` reading below the last. Both take their first reading when the loop or machine is made, so a stop before the first pass is seen.
- **`Now` and `Raw` never go back.** A reading below the last leaves them at the last value and delivers `Resumed`, so a holder ends its leases at once rather than late by the step. Whether Linux ever returns such a reading is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
- **Trace records take their tick from the pass's clock reading**, never a clock read of their own.
- **The clock read is bracketed** between two `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` reads and retried while they lie more than ε = 1 ms apart, so a stall between the reads, from descheduling or CFS throttling, does not look like a suspend and end every lease. The vDSO read is the runtime's `nanotime`, linked by name, since `x/sys/unix` makes every `clock_gettime` a syscall ([tech debt](tech-debts.md)). ε and the threshold are provisional ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). The read costs 365 ns a pass ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)).
- **The VMClock device is used wherever it exists**, since only its marker shows snapshot restores and live migrations ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)). `NewEpoll` maps `/dev/vmclock0` read-only; with no device the marker reads 0 and `Epoll.VMClock` says so, and a device that cannot be mapped, or is not a VMClock, fails `NewEpoll` rather than leave the node blind. The device is root's, mode 0600, so the daemon runs as root or a udev rule grants it read access. A sequence count the host leaves odd reads as a changed marker, one `Resumed`, rather than a hang.
- **Nothing else in `internal/loop` or `internal/driver/…` reads a clock.** `tools/sansio`'s rule set for those packages refuses clock and timer calls outside `epoll_linux.go`, channel operations, `select` and `range` over a map.

**Disk I/O goes through io_uring** (`internal/driver/uring`), a ring per loop that the driver maps and drives with raw syscalls, since Go's standard library has none; `Loop.AttachDisk` puts its eventfd in the loop's set. The ring is set up with no flags: `IORING_SETUP_SINGLE_ISSUER`, which `DEFER_TASKRUN` needs, fails submissions from any thread but the first, and a loop goroutine is not locked to one.

- **Fixed buffers.** The loop's pool is fixed in size, so the driver registers its regions once (`IORING_REGISTER_BUFFERS`) and its disks as fixed files, and a one-buffer read or write uses `READ_FIXED`/`WRITE_FIXED`, saving 5 to 8% of the CPU per I/O in page pinning ([io_uring registered buffers spike](#appendix-io_uring-registered-buffers-spike)). A gathered write stays a plain `WRITEV`, since `WRITEV_FIXED` needs Linux 6.15 and its pin is small beside a flush. Without `IPC_LOCK` registration fails with `ENOMEM`, and the driver issues plain `READ`/`WRITE`, reported in its `Status`.
- **A write and its flush in one submission.** A round's write and its `IORING_FSYNC_DATASYNC` go linked (`IOSQE_IO_LINK`), so the flush starts only after the write and is cancelled if it fails. Contract: a flush never runs before its write has completed, even when the kernel cuts a chain for want of a request (`io_submit_sqes`, Linux 6.12): the driver takes back what the kernel did not take, read from the submission queue's head, and the rest waits for the taken part. A short transfer or any error result completes with `DiskIO`, and so, at once, does a linked flush that does not follow a write of its own disk queued in the same pass, since run unlinked it would report durable a write it never waited for. Under `durability: fua` a write carries `RWF_DSYNC` and a flush submits nothing; `flush` stays the default since FUA cost 2.2 ms per 4K write on the measured drive ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)).
- **A trim zeroes the segment header.** On a raw device a `DiskTrim` is a `BLOCK_URING_CMD_DISCARD` (Linux 6.12) with a 4 KiB write of zeros over the segment's header hard-linked behind it (`IOSQE_IO_HARDLINK`); on a file it is the write of zeros alone, so the file stays written in full. A freed segment's header then reads as zeros whether the discard ran, failed or leaves old data readable, which a driver cannot learn reliably, and recovery never reads a freed segment in full ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)).
- **Raw devices** are opened `O_DIRECT` and `O_EXCL`, so a second daemon or a file system cannot open one beside the first. A ring's file table holds that claim until the kernel tears the ring down, so `Close` unregisters it first (only with nothing in flight before Linux 6.13, where unregistering waits, [`io_rsrc_ref_quiesce`](https://git.kernel.org/torvalds/c/7029acd8a950)), and the daemon retries a busy device every 10 ms for up to 2 s, the teardown of a killed process's ring, before failing the disk.
- **A failed ring is a failed disk.** `io_uring_enter` is retried on `EINTR`, `EAGAIN` and `EBUSY`; any other error marks `Status.Failed` and completes every operation, queued, in flight or later, with `DiskIO`, so the log sees a failed disk rather than the daemon panicking. The kernel may still touch buffers it was handed, so the driver quarantines them (`Pool.Quarantine`): never handed out again while the pool lives, a bounded leak on a dead disk. `Close` quarantines the same way.
- **Containers.** Docker's default seccomp profile refuses `io_uring_setup` with `EPERM`, so the daemon runs under a profile that allows the `io_uring_*` calls.

**A FUSE mount's device is read and written one request at a time** (`internal/frontend/fuse`): each read of `/dev/fuse` returns one request and each write must be exactly one reply, so the frontend owns the descriptor on the loop and writes each reply with its own `write`, outside the loop's `writev`. A `READ`'s reply is built in place, each chunk's data copied once into a pool buffer behind header room, rather than gathered from the chunk client's buffers, which would hold every part until the last arrives. The mount runs on loop 0, which hosts no MetaStore replica, so its `meta.Client` never sends to its own loop.

**Network I/O goes through the TCP driver** (`internal/driver/tcp`), on raw non-blocking sockets; the daemon passes in how to dial a peer loop and the listening socket, so tests can hand it socketpairs. A loop keeps one connection per peer loop, opened by its first send or accepted; each side's first frame is a hello naming its loop and incarnation, and a message before it ends the connection. Two loops that dial each other at once keep the dial from the lower address. A peer known only from an accepted connection is forgotten once it ends, so the operator's one-shot command processes leave nothing behind. Every socket sets `TCP_NODELAY`, since the loop already gathers a pass's frames.

- **Framing.** A 12-byte header with a format version, the kind, both lengths and a CRC32C over the whole frame, then inline bytes and data, at most 16 MiB (the layout is the package's). A bad frame, or one the pool has no buffer for, ends the connection as one `PeerLost`; a frame cut off by a reset is never delivered.
- **Reads** are one `readv`: the current frame's data straight into its own block-aligned pool buffer, plus a read-ahead for the frames behind it, 64 KiB after a small frame and 4 KiB after a large one, so a burst of small frames costs one syscall and a large frame copies at most 4 KiB.
- **Sends.** A connection holds at most 32 MiB and one frame, above the loop's queue cap, so a peer that keeps reading never reaches it; one that stops reading, or a send the pool cannot buffer, ends the connection with a `PeerLost` rather than muting the sender, since Raft and replication cannot stall for one slow peer, and the core resends what a lost connection took ([Sends, reconnects and held writes](#data-layer-chunkstore)).
- **Reconnects.** A failed dial backs off from 10 ms to 1 s, half of it jitter from the loop's seeded source, and a send meanwhile gets its `PeerLost` by the next pass, since nothing above the driver has a timeout of its own. An accept error that lasts (`EMFILE` and its kin) pauses the listener for the same backoff.

**Memory**

- **Long-lived bulk structures hold no pointers.** The memtable is a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages that refer to each other by `uint32` number, and the chunk index and similar tables are slices of integers, so collection costs under 3 ms of CPU at 20 million entries instead of 1.25 CPU-seconds per cycle ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike)).
- **Bulk memory lives outside the Go heap**, in slabs mapped with `unix.Mmap` and held as `[]byte`: memtable pages, index tables, retained Raft entries and I/O buffers. Three reasons: the heap goal is `GOGC` percent above the live heap, pointer-free memory included, so 16 GiB of memtable on the heap would let the heap grow toward 32 GiB; `O_DIRECT` and io_uring need page-aligned buffers; and a slab registers once with io_uring, SPDK or RDMA (SPDK's and RDMA's are allocated by their C libraries). A slab hands out only `[]byte` and sans-IO code may not import `unsafe`, so off-heap memory, which the collector does not scan, never holds a Go pointer. Its regions, at most 1 GiB, never move, so a registration stays valid as it grows.
- **Managed by hand, checked by generation.** A buffer used after it is freed would read another buffer's data rather than crash, so a pool handle carries a generation, and a freed or stale handle panics in every build. The pool is `core.Pool`, the same first-fit allocator over the slab as over the simulator's Go slice, so both pass one set of pool tests. Memtable pages carry no generation, since they never leave their tree.
- **Every tree's pages come from its loop's slab and go back to it.** An LSM tree carves its memtable's pages from `Config.Pages`, the page slab of its loop, which reuses returned pieces and so grows to the loop's peak, and whoever drops a tree closes it (`Tree.Close`). Sorted runs and retained Raft entries are still on the Go heap ([tech debt](tech-debts.md)).
- **I/O buffers are handles**, never `sync.Pool`, which empties at collections, has no bound and cannot be registered for DMA; `sync.Pool` is for scratch off the data path. Each loop has its own slab and a handle does not say whose it is, so another loop may read a buffer it is handed but only its own loop frees it; a record handed to a disk's owner is copied into the owner's pool ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)).
- **No Go arenas.** `GOEXPERIMENT=arenas` is on hold upstream, and pages already cost 0.0% of CPU in collection against 9.3% for a memtable in an arena.
- **`GOMEMLIMIT` budgets the Go heap**: both daemons take a budget as `-memory` (MiB) and set `GOMEMLIMIT` to it less the slabs mapped, lowered as slabs grow but never below a 64 MiB heap, since a limit near 0 keeps the collector running back to back while the soft-limited heap grows anyway. A start whose budget does not cover its pools, its slabs' first regions, its loops' [trace rings](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle) and that minimum is refused; without `-memory` the limit is left alone. The budget does not bound slab growth: memtable flushing bounds each tree's pages, and those caps do not yet come from the budget ([tech debt](tech-debts.md)).
- **Hot paths allocate nothing**, held by `testing.AllocsPerRun` tests that fail `go test` and by benchmarks that report allocations (`b.ReportAllocs`).

**Concurrency outside the loops**

- **Read-mostly tables** (the placement table, the session table) are immutable values published through `atomic.Pointer`: a reader loads the current one without a lock, an update publishes a new one, and the collector frees the old.
- **Latency paths use typed atomics and fixed slot tables** instead of a mutex, a map and a channel: queue-depth-1 p50 67 against 84 µs. Counters written by different cores are padded to a cache line.
- **CPU-heavy background work runs on worker goroutines** (cleaner copies, erasure coding, compaction) that send results to the owning loop's inbox as events, so each index keeps one writer. In stepped tests their jobs run inline as steps of the simulator's scheduler. The cleaner's copy work costs Go 1.12 to 1.24× Rust.

**Libraries and build**

- **CRC32C from `hash/crc32`** (Castagnoli table), which uses SSE4.2 on amd64 and the CRC32 instructions on arm64.
- **Encodings are hand-written** over `encoding/binary`'s `Append*` and `PutUint*` into caller-provided buffers, with no reflection (`encoding/gob`, `encoding/json`, `binary.Read`/`Write`) on the data path. Every decoder has a native fuzz test.
- **cgo only in the SPDK and RDMA drivers**, behind build tags so the default build is pure Go, and every crossing carries a batch. `unsafe` only in `internal/loop`, for the raw `read` and `writev` syscalls, and in driver packages, for rings, cgo and registered memory.
- **Release builds use PGO** (a committed `default.pgo`) and ship for `amd64` at the default `GOAMD64=v1` and for `arm64`. `GOAMD64=v3` gained under 3.5% and would exclude homelab CPUs without AVX2, such as Goldmont Plus Celerons.
- **The toolchain is pinned** in `go.mod`, and `scripts/lint.sh` runs `gofmt`, `go vet` with the sans-IO analyzer, `staticcheck`, `govulncheck` and a dependency license check.

### Package layout

One Go module, `github.com/nvcnvn/rock-solid`, with everything but the binary and tools under `internal/`:

| Package | Contents | Sans-IO |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/core` | `Event`, `Action`, `Context`, `IOBuf`, the buffer pool contract and the block pool both drivers' pools run on, IDs and the PG hash, CRC32C, seeded RNG, outstanding peer requests, the `Resumed` detector and the timer heap | Yes |
| `internal/disklog` | Segments, record codec, group commit, recovery, segment usage table, which segments the cleaner may free, TRIM pacing | Yes |
| `internal/lsm` | Page-tree memtable, sorted runs with Bloom filters, compaction, point-in-time views and restore; later a block cache | Yes |
| `internal/lease` | Loop-pair lease grants with support epochs, expiry with the drift margin, the restart wait; used by primary read leases and Raft leader leases | Yes |
| `internal/chunkstore` | Chunk index, the cleaner, replica primary, pools, placement, peering, primary read leases, failure detection | Yes |
| `internal/raftshard` | etcd-raft `RawNode` wrapped in `OnEvent`, its `raft.Storage` over the disk log | Yes |
| `internal/meta` | MetaStore shard state machine, key encoding, cross-shard transactions, sessions, inode owner, the control-plane leader's machine and its host, and a replica's `loop.Shard` on the round driver | Yes |
| `internal/node` | A storage node's machine beside its replicas (device starts, heartbeat to the control-plane leader, placement tables and disk membership for its replicas, its side of session fencing) and each device's machine | Yes |
| `internal/fuseproto` | FUSE protocol codec: request decoding, reply and notification encoding, saved `INIT` parameters | Yes |
| `internal/fsclient` | The FUSE daemon's sans-IO half per mount: requests turned into MetaStore `Txn`s and chunk I/O, handles, the write-back buffer under its space grant, and the `Mount` that answers `INIT` and frames the replies the frontend writes | Yes |
| `internal/sim` | Deterministic scheduler, simulated disk, network and clients, invariant checker, history recorder; `sim/clock`, the per-node clock model, which imports nothing else of the simulator so `looptest` can use it; `sim/toy`, a toy protocol with bugs to plant; `sim/lincheck`, the Porcupine check, kept apart since Porcupine runs goroutines | Yes |
| `internal/loop` | Event loop runtime: off-heap slabs and the buffer pool on them, the kernel seam over epoll, clocks and timerfd, inbox, the disk ring a driver attaches, round driver | No |
| `internal/loop/looptest` | Stepped kernel on scripted clocks, the loops as actors for `internal/sim`'s scheduler, and `Wire`, the framing the loop's own tests use | No |
| `internal/loop/nettest` | The shared network suite every network driver passes | No |
| `internal/driver/uring`, `…/tcp`, `…/spdk`, `…/rdma` | One package per backend; SPDK and RDMA use cgo behind build tags | No |
| `internal/frontend/fuse`, `…/ublk`, `…/s3` | Protocol adapters onto the core | No |
| `internal/csi` | Kubernetes CSI driver | No |
| `cmd/rock-solid` | The single binary that wires the drivers and frontends picked by flags | No |
| `tools/sansio` | The `go vet` analyzer for sans-IO packages, with a narrower rule set for the loop and driver packages | No |
| `tools/maelstrom` | The Raft shard as a Maelstrom node, for the `lin-kv` workload | No |
| `models` | TLA+ specifications | |
## Testing strategy

Most bugs live in interleavings that a real cluster hits rarely and can't reproduce. So most testing happens in deterministic simulation, and real hardware confirms what simulation can't model.

| Layer | What it catches | Tools |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Deterministic simulation testing (DST) | Interleaving bugs across crashes, partitions, disk faults and lost recalls; reproducible from a seed | Own simulator over the sans-IO core; event loops and drivers stepped on scripted clocks by the same scheduler |
| 2. Protocol model checking | Design bugs in epoch changes (per-PG intervals, peering, read leases, Raft fortification), sessions and fencing, cache-mode recalls and purges (phase 2); snapshot cuts and pool moves (phase 11); cross-shard commit (phase 13) | TLA+ specifications checked with TLC in CI, in the style of etcd-raft's |
| 3. Consistency checking | Reads that no valid ordering could produce; close-to-open violations | Porcupine (Go), Jepsen Elle |
| 4. Real-cluster fault injection | Kernel, FUSE, firmware, fsync and RDMA behavior | device-mapper, SPDK error/delay bdevs, eBPF error injection (`bpf_override_return`), `tc netem`, `nftables`, `kill -9`, power cuts |
| 5. Heterogeneous matrix and property tests | Placement and rebalance bugs on mixed hardware | Generated matrix, seeded property tests, native Go fuzzing of every decoder |
| 6. Conformance suites | POSIX and protocol compatibility gaps | `pjdfstest`, `fsx` (single and multi-client, with end-of-file pollution `-e` and mapped operations), xfstests `generic/029` and `generic/030`, `csi-sanity`, Kubernetes storage end-to-end tests, Ceph `s3-tests` |
| 7. Performance regression | Latency, CPU and recovery regressions | `fio` and metadata benchmarks nightly → VictoriaMetrics → Grafana; eBPF off-CPU and latency profiles to explain regressions |

**1. Deterministic simulation.** All nodes, disks, clients and the network run in one goroutine from one seeded random number generator (`internal/sim`, itself sans-IO). The simulator drops, delays, reorders and duplicates messages, partitions the network, crashes, restarts, reboots and pauses nodes, and runs each node's clocks at rates of their own. A failing seed replays exactly, and two runs of a seed give byte-identical traces, across processes, `GOMAXPROCS` and `-race`. Every seeded property test, one whose property must hold seed by seed, takes its seeds from one place (`internal/sim/seeds`): a few under `go test ./...`, thousands under `scripts/seeds.sh`, which splits each test's seeds across processes and which continuous integration (CI) runs on every push, and one when replaying. A failing seed prints the one-line `go test` command that replays it and, for a test with an ops file, the ops reduced to a regression case; the case, or a test of the failing seed, lands before the fix, failing without it. A change that adds a seeded test runs it over 10,000 seeds once, as Pebble stresses every test a pull request adds. Where overnight runs of millions of seeds live is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

The simulated network delivers each direction of a connection in order, as TCP does and as a primary's sends to a member rely on, so messages reorder only across connections; each takes a seeded delay plus any one-way queueing a test sets, and a duplicate arrives right behind its original. A lost message breaks its connection: everything after it is lost until one `PeerLost` at each end, or never, in runs that check the core keeps its own deadlines, as the [TCP driver](#runtime-in-go) behaves. A crashed process's sends still arrive, as its kernel would send them; a reboot, standing for a power loss, loses them. The network enforces the [driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers)'s buffer ownership and reports a machine that frees a send's buffer early.

Each node's clocks are a model in `internal/sim/clock`, shared with the event loop's stepped tests, at the bounds [Lease time](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) assumes: the raw clock within ±4%, fixed per node per seed and stepping once by up to `lease_drift / 2` after `rate_window`, `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` slewed from it by up to ±22.6%, and a reboot restarting them near 0 at a changed rate. A pause stands a node still in each of the ways the [VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike) measured, and every simulated loop pass runs `core.Resumed` over the node's readings.

The scheduler offers every pending completion, delivery, due timer, client operation, crash and restart as a step, completions in any order, and jumps time only when nothing else is pending, so an idle hour costs one step. A write's blocks reach the disk's volatile state when its completion is taken, not when it is issued, and an unlinked flush covers only writes completed before it runs. It delivers stale `Timer` events, which the driver contract allows, and a machine reports an event outside its contract as data, never a panic.

The simulated disk models:

- loss of unflushed data on crash: each unflushed block write lands or not on its own, in the order it was executed, so a crash can leave holes (a lost write before one that landed) and can keep an older write to a block without the newer one; a write still in flight is decided the same way. That is stricter than Pebble's crashable filesystem, whose kept block always holds the file's latest contents, and RocksDB's `FaultInjectionTestFS`, which keeps a prefix of each file's unsynced data;
- torn writes: only some 512 B sectors of a block land, since the [NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)'s drive is power-fail atomic for 512 B only, and a segment header, which fits in one sector, lands whole or as it was;
- injected errors, chosen by action kind, block range, action index and a seeded probability, as Pebble's `errorfs` chooses them: a failed write or trim, which leaves its blocks undecided, a failed flush, which leaves every unflushed block undecided for a later flush or crash to land, a failed read, which fills nothing the caller may trust, and a read that completes with a changed byte, as RocksDB's fault injection returns damaged buffers, which only checksums catch;
- silent bit flips over durable blocks, and misdirected writes, which land whole at another block. A misdirected record is never replayed at the wrong place, and at its own place recovery applies the tail rule; an acknowledged last record misdirected on a single disk is lost undetectably, so the single-disk "acknowledged writes are readable" invariant excuses exactly the writes the disk reports misdirected, and with replicas nothing is excused;
- TRIM: a segment header behind a discard reads as zeros, as the driver writes it; the rest of the range reads as zeros or as before, chosen per trim, and a trim is volatile until a flush;
- fail-slow: a disk 100x slower that never errors. The scheduler keeps one clock, so a slow disk delays other disks' completions as much as its own, and a comparison between disks' latencies is tested on the figures primaries report rather than in a simulated cluster;
- hangs: every action on a disk stays uncompleted until it is released, while its node and loops run.

A failed write or flush is final for the open log: nothing after it is acknowledged, and the node runs recovery again rather than retrying the flush. Besides random seeds, a short workload with a checkpoint, a compaction and a cleaner pass is crashed, and separately failed, at its k-th disk action for every k, with no, half and all unflushed blocks landing, and recovery itself crashed before each of its writes and flushes, as Pebble's `TestErrors` and `TestDBCompactionCrash` do, so rare actions such as a manifest piece, a superblock copy or recovery's own header zeroing are never left to chance.

Group commit decodes each record it is about to write and asserts that it holds exactly the submitted items, so an encoder bug fails in the round that made it instead of in a later recovery. The LSM tree does the same for runs, as RocksDB's `paranoid_file_checks` and `force_consistency_checks` do: a flush decodes every piece it built, a merge checks its output's key order against its inputs, and the run list stays ordered by sequence number. Both are settings (`Check`) that the packages' tests and the simulator turn on and release builds leave off; a setting rather than a build tag, as Pebble's `invariants` tag is, so the simulator chooses per run with the same binary. The cheap checks stay on in release builds ([The LSM tree](#on-disk-format)): entry counts on every flush and merge, and a CRC32C of every in-memory piece a merge reads or a flush rewrites, one pass 3.8 to 5.1% of the merge's CPU in the [metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike), and the two the tree makes 12% of its own, faster merge on arm64.

**Planted bugs.** The simulator proves itself on a protocol whose bugs are known. `internal/sim/toy` is a primary-backup protocol on three nodes: the primary writes each value to its disk and both replicas', resends until one replies, and acknowledges once the value is durable locally and on one replica; every message carries the primary's incarnation. Its invariant, checked after every step, is that every acknowledged value is durable on at least two disks. A setting plants one of two bugs: acknowledging once only the primary's copy is durable, and taking a reply to an earlier incarnation's write as one to the restarted primary's write of the same sequence number, which only a primary crash with that reply in flight exposes. Each bug must be found within the seeds every commit runs, its failing seed must replay to a byte-identical trace and the same failing step, and the fixed protocol must pass the same seeds. The toy is a sans-IO package like the others, allocating nothing per event.

**Metamorphic runs.** An index must read the same whatever its internal choices, so `internal/lsm` and the chunk index have a metamorphic test, as Pebble's (`internal/metamorphic`). A seed generates one ops file of writes, removals, range removals, gets, floors, scans and point-in-time views, with flushes, failed checkpoints, compactions and restarts as ops, keys reused so ops collide. The ops run under a list of option sets, each pushing one choice to an extreme: piece size, flush and compaction cadence, merges held, stepped, concurrent or restarted, run filters off or always answering "maybe", the scan's merge, failing flushes, and range removals replaced by point removals. Each run writes one history line per op, and the histories must equal each other and a map model's. One runner, `internal/sim/metamorphic`, serves both indexes. A failing seed is reduced to the fewest ops that still fail, and the reduced ops file becomes a data-driven regression case under `testdata/` that names any plant that found it. In the [metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike), two of five planted bugs showed only under the option set at an extreme.

Simulated FUSE clients (`sim.Mount`) are the kernel side of a mount as `fs/fuse` of Linux 6.18.44 runs one, speaking to the daemon only in `/dev/fuse` frames: a page cache that refills on reads, direct I/O fixed at open, `keep_cache`, attribute and entry timeouts, `auto_inval_data`, mapped pages that are not revalidated, and the attributes a write past the cached size makes the kernel discard. Its processes' operations go to the history recorder, and the file-level check of layer 3 reads them.

**Faults each area's runs inject**, beyond the network, clock and disk faults above, each landing at the step the protocol is least ready for:

- Coherence: recalls lost, delayed or crossing an owner failover; clients paused past their lease; an evicted node that still renews; racing opens of an Exclusive file; leader transfers, fences reaching some primaries late, and control-plane failovers, deposed leaders and stalls past `session_lease` while sessions renew; a client that reaches the control plane but no owner; a daemon crash with requests unanswered, then their resend, a dead daemon's write landing after its successor's reissue among them.
- Placement: an old primary partitioned with clients on its table; a disk that stops completing while its node heartbeats; interval changes faster than peering; members removed and re-added, reformatted, swapped or cloned under an old ID, flapping, or returning around `refill_after`; refills and moves whose members fail mid-recovery, below `min_live`; a primary restarted or paused around `node_down_after`; a chunk counter near its limit; a PG split under load; lease clocks at the bounds of [Lease time](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), suspends and VM stops around a lease; crashes during peering or within a granted lease; unaligned writes from two nodes into one block across a primary change; a merge's base block failing its checksum.
- MetaStore: a quiescent leader partitioned from one follower or suspended past its lease; leader transfers and configuration changes under lease reads; a voter replaced while another is down or returning mid-swap; groups campaigning together after a leader's crash; elections with transactions in flight, which their callers resend.
- Redundancy: crashes and disk losses mid-encoding, mid-return to copies and mid-recovery; PGs below `min_live`; losses beyond what a pool survives.
- Freeing space and a full pool: crashes mid-purge or mid-truncate, before the punch of a partial block; a purger losing its term with a punch in flight or repeating its `Txn` after an election; unlinks and truncates racing other nodes' writes; space grants lost, delayed or outlasting a control-plane failover; a refill onto the fullest disks while grants are out; block writes held until space is freed; a write crossing from a granted chunk into a refused one.
- Snapshots and pool moves (phase 11): a node that never acks the pause; a coordinator crash between pause and resume; a shard leader change at the cut; deletion while a clone reads through or flattens; a crash mid-copy; snapshots cut while a purge runs or a volume overwrites near full.
- On-disk format: crashes during cleaning, compaction and flushes, and between a cleaner copy and its apply, a run and its manifest, or a freed segment and the header naming the freeing manifest, with TRIM run, not run, or reading as ones; a torn tail record holding chunk writes and Raft entries; a lost chunk index rebuilt from the log. The disk log's crash and corruption cases are ported from RocksDB's log tests (which keep LevelDB's) and etcd's write-ahead log tests, each naming its source and commit.

**Invariants.** Each is registered by name, as its component is built, in the run's invariant checker (`sim.Invariants`), most specific first, so a bug that breaks several is named by the one closest to it. A check point follows every step unless a component's tests set another cadence. The chunk store checks whenever its simulated disk goes quiet and after every recovery: its invariants concern what the store holds, which a quiet store still shows, and comparing every read after every disk action would read the whole store once per action. An invariant about a moment, such as two primaries serving at once, needs a check after every step, because the violation can end before the run goes quiet. No check shares the code's own bookkeeping, so a bug cannot hide in both: the byte-range oracle is a plain model of the bytes each acknowledged request sent; the reuse check decodes the manifest a durable header names from the records written, as recovery would; and a cleaner victim's cost is the blocks its rewrites wrote, each run charged in full to every victim it spans, never the guard's own estimate.

- every acknowledged write is readable, and no read returns a version that is later lost;
- every read returns exactly the bytes of the acknowledged writes and punches applied in version order, checked against a byte-range oracle, live and at every snapshot, and no version peering keeps follows a dropped version of its chunk;
- no read returns a version before it commits, and no two members hold different data under one version;
- no PG serves reads from two primaries at once, no two replicas of a shard serve lease reads at overlapping times, a read's answer over the wire holds every `Txn` acknowledged before the read was sent, no member logs a write tagged with an earlier interval of its PG than its table's, and no write tagged with an interval completes after a later interval of its PG has activated unless that interval kept it; no write is acknowledged on fewer than `min_live` disks, a member in `maintenance` counting as one; no step check trips between two nodes neither paused nor rebooted, and each such pair holds a lease within `rate_gap` plus two round trips of first contact; once membership stops changing, every PG whose current interval has at least `min_live` live members on running nodes activates it;
- no write is refused for its interval while its PG's interval has not changed, no acknowledged version is ever dropped, and no version an activated interval kept is dropped later;
- no disk ID names two log IDs, and no log ID is up under two disk IDs;
- a truncated or punched range reads as zeros, an extension never exposes a byte a truncate cut off, no byte a truncate or unlink cut off is non-zero on disk once its purge has run, a purge rerun after a crash fails no punch, and once a purge settles, no chunk of a deleted inode or volume remains outside what snapshots hold;
- every chunk reaches its pool's redundancy (copies, or all `data + parity` shards) within the pool's failure-domain rule once recovery settles;
- an encoded version becomes readable, and copies are dropped, only after all its shards are durable;
- a chunk with fewer surviving copies or shards than it needs returns an error, never wrong data;
- at most one lock holder per lock; at most one writer per RWO volume;
- no file's `mtime` goes backwards except by an explicit `utimensat`, whatever the writers' wall clocks show, neither as stored nor as one node reports it, across entering and leaving WriteShared;
- no client reads stale data from a file in WriteShared mode, no session is fenced while its client's own lease still runs, no fenced session's write is applied, every write of a fenced lock holder that completes is ordered before every write of the next holder, and a dead client's locks are free within `session_lease` on the leader's clock plus one heartbeat interval while the leader holds its lease and has measured the client, `dead_wait` instead when it has not, or once an unreachable storage node is marked down;
- a replayed mutating request returns its first final result and changes nothing, and an internal transaction repeated after an election changes nothing it did not read;
- an orphaned inode is never freed while a live session has it open;
- a snapshot holds every write acknowledged before its cut and none issued after, in both data and metadata;
- a frozen version is never dropped, and the cleaner never frees a block, while the live version, a snapshot or an unflattened clone needs it;
- every cleaner pass frees more segments than it fills, every metadata victim frees more than its rewrites write, and no segment is reused while it holds a live block, or a piece, run or record that the newest manifest named by a durable segment header needs (a record after its replay points); after every recovery each chunk segment's live count in the usage table equals the blocks the index's records there refer to, each pool's live blocks are the blocks its maps refer to, every chunk slot in use is referred to by a map, a pending slot write or a free queue, and the log opens its next segment past every open sequence a segment header of this log on disk holds, so open sequences stay unique and increasing;
- a cleaner move is applied only where a map still points at the location it copied from, live, in replay and in a rebuild, so a write that lands during cleaning is never replaced by the older copy;
- a disk that is full except for its reserve can still finish a checkpoint, a merge step and a cleaner pass, and the bytes held beyond the manifests never exceed the reserve;
- no ordinary write fails with `NoSpace` while the cleaner has a victim;
- a flush under a space grant is never refused for space, and on every disk of a pool (1 + k) times the grants outstanding, plus the flushes not yet in fill reports, never exceed the gap between the larger of its fill and `write_limit` and its log reserve;
- a disk passes `write_limit` only through granted flushes, overwrites of blocks it holds live and unshared, punches and recovery;
- a PG whose copying has made no progress for `stuck_after` makes its pool `Stuck`, and a node reboot changes no capacity figure;
- every failed write the daemon acknowledged reaches each handle open on its file at the time, once, at its next `write()`, `fsync` or `close`;
- no block write that needs new space completes with an error while the pool is full, unless an operator failed it;
- a chunk index rebuilt from the log equals the one recovered from its manifest, replay rebuilds every map and record count the store held, a chunk's live map never holds two entries for one block, and a shard recovered from its manifest and log equals its state before the crash;
- at most one Raft leader per shard per term, a committed entry is never lost, and a replica keeps its term and vote across a crash;
- no node's table epoch or PG interval number goes backwards, no PG's last activated interval goes down, every activation a table records happened, and no two writes of a chunk get one version.

**Crash oracle.** As RocksDB's crash test does with its expected state and trace, the simulator keeps (`sim.Oracle`), per disk, the state every index had at the last acknowledged LSN and the records written since, in LSN order. After a crash, recovery reports an LSN between the acknowledged one and the last in flight; the oracle replays its trace to that LSN, and every index on the disk, the chunk index and each shard replica, must equal the result, with no hole. The oracle's state is independent of replay and of the index: the harness decodes each round from the write as it goes to the disk, with each segment's open sequence taken from the write that opened it rather than read back, and checks every decoded entry against the item submitted under its ticket, so a codec bug the oracle would share with replay still fails. Every ticket resolves: when a log is dropped at a crash, and at the end of a test, each ticket has been answered `Durable`, `InDoubt` or `Failed` exactly once, or its item is still queued or in flight. The real-disk crash test keeps its oracle in a file its driver writes outside the process it kills, and since the chunk store orders changes only within a chunk, it checks a prefix per chunk; a `kill -9` leaves the page cache and the drive's cache intact, so it tests rounds in flight, not a power cut ([build checklist](build-checklist.md), Crash tests on real disks).

**Driver tests.** The event loop and the drivers cannot be sans-IO, but most of what they decide is made from what a pass reads, so they are tested in three tiers ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)):

| Tier | Runs | Covers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stepped | Every commit, seeded; the io_uring cases on a host or in a container that allows io_uring | `internal/loop/looptest` puts a stepped kernel behind the [kernel seam](#runtime-in-go): `Wait` polls the loop's real descriptors with `poll(2)` without blocking, so the tier runs on macOS too; `Clocks` reads the simulated clock model, and scripted jumps (a suspend, a slew, a VM pause, the next deadline) land inside `Wait`. The scheduler steps many loops and their worker jobs in a seeded order over real AF_UNIX socketpairs, which deliver inside the writer's call (`net/unix/af_unix.c`); kernel I/O still under way is waited for without advancing scripted time, so no deadline fires while a segment crosses loopback. A seed gives the same results and per-loop event order, and traces name connections by loop-assigned IDs, so two runs' traces are `cmp`-equal; over io_uring and TCP only pass boundaries can differ |
| Real kernel | Every commit in the gate workflow, natively on Linux, or in a container whose seccomp profile allows `io_uring_*` (Docker's default refuses `io_uring_setup`); `scripts/gate-branch.sh` runs those of the packages a branch affects in Docker on macOS | Syscall counts per pass, allocations and goroutines under 1,000 connections; a loop that really blocks and wakes; no false `Resumed` from stalls under CPU throttling; the timerfd's clock and absolute deadline; the io_uring ring on a file-backed disk; TCP errors made on loopback; cgroup sizing under `docker run --cpus=2.5`. These tests assert counts, orderings and lower bounds, never exact timings, as TigerBeetle's IO tests do |
| VM, nightly | The [VM pause](#appendix-vm-pause-spike) harness on QEMU and Firecracker | The VMClock rows with the real marker, a wall-clock step, device-mapper targets |

The stepper differs from production in three ways, each covered elsewhere. It runs one goroutine, so the inbox gets a stress test under `-race` with real producers and a consumer loop that really blocks. Its clocks are scripted, so the clock slew and VM pause spikes measured what real clocks do and the VM tier keeps checking it. It never sleeps, so a stepped loop that calls `Wait` to block while `Pending` reports work fails the test at once, and the real-kernel tier covers wake-ups that really sleep. The drivers' pure pieces, building io_uring submission entries, decoding completions and the frame codec, get table tests and fuzzing rather than a fake.

`testing/synctest` is only for goroutines off the data path that wait on channels, `time.Sleep`, Go timers or `context` deadlines and make only syscalls that return at once: worker retries, the CSI server's timeouts, the daemon's restart deadlines. It fakes only the `time` package, and a goroutine inside a system call such as `epoll_wait` keeps its bubble's clock from moving, so it can test neither a loop nor a goroutine that blocks in the kernel.

**2. Protocol models.** A TLA+ model describes a protocol as a state machine: the variables each node holds, and every step any node, message or fault can take. TLC walks every reachable state of a small instance and prints a step-by-step trace of any state that breaks a property. It checks the design; the simulator checks the code. The bugs models find lie in fault and recovery paths, at depths a simulator rarely reaches: Amazon's models found a 35-step data loss in DynamoDB's replication and membership protocol that had passed design reviews, code reviews and fault injection on a simulated network ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)). So each protocol is modelled before its code: the four of phase 2 first, and each later one in the phase that builds it, since the snapshot cut and cross-shard commit build on designs phase 2 is still checking ([roadmap](#roadmap-and-open-questions), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered)).

A model is `models/<name>/`: the spec `<name>.tla`, whose header says what it covers and what it takes as given, and its configurations, each opening with a comment that says what it holds. A spec states its properties and numbers them where there are many; each rule it states can be removed by a constant, and a negative configuration (`neg-*.cfg`) removes one and names on its first line the property it must break, never one that only restates the removed rule. TLC is pinned to 1.7.4 and the specs use no CommunityModules operator that needs the 1.8 pre-release ([tech debts](tech-debts.md)); etcd-raft's own spec (`tla/` in its repository) is the template. `scripts/models.sh` checks every model at three depths, and fails a spec that lacks a `ci` or a `deep` configuration:

- **`ci`**: exhaustive, for the safety properties and for liveness with a time bound, written as an invariant over the model's clocks, within 600 s with 4 workers on the M4 Pro, together with the negative configurations, each of which must stop at its counterexample. A negative that passes means the model is too weak, and one cut at its budget is over budget, not passed. At the 30,000 to 430,000 distinct states a second measured on 4 workers, 10 minutes covers 20 to 260 million states, enough for CockroachDB's two published models and MongoDB's two-shard transaction model, though not for etcd-raft's spec, which did not finish in 15 minutes cut down to 3 fixed servers ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)). A configuration past the budget is split, or its largest constant moves to `deep`; the budget is not raised.
