A spec too large for one `ci` configuration gets several, each heavy on one part, rather than being split into specs: the epoch change model's property that no PG serves reads from two primaries needs peering and leases together, so it has configurations heavy on leases and others heavy on peering. Where any membership change at any time is too much, a configuration runs *plans* instead: each a short sequence of changes taken from the model's list of faults, with every interleaving of nodes, messages, writes and faults around it checked exhaustively; a larger configuration adds a plan of any two changes at any time. A model with two clocks per node lets time pass only once every step that is due has happened, so that time multiplies only its choices; the cost is that while time passes, only a node cut off from the control plane holds an old table, which its lease plans therefore use for every stale primary.
- **`deep`**: one more node, chunk, crash or term, plus the unbounded liveness properties, exhaustive, within 8,100 s on the M4 Pro. Liveness is the expensive part: CockroachDB's parallel-commit model checks its safety in 19 s and its temporal properties in 13 minutes. Nightly CI checks each `deep` configuration with no recorded pass for its spec, configuration and TLC version, so no author waits for one. A configuration past that budget is `heavy`: `scripts/models.sh heavy` runs it on the M4 Pro at milestones, capped at 12 hours, and CI never does.
- **Simulation**: TLC's random simulation (`-simulate`, depth 100), nightly, about an hour per model split among its `ci` configurations, each with a fresh seed printed for replay. It leaves out the configurations under fairness: TLC 1.7.4's simulation reports a liveness property violated wherever a trace ends at a state with no step enabled, even one at which it holds ([tech debts](tech-debts.md)); their exhaustive runs check those properties. An unchanged spec gives the same exhaustive answer every night; only simulation finds anything new.

When each run happens in CI is under **Continuous integration** below.

**Model-to-simulator map.** Each property a model checks names the simulator invariant that checks it in the code, so a property TLC showed holds of the protocol is also asserted on every seeded run of the code; the table says why where none does. Invariants are the Go constants their runs register, in the package named.

| Model | Spec property | Simulator invariant |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Epoch change (`chunkstore`) | 1 `OneReader` | `invOneReader`; for the lease clocks, `internal/lease`'s pair run (`TestNoLeaseOutlastsItsGrant`) |
| | 2 `OneLeaseholder` | `raftshard`'s `invOneLeaseReader` |
| | 3 `NoLateCompletion` | `invLateLog` |
| | 4 `NothingCommittedDropped` | `invAckedKept`, `invKeptWritten`, `invMappedAnswers` |
| | 5 `DropsAreSuffix` | `invKeptAfterDrop`, `invRecovered` |
| | 6 `OneWritePerVersion`, `VersionsGrow` | `invVersions`, `invOneVersion` |
| | 7 `UnchangedIntervalAccepts` | `invSameInterval` |
| | 8 `MergesOnKept`, `ImageRecoveryKept` | `invReads`, the byte-range oracle, which a merge on a missing base breaks |
| | 9 `TablesForward`, `Monotonic` | `invForward`, `invActivations` |
| | 10 `LeaseForms` | The lease pair run's quiet seeds, in which the step check never trips between a pair no fault touched |
| | 11 `NoResurrectedWrite` | `invReads`, `invIncarnation` |
| | 12 `PeeringSettles` | `invActivates` |
| | 13 `RemovalKeepsCopies` | `invRemoval` |
| | 14 `ReadsAcked`, `ReadsMapped` | `invReads`, `invReadVersion`, `invLinearizable` |
| | 15 `IntervalEnds` | `invAsks` |
| | 16 `AllAnswered` | `invOneReply` |
| | 17 `AckedOnMinLive` | `invAckCopies` |
| | 18 `NoStranded` | `invStranded` |
| | 19 `PrimarySource` | `invHasSource` |
| | 20 `GatesSettle` | `invRecovered`, which a member that keeps replayed writes gated breaks |
| | 21 `LeftGatesSettle` | `invLeftGated` |
| | 22 `HeldWritesOrdered` | `invHeldOrdered` |
| | 23 `GiveUpOnlyLost` | `invOnlyLost` |
| | 24 `AppliedOnce` | `invAppliedOnce`, and the chunk history check (`TestAResentWriteIsAppliedOnce`) |
| | 25 `StaleOnlyHeard` | `invStaleOnlyHeard` |
| Sessions and fencing | `NoFenceWhileLeased` | `meta`'s `invSessionLease` |
| | `NoFencedWriteApplied` | `chunkstore`'s `invFencedWrite` |
| | `LockOrder` | `chunkstore`'s `invLockOrder`, and `meta`'s on the leader side |
| | `AtMostOneHolder` | `meta`'s `invOneHolder` |
| | `NoSpuriousTrip` | `meta`'s `invNoTrip` |
| | `FenceReleasedInTime`, `CrashedClientReleased` | `meta`'s `invLocksFree` |
| Cache-mode recall | `NoStaleReadWriteShared` | None yet: phase 7 (Tier 2) registers it |
| | `CloseToOpen` | None needed: the consistency checker checks close-to-open on every history, and `fsclient`'s `invCloseToOpen` on its runs |
| Purge | `RerunSafe`, `TruncatedBytesZero`, `ExtensionNeverExposes`, `NoChunkOfDeletedInode` | None yet: the phase 6 items their `scripts/model-rules.tsv` rows name register them |
| | `NeverDeletesNewer` | The byte-range oracle |
| | `OrphanNotFreedWhileOpen` | `meta`'s and `fsclient`'s `invOrphanOpen` |
| | `PurgeSettles` | None needed: a purge entry pending for ever leaves a chunk the end-of-run settle check finds |
| Voter replacement (`raftshard`) | `AtMostOneLeader` | `invOneLeader` |
| | `CommittedNeverLost`, `CommittedAgree` | `invNeverLost` |
| | `VotersCaughtUp` | `invCaughtUp` |
| | `DeletesOnlyRemoved`, `LearnsOnlyRemoved` | `invOnlyRemoved`, which also checks each replica's report of its removal |
| | `OneLeaseReader` | `invOneLeaseReader` |
| | `LeaseReadsCurrent` | `invLeaseReadsCurrent` |
| | `SwapCommits` | None: the voter replacement tests time the swap |
| | `ReportsHold` | None: `TestALeaderThatCrashesAfterCommittingTheLeaveRestartsWithItCommitted` |
| | `NewLeader` | `invNewLeader` |
| | None: no spec step of a halted replica takes an append or a vote | `invHaltedStill` |

What no model checks, and what does instead:

- The sessions model starts a session with no fetch and no start request to answer, so "a live session's write is never refused" is in no model; the Go tests of the Control-plane sessions and Storage node session wiring items check it. Its leader measures a client's rate once two renewals reached it, so pairing an answer with the heartbeat it names, lost and repeated requests and a leader's own stop are checked only by Control-plane sessions' Go tests.
- Snapshot cuts and pool moves are modelled in phase 11 and cross-shard commit in phase 13, each before its code ([roadmap](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).
- The epoch model leaves out:
  - Splits beyond the one it models: a second split; a split of a pool whose PGs hold chunks in both halves, whose halves inherit the parent's state on every member as the modelled half does; and the reports placement ignores in the table that splits a pool, a table the model's folds never share. A node's per-PG state across a split is outside it too: the model follows a PG across the split, where a node matches its messages, leads, asks, failed versions and peering images by PG number. The code refuses a message tagged with a table older than its pool's split epoch, waits for a newer table on a message whose chunk its own `pg_count` puts in another PG or on a peering message tagged with a newer table (a sender's retry replacing the copy that waits), and drops that state of a pool that splits, so no half takes another PG's. The leader keys its watch of each PG's primary by number too, and drops a lead state whose `pg_count` is not the pool's (`TestAGiveUpAfterASplitDropsNoChunkOfAnotherPG`).
  - A change of `replicas`: neither the leaving mark that narrows a PG when `replicas` goes down nor a leaving member kept because a raised `replicas` needs it is modelled, though a leaving member that takes a missing member's place is, as `Stay`.
  - Moves beyond what its plans make. No plan puts a mark on one member and removes a leaving member in one table, which placement prevents by ignoring a report in a table that marks another member, nor a removal and a new move of the PG in one table, which placement prevents since a move needs the PG's members unchanged from the table before. The rule that a move starts only from an interval reported recovered is out as well: the model's refills are planned, not chosen by whether peering waits for a member, so the loss that rule prevents cannot arise there. The model checks that moves keep copies, never that a move completes; the placement tests that settle every move check that.
  - Failures: a member refusing a version it logged, whose acknowledgment was lost, which leaves a committed version waiting behind a failed one until `Settle`; a refusal from a member that already left the interval, which the model has it hold silently and the code answers; a failed recovery write, and a recovery write refused at a version the member does not stand at; a version held in part, which peering treats as not held; and a record failing below a later one on the primary's own log, which the store reports as held whole up to the failure, so that only the newest version on that log fails.

Some rules and properties of the epoch model have no negative configuration, and why:

- A primary asks for the next interval only once nothing of the PG waits (`AskNext`). Removing it leaves every `ci-faults` property passing, since a version a request came too soon for is settled by peering like any other; what the rule saves is refusing clients whose write would have completed, which no property states.
- A decision may list a member that logged a version in part. The model's logs hold whole versions, so `ci-tears` has the decision list such a member by `ListOf`, which shows extra listing is safe and not that it is needed. That a torn member must be listed rests on the Go plant `unlistTorn` (`TestUnlistTornMapsADroppedVersion`).
- Properties 3, 8 and 9 have no negative configuration of their own, since the only rules whose removal breaks them are the ones they restate.

**From spec to code.** The aim of this layer is to know that the code runs a protocol TLC has checked. New bugs are welcome, but they are not what it is measured by. That splits into two questions, with two checks:

- **Is the protocol sound?** TLC answers this on the spec. A spec states the protocol, not every line of code, so weak details can remain in it; that is fine, and a run need not be repeated until the spec changes.
- **Does the code run that protocol?** The spec conformance check (below) answers this from simulator runs: it catches code drifting away from a checked protocol, however good the spec.

The work goes in this order:

1. Model the protocol and check it with TLC before its code.
2. Build the code under the model gate (below). A protocol no model covers gets its model before its code, and a commit of that code names the model's commit in its `Model:` line.
3. Check conformance. It holds when all three parts hold: **every trace passes**, as a behavior of the spec; **traces can fail**, as removing a rule from the code fails one; and **traces cover the spec**, as every spec action appears in some trace, and any that does not is listed. A clean run is the result wanted, not a sign the check is idle: there is no quota of bugs to find.
4. Settle each discrepancy as one of four kinds: the code is wrong, the spec left out or got wrong a detail, a spec action compresses steps the code takes apart, or the logging is wrong. Fix the code, or patch the spec, and go on. A patched spec is the protocol kept, not thrown away, and it passes its `ci` run before it merges, since a patch can break a property.
5. Run the heavy checks at milestones: the end of a protocol's item, or of a phase. A commit that changes only code runs no state search, and a review does not wait for `deep`.

**Model gate.** On every commit `scripts/model-gate.sh`, run by `scripts/lint.sh`, checks what needs no reading, since nobody re-reads a spec and its code to compare them and an agent's reading of either proves nothing.

- A commit that changes a protocol file changes a spec or configuration under `models/` too, or says why not in a body line `Model: none, <reason>`. A new step, message or rule of a protocol needs the model; timers, buffers and accounting do not. Every `.go` file of a protocol package is a protocol file unless the script excludes it, as it does tests and files that hold no model's rule, so a new file starts covered. A merge whose resolution changes a protocol file is held to the same rule.
- The rule map, `scripts/model-rules.tsv`, gives every negative configuration one row: the Go plant that removes the same rule from the code and the test that fails under it, or the open checklist item or phase that still owes them, or `none` and why no plant of the rule can break a run. A row's plant must be in its package and its test's file must name it; a row open on a ticked item fails. That the test fails under its plant is the test's own assertion: the gate keeps the link from going missing. Most rows are open, as their code is not built; where a rule has its plant, deleting the rule from the code fails that test.

The gate checks the commits since `MODEL_GATE_BASE`, the merge base with `origin/main` by default: a push to main the commits it adds, and a push to another branch every commit since the merge base, so a commit on top of a failed one does not hide it. A force push to main has no old commit to start from and checks nothing, which branch protection covers; no base at all warns, and fails in CI. `scripts/model-gate-test.sh`, in CI's lint job, plants each case in a throwaway clone. Before changing a protocol file, `scripts/model-gate.sh for FILE` lists each rule whose plant the file holds, with the property it breaks, its lines and the rule in words; no output does not mean no rule applies, since many rules have no plant yet. The gate does not show that a model fits its change, or that a stated reason is true, which review judges.

**Spec conformance check.** A simulator run records fixed-field `internal/trace` records of each protocol step. A tool turns the trace into a TLA+ data module and decides nothing else: `tools/tracedata` for the epoch change model, `tools/votersdata` for the voter replacement model. A hand-written trace spec, `models/epoch/trace_epoch.tla` or `models/voters/trace_voters.tla`, takes each line by the spec action it names, with the spec itself unchanged, compares what the record saw with the state that action leaves, and passes when it reaches the last line with the safety invariants intact. TLC 1.7.4 checks each trace on one worker, so a trace, spec and mapping give the same verdict on every run. `scripts/conform.sh` runs the `TestConform*` traces, the plants (`plants`) or named trace files. Its rules:

- **No line is filtered out to make a trace pass.** Every record kind maps to a spec action or a stutter, or is one of the event loop's steps below the protocol (`belowProtocol` in `tools/tracedata`), and a tool stops on any other. A spec action with no record kind, or a record kind with no spec action, fails.
- **The trace spec holds no protocol logic.** The spec it wraps stays the only statement of the protocol. Where a spec action compresses several code steps, its mapping names them, and the code's other steps are stutters whose guard says which step holds them. The mapping's judgement sits in named functions with the reason beside each: in `tools/tracedata`, `classify` (a table's changes), `landOpen` and `crash` (versions made durable with no record), `recoveredAt`, `repliedLater` and `heard` (where a line goes); in `tools/votersdata`, `state`, `replicate`, `support` and `elect`. A line may move only to where the spec takes the step the code took apart from it.
- **A compared value may run behind the spec only where the line pins what it counts.** The voter trace checks a commit or applied index as at most the spec's, because each `replicate` line carries the follower's log terms, a prefix of the spec's log, so no trace passes in which the code commits an entry the spec never does. The spec may then run ahead of the code: a guard can hold in the spec's state that the code's did not meet, which the plants of those guards still show is not hiding them, and a step the spec refuses there is a discrepancy.
- **A trace starts at the spec's `Init`.** The first `Table` record is `Init`, so interval 1's bootstrap peering is out of scope, and a trace with no line but tables gives no data and cannot pass.
- **Three verdicts.** A trace passes, fails at the line no state could take or the invariant a line broke, or gives no verdict when TLC runs out of time or heap; no verdict is neither a pass nor a failure.
- **Traces can fail.** `scripts/conform.sh plants` runs each Go plant of the model gate's rule map in the run its baseline comes from, on seeds whose unplanted trace passes. A plant has fired only when its trace's lines differ from the baseline's, which can be timing alone; identical lines are no evidence. A rejection counts once its failing line has been read to be the planted behaviour, and it is clean when every invariant of the run still held. A plant that leaves a step out, or acts only on leases or on liveness, cannot be caught: no line names a step not taken, and a trace checks no lease or liveness property. The plants expected caught are listed in `scripts/conform.sh` (`ecaught`, `caught`); the run fails when a baseline fails, when an expected plant fires and is rejected on no seed, or, over seeds 0 to 39, where each fires on some, fires on none.
- **Traces cover the spec.** Coverage is read from the behavior TLC prints for each pass: a line took its action when a spec variable moved, and stuttered when none did. TLC's `-coverage` does not fit: it counts every evaluation over abandoned branches and `ENABLED` too. A pass shows one behavior, so a stutter may also have had a real step open to it. The run ends by listing the actions of `Next` no trace took; each has its reason beside the list in `scripts/conform.sh`, and what is owed is in the checklist's **Spec conformance of the untraced protocols**.
- **Discrepancies are settled, not tolerated.** Each is classed as step 4 says and settled in the code or the spec; a patched spec passes `scripts/models.sh ci` on its model before it merges. A claim of conformance states the seeds it ran and which actions and rules it covered.

**Where conformance runs.** A seeded epoch trace takes up to 400 s of one core and 2 GB of heap on the M4 Pro, and one TLC cannot settle gives no verdict, which a gate could neither pass nor fail; so conformance is no per-commit check ([Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered)). The `gate` workflow runs it for a change to a path in `scripts/conform-paths` (the epoch and voter models and trace specs, `internal/chunkstore`, `internal/lease`, `internal/raftshard`, `internal/trace`, both tools and the script): seeds 0 to 39 of each model with the scripted traces, and the plants over seeds 0 to 9, on the self-hosted SYS-2 runner, so a pull request's gate skips it. The nightly `conform` workflow checks a window of 500 seeds that moves each day, from seed 40 up, of the primary-change run, its resending variant and the voter replacement run, giving a trace 40 minutes and stopping inside the job's limit so every line prints; a failure names its seed and the command that replays it. `scripts/gate-branch.sh` runs the local default on a branch that touches those paths. Locally `JOBS` sets how many traces run at once, with no effect on a verdict, and `KEEP=dir` keeps the traces and data.

Together these support one statement: TLC showed the protocol safe within its bounds, and every action of it was seen in the code's runs with no step outside it. That is evidence, not proof. It covers only paths the seeds reach, and it says nothing about whether the spec is the right protocol, which is TLC's and review's part.

**3. Consistency checking.** Every client operation is recorded as `(invoke_time, complete_time, op, result)` (`sim.History`), from simulation and real clusters alike, and `lincheck.Illegal` (`internal/sim/lincheck`) checks the history with Porcupine: a linearizable order for write-shared files and for chunks, and close-to-open for other files, where an open that starts after another node's close sees that close's data. In simulation the times are the scheduler's own, not a node's `ctx.Now`: nodes' clocks drift and slew apart, and a linearizability check needs one timeline. A machine answers a client with a `NetSend` marked `Reply`, and the history completes the operation from that send, through a decoder the run gives it, so no machine carries a hook for the recorder; the send comes after the operation took effect, so a correct run fits the narrower window.

On a real cluster each client process stamps its own operations on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, the invocation before it sends, rounded down, and the completion after it hears, rounded up, so the window holds the effect. That clock is one timeline for every process on a host and no time daemon slews it; clients on other hosts map their stamps onto one host's clock by an offset measured from round trips each second and widen each operation by half the round trip plus the drift since, so the check never flags an order the clocks could not tell apart, though it misses a violation shorter than the bound. One recorder stamping every event on arrival was rejected: its stamp may fall after the operation's effect, so it is sound only if every client waits a round trip before it sends. Each process appends its events (`sim.PutEvent`) to its host's history file with single `O_APPEND` writes, which a local Linux filesystem lands whole, so processes interleave without tearing; a shared file across hosts gives no such promise, so each host keeps its own, and `sim.ReadHistory` joins them, refusing a torn record. Recording costs a clock read and a write and allocates nothing.

An operation that never completes stays in the history with an infinite return time, as Jepsen records an indeterminate operation: it may have taken effect any time after its call, or never; dropping it would flag every later read of its write. A client's pipelined operations overlap under one client ID. Porcupine's search is exponential in such operations, so a check stops at a timeout and fails rather than run on, and the chunk model settles each one a later peering decided (`settle` in `internal/chunkstore/chunkhist_test.go`). When no order is legal, the check names an operation the longest legal prefixes leave out, the same one every run.

**4. Real cluster.** Run Jepsen-style nemesis schedules nightly: random kills, partitions, disk errors and slow disks during a multi-node file workload, checked at the end.

- **FUSE behavior** is pinned by the spike harnesses in [spikes/fuse-coherence](../spikes/fuse-coherence/) and [spikes/fuse-restart](../spikes/fuse-restart/), run in CI against each supported kernel.
- **Daemon restarts:** `kill -9` and rolling upgrades of the daemon under multi-client `fsx` and a metadata workload. No request hangs, no mutating request runs twice, and lost buffered writes surface as `EIO`. The same for the node plugin: killed and upgraded alone, no mount sees it; `rock-solid upgrade` from the previous release keeps every mount and device and never restarts a node's daemon and plugin together.
- **Held block writes:** a pod on an RWO block volume keeps writing into a full pool for 6 hours, and another node's daemon is stopped for 30 minutes with its ublk devices waiting for recovery. Both nodes stay Ready with kubelet's pod lifecycle checks healthy, other pods on them start and stop, `NodeGetVolumeStats` answers, and once room returns or the daemon restarts the held pods carry on and their filesystems check clean.
- **Power cuts** via the Intelligent Platform Management Interface (IPMI) or a smart power strip are the only honest fsync test. Write checksummed patterns with `fio --verify=crc32c`, cut power, boot, verify. Some consumer NVMe drives acknowledge flushes they haven't done.
- **Soft-RoCE (`rdma_rxe`)** runs the RDMA code path on any ordinary network card, and `tc netem` can inject faults under it. It is a functional stand-in only: it is slower than kernel TCP at every message size, and it treats `retry_cnt=7` as "retry forever", unlike real NICs. RDMA performance decisions need a real card.
- device-mapper can't see SPDK I/O, so the SPDK path uses SPDK's own error and delay bdev modules.

**5. Test matrix.**

```
transport:  TCP-only | RDMA-only | mixed
disks/node: 1 | 4 | mixed sizes | mixed speed (NVMe + SATA SSD)
cluster:    3 nodes | 5 nodes | add node mid-run | remove disk mid-run
clients:    storage nodes only | plus compute-only nodes
backend:    io_uring | SPDK
pool:       none | rep2 | rep3 | ec2-1 | ec4-2 at 2 per node
```

Property tests on placement: after any random add, remove and failure sequence, every placement group keeps its pool's width with no more than `max_per_domain` members per failure domain, targets follow each disk's capacity, so after a disk is added to a pool holding data the second computation proposes no move, the balance loop converges with every disk within its fill margin, available + held then lies between balanced ÷ (1 + the largest margin) and balanced, and the members moved stay within a quarter above the fewest that could bring every disk within its margin, plus the sum of the disks' margins. Property tests on the capacity figures: available + held ≤ balanced ≤ Σ capacity ÷ b, the domains' stranded rooms sum to Σ capacity − b × balanced, the stranded fraction does not change as a pool fills at balanced proportions, and for copies, balanced without the largest-capacity domain is the least over single domains and at most balanced after any one disk loss.

**7. Performance.** Track metadata operations per second (create, stat, rename), cached and write-shared read latency, 4K block latency at queue depth 1, CPU per operation, 99.9th-percentile latency during recovery, and cleaning write amplification with disks held at 80% and 90% full. Latency runs include idle gaps, because a drive that has idled for 100 ms adds about 1 ms to the next durable write (NVMe flush spike, N6). A 20% overnight regression is treated as a bug.

**Continuous integration.** GitHub Actions runs the checks on its standard hosted runners, free for a public repository: 4 vCPUs and 16 GB each, about 20 jobs at once, and at most 6 hours per job. A change reaches main only through a passing run of the `gate` workflow on its exact commit, pushed as a `gate/<name>` branch; the workflow also runs on every push to main and every pull request. It runs `scripts/lint.sh`; `go test ./...` and `scripts/raft-tests.sh`; `scripts/seeds.sh` at its per-commit seeds, split by test name over 12 jobs so that each ends well inside the hour; each protocol model's `ci` and negative configurations, one job each; and, when the change touches a path in `scripts/conform-paths`, the spec conformance check and its plants on the self-hosted SYS-2 runner (8 threads, 20 GB), never for a pull request, whose code may come from a fork. Its last job, `gate`, passes only when every other one passed or was skipped. When Actions is unavailable, `scripts/gate-branch.sh` runs the same checks on the M4 Pro, for the packages a branch affects.

Each `ci` and negative configuration is hashed with its model's spec and the TLC version and checksum lines of `scripts/models.sh`; a pass is cached under the hash by a push to main or a `gate/` branch, and a configuration whose hash has one is skipped and named in the run's summary. A pull request caches to its own scope, so it skips nothing else. A TLC bump therefore reruns every configuration and a spec change every one of its model's, and a cache entry GitHub evicts after 7 days unused runs again. A rule removed from a spec with no negative configuration changed fails some job. A configuration past 2,000 s on the runner fails its job, as its runner checks about 3 times slower than the M4 Pro; one past 600 s on the M4 Pro is split, as `ci-restarts` and `ci-restarts-moved` are, or its largest constant moves to `deep`.

Nightly, the `models` workflow runs the models' simulations and, one job each, every `deep` configuration with no cached pass, under `DEEP_LIMIT` 19,800 s, storing its result line; a failure stores nothing and runs again the next night. No job runs a `heavy` configuration. The `conform` workflow runs conformance on seeds no local run reaches, on the SYS-2. The `maelstrom` workflow runs `scripts/maelstrom.sh` (`tools/maelstrom`) on every push and pull request that changes the Raft shard, what it builds on, or the harness. Each action is pinned to a commit, and Go and Java to a version ([tech debts](tech-debts.md)).

**Starting hardware.** One big machine is enough at first: a few virtual machines, QEMU's emulated NVMe devices (SPDK runs against them for correctness, not speed; this needs root, `vfio-pci` and hugepages inside the virtual machine). SPDK's file-backed aio bdev needs none of that and runs in CI as a normal user, and Soft-RoCE between the virtual machines.

### Testing the design decisions

Every decision above is held by a test, and the test is written with the decision, before or with the code that implements it. Which kind of test depends on what the decision is about:

| Decision is about | Held by | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A byte layout (superblock, headers, manifests with their merge bounds, keys, chunk IDs, the PG hash) | Golden fixtures under `testdata/` that the code must reproduce byte for byte, round trips, an exhaustive single-bit-flip test, a torn-write property test over every subset of landed blocks, and a native fuzz test on every decoder | `internal/core/testdata/chunk_id_golden.txt`, `internal/disklog/testdata/log_format_golden.txt` |
| What a state machine emits (one flush per round, write and flush emitted together, `commit_depth` rounds in flight, peering before serving, a freeze entry with the first write after a snapshot) | Trace checks: a test feeds events, records the actions, and asserts on the trace, in the unit test and in the simulator over thousands of seeds; cases whose input is data (compaction picking, fragments, recovery from a scripted disk, a reduced failing seed) are data-driven files (`github.com/cockroachdb/datadriven`, as Pebble and etcd-raft use) that `go test -rewrite` regenerates for review | "exactly one `DiskFsync` per round"; "no `NetSend` of a write before the peering reply" |
| What must hold across a whole run (acknowledged writes readable, one primary per PG, versions never reused, a live map with one entry per block) | Simulator invariants checked after every step, plus a TLA+ model for each protocol | the invariant list above |
| An internal choice that must not change what an index reads (piece size, flush and compaction timing, filters, failed checkpoints, restarts, range against point removals) | A metamorphic test: one ops file per seed run under option sets at their extremes, histories compared with each other and with a model, failures reduced into data-driven regression files | `internal/lsm`'s metamorphic test; `testdata/regressions` |
| What the runtime does (one loop per core, no goroutine per connection, one `writev` per descriptor per pass, bounded passes, no allocation per operation, loops woken only through epoll, clocks read only through the kernel seam) | Static checks where the rule is about code: the sans-IO analyzer and its rule set for the loop and driver packages. Stepped runs: a seed gives the same results and per-loop event order twice. Trace checks where it is about behaviour: the event loop records every syscall it makes, and a test asserts on the counts per pass (`writev` ≤ 1 per descriptor, bytes read ≤ the budget, `epoll_wait` once) and finds every syscall the thread made (`/proc/thread-self/io`) in the trace. Allocation checks: `testing.AllocsPerRun` and `b.ReportAllocs` report 0. Goroutine checks: `runtime.NumGoroutine` is the same with 1 and with 1,000 connections | `tools/sansio`; the event loop item of the [build checklist](build-checklist.md) |
| A number the design relies on (CRC32C per block, collection cost of the page tree, cost per group) | A benchmark with the spike's figure as its bar, run nightly, and a 20% regression is a bug | `BenchmarkCRC32CBlock` against 167 ns on x86-64 and 359 ns on arm64 |

**Traces, not logs.** On the data path the daemon writes no free-form log lines; the sans-IO analyzer rejects `log`, `log/slog` and `fmt.Print*` there. Every event a loop handles, every action it executes and every syscall it makes is a fixed-field trace record (`internal/trace`, 64 bytes, pointer-free), the same record the simulator keeps for its action trace, so one checker library reads both: a seed's trace in a unit test, and a real daemon's trace, which each loop writes to a ring in memory and dumps as text into a [support bundle](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle). The format is one record per line, `tick loop kind fields...`, with fields in a fixed order per kind and no field holding a pointer, a wall-clock time or a map's order, so two runs of a seed are compared with `cmp` and a check is a scan over lines that names the first line breaking a rule. A fourth kind, `output`, records what a machine hands its owner (the log's `Durable`, `Failed`, `CheckpointDone`), which no driver delivers and so is not an event. In the simulator `tick` is the scheduler's global time, so the nodes' records merge into one file in order whatever each node's clock reads, and `loop` is written `node.loop`; a daemon writes its loop number alone. Each loop's ring holds `trace_records` records (default 65,536), and the dump runs on the loop that owns the ring, as an inbox request, so it never sees a record half-written. The daemon's operator logs (startup, configuration, a disk marked down) go through `log/slog` off the data path.
## Alternatives considered

Each choice below favors a short hot path, a single binary and testable correctness over peak features. This table is the index of what was weighed; the linked section owns each decision and explains it in full.

| Decision | Chosen | Alternatives weighed | Why the chosen one |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| First product | RWX filesystem | Block first; S3 first | RWX is where homelab users have no good option; block alone competes with Longhorn v2 and Mayastor |
| SMB clients | From phase 6, one Samba gateway per volume over the FUSE mount, `oplocks = no` on shares pods also write ([What is not free](#what-is-not-free)) | SMB left out of v1; a VFS module and coherent leases first; several gateways with CTDB; `kernel oplocks` | The gateway is an ordinary pod on the mount and gives macOS and Windows clients a share in the first product; coherent leases need Tier 2's recalls, and `kernel oplocks` stay local to one FUSE mount ([Samba spike](#appendix-samba-gateway-spike)) |
| A peer that stops reading | End the connection with one `PeerLost` once its 64 send buffers (32 MiB) are full ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | Mute the producer, as the loop mutes clients at its queue cap; an unbounded queue | A loop's machines serve every peer, so muting them for one slow peer stalls the rest, and an unbounded queue is what collapsed the [open-loop latency spike](#appendix-open-loop-latency-spike)'s loop. The core already resends what a lost connection took |
| S3 request handling | `net/http` and `crypto/tls`, a goroutine per connection, chunk writes handed to the owning loop's inbox ([S3 frontend](#s3-frontend-later)) | The event loops' rules with its own HTTP parser and TLS; `fasthttp`; an epoll HTTP library such as `nbio`; S3 over the file frontend | Object bodies spread per-request cost over MiBs, so a goroutine per connection barely shows and Go's HTTP and TLS stack stays usable. `fasthttp` still runs a goroutine per connection, `nbio` adds a second set of event loops, and through files a `PUT` costs several metadata round trips instead of one transaction |
| Language | Go | Rust; C++ (Ceph, 3FS) | Easier to read, review and extend, and the language of etcd-raft, the CSI tooling and Porcupine. With the [runtime rules](#runtime-in-go) it costs 1.07× Rust's CPU on the replicated write path ([Go and Rust codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)), against 1.5 to 1.7× with a goroutine per connection ([tuned Go spike](#appendix-tuned-go-spike)); bulk structures are pointer-free pages because a pointer per entry costs a quarter to a third of the CPU in collection ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike)) |
| File client | FUSE | Own kernel module (Weka, CephFS); NFS gateway pod (Longhorn RWX) | The spike shows FUSE can switch cache modes per file; it is safe to iterate on, and a crash doesn't take down the kernel. A gateway funnels all traffic through one pod |
| Where write-back lives | In the daemon | FUSE `writeback_cache` (kernel page cache) | The kernel option is mount-wide, hides writes from the daemon and can't be revoked per file, so write-shared mode couldn't be enforced ([FUSE coherence spike](#appendix-fuse-coherence-spike) T9) |
| Full pool under write-back | Space grants per node and pool from the control-plane leader on the session heartbeat, divided by water-filling and reused for at most k = 2 grants' worth of flushes, so every disk's gap above `write_limit` covers them; write through without one; a later failure reported once per handle ([Client design](#client-design)) | Late reports only (Linux NFS client, CephFS, JuiceFS); writing through only near full (CephFS `nearfull`); grants per disk; a near-full flag on replies; reuse for a whole session lease or a time window; Lustre's first come; shares proportional to asks; counting a grant above the water level at buffered + k × L | Applications that ignore `close()` would lose acknowledged data, and a near-full flag is learned after bytes were acknowledged. `write_limit` is soft, so a pool grant capped by the room above it suffices without asking every storage node ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). Reuse for a lease or a time window depends on clocks and lets a node cut off from the leader overrun a disk; first come and proportional shares let one streaming node crowd out small writers ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)). Counting buffered + k × L above the level would grant more only near full, against a level that moves with every heartbeat |
| Recovery under space grants | A primary copies a chunk no client write waits on only while each target disk keeps its log reserve plus (1 + k) × the grants outstanding on its pools; otherwise the refill waits and goes `Stuck` ([Client design](#client-design)) | Recovery passing `write_limit` up to the reserve; each disk keeping only its share of the grants; the leader counting a refill's copies before it grants | Grants are promises against acknowledged writes, and one buffer can land on one disk, so every disk must keep the whole sum. The leader sees neither which copies go where nor when, and cannot take grants back once a refill starts |
| A write of several records that fails or crashes part-way | Its durable items stay mapped under its version, so an unacknowledged write may be partly visible, and a failed one's error carries the version | All or nothing, with a commit record per write | A local disk gives multi-block writes no more; whole chunks, the common large write, go to [chunk slots](#on-disk-format) in one record ([Operations on one disk](#data-layer-chunkstore)) |
| Coherence | Tiers 1 → 2 → 3 | Close-to-open only (NFS, JuiceFS); full capabilities from the start (CephFS); caching left to the user (DAOS) | Ships useful RWX early; the Weka-style mode switch gets most of the benefit of capabilities with three states and one message type |
| Close-to-open at open | Every open by path looks the file up: regular files' entries time out at once, `LOOKUP` answers current attributes, an open of a changed file gets no `keep_cache`, and no attribute reply fetched before a later write of its inode is written ([Cache coherence tiers](#cache-coherence-tiers)) | `keep_cache` dropped alone with 1 s entries; `inval_inode` at open; attribute timeout 0; direct I/O for writers of a changed file | The kernel keeps a file's size until its attributes expire and discards attribute replies during a write past it, so cached entries read short after another node's close and open the old inode after a rename-over. `inval_inode` makes the kernel drop a fresh reply already in flight; timeout 0 costs a `GETATTR` before every cached read; direct I/O gives up the page cache and still reads at the kernel's size |
| Finding the writer of an Exclusive file | A second node's open recalls the holder, whose acknowledgment says whether it has written ([Cache coherence tiers](#cache-coherence-tiers)) | Every first write waits for the owner to record it | The round trip falls on the rare second open instead of on every file's first write; a second open waits for the holder even when it never wrote |
| Keeping cut bytes from showing after a truncate | The purger drains the file through its owner before it runs a truncate's entry, and extensions write nothing into their gap ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)) | An extension punching its gap before its size commit (XFS's `xfs_zero_eof`); the gap committed as a purge entry and zeroed by the purger | A gap punch before the size commit can zero another node's acknowledged extension, and a gap zeroed through the purge list still lets a write that read the old size land in it. The drain costs a round trip to other openers per truncate, not a cost on every write past the end |
| Open-file state | Soft state at the inode owner, rebuilt by re-registration in a grace period after every leader change, a planned transfer included | Durable open records in Raft | Keeps `open` to one round trip with no fsync, which matters for small-file workloads |
| Unlinking a file's last link | Always makes an orphan, which the owner frees in a transaction of its own once no node has it open ([Unlink](#freeing-space)) | The unlink frees the file at once when no open is seen | The open registry is not in the log, so the unlink's transaction cannot consult it and would miss an open registered between proposal and apply; the cost is one more transaction per unlinked file |
| How a new inode owner gets the opens back | It asks each session in a session table fetched after its term began to re-register, by a message to the session's node, and waits for every one or its acknowledged fence ([Inode owner](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Sessions poll each group's leader for a term change; the control plane relays the ask; the grace period ends after a fixed time | Polling costs every session a message per shard however few failovers happen, a relay adds a hop and a piece to fail, and a fixed time can free an orphan a slow node still reads |
| Session expiry | Sessions owned by the control plane: renewed on each client daemon's heartbeat to the leader, expired on the leader's clock stretched by the measured rate ratio (capped at 2), fenced in one versioned session table every storage node acknowledges ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)) | Renewal with every inode owner (CephFS, Lustre); a session key in one MetaStore shard; node liveness as the session; CephFS's epoch barrier on the next holder's writes in place of acknowledgments; a client margin covering every voter's clock, with a restarted voter leading only once every live client has measured it; a rate window per session at the leader; an uncapped stretch | One verdict per session and a renewal cost per node, not per owner or shard. Per-owner expiry fences a client cut off from one owner; a session shard adds a hop and a failover pause; node liveness turns 5 s stalls into application errors; the barrier saves an acknowledgment round that costs milliseconds at 10 nodes ([session expiry study](../studies/session-expiry/README.md)). Covering every voter costs a ping per voter and stops clients while a voter is unmeasured, and a restart wait on time alone is safe only if the restarted voter's new clock is not fast, which nothing bounds. A window would only shorten the wait after a lost answer, which the last pair turns into `dead_wait` at worst; an uncapped stretch lets a paused-then-dead client hold locks for hours |
| Kernel bypass | Opt-in flag | Mandatory DPDK and SPDK (Weka, Mayastor, Longhorn v2) | Homelab links are network-bound, so bypass buys latency, not throughput. Mandatory hugepages and pinned cores break "plug and play" |
| Core abstraction | Two narrow primitives: ChunkStore + MetaStore (Tectonic, Colossus, 3FS) | One rich object layer (Ceph RADOS) | Each frontend pays only for the metadata it needs |
| Metadata store | Embedded multi-Raft | External FoundationDB (3FS), TiKV, Redis (JuiceFS), etcd | The single-binary goal rules out a second system to operate |
| MetaStore `Txn` | Six ops (`Put`, `Delete`, `DeleteRange`, `Check`, `CheckEmpty`, `Update`) evaluated at apply time, checks first; a replay record stores only final results; followers redirect ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Compare-and-set, put and delete only; etcd's compare-then-branch; proposer-evaluated writes (CockroachDB); forwarding to the leader | With compare-and-set only, concurrent creators in one directory commit one per round on its `mtime`; `Update` never conflicts, as FoundationDB's atomic ops. Proposer evaluation makes a write wait for the one before it on its key; forwarding hides the result from the proposer and bypasses the owner's open registry ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)) |
| A committed entry a replica cannot decode | The replica halts at it, refuses requests, hands off leadership and takes no appends, until the control plane replaces it after `refill_after`; new entry formats wait for the format level | Skipping the entry; panicking; a halted replica that keeps taking appends | Skipping leaves replicas on different releases with different states under one log; a panic restarts into the same entry forever and takes the node's other shards down; a halted replica that kept taking appends could never compact them and would fill its disk ([How a `Txn` applies](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| Consensus shape | Leader per shard, one shard at first | Single global Raft group; leaderless Egalitarian Paxos or Accord; quorum or CRDT with no consensus (Garage) | CAS is required for locks and rename, which rules out quorums and CRDTs. Per-shard leaders remove the single-leader bottleneck |
| Raft implementation | etcd-raft (`go.etcd.io/raft/v3`) used directly, `Ready`-style, in a fork that seeds election timeouts | A Raft written from scratch; hashicorp/raft; etcd-raft's `Node` with its goroutine | Only the `Ready` shape fits `OnEvent` and lets the driver share flushes, batch per connection and quiesce shards; the others run goroutines and own their storage. Upstream brings its tests and years of fixes |
| etcd-raft version | v3.6.0, the base of the pinned fork, with v3.7.0's ReadIndex fix cherry-picked | v3.7.0 (protobuf-go pointer messages), with or without recycled messages | v3.7.0 makes 6× the allocations per message and 1.64× the CPU per proposal, mostly inside etcd-raft where recycling cannot reach, and its `[]*pb.Entry` storage API defeats off-heap entries ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)) |
| Where the etcd-raft fork lives | A copy in this repository, `third_party/raft`, through a `replace`: the pristine tag as one commit and each patch as one more | A fork in a repository of its own | Patches are reviewed next to the code that needs them, and a tag update is one change tested in this repository's CI ([Raft shard](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| Ticks after a long gap | At most one heartbeat of ticks per event, the rest of the gap dropped | Every tick the gap holds, at once | The leader's messages may be queued behind a late timer, so crediting the gap starts needless campaigns; no safety rule counts ticks, so dropping time only delays an election ([Raft shard](#metadata-layer-metastore)) |
| Bounding a round | Size only, `max_group_commit_bytes` 1 MiB; one loop per core; collect only changed shards | A time or CPU bound per round; a 256 KiB cap; one loop per node | At a fixed number in flight latency is in-flight ÷ throughput, and under open load rounds never reach 1 MiB; a CPU bound changed nothing and 256 KiB cost a third of throughput, while per-core loops cut p99 from 51 to 5 ms ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). RocksDB caps a write group at the same 1 MiB |
| Cross-shard transactions | Transaction record plus intents (CockroachDB style) | Percolator with a central timestamp oracle (TiKV); a central sequencer (FoundationDB) | No extra central service; the commit point rides on one shard's Raft |
| Redundancy | Per pool, chosen by the user: 1 to 3 copies, optionally erasure coding | One scheme for the whole cluster; a replica count per volume (Longhorn) | Placement groups spread every volume over every disk, so a scheme belongs to a set of PGs, not one volume. Pools also let one cluster mix scratch, block and media data |
| Erasure coding of mutable data | Never in place: encode whole new chunks and idle chunks, overwrite only copies (Windows Azure Storage) | In-place stripe overwrites (Ceph `allow_ec_overwrites`, Vitastor); erasure-code everything with redirect-on-write (Weka) | Avoids read-modify-write and the write hole without cluster-wide stripe garbage collection. Closing the write hole in place is hard: Vitastor's chaos test failed its two-phase erasure-coded configurations before its [3.2.0 fixes](https://vitastor.io/en/blog/2026-08-31-v3.2.0.html) |
| Snapshot cut | Pause every writer of the volume for one round trip, then resume under a new snapshot epoch | Multi-version MetaStore and chunks with hybrid logical clocks (CockroachDB); tagging writes with the new epoch without a pause | Reuses the recall fan-out and Raft's point-in-time view, with no timestamps in the key encoding. Tagging alone can capture an effect without its cause |
| Restore and clone | New volume with a parent pointer, flattened in the background when `flatten` is on (Ceph RBD) | Copy all data at restore | Restore is instant and costs no space; Velero's temporary PVCs never pay for a copy |
| Replication protocol | Primary fans out to replicas in parallel (Ceph, Vitastor) | Chain replication or CRAQ (3FS); leaderless quorum writes | One added hop instead of one per chain link; the primary also gives each chunk a single write order |
| Placement | Chunks hashed into placement groups; a table of PG → members and interval, versioned by a table epoch and computed by the control plane | Per-chunk table; placement every node computes (Ceph CRUSH, Weka) | Exact balance on small clusters with mismatched disks and explicit, throttled moves; Ceph adds `pg-upmap` to CRUSH for the same reason. The table stays a few hundred KiB, and epochs and intervals are easy to simulate |
| Pool capacity | Available from the placement table (the smallest room ÷ share); placement targets each disk's capacity capped by the failure-domain rule, with a fill margin of max(5% of target, 1 member) ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [placement table](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Free raw space ÷ overhead (MinIO); Ceph's `MAX AVAIL`; Vitastor's linear program; figures over free room; a 5% margin with no floor | Room ÷ overhead overstated by 1.36 to 3.3× on unequal failure domains, while available matched the first refused write within 0.3%, and size-proportional targets never converged where capped ones did ([capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike)). Figures over free room drift as the pool fills and cost more moves; 5% is under one member on a small disk ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)) |
| Balance moves | A move starts only on a PG recovered in its current interval; it adds the new member and marks the old one leaving in one table, and a later table removes it once the primary reports the current interval recovered; ties go to the lowest disk ID, then PG ([placement table](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | A swap in one table; keeping the swapped-out member as a recovery source; removal on a report from any interval since the move; a move from any interval; random tie-breaks | A routine move never drops redundancy, as Ceph's `pg_temp` and CockroachDB's learners keep the old copy until the new one is complete; a swap leaves chunks a copy short until recovery. Only a report for the current interval proves the others hold everything, since every new interval may let a member miss writes; a move from an unrecovered interval may let refill replace the only complete member. Fixed tie-breaks keep the computation a pure function |
| Pool conditions | `Full` and `NearFull` on the admission predicate, clearing 1 point down; `Stranded` over 10% and 5 points above the confirmed layout; `Unbalanced` when placement has stopped short of 0.9 × balanced; `CannotReprotect` against the worst likely loss; `Stuck` after 60 s without copying ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)) | Ceph's strict per-disk states without hysteresis; `Stranded` whenever over 10%; `Unbalanced` after a timer (Ceph 60 s, Vitastor 600 s); `CannotReprotect` on the largest domain by size | Strict states leave windows where writes are refused but the pool is not `Full`, and without a gap each discard re-raises it. Stranding on mismatched homelabs is permanent, so it is confirmed at creation and the condition reports only later change. The leader knows when placement stops, which a timer guesses at ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)) |
| On-disk format | One log per disk for chunk data, Raft logs and LSM indexes, with a cleaner | Journal plus in-place data (Vitastor, BlueStore); an embedded key-value engine (`redb`, bbolt, `fjall`, Pebble) for indexes and Raft logs; chunk data inside that engine | One write and one flush per durable write, shared by every shard on the disk; the disk driver touches every byte, so SPDK and the simulator cover metadata too; sequential writes and TRIM suit consumer SSDs. As a Raft log, `redb` wrote up to 112× the payload and `fjall` stalled and kept 179 MB for 4.9 MB live ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). Costs a cleaner and an LSM tree |
| Log streams | Three: hot (new copies and their Raft entries), cold (cleaner copies and shards), metadata (sorted runs, compactions, manifests) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Two streams; more by temperature (F2FS, SepBIT); Raft entries in a stream of their own | Runs mixed with cleaner copies raised write amplification by 25 to 54% near full. Age classes helped skewed workloads but raised uniform write amplification by up to 63%, and a Raft stream would split each commit into two flushes ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). No engine separates GC output by temperature ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)) |
| Whole-chunk layout | Chunk slots: 16 data-only slots per 64 MiB segment, each slot write's record in the hot stream's slot lane ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Whole chunks in log segments; smaller segments for the whole disk; slots carrying their own headers; a stream of their own | Slots cost no cleaning (1.004 at any fill, against 4.9 at 90% in the log), where a stream of their own changes nothing because whole chunks die as randomly as small writes. Smaller segments still cost 1.5 and multiply the usage table; slots with their own headers fit 15 per segment and cap a pool near 93% full ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)) |
| Slot record headers | Left in place, in a slot lane of their own, never a victim while a chunk they cover lives ([Chunk slots](#on-disk-format)) | Among small writes in the shared lane; copied forward by the cleaner into a copy entry a read follows | Among small writes each header strands the small writes' dead space and the cleaner ran out of victims at 50% full. In their own lane they hold at most 1.2% of the disk, which copying forward would win back only with a new entry kind and read path ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)) |
| Chunk index | A non-overlapping live map per chunk, trimmed and split on insert, plus a frozen copy per snapshotted version (BlueStore, Btrfs) | One entry per version, resolved at read time (RocksDB-style merge) | A read of the live version costs one lookup per block whatever the chunk's history. The price is a map copy when a snapshot freezes a written chunk, and a primary maps a write only once it commits |
| Sub-block writes | Merged at the chunk primary against the newest version it has ordered, pending ones included; whole merged blocks sent to replicas ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Client read-modify-write with compare-and-set on the version (VitastorFS); a byte-range delta merged on every member (BlueStore); merging against the committed version | Merging on committed versions, or on the client without compare-and-set, lost bytes; with it, clients retried up to 1.28 times per write since the version is per chunk ([sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)). Deltas cost a base read on every member. The primary's merge needs peering to drop only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions |
| A version that fails within an interval | Ends the PG's interval: its clients are refused and peering keeps or drops it ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Restore writes in the same interval over the failed versions | A restore write can fail in turn and leave the failed version for replay to map back, a second protocol the model does not check; a failure means a member is full or gone, which starts a new interval soon anyway |
| A version sent again to a member whose first copy is done | Acknowledged when the member's index holds the version whole in the current interval, refused otherwise ([Sends, reconnects and held writes](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Always refused; acknowledged when the member's newest version is at least that one | Refusing costs an interval change per lost acknowledgment. A newest-version compare misses an older version durable only in part after a failure; the flag on each version's last entry does not |
| A client write sent again after its first attempt may have committed | Answered from a resend table each disk keeps for the versions it logs, carried by recovery, trimmed by the client's oldest outstanding request ID behind a watermark that answers a trimmed write `Stale` ([Single order per chunk](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Applied again; a last-writer record per chunk; a table in the primary's memory; Ceph's count of recent request IDs per PG; trimming with no watermark | Applied again, a resent write can land after another node's write to the same bytes, and a resent `expect_ver` write fails although it succeeded. A last-writer record is overwritten by pipelined writes, a memory table is lost on exactly the primary crashes that leave writes unanswered, and a count lets a busy PG trim a write still unanswered |
| A recovery write's version | None of its own: logged under the newest version the interval ordered before it, by the members that list the chunk ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | The chunk's next version, sent only to the members it recovers or to every member | A new version sent only to recovered members is dropped by the next peering, and one sent to every member lists every member that logged it, so overlapping faults leave chunks with no source |
| Which of a returning primary's pending versions peering keeps | Those at or below the kept version in its interval and every one below such a version; the others dropped and refused ([Data layer: ChunkStore](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Every pending version at or below the kept one by number; members reporting their chains of versions | An interval the primary missed may have built the kept version on an older one, so a number compare could answer a lost write OK. Chains would grow every peering reply, for writes a refusal leaves to the client's retry |
| When a version peering could prove unacknowledged is dropped anyway | Kept when the member lacking it lists the chunk, or every up member holds it ([Epoch changes and failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Drop on any proof (Ceph's authoritative log); use a departed disk's clean copy as a source; keep only while an up member holds it clean | Dropping left every member listing the chunk with no disk lost. A departed disk is reachable only by the next peering, so a restart then strands the chunk ([epoch change model](#testing-strategy)) |
| When peering answers a kept version's client OK | Only with `min_live` copies the decision proves; otherwise refused, the version kept ([Data layer: ChunkStore](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Answering every kept version OK; waiting for recovery to make the copies; dropping the refused version | Answering every kept version acknowledges a write on one disk; waiting holds activation on a copy that may have no source; only the ordering primary could prove a refused version unacknowledged, and only until a decision kept it |
| A PG whose last activated interval has no member left | Stays stopped until an operator gives up its intervals, which peering then skips ([An operator's give-up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Freeing it when its disks are declared lost (Ceph's `osd lost`) | Peering cannot tell which chunks those intervals wrote, so a freed PG would serve older versions of acknowledged writes silently, and a mistaken declaration would lose them |
| How an operator ends an unfound chunk | Every member drops it, and it works again as an empty chunk (Ceph's `mark_unfound_lost delete`); refused while a candidate may return ([An operator's give-up](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Rolling back to an older version (`mark_unfound_lost revert`); leaving it unfound for good | A store keeps one image per chunk and a listing member may hold a dropped version's bytes, so no older image can be trusted; an unfound chunk leaves a hole the volume cannot even punch |
| A client write to a chunk with fewer than `min_live` clean copies, or that the primary itself lists | Held unordered until recovery takes the chunk ([A write to a chunk with too few clean copies](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Accept the window; hold the OK instead of the order; count the clean copies alone | Accepting lets an OK rest on one clean copy, the exposure `min_live` exists to rule out. Counting clean copies alone lets a listing primary order a write the next peering keeps with no clean copy. Holding the OK works but keeps a pending version and its buffers and leaves peering one more version to decide |
| When a new primary's partial-block write to a chunk waits for recovery | Whenever its own store lists the chunk, as any write does | Only while its index maps a version other than the kept one | The index misses a block that still lacks a kept version under whole-block writes raised to the kept one, and a merge there loses an acknowledged write's bytes |
| A primary's image across a restart and a lost lead | Unsettled versions stay gated until a persisted decision settles them, and the primary flags a missing-list entry made only for its own dropped versions ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Gating alone; recovering its own dropped versions before persisting its list; stating the gap as a limit | Gating alone misses a restart after the decision; only the flag tells that entry from one for a missing kept block. Recovering first adds a peering step, and the limit leaves a chunk unfound with its bytes on the primary |
| Settling a restarted member's replayed writes | The restarted node asks for a new interval of every PG it is a member of, each second until a table moves it ([Restarts](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | The member settling its replayed versions itself when its table shows the interval unchanged | Settling alone races a decision on a possibly stale view and adds a second settle path to check; the ask costs one peering per PG, as Ceph peers every PG of a restarted OSD |
| Ending the gated writes of a PG that left the disk | Dropped once a table names the disk in no interval of the PG since the last activated one ([Restarts](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Dropping them once the current interval leaves the disk out; keeping them until the PG returns | A listed past interval can still be peered, and a reply without them vetoes an acknowledged version; kept for good, they pin the replay point until the log fills |
| Wire frame | A frame of at most 256 bytes inline, with a write's or read reply's whole blocks as its own pool buffer; snapshot epoch, session ID and incarnation in every write and punch from phase 3 ([Wire messages](#wire-messages)) | A frame with its header padded to a block ahead of the data; only the range's bytes; each field added in the phase that checks it | The driver contract carries frame and buffer apart, so data lands aligned with no copy or padding. Whole blocks are the layout the store merges in place. Fields carried from the start spare a wire version later |
| Small files and fsync'd appends | A whole 4 KiB block per copy; a fsync'd append rewrites its tail block ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Inline data in the inode (CephFS, ext4, Lustre DoM); byte-granular patches merged on read (JuiceFS, ZIL); short last blocks; smaller blocks | MetaStore runs live in memory, so inline data costs 12.5× the per-file budget. Patches gain only with their bytes in header blocks, which would make headers live; smaller blocks multiply checksums and index entries. Patches and short blocks stay [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions) ([sub-block writes study](../studies/sub-block-writes/README.md)) |
| Stale requests | A member accepts a request only in its PG's current interval, which the table carries per PG; tables pushed as deltas with the resulting table's CRC32C ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | One table epoch for the whole cluster; comparing the PG's members across tables; nodes walking every table (Ceph); recording activations in the table (Ceph `up_thru`) | A table change disturbs only the PGs it changes: 4.4 to 46 times fewer disturbed writes on 10 nodes in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike). Member lists can change and change back; walking tables makes every delta mandatory; `up_thru` costs a table change per peering ([PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)) |
| Failure detector heartbeats and clock | Each disk's owner loop heartbeats for its disk; a disk silent for `node_down_after` on the leader's `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` is down ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | A node heartbeat gathered from its loops; counting on both clocks as leases do | One rule covers a crashed node and a stopped loop. A mark only changes liveness and leases keep a wrong guess safe, so a slewed clock costs at most a late or early mark |
| Disk record across a control-plane failover | The record commits each disk's log ID, device identity, mark, drain, class and, while down, its age in whole minutes; the rest stays in the leader's memory ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Committing the time a disk went down; an age on every step; everything heartbeats report; the class only in memory | Two leaders' `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`s share no zero, so only an age carries over, at one small commit per down disk per minute. Nodes report the rest within a second of a failover. A class held in memory would let a new leader move a disk's PGs before its first heartbeat, as Ceph sets a device class once |
| Hung disk detection | Each disk's oldest unfinished I/O, aged on its owner loop, carried on every heartbeat and answered to a probe after a primary's `member_timeout` report; a disk stalled past `member_timeout` is down ([Hung disks](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | A probe of the node or loop only; a probe that writes and flushes; the kernel's NVMe `io_timeout`; fail-slow alone | A ping passes on a hung drive and fail-slow takes a whole window. A probe write queues behind the hung I/O anyway, while the age is already in the loop's state |
| Refill after a member goes down | A down member stays in its PG and is refilled after `refill_after` (10 minutes), after `degraded_refill_after` (1 minute) below `min_live`, at once for a lost log; it stays as a leaving member until the recovered report; a refill member counts toward `min_live`; flapping disks are damped unless a PG needs them ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Refilling at once; deleting the refilled member; one leaving member per PG; counting a refill member only once recovered; Ceph's damping that stops the OSD; no damping | A reboot or hiccup then costs only the writes it missed, where refilling at once copied every member away and back ([PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)); RADOS separates down from out for the same reason. A deleted refilled member strands chunks it is the last source of, and one leaving member per PG froze repair on a second failure. Counting the refill member resumes writes at activation. Damping without the `min_live` guard would stop a two-node `rep2` pool |
| Default `write_limit` | Per disk by segment size: 90% on disks of 128 GiB and up, stepping down to 55% from 2 GiB, with wear projected per disk and 80% advised for random-overwrite block pools on consumer drives ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)) | 90% everywhere; a cheaper net-gain guard for small segments; smaller records on small segments; 80% for block pools; a default per drive class | Each default is the fill the cleaner can sustain at that segment size: small segments run out of victims sooner. 90% is where the cleaner's write amplification is about 11 rather than about 30 at 95% ([cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike)). Wear depends on workload, which a default cannot know but the projection shows |
| Disk identity | A disk ID bound to the log ID drawn at format time and never reused ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | A disk ID kept by node and device path (Ceph's `osd destroy` and reuse); a disk ID in the superblock | Peering's proofs assume a member kept its whole log; a new ID costs a refill the lost data needs anyway and keeps the rule in the control plane |
| Drive classes | Solid-state drives; HDDs later, as one log over a flash partition and an HDD, every address in the log's own space ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | HDDs now, each with its own log; HDD-only pools; a cache tier (Ceph); automatic tiering | On an HDD alone every flush costs a seek, so its MetaStore shards commit at HDD speed. The two-device log is BlueStore's and ZFS's answer and can wait; log-relative addresses mean no format migration. Ceph deprecated cache tiering |
| Version layout | 64 bits: the PG interval number in the high 32 over a per-chunk counter in the low 32; a counter that runs out takes its PG to a new interval (Vitastor) ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | The table epoch over a 40-bit counter; Ceph's `eversion_t` in 12 bytes; 128-bit versions; a plain counter | Two primaries can never give one version to different data, so peering compares versions, not contents. Table epochs follow the whole cluster and a flapping node fills 24 bits in 19 hours, where the busiest PG's intervals take centuries to fill 32 ([PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)). 128 bits would add 8 bytes to every entry and extent |
| LSM tree shape | Memtable, in-memory sorted runs over the whole key range, universal compaction from the first commit | A memtable checkpointed by full snapshots; leveled runs (Pebble, RocksDB) | A full snapshot per `max_replay_bytes` costs the index's whole size each time; a run costs what changed. Leveled rewrote 2.8 to 17.7× the flushed bytes and replaces runs piecewise, where whole runs die together in the metadata stream ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)) |
| Compaction choice | RocksDB's universal compaction (size amplification 200%, size ratio 1%, auto-tuned run limit), merging only runs adjacent in age ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Cassandra's size-tiered buckets; buckets with universal's fallbacks; a fixed run limit; buckets over runs sorted by size | Buckets left a run per flush once flush sizes varied; universal kept 2.3 to 6.7 runs under every sequence. A fixed limit of four costs up to 100× on a large index. Sorting by size needs a sequence number per entry ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)) |
| Loops and `GOMAXPROCS` under a CPU limit | Loops = Go's default `GOMAXPROCS` − 1, `GOMAXPROCS` left to the runtime ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | Loops = limit with `GOMAXPROCS` = limit + 1; loops = `GOMAXPROCS` = limit | The only shape that keeps a spare P for wakeups and never exceeds the quota: limit + 1 Ps was throttled, and as many Ps as loops starved the collector and held loops up to 25 ms ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)). It costs one core of loop capacity |
| Removals in the chunk index | Range removals; a write removes the keys of the extents it covers with one, when there are two or more | Point removals left to merges; merges triggered by removals (RocksDB, Pebble) or by a lookup's step count; a floor that keeps each layer's candidate | Point removals cost 200 to 730 µs per lookup of a chunk rewritten over small extents and never got merged away; triggered merges leave the memtable phase slow and rewrite whole runs. Pebble found the run-wide removal ratio "somewhat ineffective" and counts dense blocks instead. Range removals take two searches from the moment the write applies ([LSM runs spike](#appendix-lsm-runs-spike), [LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)) |
| Kinds of removal | A removal, and a range removal | `SingleDelete` (RocksDB, Pebble); Pebble's `DeleteSized` | Flush and merge already drop a removal once no older run holds its key, and replay applies records again, the duplication `SingleDelete` forbids. `DeleteSized` feeds garbage-ranked picking, which universal compaction does not do |
| Memtable range removals | Non-overlapping fragments in a second page tree, a later write cutting its key out | An unfragmented list fragmented on read (Pebble, RocksDB) | Fragmenting on read keeps sequence-number stacks for snapshots; our views pin pages and runs, so a layer's fragments can be kept fragmented as written ([LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)) |
| Run format | LevelDB's block format with restarts every 16 entries; range removals and the filter in blocks of their own | One entry per line with a full key; Pebble's columnar blocks | Prefix compression brings a run to 133 B per file against 199 B in the memtable, and Pebble's block tests port unchanged. Columnar blocks serve faster seeks and CockroachDB's MVCC column; with [run filters](#on-disk-format) a lookup searches about one run, so in-block search is not the main cost |
| Run filters | A cache-local Bloom filter per run, 10 bits per key, of whole keys or a prefix the index names, on every run from the first | No filters and a cap on runs; binary fuse (Pebble) or Ribbon (RocksDB); more bits for newer runs (Monkey); no filter on the oldest run | Over 24 runs filters cut a negative lookup from 13 to 1.9 µs for under 3% more memory, which beats six unfiltered runs, so no cap is needed. Binary fuse needs every hash at once, and a run can be one 58 GiB object. The oldest run's filter is in memory and misses are common ([LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike)) |
| Block cache | One CLOCK cache per index owner loop over off-heap buffers; fence pointers and filters pinned outside it | Pebble's sharded Clock-PRO; RocksDB's HyperClockCache or LRU with index and filter blocks cached | One loop owns an index, so the cache needs no shards or locks. Pinned fence pointers and filters keep a lookup to one block read per run searched ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) |
| Peer connections | One per pair of event loops, opened on demand, requests routed to the owning loop (Seastar) | One per node pair | Keeps a request and its reply on the two loops that own its ends; one per node pair adds two inbox hops per operation or pins a pair to one core |
| Protocols on one connection | A protocol byte first in every message: 1 for chunkstore, 2 for a Raft group's ([The router on the wire](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | A tag per connection; telling them apart by existing first bytes | One byte compare per message; a tag needs a connection per protocol per loop pair, and the old first bytes collide |
| Finding the control-plane leader | A node holds every voter's address; a non-leader answers with the leader it knows, and the node moves there or to its next address ([`ctl/` uses the same API](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | One fixed address; sending to every voter | A fixed address works only with one voter; sending to every voter multiplies a node's messages by the voters |
| The operator's commands | A command kind of the control-plane group's framing on the voters' listener, handled by the leader under its lease and answered once it commits, the node actions included ([The operator's commands](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Commands over HTTP on the admin port; answering at staging | A command must reach the leader under its lease and survive a resend, and the voters' listener already finds the leader and replays a request; the admin port answers each node's own facts and changes nothing, which is what makes it safe for `curl` and any collector to read. An answer at staging would report success for a change a failover drops |
| The control-plane group's log | On a disk log of each voter's own, through the Raft host every shard uses ([The control-plane host](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | In memory, with one voter and the control plane's data disposable | A restart from memory hands session IDs out again, counts placement epochs from 1 over disks with data, and could vote twice in a term; the host and its replay already existed |
| Waiting for disk completions | The loop waits in `epoll_wait` with the ring's eventfd among its descriptors and submits with `min_complete` 0 ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | Waiting in `io_uring_enter` when the ring is all the loop has in flight | A wait in `io_uring_enter` needs a bound for the loop's timers and a wake for its inbox and sockets. It saves CPU only at low load, where CPU does not limit throughput, and gave no consistent latency gain under the `performance` governor (`TestDurableWriteBenchmark`) |
