| Disk logs and event loops | One log per disk, owned by one loop; other loops hand it their records once per pass through its inbox (TiKV's store writer) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A log per loop on every disk (ScyllaDB); every shard pinned to its disk's loop | Keeps one flush per disk per round and the format's single LSN sequence, for about two inbox wakeups per loop per round ([log per core spike](#appendix-log-per-core-spike)). Logs per loop give a disk more flush streams than a consumer drive overlaps; pinning puts a one-disk node's metadata on one core |
| Raft leader leases | Fortification on the loop-pair leases that back primary read leases, as CockroachDB's leader leases on store liveness ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Node-liveness epochs (CockroachDB's epoch leases); etcd-raft's `CheckQuorum` lease; ReadIndex for every read | One lease mechanism for primaries and Raft leaders, renewed per loop pair whatever the group count, so quiescent groups keep leases; no central record whose outage expires every lease. etcd-raft's lease counts ticks a quiescent group does not take; ReadIndex costs a round trip per read |
| A read without the lease | Refused with `NotLeader` naming none; the router retries after an election timeout ([The router on the wire](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Served through ReadIndex until the lease holds | A refusal adds no server path and only delays reads for a new leader's first moments |
| Replacing a Raft voter | Placed by the control-plane leader in the shard map; replaced after `refill_after`, at once for a lost log; the new replica joins as a learner and one `ConfChangeV2` swaps it for the dead voter through a joint configuration; a removed replica stays a voter of the joint configuration, its state machine stopped, until every new voter has reported the leave, and then is deleted ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Add a voter then remove; remove first then add; voters chosen by each group; promotion at the leader's current index; a removed replica deleting itself or no longer campaigning once it learns of its removal | An empty voter in a group with one voter down joins every quorum while catching up (Raft paper §6). Removing first takes two changes; the joint change is one proposal. Only the control plane sees replicas across groups. A learner held to the current index never catches up under steady writes. A dropped voter that deletes itself or stops campaigning can leave the group with no leader for good, since it may hold the outgoing half's newest entry ([voter replacement model](#testing-strategy)) |
| A storage node's MetaStore settings | The shard map in every membership answer; each group's `Boot` voters boot its first configuration; a device recovers its replicas once its node holds a membership ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | A boot count in the manifest; settings in a file of the node's own; the map as the record of a disk's replicas | The map is already the plan every replica follows and reaches a drive wherever it moves; a boot count changes the format and still waits for a membership to reach peers |
| A restored Raft snapshot on a storage node | Its data in the replica's runs; Raft is told it durable once a segment header names the manifest holding it ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | The data in the round; telling Raft once the manifest is durable | The round held the whole state in memory and wrote it twice. A restart loads only a manifest a header names, so a durable manifest alone could lose an acknowledged snapshot |
| Election after a lease lapse | A follower whose fortification lapses campaigns, pre-vote first, after a random `raft_lapse_campaign` (150 to 300 ms) ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | A whole `raft_election_timeout` after the lapse; a shorter timeout for every group | The lapse already shows the leader gone, so a whole timeout only lengthens failovers that already took 1.4 to 5 timeouts over many groups ([multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike)); 150 to 300 ms keeps split votes rare (Raft §9.3). A shorter timeout everywhere would elect when a busy loop merely delays heartbeats |
| Lease clock | Both `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`: the holder ends a lease on whichever runs faster, the grantor on whichever runs slower; a holder ends its leases on resume, bounds each grantor's rate from grant round trips per grantor run ID, and takes no lease before that bound ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` with a 1% or 37% margin; `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` alone; the rate `adjtimex` reports; stopping leases while clocks disagree; requiring chrony's `maxslewrate`; synchronized clocks with a maximum offset (CockroachDB); before the first bound, a lease on `lease_drift` alone or no lease until a whole `rate_window`; the margin `1 − 1/B` alone; a count of `Resumed` in place of a run ID | A time daemon may run `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` 22.6% off and chrony slews 8.3% by default, so a 1% margin lets a 4 s lease overlap by 1.85 s, and no margin covers the unbounded knobs. The raw clock escapes the daemon but not the boot calibration error, so each clock covers the other and the rate bound covers both. A lease on `lease_drift` alone before the first bound outlasts its grant by 39 ms at ±1% and 279 ms at 4%, a calibration error that has shipped (4% on Skylake-X), and no wider margin is safe for an error the kernel does not bound; refusing leases for a whole window costs 10 s of reads at every start and detection. `1 − 1/B` alone is exact but leaves no headroom: a 0.4% rate change after measurement already overlaps. A count of `Resumed` held in memory starts again with the daemon, so it can repeat a value a holder saw across a pause no loop counted, where a random run ID does not. Stopping leases during a slew stops reads for minutes; a maximum offset stops nodes once a time service fails ([clock slew spike](#appendix-clock-slew-spike), [lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md), [clock skew study](../studies/clock-skew/README.md)) |
| Lease across a VM pause | A holder ends its leases when the VMClock disruption marker changes (read each pass), or when a grantor's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` on a heartbeat moved further than the margin allows, or on a grantor's new run ID; peer messages are handled before reads ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | The rate bound alone; the emulated RTC; `ptp_kvm` each pass; `poll()` on `/dev/vmclock0`; confirming each batch of reads; requiring hosts that never stop VMs | A pause can freeze every guest clock, so the rate bound alone serves on a lapsed lease. The marker costs 3 ns and changes before a restored or migrated guest runs; a stop in place shows in the holder's clocks or the grantor's. `ptp_kvm` costs 1.6 µs a read, the RTC counts whole seconds, and confirming reads costs a round trip per batch ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)) |
| Metadata keys and inode IDs | Keys grouped by inode under one-byte tags, fixed-width fields and one variable last component; inode IDs of a slot plus a sequence, reserved 64 at a time; values over 1,000 bytes in piece keys | One global transaction per create (3FS, JuiceFS); inodes inside directory entries (CephFS, HopsFS); escaped variable-length parts (CockroachDB); the slot counter in every create | `create` and `mkdir` stay single-shard with no global service, and `st_ino` stays stable. Escaping is needed only when several parts vary; a counter in every create would conflict ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)) |
| Volume records and their IDs | `volume/{id}/record` beside a counter at `volume/{0}/record`, written with the root in one replayed `Txn` the command line sends to the data group's leader ([A volume's record and its ID](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | The counter under `ctl/`; the control-plane leader creating the volume; a request index of its own | Record, counter and root land in one shard as one `Txn`, and a resend is answered from the replay record, which needs the same request ID, so the command that read the ID resends it |
| Case-insensitive names | `case_index` folds each rune to the least rune of its `unicode.SimpleFold` orbit, so names collide exactly when `strings.EqualFold` matches ([Key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Lower-casing; full case folding (ß as ss) | Lower-casing misses the Kelvin sign, the long s and the final sigma; full folding changes a name's length and is not what Go or SMB clients use |
| Snapshot epoch of a chunk's first write | A `freeze` of version 0 in the record of its first write after a cut ([Versions share blocks](#on-disk-format)) | The snapshot epoch in every write entry (Ceph's `SnapSet.seq`); the version's high bits | No format change and 37 bytes once per new chunk instead of 8 bytes on every write; the version's high bits count intervals, not snapshots ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike)) |
| Replay past pending writes | The replay point stays at the oldest pending write, and replay applies the committed records after it again in LSN order ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Skipping a write whose blocks hold a newer version; a checkpoint that waits for pending writes | Applying again is harmless when a primary maps a chunk's writes in version order ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike)). Writes of different chunks commit out of LSN order, so no wait makes the mapped set a prefix, and a version check cannot catch a dropped write. The cost is at most `node_down_after` of extra replay |
| Changing a volume's pool | In place: cut, read-through to the old pool, background copy | Snapshot and restore into a new PVC only | Keeps the PVC, so applications are not rebound; reuses the snapshot machinery |
| Manifest | An edit per checkpoint and a snapshot once the edits exceed the smaller of it and the next (Pebble's `RotationHelper`) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A full snapshot every time; RocksDB's 500% rule; rewriting only dirty usage-table blocks (Sprite LFS, F2FS) | The full table grows with the disk, 6.6% of the log per checkpoint at 16 TiB, while an edit stays 0.01 to 0.2%. RocksDB's rule makes recovery read up to six snapshots; random overwrites dirty nearly every block ([durability study](#appendix-lsm-study-durability-spike)) |
| Tail rule | A gap ends the log only if no later header names it durable, every later header lies within `commit_depth` LSNs, and no segment header names a later manifest; otherwise Corrupt ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | The depth window alone; RocksDB's point-in-time recovery; a clean-shutdown record | The durable LSN, Pebble's `syncedOffset`, proves corruption independent of the configured depth. Truncating drops acknowledged writes a replica must not lose silently (Protocol-Aware Recovery); a clean-shutdown mode helps only planned restarts |
| Replay with several open segments | A lane per merge worker beside each stream's shared lane, named in the segment header; replay merges the lanes by LSN ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | The per-stream rule; merge output in the shared segment; the manifest recording each open segment (F2FS); merge records outside the LSN sequence (Pebble, RocksDB); a lane per merge | Per stream, replay missed records with merges running; the shared segment stranded several times the segments. Recorded positions save little read for an open list in every manifest, and records without LSNs would change the header, tail rule and rebuild ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)) |
| Freed segments' headers | `NextSeq` in every manifest: a header below it is judged by the usage table, one at or past it was opened after the manifest; freed segments reusable at once ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Every header counts as used; zeroing before the manifest; an open sequence per usage row; F2FS's prefree segments | Pebble and RocksDB judge files by the manifest's next file number the same way. The others keep freed segments used, need an ordered write per manifest, cost 2 MiB per snapshot, or still need `NextSeq` ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)) |
| Stale records past a recovered tail | Zero the first block of every record header found past the end, then flush ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A new log number per restart (Pebble, RocksDB) | One write per found header and a flush recovery needs anyway; fresh segments would strand the rest of every open segment at each restart |
| Rounds in flight | `commit_depth` 1 by default; durability a prefix ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Depth 2 across streams; RocksDB's cap on a group led by a small write | Pebble and RocksDB keep one sync in flight per log. On a consumer drive depth 2 was no better than one round holding both streams' records under one flush, because a flush drains the whole write cache ([stream overlap spike](#appendix-stream-overlap-spike)) |
| Checkpoint lag | Producers other than the checkpoint and the cleaner wait once twice `max_replay_bytes` of their records follow the newest manifest ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | No bound; refusing with an error; counting the checkpoint's own records | Pebble and RocksDB stop writes at two memtables; waiting keeps items in order where an error makes every producer retry |
| Superblock repair | From its copy only while block 0 is unreadable or this format's | Repair from any valid copy (ZFS labels); copies used only offline (ext4, XFS, Btrfs) | A bad block 0 needs no operator, and a disk given to another system is never written; ZFS has rewritten its labels over a reformatted disk |
| Cleaner index updates | One index update per move, applied by the index's owner only where a map still stores the copied blocks at their source (WiscKey, Titan) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A forwarding table per block run or per original record (Pebble's blob rewrite); relocating only in compactions (BlobDB, Titan) | The index is in memory, so a move is one 42 to 72 ns put. A forwarding table sent most reads through an extra lookup and forced copies into log order; relocating in compaction rewrites the whole index and follows key ranges, not free space ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)) |
| Live run pieces in a cleaner's victim | Mark the run for a rewrite, a merge of it alone in steps; no piece is named by two roots ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Copy the piece and re-root every root naming it | Re-rooting needs every root rewritten together and a count per piece; a rewrite reuses merge steps and keeps the reserve at one step per worker |
| Usage table live counts | Count changes reported by the owners as they happen, recounted from the index at every open; a manifest's counts only advice ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Records live until their count is zero; distinct blocks; counts carried across a crash | Whole records overstate partly dead ones under 4K overwrites; distinct blocks cost a lookup per frozen version; replay applies again records carried counts already reflect |
| Cleaner copies in replay | A `copy` entry names its source location; replay applies it where a map still stores the blocks there, as the owner did live ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A plain write entry; a copy checked by version only | A write entry would bring back a block replaced before the copy. A version check differs from the owner's live test; the source costs 8 bytes per entry |
| Index rebuild after a failed run check | Reload the runs the newest named manifest lists, each piece checked against its CRC32C, and replay after them, as an open does; a damaged piece is `Corrupt`, repaired from a replica ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Replay from LSN 1; scan the record headers of segments still in use | Freed segments break both: replay from LSN 1 meets missing LSNs, and a scan loses the punches, freezes and copy sources they held. Reloading reuses open's code |
| Entries in segments the cleaner frees | Carried nowhere: a rebuild starts from the named manifest's runs, which hold them ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | The cleaner copying forward punches and freezes older records still need | Carrying them forward makes the cleaner read metadata it otherwise never reads, only to serve a rebuild |
| Room for a merge | Output installed in steps of `compaction_step_bytes`, inputs bounded below by the step's last key, output in its worker's lane (ScyllaDB's incremental compaction) ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Output installed whole with a reserve for the largest merge (RocksDB `SstFileManager`); a size cap per merge | Installed whole, a merge held up to the whole index beyond the manifests; in steps, two segments. A size cap suits levels cut into files but leaves whole-key-space runs unmerged ([LSM space spike](#appendix-lsm-space-spike)) |
| Naming a merge step when no checkpoint is due | A checkpoint begun for the step alone, the memtable unflushed and the replay counts kept ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Waiting for the next due checkpoint; an ordinary checkpoint | Waiting stalls a many-step merge on an idle disk; an ordinary checkpoint adds a small run per step and restarts the count that bounds replay |
| A merge step's filter | One filter partition per step, fenced by the step's bound (RocksDB's partitioned filters) ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | The whole filter written with the last step; the output answering "maybe" until then | Whole at the last step, a large merge held hundreds of MB beyond the manifests; per step, one partition, and a lookup still probes one partition per run |
| A merge step against the reserve | The next step runs only once the manifest naming the previous one is durable, and a merge keeps its lane until its last step is named ([Free space](#on-disk-format)) | Metadata lane 0 first when only the reserve is free; merge lanes leaving a checkpoint's worth free | Only this rule bounds the reserve at one step per worker, and kept in the tree it needs no other owner |
| Writers when cleaning falls behind | Slow chunk writes from 8 free segments to the rate the cleaner measurably frees space net of all its own writes, smoothed over windows; `NoSpace` only when nothing can be freed (CockroachDB's admission control) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | `NoSpace` once only the reserve is free; RocksDB's fixed delayed write rate; Pebble's stops only; the rate net of copied blocks alone; each window's rate alone | Failing writes the cleaner is about to make room for turns a lag into errors; a fixed rate needs tuning per drive, and stops alone give the latency cliff admission control avoids. Counting only copied blocks overstates the room, and an unsmoothed rate holds writes through windows that measured nothing |
| TRIM of freed segments | Paced at 128 MiB/s, one segment per discard, faster only for a backlog older than 5 minutes (Pebble's delete pacer) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A TRIM per segment as it frees; faster when space is low | Bursts of discards stall some SSDs; a TRIM frees no log space, so low space is no reason to hurry |
| A freed segment's header after its TRIM | The driver writes 4 KiB of zeros over the header behind every discard, hard-linked so a failed discard still zeroes it ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | Zeroing only where a discard does not read back as zeros (`discard_zeroes_data`, NVMe `DLFEAT`, a read-back) | The sysfs flag reads 0 since Linux 4.12, `DLFEAT` needs a passthrough command and can be wrong, and a read-back adds a step; one paced 4 KiB write is cheaper and always right |
| Cleaner threading | Worker goroutines verify and sort the copies; the index's owner lists live blocks and applies the moves ([parallel cleaner spike](#appendix-parallel-cleaner-spike)) | Inline on the writing loop; workers updating a shared concurrent index | Inline cleaning cost the writer 5.9 to 11 µs per write and a p99 of hundreds of µs. A shared index saved the owner under 0.6 µs per write and needs a concurrent index, which a loop per core avoids |
| ublk recovery mode | `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY` with `REISSUE`, plus `QUIESCE` for upgrades; a dead daemon's writes fenced by incarnation ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | User recovery alone (in-flight requests fail with `EIO`); `FAIL_IO`; no recovery | A failed write takes the pod's filesystem down, so only a reissue keeps a restart invisible, as SPDK's ublk target chooses. Block writes are idempotent |
| Incarnation fencing | The incarnation in every chunk write and punch, checked when the primary orders the write ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | The incarnation in the connection hello only; a table of newest incarnations kept across primary changes | 8 bytes per write instead of an ordering rule between loops; the interval and held-write rules make a surviving table unnecessary ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)) |
| Upgrade rollout | Both DaemonSets `OnDelete`; `rock-solid upgrade` rolls each node's daemon, then its node plugin, then waits for its PGs ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | `RollingUpdate` on both; daemon and holder in one pod; a holder DaemonSet with `maxSurge` | Independent rolling updates can restart daemon and holder together and drop every descriptor; one pod's containers restart together; two node plugins contend for kubelet's one CSI socket |
| FUSE protocol layer | Own codec over raw `/dev/fuse`, driven from the event loops ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | go-fuse; libfuse with a custom-I/O build; FUSE over io_uring | go-fuse runs a goroutine and allocations per request, and neither library adopts an initialized descriptor or sends a resend ([FUSE restart spike](#appendix-fuse-restart-spike), [FUSE handoff spike](#appendix-fuse-handoff-spike)). The io_uring transport strands requests on a crash. The protocol is a fixed binary layout, so a codec is small and fuzzable |
| Block frontend | ublk locally, NVMe-oF/TCP remotely | NBD (single-threaded overhead); iSCSI (old, heavy) | ublk rides io\_uring with little overhead; NVMe-oF is the native remote protocol for NVMe |
| Block volume logical block size | 4 KiB, with 512-byte sectors per volume through the primary's merge ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) | 512 bytes by default (krbd) | Linux filesystems write 4 KiB and QEMU pads its guests, so 512 by default would make small writes pay a base read; Vitastor exposes 4 KiB too |
| Block volumes on a full pool | Overwrites of live, unshared blocks and discards pass `write_limit`; writes needing new space are held until the pool has room, with no time limit; CSI-created filesystems mount with `discard` ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) | Fail at once (dm-thin `error_if_no_space`); hold 60 s then fail (dm-thin default); an emergency reserve; thick provisioning of filesystem metadata; `fstrim` on a schedule | A hold that ended when the pool grew left ext4 and XFS clean; failing lost refused data, aborted ext4's journal and shut XFS down ([block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike)), so a hold that ends in failure adds an outage to the wait, and a reserve only postpones the hold while taking room from recovery and space grants. Metadata cannot be provisioned ahead, since ext4 and XFS place bitmaps, extent blocks, inode chunks and directories wherever they allocate. Ceph RBD pauses the same way. Without discard, deleted files never free pool space |
| Testing the indexes | A map model plus a metamorphic test: one ops file per seed under option sets at their extremes, histories compared, failures reduced into regression files ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | A model only; RocksDB's `db_stress` with options randomized per run | Internal choices must never change a read, and a history diff checks that for ops a model is costly to write; some planted bugs showed only under an option set at its extreme ([metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike)) |
| Operations that never complete | Kept in the history with an infinite return time ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | Dropped; ended at the crash | A crashed node's write may have reached the disk; dropping it flags every read that saw it, and ending it at the crash forbids a later reissue. Jepsen and Porcupine do the same |
| Time base of real-cluster histories | Each client stamps its own operations on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`; across hosts, mapped by a round-trip offset widened by its bound ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | One recorder stamping every event; one driver process (Jepsen); wall clocks | A recorder's stamp can come after the operation's effect; one process cannot issue a mounted filesystem's calls on other nodes; wall clocks step, and slewed clocks part faster than raw ones |
| Regression cases from data | `datadriven` files (`github.com/cockroachdb/datadriven`, as Pebble and etcd-raft) | Go table tests; one golden file per test | A reduced seed or a recovery script is data; `-rewrite` regenerates expected output for review |
| Checks inside the code | Settings the tests and the simulator turn on (`Check` in group commit and the LSM tree) | Pebble's `invariants` build tag with `Sometimes`; RocksDB's debug asserts | A setting is chosen per run with one binary; `Sometimes` uses the global random source, which breaks seed replay |
| Crash model of the simulated disk | Each unflushed block write lands or not on its own, in execution order | Pebble's crash clone; RocksDB's random prefix of unsynced data | A volatile cache can persist any subset, so holes and an older overwrite without the newer must be recoverable; Pebble's model never keeps an older overwrite, RocksDB's makes neither |
| Driver test time | A kernel seam in `internal/loop` with clocks read after the wait; a stepped kernel on scripted clocks over real socketpairs; real-kernel tests in CI and VM tests nightly ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | `testing/synctest`; time through Go's `time` package; syscall interposition (madsim, `LD_PRELOAD`, `ptrace`, seccomp); time namespaces; VM-only clock cases; a sans-IO loop; a fully simulated network | synctest fakes only the `time` package, which reads neither lease clock and does not move timerfds or `epoll_wait`. Raw syscalls bypass libc, tracing is slow and nondeterministic, and a time namespace's offsets freeze. VMs take minutes per case with no seed, and a fully simulated network leaves the drivers untested ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)) |
| Simulation | Own simulator over a sans-IO core | Antithesis; Jepsen-only testing | We need a detailed disk and page-cache fault model and thousands of seeds per commit on our own machines. Jepsen alone can't reproduce failures |
| Continuous integration | A `gate` workflow on GitHub Actions' hosted runners that every change passes on its exact commit before main ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | Self-hosted runners for the gate; a gate script run by hand | Hosted runners cost nothing here and run about 20 jobs at once, where one machine runs model configurations for hours; a self-hosted runner would run pull requests' code on the owner's machine; a manual check depends on memory |
| Protocol models | TLA+ models checked by TLC in CI: per model a `ci` configuration under 10 minutes and a `deep` one up to 2 hours 15 minutes, recorded at milestones, plus nightly random simulation; each protocol modelled before its code ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | Models checked by hand (CockroachDB, etcd-raft); deep runs every night; trace validation as a gate from the start; two epoch specs with a refinement check; all models in phase 2 | Models checked by hand went stale (CockroachDB #54130); an unchanged deep run repeats its answer. Trace validation runs nightly, not per commit, since a trace takes up to 400 s and one TLC cannot settle gives no verdict, with the [model gate](#testing-strategy) per commit meanwhile. One epoch spec covers the read property, which needs peering and leases together, without a refinement to keep in step. All models in phase 2 would put the snapshot cut and cross-shard commit on designs phase 2 is still checking, and cross-shard commit is not designed yet ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)) |
| Where the operator's view comes from | Each node's facts endpoint, answered from its own memory and disks with no quorum, compared by a collector off the cluster ([Operating the cluster](#operating-the-cluster)) | A status API on the control-plane leader (Ceph's monitors, Longhorn's manager); an in-cluster dashboard | A cluster's account of itself is missing or stale when it is in trouble, and a dashboard in the cluster fails with it |
| Facts format | JSON over plain HTTP on an admin port, under a versioned schema | The voters' framing; gRPC; Prometheus text alone | `curl` reads it with nothing installed; the framing needs our client, gRPC a generator, and labels flatten member lists. Prometheus reads the collector's verdicts |
| Kubernetes access from the CLI | `kubectl` with the operator's own context | `client-go` | `kubectl` already handles every kubeconfig credential plugin; `client-go` adds dozens of modules pinned to Kubernetes releases |
| Admin port authentication in v1 | None, like every daemon port; the audit log's operator name is self-declared | Mutual TLS on the admin port alone | v1 trusts the cluster network, so the open data ports would leave TLS on one port protecting nothing, and certificates can lock an operator out mid-outage |
| Alert rules | Written once in Go; `rock-solid exporter` publishes the state and each rule firing as gauges, and the chart's `PrometheusRule` alerts on them and on their absence ([Alerts and capacity](#alerts-and-capacity)) | Each rule again in PromQL over per-node metrics, held equal by `promtool` tests | Two forms of every rule drift, and a cluster-wide rule in PromQL becomes joins across per-node series, where some cannot be written |
| Marking a node out of service | A fact the leader commits once the node's disks are down and its sessions fenced, which refuses the node's session starts, then Kubernetes' taint ([Actions](#actions)) | The taint once the node's sessions are fenced | A fenced client starts a new session, so a node whose storage network heals before its kubelet learns of the deleted pods would write beside their replacements |
| The auditor's checks | Go checks named after the spec properties, tested over simulator runs and planted bugs ([The auditor](#the-auditor)) | Evaluating the TLA+ invariants with TLC over gathered facts | TLC needs Java on the operator's machine, and facts are not the spec's state, so it would need a second trace mapping |
| A failing disk that still answers | An alert and a recommended drain ([Alerts and capacity](#alerts-and-capacity)) | Draining automatically on SMART or read-error thresholds | A false alarm costs every other disk a full copy, and on three nodes there is often nowhere to drain to; stalled and slow disks are already marked down |
| Support bundle names | Replaced by an HMAC under a key kept beside the bundle ([Flight recorder and support bundle](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle)) | Plain hashes; removing names | A plain hash of a hostname is found by hashing guesses; removed names stop events lining up across nodes |
| License | Apache 2.0 | AGPL (MinIO); network copyleft (Vitastor) | Storage is adopted slowly and its license is part of the trust decision; anyone can embed, fork or ship Apache 2.0 code, and its patent grant covers contributors. Proprietary forks are the accepted price. The name Rock Solid states the priority: correctness before features |
## Roadmap and open questions

Build order puts the simulator before any real driver and ships RWX file storage as the first usable product. Each phase ends with something that runs and is tested. Phases 1 to 6 are broken into reviewable components, with their state and tests, in the [build checklist](build-checklist.md).

1. **Sans-IO core and simulator.** The Go module with its lint and sans-IO analyzer; a single-node ChunkStore with the per-disk log, extent index, partial blocks merged into whole ones, punch, its cleaner and the LSM index; the simulated disk with the full fault model; the invariant checker.
2. **Protocol models.** TLA+ models, checked with TLC, of epoch changes (per-PG intervals, peering, restarts, primary read leases and Raft leader fortification on the same loop-pair leases), sessions and fencing, cache-mode recalls, and purges, before the real protocols. Epoch changes are one spec with configurations weighted towards leases or peering, since its central property needs both. Later protocols get their models in the phases that build them.
3. **Replication and placement.** Pools with 1 to 3 replicas and `min_live`, primary-based replication, placement groups and the placement table computed by the control plane, per-PG intervals, peering with missing lists, read leases, failure and fail-slow detection with flap damping, refill and recovery per PG, `write_limit` admission. Fault-injected in simulation from the first commit.
4. **Embedded MetaStore.** etcd-raft as the Raft shard with its upstream tests, group commit on the disk log, voters placed by the control plane and replaced through learners, one shard behind the sharded API. Sessions, pool capacity and space grants in the control-plane group; inodes, directory entries, locks, purge lists and replay records in the data group.
5. **Default drivers.** The event-loop runtime (epoll loops behind the kernel seam, inboxes, timer heaps, off-heap slabs), then `io_uring` and kernel TCP behind the `Action` interface, with the driver contract (driver-owned buffer pools, readiness fd, `PeerLost`) from the start.
6. **File frontend with Tier 1: first usable product.** FUSE client with daemon-side write-back, close-to-open through the inode owner's open registry, flock, orphans, unlink and truncate through purges, PVC quotas, space grants, fencing, and restart by descriptor handoff. CSI driver with RWX, fsGroup delegation and the descriptor holder; `rock-solid upgrade`. The operator's command line and web UI over each node's facts, with the auditor, offline `inspect`, the flight recorder and support bundles, and node-scoped actions ([Operating the cluster](#operating-the-cluster)). One Samba gateway per SMB-shared volume. Passes `pjdfstest`, single and multi-client `fsx`, `csi-sanity` and a basic subset of smbtorture; real-cluster histories, nightly nemesis runs and power-cut tests. From this release on, the simulator opens the previous release's disk images before its own seeds run.
7. **Tier 2.** The open registry handed over on planned leader transfers, cache modes, recalls, revalidation of old handles, mmap invalidations, and the FUSE coherence spike's harness in CI. A Samba VFS module that registers a gateway's opens with the inode owner and turns a recall into a lease break; several gateways per volume through CTDB after it.
8. **Block frontend.** The block cache, unless an earlier phase needed it ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)), then ublk with 4 KiB logical blocks, discard, writes held while the pool is full, user recovery with reissue and incarnation fencing, CSI for RWO, then RWX block (Tier 0).
9. **Opt-in fast drivers.** SPDK and RDMA behind the same `Action` interface; a Soft-RoCE lab on virtual machines. The RDMA driver joins the shared network suite (`internal/loop/nettest`) by implementing its `Net` interface.
10. **Erasure-coded pools.** Encoding on write and when idle, return to copies on overwrite, Reed-Solomon with `klauspost/reedsolomon`, degraded reads, shard recovery.
11. **Snapshots, clones and pool moves.** Snapshot cut, per-block copy-on-write, restore with read-through and flatten, in-place pool moves through `ControllerModifyVolume`, CSI snapshots. A TLA+ model of the cut and the move before the cut's code. Passes the `csi-sanity` snapshot tests and a Velero backup and restore.
12. **S3 frontend.** HTTP through `net/http`, then seal plus MetaStore transactions; Ceph `s3-tests`.
13. **Scale-out and Tier 3.** Range splitting and leader balancing; cross-shard transactions (transaction record and intents) for cross-directory rename and snapshot markers, designed and modelled before their code, with commit timestamps and multi-version keys from the start ([Cross-shard transactions](#metadata-layer-metastore)); Raft messages batched per connection and quiescent shards; byte-range locks; directory caching with pushed invalidations.
14. **Transactional key-value and SQL layer.** The MetaStore's ordered keys and transactions as a product of their own, with a stateless SQL layer on top as one more client, as CockroachDB and TiDB are built: a row and its index entries are keys written in one transaction. The storage side needs transactions across any keys (phase 13), MVCC so a long read sees one snapshot, and filters and aggregates run on the shard leader (as TiKV's coprocessor). The layer reuses an existing Go SQL engine, speaks the PostgreSQL wire protocol, and is checked with Elle and SQLite's `sqllogictest`. A database that replicates itself belongs on a one-copy pool or local disks instead, or it keeps nine copies under two failover mechanisms.

**Open questions**

Each of these needs a measurement, a spike or a model run, except where it names a rule still to choose.

*Safety and availability.* Each leaves a known gap: a lease that can overlap, a write that can be lost or read stale, or a PG that can stop.

- [ ] Stops in place that no clock shows: a QEMU guest on kvm-clock hides a `stop`, an internal snapshot or a cancelled migration from every clock and from the disruption marker ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)), and a CRIU checkpoint and restore or a suspend under 1 s may hide the same way. A holder then catches the stop only through a grantor message queued during it, which the epoch change model assumes; a stopped grantor or control-plane leader also measures a rate too low across the stop. How many messages does a tap queue hold under replication load before it drops? If it drops them, should storage VMs on QEMU require `+invtsc`, should a holder read `ptp_kvm` on each pass that serves lease reads (1.6 µs), or confirm reads with its grantors per batch? And how does a step seen on one loop reach the loops serving reads before they serve a request that waited behind it?
- [ ] A grantor's raw rate rising past the margin's headroom of `lease_drift / 2` after it was measured (a clocksource switch, a live migration onto another calibration) while its heartbeats are lost: until the next heartbeat's step check a lease can outlast its grant by up to `(Δρ − lease_drift / 2) · primary_lease`, a case the epoch change model leaves out ([lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md)). Must a grantor resumed from a marker change count its outstanding grants from the resume, a full `primary_lease` on both clocks?
- [ ] Recovery sources across overlapping faults: a non-primary member an earlier interval listed is never a recovery source, even when its index holds the kept image, so two overlapping faults can leave a chunk unfound although a member holds it ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). Is it safe, and worth it, to flag such a member's entry as the primary flags its own?
- [ ] A read at a primary whose own missing list holds the chunk: whether it waits for recovery, forwards the read to a clean member, or returns an error ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).
- [ ] Peering's cost: a new primary collects the newest version of every chunk from every member, and on 3 nodes a node event re-peers every PG, so a reboot compares about a million chunk entries per 4 TB disk while writes wait ([Which versions survive](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). How long does that take on real disks? If too long, each member keeps a bounded per-PG log of recently written chunks, with the full comparison as fallback, as RADOS does with PG logs (Weil et al., §3.4.1).
- [ ] How an empty refilled member is filled: a PG is one key range of the chunk index, so a scan of `(pool, h)` over that range would fit ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).
- [ ] Does a consumer drive without power-loss protection honour Flush? The measured drive completes some flushes in about 0.2 ms and charges the rest to the next write ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)); only a power-cut test shows whether the data was on media. The disk log's recovery assumes it was.
- [ ] A `log-writes` crash cut placed where the kill lands (a mark sent before the kill), so the power-cut test does not depend on a flush in flight at the kill.
- [ ] A clock reading that goes backwards: does Linux return one after a clocksource switch or a VM restore? The loop holds `Now` and `Raw` at their last value and delivers `Resumed` ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)); if it happens often enough to cost availability, the choice is between that and stopping, as TigerBeetle panics.
- [ ] FUSE timeouts across a suspend: the kernel keeps entry and attribute timeouts in jiffies, which stop during suspend, while the daemon ends its [session lease](#sessions-and-fencing) on resume. Does the kernel trust cached attributes, and so cached pages, after the session is fenced? A spike that suspends a VM holding a cached file answers it; if so, the daemon invalidates every cached inode before the kernel serves a read after a resume.
- [ ] An extending `write()` on a cached handle after the page past the old end was dirtied through a shared mapping: does the kernel keep showing those bytes (`fsx -e`)? If so, the daemon invalidates that page when it sees the extension.
- [ ] Byte-range `fcntl` locks before Tier 3: SQLite in rollback-journal mode locks single bytes. Refusing with `ENOLCK` breaks SQLite even from one node; widening to the whole file keeps exclusion but makes its reserved lock conflict with every reader. Which, checked with SQLite's test suite from one node and two?
- [ ] A link down between a primary and one member while both reach the control plane: the probe answers healthy and the PG's writes wait as long as the link stays down. Should the control plane mark the member down for that primary's PGs, move the primary, or count reports from several primaries (Ceph's `mon_osd_min_down_reporters`)? Asymmetric partitions have flapped Ceph OSDs, so the rule goes into the epoch change model's faults first.
- [ ] The stall threshold `member_timeout`, 1 s, is a default, not a measurement ([Hung disks](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). How long do healthy consumer NVMe, QLC and SATA drives hold an I/O under load at worst? The [NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)'s harness could record the tail over hours.
- [ ] Deletes on a full disk: punches pass `write_limit` but wait like any item once only a disk's reserve is free, and so do the Raft entries for `unlink`. Should they draw on a slice of the reserve, as ext4, XFS, Btrfs, ZFS and CockroachDB each keep one ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md))? How many segments does purging a full disk's largest volume take?
- [ ] A write whose client heard a final error other than OK while another attempt still waits at another primary can still be ordered afterwards, and nothing fences it. The rule is still to choose: hold the error until every primary sent the write has answered, or let the next update's watermark refuse the waiting attempt.

*Performance and defaults.*

- [ ] The cost of a slot write's round of its own ([Three streams](#on-disk-format)): a whole chunk among small writes splits them into two rounds, one more flush per slot write. Measure against slot writes sharing a round, at `commit_depth` 1 and 2.
- [ ] Failover with lapse campaigns: 3.2 to 4.3 s from a crash to a new leader ([Raft shard timing](#metadata-layer-metastore)) is reasoned, not measured. Rerun the [multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike)'s M3 with a 10 ms tick and lapse campaigns and count colliding groups; it also shows how often a control-plane failover outlasts the 6.3 s a fast-clocked client keeps its session lease.
- [ ] Flush latency of other drive classes: consumer drives with DRAM, QLC, SATA, and drives with power-loss protection, where `durability: fua` may beat write plus flush. `spikes/nvme-flush/trace-root.sh` gives the device-versus-software split.
- [ ] Shorter commits under `commit_depth` 1: a commit waits for three rounds. Can a loop collect, during its round in flight, the `Ready` of shards that only send or apply, as Pebble and RocksDB apply while the sync runs, and so commit in two rounds with durability still a prefix?
- [ ] Does one loop per disk keep up with a drive that has power-loss protection, where flushes cost tens of µs and the loop becomes CPU-bound? If not, the drive splits into two partitions, each a disk with its own loop, in one failure domain.
- [ ] One flush for both streams: a round holding one record per stream under one flush cut a hot commit next to 1 MiB cold records from 3.0 to 2.35 ms ([stream overlap spike](#appendix-stream-overlap-spike)), at the cost of the "one record per round" rule and the tail rule. Separately, a cap on a round's cold bytes while a hot commit waits trades cold throughput for hot latency; what default, per disk?
- [ ] A temperature separation that never loses to two streams: age classes helped skewed overwrites and hurt uniform ones ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). Does a victim score from each class's measured death rate, or classes enabled only under skew, keep the gain? Needs block-volume and small-file traces.
- [ ] Cleaner moves once the chunk index outgrows memory: the owner's check before each move becomes a lookup that can miss the cache (0.15 to 1.4 ms cold). Do moves batched by chunk stay cached, or does a forwarding table then pay ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike))?
- [ ] Regrouping chunks overwritten at random: the cleaner leaves them at about one extent per block, so sequential reads stay random ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). Should a chunk past some extent count be rewritten whole into a slot, as Pebble rewrites values referencing too many blob files? Needs block-volume traces.
- [ ] Prefix filters on a block volume's chunk index: under random overwrites a read still searches about 7 of 24 runs ([LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike)). Does a finer prefix, or merges triggered by reads (Pebble), bring it near one?
- [ ] Filter hash: the filter uses Pebble's 32-bit hash, which passes 1.85% of absent sequential integer keys against 1.17% of random ones, and inode numbers arrive in order. Does XXH3, already the PG hash, keep false positives near 1.2%? A hash change is a run format change.
- [ ] Releasing a frozen version against replay: the snapshot deletion sweep's range removal can be undone by a replay that starts before the `freeze(v)` record. Either log the release as an entry of its own, or wait until every replay point has passed the freeze ([chunk index](#on-disk-format)).
- [ ] Merge lanes shared between merges: a worker's next merge continues the segment its last one left. What does that mixing strand ([LSM space spike](#appendix-lsm-space-spike))?
- [ ] Admission across a placement group: a slowed member delays its acknowledgments until the primary's capped replication queue stops reading clients. Should acknowledgments carry admission rates, as CockroachDB's replication admission control returns follower tokens?
- [ ] Cleaning-debt thresholds: `clean_free` 16, a second worker at 14 and slower writes at 8 are RocksDB's level-0 proportions, not measurements. Should they scale with the cleaner's write amplification at the current fill, and does the slow-down band need a rate floor or a shorter window?
- [ ] Write amplification of the Go log under write admission is above the [cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)'s at 90% full: admission makes rounds smaller, and the chunk index writes more than the spike modelled. Should a round wait for more writes while admission holds them, and would a leaner extent encoding or a different index compaction rule pay for itself? What `write_limit` default runs on disks under 256 MiB?
- [ ] Holding NVMe over Fabrics writes: does a stock Linux host requeue a write answered with Capacity Exceeded and the longest Command Retry Delay without resetting the controller? Needs a real initiator and the phase 8 target.
- [ ] The grant reuse budget k = 2 is reasoned, not measured. Does it hold under a simulated control-plane failover with streaming writers, and do early heartbeats fit the leader's loop ([Client design](#client-design))?
- [ ] Run checksums on arm64: Go's `hash/crc32` runs one CRC stream on arm64, against three on x86-64, so a merge's two CRC32C passes cost 12% of its CPU on an M4 Pro ([The LSM tree](#on-disk-format)). Is a three-stream arm64 implementation, or a cheaper check, worth it off the owner loop?
- [ ] Merges under wide range removals step through every entry a removal hides to count what they drop, several times slower than seeking past ([The LSM tree](#on-disk-format)). Do whole-chunk punches make that matter, and would per-block entry counts let a merge seek again?
- [ ] Metamorphic cost in CI: how many option sets per seed, and how many random (Pebble runs as many random sets as standard ones), find the most planted bugs per CI minute ([Testing the indexes](#testing-strategy))?
- [ ] The consistency check's time on a long history of one chunk, such as 3 clients × 400 operations, which no run records ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)).
- [ ] Overnight seed runs: where millions of seeds a night run, how their first seed advances, and the timeout they take ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)).
- [ ] Chunk slots for encoded shards: a shard is 4 MiB ÷ k, so pools with different k need slots of different sizes. How much do partly used slot segments strand, and must the cleaner empty them when the log runs short?
- [ ] Write amplification inside the drive under this log: does it fall with larger segments written sequentially and TRIMmed whole? Measure with SMART host and NAND write counters; the [cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike) models the host side only.
- [ ] CPU limits below 3 cores: one loop at a 2-CPU limit loses half the loop capacity. Does a loop with `GOMAXPROCS` 2 and the background confined to one worker serve it better, and how far does the cleaner fall behind?
- [ ] The loop's queue cap (`QueueCap`, 16 MiB): a value from measurement, and what the daemon does when its CPU limit is raised, or set fractional.
- [ ] The loop's cost after idling: a durable write after an idle gap takes 0.1 to 0.45 ms longer though the drive and the loop's CPU do not account for it (`TestDurableWriteBenchmark`). Is it the deep C-state exit, or the timer path only a gap runs? Does holding the core in shallow C-states win it back, at what power?
- [ ] Nightly regression bars: CPU per replicated 4 KiB write against the codegen spike's figure on the CI host, the durable write benchmark against fio, and the inbox hop's cost, each with a tolerance (a 20% regression a bug).
- [ ] The io_uring ring depth for recovery's segment-header reads, and a target for a full disk's header recovery.
- [ ] Polled completions on NVMe: how much CPU per 4 KiB I/O does `IORING_SETUP_IOPOLL` save or add at queue depths 1 and 32, and does a fixed buffer still save what it saves with interrupts ([io_uring registered buffers spike](#appendix-io_uring-registered-buffers-spike))?
- [ ] FUSE over io_uring once the kernel requeues the requests a crash strands: does the ring then cut CPU per request enough to use for new mounts? `spikes/fuse-handoff/run.py U1 U2 U3s` rechecks the crash paths on a new kernel.
- [ ] `/dev/fuse` across event loops: does every loop read its own `FUSE_DEV_IOC_CLONE` of each mount, or does one loop read and pass requests to the inode owner's loop? Measure CPU and wakeups per request.
- [ ] `recall_timeout`, 30 s, is a default to tune. It must exceed a full write-back flush and a client's longest healthy stall; longer leaves a writer waiting on a node that renews but does not answer. Nightly runs under load should record recall acknowledgment times.
- [ ] The primary's base read for a partial block: its latency at queue depth 1 on NVMe and SATA, and how large a per-disk cache of recently merged blocks keeps write-shared appenders and 512-byte block writes from reading at all.
- [ ] Patch entries for fsync'd small appends: bytes kept in the record header would halve a 100-byte append's cost ([sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)), for header blocks the cleaner must copy and patch lists that reads merge. How often do homelab workloads fsync appends smaller than a block? A trace of FUSE writes and fsyncs from real pods answers it.
- [ ] Short last blocks for small files and file tails: storing only the bytes up to a file's end would cut a 1.5 KiB file's raw bytes by 2.6×, for a stored length per extent and byte-counted liveness. How much of a homelab volume is files under 4 KiB and partial tails?
- [ ] SPDK interrupt mode on a real NVMe drive: its latency cost at queue depth 1 and interrupts per I/O at depths 32 and 128, which decide whether the driver sets interrupt coalescing. `spikes/spdk-nvme-interrupt/run-real.sh` measures both; needed before phase 9.
- [ ] The `Resumed` threshold (1 s) and the clock bracket's ε (1 ms) are provisional ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). `Epoll.Bracket` counts reads wider than ε and the widest gap; read on real hosts under load, it measures the gap.
- [ ] io_uring under Kubernetes' `RuntimeDefault` seccomp profile: Docker's default refuses `io_uring_setup`; do containerd's and Kubernetes' defaults, so the chart must set a profile?
- [ ] Pool condition thresholds (`Stranded`'s 10% and 5 points, `Unbalanced`'s 10%, the 1-point clearing gap, `Stuck`'s 60 s) were checked only against one disk addition ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). Do they hold after removals, refills, several additions, erasure-coded pools and real layouts? Two pools re-protecting at once also compete for room each computes alone.
- [ ] S3 requests in ordinary Go ([S3 frontend](#s3-frontend-later)): what do `PUT` and `GET` cost per byte through `net/http` against an epoll HTTP path, and at what object size does the gap stop showing? How does a loop wake the handler goroutine waiting for its write, given that a loop uses no channels?

*Operating.* Facts the [operator's view](#operating-the-cluster) needs that the design does not yet define.

- [ ] Bytes per PG and per volume: rebuild estimates and a PG's share of each volume need them, but the chunk index counts data per pool only. Keep counts per PG and per volume beside the pool's, or scan a PG's key range when a facts request asks ([Alerts and capacity](#alerts-and-capacity))?
- [ ] A lossy link: which fact shows that node-3's link drops packets, the kernel's per-connection retransmits (`TCP_INFO`) sampled each heartbeat or a probe of its own, and what loss raises the rule?
- [ ] Read errors that nothing repairs: a checksum failure makes a block unreadable on that disk, and no scrub finds rot in blocks nobody reads, so the failing-disk rule counts only what reads hit. Should a scrub run, and should a primary that fails a block's checksum repair it from a member?
- [ ] Draining on SMART counts: an automatic drain moves a failing disk's share while it still serves, before a second failure; a false alarm costs every other disk a full copy, and on three nodes under `rep3` there is often nowhere to drain to ([No automatic drain](#alerts-and-capacity)).
- [ ] A recovery rate limit and a pause: recovery is bounded only at 4 chunks per disk ([Recovery](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), so its effect on client latency is whatever the disks give. A limit or a pause protects applications at the price of PGs staying on fewer copies longer; which, and who sets it?
- [ ] Authentication, once daemons authenticate: how an operator off the cluster gets a credential that works while the Kubernetes API is down (a client certificate from a cluster authority, valid for days, is one answer), and how the [audit log](#actions) then names a verified operator.
- [ ] Conformance on a support bundle: a field trace starts mid-history, and the [spec conformance check](#testing-strategy) needs the spec's `Init`. A trace spec whose `Init` is the window's first state, built from the bundle's facts, would check the field run itself rather than a [replay](#flight-recorder-and-support-bundle) of its faults.
- [ ] The recorder's defaults, 7 days or 2 GiB: how many records a day the persisted kinds take on 10 nodes under churn and a rolling upgrade, and whether a node's root filesystem is the place for them.

## Learning project

Each learning track starts with a small throwaway exercise, then lands as a real component, so learning time also builds the project. The order follows the roadmap: correctness tools first, kernel-bypass hardware last.

| Track | Warm-up exercise | Lands in the project as | Roadmap phase |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Go for systems code | Write a sans-IO echo state machine plus a tiny simulator that drops and reorders messages; check it allocates nothing per event and passes the sans-IO analyzer | `internal/core` and `internal/sim` | 1 |
| Correctness and consensus | Fly.io Gossip Glomers challenges on Maelstrom, through to the linearizable key-value one; then read etcd-raft against the Raft paper, running its tests | The Raft shard, MetaStore shards | 2, 4 |
| Model checking | A TLA+ model of a 3-node lease, checked with TLC: at most one holder per epoch | Models of placement intervals, sessions, cache-mode recalls, purges; then snapshot cuts and pool moves, and cross-shard commit | 2, 11, 13 |
| Consistency checking | Feed a recorded history from your simulator into Porcupine (Go) | History recorder plus checker in CI | 1, 3 |
| io\_uring | Raw-device read/write benchmark through an io_uring ring driven by raw syscalls from Go, with and without fixed buffers, compared against fio | Default disk driver | 5 |
| FUSE | Extend the coherence spike: add a second inode and directory-entry invalidation | `internal/frontend/fuse` | 6, 7 |
| eBPF | bpftrace: a flush-latency histogram for your NVMe drives; count FUSE reads served by the daemon versus the page cache during spike tests T3 and T5 | Answer to the flush-latency open question; kernel-side checks in the spike CI; tracing and fault-injection tooling in Go with `cilium/ebpf`. eBPF stays out of the data path: the verifier's limits make it the wrong home for storage logic | 1 (spike), 6 onward |
| Kubernetes storage | A toy CSI driver that provisions directories, run through `csi-sanity` | `internal/csi` | 6 |
| SPDK | Run SPDK's NVMe perf tool and hello\_bdev against a QEMU emulated NVMe; then call one bdev from Go through cgo, batching submissions and completions per call | Opt-in SPDK disk driver | 9 |
| RDMA | rdma-core's ibv\_rc\_pingpong over Soft-RoCE between two virtual machines; then a Go RDMA write-and-completion loop through cgo | Opt-in RDMA network driver, transport negotiation | 9 |
| Erasure coding | Reed-Solomon over GF(2^8) from scratch: encode, lose shards, decode; then benchmark it against `klauspost/reedsolomon` and ISA-L | Encoding in erasure-coded pools | 10 |
| DPDK | DPDK's l2fwd or testpmd on a spare port or virtual function; measure the busy-poll core cost. Compare with AF_XDP, which skips most of the kernel network stack without hugepages or a dedicated card | Side experiment only; informs the opt-in decision | Any |
| Performance work | Profile one hot path with perf and flame graphs; count CPU per operation | Nightly performance tracking | 6 onward |

**Done-when checkpoints**

- [x] Simulator replays a failing seed exactly, and finds a bug planted in a toy replication protocol.
- [x] Raft passes the Maelstrom linearizable key-value workload under partitions.
- [ ] Same `ChunkStore` test suite passes on the simulated disk, io\_uring and SPDK.
- [ ] Two pods on two nodes share an RWX PVC, one node is power-cut mid-write, and every file that was closed before the cut verifies after recovery.
- [ ] Two nodes write one file concurrently under `fsx` with Tier 2 on, and the consistency checker finds no stale read.
- [ ] RDMA and TCP drivers pass the same network test suite; a mixed 3-node cluster negotiates per pair.

## Appendix: FUSE coherence spike

**Question.** Can a FUSE daemon stop a node from serving stale cached data for a file that another node is writing, including through handles that were already open, without a kernel module?

**Setup.** Linux 6.17, libfuse 3.14: two FUSE mounts standing in for two nodes serve one backing file, with a control channel that triggers invalidations and changes open and attribute policy; findings checked against `fs/fuse/file.c` and `inode.c`. Code and raw output: [spikes/fuse-coherence](../spikes/fuse-coherence/).

| Test | Result |
| --- | --- |
| T1: B has the file cached, A writes | B reads stale data. A coherence protocol is needed |
| T2: daemon sends `notify_inval_inode` to B | B's next read is fresh. Cost: 2 to 25 µs |
| T3: B reads again, then A writes again | **Stale.** The old handle refills the page cache, so one recall at the mode switch is not enough |
| T4: new opens get `direct_io`; an old cached handle stays open | New handle fresh, **old handle stale**. The direct-I/O flag is fixed at open (`ff->open_flags`) |
| T5: `auto_inval_data` + attribute timeout 0 + data version as mtime | Old cached handle fresh on `read()`. One attribute request per read (about 5 to 6.5 µs locally) |
| T5b: same, but read through `mmap` | **Stale** until `notify_inval_inode`. Mapped pages are not revalidated |
| T5c: daemon reports a new change counter on every attribute request | Old cached handle always fresh. About 15 to 17 µs per 4K read locally |
| T6: open without `keep_cache` | Stale pages dropped at open. Close-to-open holds for a write that keeps the size; the open brings no new size ([Cache coherence tiers](#cache-coherence-tiers)) |
| T7: 4K read cost on B | Cached 0.3 µs; `direct_io` 5.4 to 6.7 µs; revalidated as in T5c, 15 to 17 µs |
| T9: B mounted with `writeback_cache` | B's writes don't reach the daemon until flushed; `auto_inval_data` has no effect on regular files in this mode, so B stays stale |

**What it decided** ([File frontend](#file-frontend-rwx-filesystem))

- FUSE is enough for Tier 2; a kernel module is not needed.
- Never enable `writeback_cache`; buffer writes in the daemon.
- A recall must drop pages, switch new opens to `direct_io`, and put old handles into per-read revalidation.
- mmap readers need an invalidation after each write.

## Appendix: kernel-bypass drivers spike

**Question.** Can SPDK and RDMA be added later as opt-in drivers behind the `Action` interface without changing the core, the default build or the no-hugepages install, or must something in them shape the interface now?

**Setup.** Linux 6.17 laptop, SPDK v26.09 inside the spike's own event loop on file-backed bdevs, rdma-core v50.0 on Soft-RoCE, all as a normal user. Code and results: [spikes/bypass-drivers](../spikes/bypass-drivers/).

**What it decided** ([Modularity](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers))

- Both fit the driver slot without core changes, but the driver contract (driver-owned buffers, a readiness fd with event mode by default, connection-level failure) must exist from the default drivers on, or they would force a retrofit of every `Action`. Interrupt mode cost SPDK the same latency as polling at a fifteenth of the CPU per I/O.
- SPDK's opt-in has two tiers: file-backed bdevs need no privileges; real NVMe needs root, `vfio-pci` and very likely hugepages, on a dedicated drive.
- RDMA needs `IPC_LOCK` or a large memory-lock limit in the daemon pod, TCP to bootstrap every connection, and a fallback to TCP when the library or device is missing. A send from unregistered memory breaks the queue pair until it is rebuilt.

## Appendix: Samba gateway spike

**Question.** If SMB is served by Samba on top of the file frontend, how does Samba handle client caching, share modes and byte-range locks, what breaks with more than one gateway, and what does it need from the filesystem under it?

**Setup.** Linux 6.17, Samba 4.19.5 unprivileged, a Python SMB 3.1.1 client recording lease breaks, over a counting passthrough FUSE filesystem; two mounts stand in for two nodes. Code and results: [spikes/samba](../spikes/samba/).

**What it decided** ([Key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore), [What is not free](#what-is-not-free))

- SMB leases map onto Tier 2 cache modes: a lease break is a recall. A Samba VFS module that registers the gateway's opens with the inode owner would make SMB clients coherent with pods on other nodes; until then, shares that pods also write need `oplocks = no`, since share modes and leases bind only SMB clients of one gateway and `kernel oplocks` stay local to each FUSE mount.
- One gateway per volume: two gateways without CTDB both granted RWH on one file. CTDB across nodes needs a cluster lock and cluster-wide FUSE `lock`/`flock`.
- Shape the frontend now: inode IDs as `st_ino`, small xattrs stored with the inode and cached by the daemon (an SMB listing costs `user.DOSATTRIB` and `listxattr` per entry), readdirplus, and a case-folded name index, since Samba otherwise scans the directory on every create.

## Appendix: multi-Raft spike

**Question.** The MetaStore must stay cheap at up to about 1,000 Raft groups per node. Is `openraft` cheap and mature enough, or should it use raft-rs, whose `Ready` API is close to the sans-IO core, or its own implementation?

**Setup.** One process holding 3 simulated nodes × 1,000 groups on openraft 0.9.25 and raft-rs 0.7.0, in-memory network and logs, fsync as a 1 ms sleep. Code and results: [spikes/multi-raft](../spikes/multi-raft/).

**What it decided** ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore))

- Neither library as it is. openraft cost 150 to 670 KiB and 11 tasks per replica and 3.5× raft-rs's idle CPU, offers no hook to batch per node pair, and replays nondeterministically; raft-rs is unmaintained and fails cargo-deny. The Raft shard takes the `Ready` shape raft-rs copied from etcd-raft, with a seeded RNG.
- Ticks, messages and persistence belong to the driver, which shares one flush across many groups' appends, batches messages per node pair and quiesces idle groups.
- Tick the election timer finer than the heartbeat: a failover over many groups took 1.4 to 5× the maximum election timeout, and a 10 ms tick halved it. The Raft shard pays that only when campaigns collide, since a follower campaigns 150 to 300 ms after its fortification lapses.

## Appendix: FUSE restart spike

**Question.** Can the FUSE daemon restart or upgrade without breaking mounts by handing its `/dev/fuse` descriptors to a new process? What happens to requests the old process read but never answered, and where do the descriptors live while no daemon runs?

**Setup.** Linux 6.17, libfuse 3.18.3, no root; a hand-written `/dev/fuse` passthrough that hands its descriptors to a successor, to itself through `execve`, to a holder process or to the systemd fdstore. Code and results: [spikes/fuse-restart](../spikes/fuse-restart/).

**What it decided** ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade))

- Pass the descriptors: a mount survives a handoff with no new `INIT`, and one process per volume would still need the handoff to survive its own crash.
- Something must hold every descriptor, clones included, at every instant from mount time on, since the last close aborts the connection; the CSI node plugin is the holder. Save the negotiated `INIT` with them.
- A request read and never answered blocks its caller forever; a successor recovers it with `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND`, which is safe only for idempotent requests (a resent `mkdir` failed with `EEXIST`), so mutating requests carry idempotency keys that survive the crash.
- Never negotiate a request timeout shorter than a restart: the kernel aborts the whole mount. Negotiate `FUSE_PARALLEL_DIROPS`; unmount only from CSI.
- The FUSE layer must adopt descriptors, send raw notifications and echo full 64-bit request IDs, which libfuse does not do by default.

## Appendix: FUSE handoff spike

**Question.** Does the descriptor handoff survive a daemon crash with a separate holder process, `allow_other`, mount namespaces and FUSE over io_uring? Can go-fuse adopt the descriptor, or must the frontend speak `/dev/fuse` itself?

**Setup.** Linux 6.18.44 as root, a daemon SIGKILLed under four verifying writers while a cold successor takes the descriptor from a holder, with mount namespaces standing in for the containers; go-fuse v2.12.0. Code and results: [spikes/fuse-handoff](../spikes/fuse-handoff/).

**What it decided** ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade), [Kubernetes integration for RWX](#kubernetes-integration-for-rwx))

- A successor needs only the descriptor and its saved state, whatever namespace it and the holder run in: crashes with 16 requests in flight gave no application error, and the mount outlived the daemon's namespace.
- The daemon's container needs Bidirectional mount propagation; the holder's socket lives on a shared hostPath and checks its peer.
- Never negotiate FUSE over io_uring until the kernel requeues the requests a crash strands: resends skip the ring queues, and a request handed to the dying daemon fails with `ECANCELED`.
- Speak `/dev/fuse` with our own codec: go-fuse cannot adopt a descriptor (it takes the first request for `INIT`) or resend, and its goroutine and allocations per request break the runtime rules.

## Appendix: NVMe flush spike

**Question.** What does a durable write cost on a consumer NVMe drive, and how does the cost change with the bytes written per flush? The answer sets the durable-write floor, the group-commit size of the [disk log](#on-disk-format), and whether a commit may use more than one flush.

**Setup.** Linux 6.17, a DRAM-less PCIe 4.0 consumer drive with a volatile write cache and no power-loss protection, a C harness of `O_DIRECT` writes and `fdatasync`, with eBPF separating device from kernel time. Code and results: [spikes/nvme-flush](../spikes/nvme-flush/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), the [performance targets](#goals), [Testing](#testing-strategy))

- A drive without power-loss protection floors at about 1 ms per durable write (0.95 ms p50, 98% of it inside the drive), 2 ms after idling, so a faster driver path saves at most 20 µs and benchmarks must include idle gaps.
- Cost is flat from 4 to 64 KiB and grows above, so a group commit is capped at `max_group_commit_bytes`, 1 MiB, written as one contiguous write.
- Exactly one flush per commit, since each extra flush costs a full millisecond: the header makes a torn record detectable on its own, and the per-block checksum catches a torn 4 KiB block, as the drive guarantees only 512 B atomic.
- Write + flush, never FUA or `O_DSYNC` (2.3× slower); `durability: fua` stays an option for drives with power-loss protection. Write file-backed segments in full once, since allocating writes commit the ext4 journal.
- One commit loop and one flush per disk: neither the kernel nor the drive merges flushes the log does not merge itself.

## Appendix: SPDK NVMe interrupt spike

**Question.** Does SPDK's PCIe NVMe driver support interrupt mode, so its thread can sleep in `epoll`, and what does that cost against polling? It decides whether the SPDK driver can follow the [driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers) on real NVMe.

**Setup.** SPDK v26.09 against two stand-ins, the vfio-user emulated controller and a QEMU guest with an emulated NVMe on `vfio-pci`, and the source at the exact tags. Code and results: [spikes/spdk-nvme-interrupt](../spikes/spdk-nvme-interrupt/).

**What it decided** ([driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers))

- Interrupt mode works on PCIe NVMe through `vfio-pci`, with one eventfd per queue pair, so the SPDK driver keeps the readiness-fd contract; it cut CPU per I/O 1.3 to 4.5× and idle CPU to nothing on the emulated device.
- Read the mode back after attach: when enabling fails, SPDK silently attaches in polling mode and a loop sleeping on the fd hangs.
- Size the DMA pool explicitly, at most 128 MB without hugepages (vfio's mapping limit).
- Real-drive latency and interrupts per I/O at high queue depth stay an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

## Appendix: KV engines spike

**Question.** Should an embedded key-value engine, `redb` (copy-on-write B-tree) or `fjall` (LSM tree), hold the Raft logs, MetaStore shard state and chunk index, or a plain log? The figures also set the bar for the LSM tree inside the [disk log](#on-disk-format), which the design chose instead.

**Setup.** The NVMe flush spike's drive under ext4, redb 4.4.0 and fjall 3.1.12 against `write` plus `fdatasync` on prewritten segments, crashes by `kill -9`. Code and results: [spikes/kv-engines](../spikes/kv-engines/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Neither engine suits a Raft log: only prewritten segments reached the flush floor, at write amplification near 1 and with free truncation, where redb wrote up to 112× the payload and fjall stalled for 100 ms and kept 179 MB on disk for 4.9 MB live.
- Commit every shard of a round together: one combined commit for 100 shards took 1.2 ms against 114 ms separately, and neither engine merges concurrent commits, so the round belongs to the driver.
- The speeds the disk log's LSM tree must match: shard gets around 7 µs, a 1,000-entry listing around 200 µs, the chunk index around 50 B per entry. Cold index lookups cost 0.15 to 1.4 ms, so the hot index stays cached.
- Bound what a restart must replay, and copy [snapshot metadata](#snapshots-and-clones) out of long-lived read views, which hold space.

## Appendix: cleaner write amplification spike

**Question.** What cleaning write amplification does the [disk log](#on-disk-format)'s cost-benefit cleaner give block volumes with random overwrites as a disk fills, what segment size should it use, and how full may a disk get?

**Setup.** A deterministic Rust simulation of a 16 GiB disk of 4,096 segments under uniform, hot/cold, Zipf and whole-chunk workloads, validated against the analytic model (Desnoyers, SYSTOR 2012). Code and results: [spikes/cleaner-wa](../spikes/cleaner-wa/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [Testing](#testing-strategy))

- 95% full is too full for random overwrites (write amplification 17, against 7 at 90%), so `write_limit` defaults to at most 90%, and only pools whose data dies in write order should raise it.
- Keep cost-benefit with two streams, guarded by net gain and an age cap: uncapped age livelocked the cleaner.
- Size segments by count, min(64 MiB, disk ÷ 4096): segment size barely matters at a fixed count, but 256 segments gave 70 at 95%.
- Keep headers compact: header blocks are lost capacity and near full cost far more than their bytes.
- Count snapshot-pinned blocks toward the fill.

## Appendix: MetaStore memory spike

**Question.** How many bytes of RAM does one file cost in a MetaStore shard held only in memtables, and so how many files a node holds before its index must be read from the log?

**Setup.** The Rust `MemTree` loaded with the [key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)'s keys under a counting allocator, file names sampled from real local names. Code and results: [spikes/metastore-memory](../spikes/metastore-memory/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format))

- Budget 270 B per file plus about 125 B per xattr on Linux: about 21 million distinct files per node with 16 GiB for the MetaStore at three replicas, fewer with SMB xattrs.
- A snapshot streams from a frozen view, building each piece at its final size, so its peak is the tree plus one piece.
- Prefix-compressed sorted runs are 1.7× smaller than the memtable, so an index held as in-memory runs goes 1.7× further.

## Appendix: cleaner streams spike

**Question.** What segment layout keeps whole chunks deleted at random from paying 64 MiB segments' write amplification? Do more than two temperature streams pay? And where should the log's own Raft entries, sorted runs, compactions and manifests go?

**Setup.** The cleaner spike's simulator extended with 1 MiB records, chunk slots, F2FS and SepBIT stream policies, and checkpoints that flush the chunk index as runs. Code and results: [spikes/cleaner-streams](../spikes/cleaner-streams/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Put whole chunks in data-only chunk slots, 16 per 64 MiB segment, with their records in a lane of their own: they cost 1.004 at any fill against 4.9 at 90% in the log, so a pool of them can fill to 95%. Slots with their own headers fit only 15 and cap the pool near 93%.
- Keep sorted runs, compactions and manifests in a stream of their own (runs among cleaner copies raised write amplification by 25 to 54%), and Raft entries with new copies, so a commit stays one round.
- Add no temperature streams until a policy beats two streams on every workload: age classes cut skewed write amplification by up to 30% but raised uniform by up to 63%.
- Compaction needs a rule that merges when flush sizes vary, which universal compaction is ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)), and room for its largest output.

## Appendix: parallel cleaner spike

**Question.** Can the cleaner run on worker threads, so its copy work leaves the thread that owns the index, and can the workers hand their moves back to the owner instead of updating a shared index?

**Setup.** A Rust model of a 1 GiB in-memory disk under uniform 4K overwrites, cleaning inline, on workers that send moves to the owner, or on workers updating an atomic index. Code and results: [spikes/parallel-cleaner](../spikes/parallel-cleaner/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Testing](#testing-strategy), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Run the copy work on a worker: it takes 4 to 6× less work per write from the owner and cuts its p99 from hundreds of µs to about 6 µs.
- The owner applies the moves (42 to 72 ns each), so the index stays single-writer; a move applies only if the index still points at the copied block.
- A worker costs 7 to 30% more total CPU, so a disk gets one and adds more only when writes stall for free segments.

## Appendix: stream overlap spike

**Question.** Can the log's two streams commit concurrently, so a hot 4K commit no longer waits behind a large cold record, without costing the cold stream its throughput?
