**Setup.** A C harness of one hot 4K stream beside a cold stream of 64K to 1M records, committing in alternating rounds, in one round with one flush, or in two concurrent loops, on the NVMe flush spike's drive and a VM disk. Code and results: [spikes/stream-overlap](../spikes/stream-overlap/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A flush drains the drive's whole write cache, so a hot commit next to 1 MiB cold records takes a 1 MiB commit's time, about 2.3 ms, however the streams are scheduled; hot latency is set by the cold bytes under its flush.
- `commit_depth` stays 1: two loops in flight gained nothing over one round holding both streams' records under one flush, which cut the hot commit from 3.0 to 2.35 ms. Whether rounds should do that is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

## Appendix: Go memtable spike

**Question.** If the MetaStore were written in Go, what would its memtable cost the garbage collector at 10 to 20 million files per node, and which layout avoids that cost?

**Setup.** Go 1.27.0 and Rust on a 4-vCPU VM: a B-tree of `[]byte` entries, the same over handles into slabs, and a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages in pointer-free slabs, beside a goroutine making 256 MB/s of garbage. Code and results: [spikes/go-memtable](../spikes/go-memtable/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- The pointer layout, not the collector version, decides the cost: a pointer per entry spent 23 to 35% of process CPU in collection under load, pointer-free pages none, and pauses stayed under 1 ms either way.
- The memtable is a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages in pointer-free slabs: no collection cost, the fastest gets, and Rust's memory per file once restored in key order. It needs fuller pages in create order and a bound on what a snapshot may copy.

## Appendix: Go CPU per operation spike

**Question.** On drives with power-loss protection, where the data path is CPU-bound, how much more CPU per operation and latency would the same design cost in Go than in Rust?

**Setup.** On one VM, interleaved: CRC32C in each language, the parallel cleaner harness ported to Go, and a replicated 4K write over loopback TCP (Rust an epoll loop per node, Go a goroutine per connection). Code and results: [spikes/go-cpu-per-op](../spikes/go-cpu-per-op/).

**What it decided** ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Go costs 1.1 to 1.25× Rust on compute-bound code, and about 1.5× on a CPU-bound small-write path with a goroutine per connection.
- Syscalls per operation matter more than the language: with the same write pattern both cost the same.
- Go's three-stream hardware CRC32C (167 ns per 4 KiB) beats Rust's default build; Rust needs x86-64-v2 for it.

## Appendix: tuned Go spike

**Question.** How much of Go's gap on the replicated write path closes with Go-specific work (fewer syscalls, atomics, Rust's event-loop design, PGO, `GOAMD64=v3`, no bounds checks), and do Go arenas cut a pointer-per-entry memtable's collection cost?

**Setup.** The Go CPU spike's VM and write path in four designs, each compiler option, and the `[]byte` memtable on the heap and in an arena. Code and results: [spikes/go-tuned](../spikes/go-tuned/).

**What it decided** ([Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A Go data path runs an event loop per node on raw sockets, the sans-IO driver's shape, at about 1.2× Rust's CPU; a goroutine per connection costs 1.5 to 1.7× whatever its synchronization.
- Compiler options are no lever here (all within 3.5%).
- Arenas suit memory freed whole, not a long-lived memtable: they cut its collection cost about 2×, where pointer-free pages cut it to nothing.

## Appendix: Go and Rust codegen spike

**Question.** With the same epoll design and syscall counts, Go still costs 1.21× Rust's CPU per replicated write with no collector involved. Where do the extra cycles go?

**Setup.** The tuned Go spike's harnesses on one VM, microbenchmarks of each part of a write, `perf` software events, Go's profiler and the generated assembly; gccgo checked too. Code and results: [spikes/go-rust-codegen](../spikes/go-rust-codegen/).

**What it decided** ([Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go))

- The compilers share no backend, but the generated code costs about the same on this loop. About 61% of the gap is runtime scheduling: with as many busy goroutines as Ps, `sysmon` takes the P of a goroutine blocked in `epoll_wait`, so it wakes without one; syscall wrappers are another 16%.
- A Go event-loop driver needs `GOMAXPROCS` above its number of blocking loops, raw syscalls for non-blocking socket I/O, and structs filled in place on hot paths: together they took Go from 1.26× to 1.07× Rust.
- Benchmarks must read what they write, or LLVM deletes the work.

## Appendix: open-loop latency spike

**Question.** At the same offered load, how do latency and CPU differ between the Rust and Go replicated write paths in the same epoll design as load passes saturation, and where is each one's knee?

**Setup.** Both event-loop harnesses as servers under one open-loop C load generator, 10,000 to 115,000 writes/s over 16 connections. Code and results: [spikes/open-loop-latency](../spikes/open-loop-latency/).

**What it decided** ([driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- An event-loop pass does bounded work (at most 256 KiB per connection), with capped replication queues as backpressure: a loop reading each socket until it would block collapsed under overload in both languages, its output buffers growing to 200 MB.
- With bounded passes Go's p99 knee comes at a 1.2× lower rate than Rust's (50,000 against 60,000 writes/s), at the same throughput up to 100,000 writes/s.
- An event loop's busy cores do not measure its headroom; capacity is planned from latency against offered load.

## Appendix: log per core spike

**Question.** With a loop per core, should each disk's log belong to one loop that collects other loops' records through its inbox, or should each loop append to its own log on every disk? What does an inbox hop cost in Go?

**Setup.** Go 1.27.0 epoll loops with ring-plus-eventfd inboxes on a VM's virtio disk, a log per loop against one owner fed by K producer loops. Code and results: [spikes/log-per-core](../spikes/log-per-core/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A disk keeps one log owned by one loop: handing it records costs about two inbox wakeups per producer loop per round, not per operation, kept one flush per record, and outran logs per loop at 8 loops.
- An inbox hop costs one wakeup (13.6 µs of CPU alone, 0.07 µs per item at 64 items), so a loop batches everything it has for a peer loop into one message per pass.
- The flush side needs the NVMe drive: `DEV=nvme0n1 ./run.sh nvme`.

## Appendix: chunk epochs spike

**Question.** Does a chunk first written after a cut read correctly at that snapshot when write records carry no epoch? And is a primary's restart correct when its replay point sits before its oldest pending write?

**Setup.** Seeded property tests of the real `internal/chunkstore` index, and a copy with the absent freeze, against a model of whole-chunk copies. Code and results: [spikes/chunk-epochs](../spikes/chunk-epochs/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A chunk's first write after a cut freezes version 0, the absent chunk: without it, snapshots read chunks created after their cut. One 37-byte entry per new chunk, no format change.
- Replaying committed records again in LSN order is harmless provided a primary maps a chunk's writes in version order; commits in any order went wrong.
- A write peering dropped comes back from replay unless it is superseded in the log of every member that logged it.
- Replay grows by what the disk logs while its oldest write is pending, at most `node_down_after` of writes.

## Appendix: LSM runs spike

**Question.** How many runs does size-tiered compaction of in-memory runs leave, what does a lookup cost over them without filters, and do range removals or removal-triggered merges bring a chunk rewritten whole after many small extents back to one search?

**Setup.** The real `internal/lsm` and `internal/chunkstore`, and copies with the candidate fixes checked against a map model, under MetaStore, whole-chunk and block-volume workloads. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-runs](../spikes/lsm-runs/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Each unfiltered run adds 0.3 to 0.6 µs to a lookup, which led to [run filters](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike). Size-tiered buckets settle at about three runs per size class under steady flushes but leave a run per flush once sizes vary, so compaction follows universal's rules ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)).
- A point removal of a key an older run holds can outlive a disk's worth of writes: a rewritten chunk's lookups cost 200 to 730 µs. Range removals end the cost when the write applies (two searches); removal-triggered merges leave the memtable phase slow and rewrite the run holding the old keys.

## Appendix: GOMAXPROCS under a CPU quota spike

**Question.** The loops need `GOMAXPROCS` above their count. Under a Kubernetes CPU limit, does the extra P get the process throttled, and should the daemon run one loop fewer than the limit? What does Go 1.27 already do in a cgroup?

**Setup.** A Go server with daemon-shaped loops and background work inside a CFS quota of 2 or 3 CPUs, under an open-loop load generator outside it. Code and results: [spikes/gomaxprocs-quota](../spikes/gomaxprocs-quota/).

**What it decided** ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Go's default `GOMAXPROCS` in a cgroup is `min(CPUs, max(2, ceil(quota)))`, reread as the quota changes. One P above the quota was throttled in up to 6.5% of periods; as many Ps as loops starved the background and held loops up to 25 ms.
- Under a limit the daemon runs Go's default `GOMAXPROCS` − 1 loops and leaves `GOMAXPROCS` to the runtime; with none, one loop per CPU and one extra P.
- A limit costs a core of loop capacity, so deployments use an integer limit of at least 3 CPUs, or none.

## Appendix: io_uring registered buffers spike

**Question.** Does registering the disk driver's slab and descriptors with io_uring cut CPU per 4 KiB I/O enough to justify pinned memory and the memory-lock limit?

**Setup.** A raw-syscall C harness of 4 KiB I/Os into a 64 MiB slab, plain against fixed file and fixed buffer, on bare-metal NVMe and on a VM. Code and results: [spikes/uring-registered](../spikes/uring-registered/).

**What it decided** ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go))

- The disk driver registers each loop's slab once, when it sets up the ring: a fixed buffer saves `O_DIRECT`'s per-I/O page pinning, 0.1 to 0.3 µs and 5 to 8% of the CPU per 4 KiB I/O on NVMe.
- The daemon pod carries `IPC_LOCK`, since the default 8 MiB lock limit refuses a 64 MiB slab; without it the driver falls back to plain reads and writes on the same buffers.
- Fixed descriptors are nearly free and nearly worthless; the driver registers its few disk descriptors anyway.
- io_uring measurements need a device with `BLK_FEAT_NOWAIT` (`null_blk` or a real drive); zram measures the io-wq path instead.

## Appendix: raft rounds spike

**Question.** A MetaStore loop collects every shard's `Ready` once per round and makes the round durable with one flush. What commit latency does that give at 1,000 groups per node, must rounds be bounded by size, time or per-core loops, and what does etcd-raft cost in Go?

**Setup.** etcd-raft v3.7.0 and v3.6.0, 3 nodes × 1,000 groups in a discrete-event simulation charging each pass its measured CPU, under closed and open load with the NVMe flush curve. Code and results: [spikes/raft-rounds](../spikes/raft-rounds/).

**What it decided** ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore), [On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- One entry per group per round keeps rounds short: it took 32,000 proposals in flight from 178 to 15 ms p50, since at a fixed number in flight latency is in-flight ÷ throughput.
- Bound a round by `max_group_commit_bytes` (1 MiB) only: a 1 ms CPU bound changed nothing and 256 KiB cost a third of throughput.
- Commit latency is about three rounds and follows loop utilization: one loop per core, each under about half busy (open-load p99 from 51 to 5 ms).
- Collect from the shards that changed: scanning every group's `HasReady` took 54% of loop CPU.
- Use etcd-raft v3.6.0: v3.7.0's pointer messages cost 6× the allocations per message and 1.64× the CPU per proposal.

## Appendix: LSM compaction study

**Question.** Which compaction policy keeps the LSM tree's run count, rewritten bytes and space bounded whatever sizes checkpoint flushes have, and do entries need sequence numbers for it?

**Setup.** A size model of each policy, checked against the real `internal/lsm`, under steady, alternating, bursty and log-uniform flush sequences at three index scales, with Pebble's leveled compaction run for real. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-compaction](../spikes/lsm-study-compaction/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Buckets over runs adjacent in age merge only under steady flushes, leaving a run per flush otherwise. RocksDB's universal rules with the auto-tuned run limit bound the runs under every sequence, at about 2 + log2(largest run ÷ 1 MiB).
- A fixed run limit is a per-index override, not a default: four runs rewrote 100× the flushed bytes on a 1.6 GB index.
- Universal merges only adjacent runs, so entries need no sequence number.
- Leveled keeps fewer runs for 2.8 to 17.7× the bytes and replaces runs piecewise; worth weighing again when runs are read from disk.

## Appendix: LSM read path spike

**Question.** What do Pebble and RocksDB put on the read path, which of it transfers to in-memory runs, and does the MetaStore need per-run Bloom filters, or a cap on runs, before the block frontend? The chunk index reads by floors inside one chunk's prefix, which a whole-key filter cannot serve.

**Setup.** The real `internal/lsm` and Pebble's `bloom` and `binaryfuse` packages over about 2 million entries in 6 to 24 runs, for MetaStore, whole-chunk and random-overwrite indexes. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-reads](../spikes/lsm-study-reads/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Every run carries a Bloom filter from the start, 10 bits per key, filled as the run streams out: over 24 runs it cut a MetaStore miss from 13.3 to 1.9 µs and a chunk-index read from 31.5 to 5.1 µs, beating six unfiltered runs, so no cap on runs is needed.
- Filters hold what an index reads by: the chunk prefix for the chunk index, whole keys and inode prefixes for the MetaStore; a floor probes the prefix filter.
- Filters stay on the oldest run; probes cost more than false positives at 24 runs. The scan merge uses a heap.
- A block volume's chunk index under random overwrites still searches about 7 of 24 runs, an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

## Appendix: LSM deletions study

**Question.** Do the tree's range removals match Pebble's and RocksDB's range deletions? What do those engines do that the tree lacks (dropping whole files under a deletion, density-triggered compactions, `SingleDelete`), and how should deleting a chunk, a volume or a queue's head be expressed?

**Setup.** Pebble master and RocksDB v11.8.1 read from source, Pebble v2.1.7 driven in memory, and a copy of the tree, on a rewritten chunk, a dropped volume and a queue. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-deletes](../spikes/lsm-study-deletes/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Freeing space](#freeing-space), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- The tree's range removals are the engines' without sequence numbers: a fragment's age is its layer's, and a layer's fragments unite. They live in a page tree of the memtable; filters and run bounds leave them alone.
- Blocks a fragment covers whole can leave older runs without a merge, as Pebble's delete-only compactions do; it pays only once a trimmed run can name the pieces it keeps.
- Density-triggered merges helped in neither engine's measured cases. A queue consumed from its head removes the consumed prefix as one range (one step per seek against 10,820).
- `SingleDelete`, `DeleteFilesInRange` and excise do not fit an engine that replays records again and keeps point-in-time views.

## Appendix: LSM study: durability spike

**Question.** Do the disk log's group commit, record header, tail rule, checkpoints and manifest follow Pebble's and RocksDB's WAL, commit pipeline, MANIFEST and recovery, and which of their practices should replace or extend ours?

**Setup.** A reading of Pebble master and RocksDB v11.8.1, and a segment-level model counting what a full usage table and an edit carry per checkpoint. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-durability](../spikes/lsm-study-durability/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore), [Testing strategy](#testing-strategy), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Keep `commit_depth` 1 and durability as a prefix: neither engine keeps two syncs in flight on one log.
- Every record header names the LSN durable when its round was built (Pebble's `syncedOffset`), and the tail rule reports a gap that a later header names durable.
- A checkpoint writes an edit, and a snapshot by Pebble's rotation rule: a full usage table costs 0.4% of the log per checkpoint at 1 TiB and 6.6% on small segments, an edit about 0.01 to 0.2%.
- Producers other than checkpoints and the cleaner wait once twice `max_replay_bytes` follow the newest manifest, as both engines stop at two memtables.
- Flushes and merges count entries, and a merge verifies each in-memory piece's CRC32C.

## Appendix: value separation spike

**Question.** The disk log is value separation, as in WiscKey, BlobDB, Titan and Pebble. How should a relocated block's new location reach readers (an index write per move, relocation in compactions, a forwarding table) for an in-memory index whose entries the cleaner mostly causes? How should replay apply a copy of a replaced block, and does any engine separate cleaner output by temperature?

**Setup.** Source reading of the four engines, and a deterministic Go simulator of an 8 GiB disk counting index writes under each scheme. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-blobs](../spikes/lsm-study-blobs/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Keep one index update per move, applied by the owner where a map still holds the source, as WiscKey and Titan do. A forwarding table per original record writes 19 to 33× fewer entries but sends 63 to 96% of reads through an extra lookup, which pays only where references sit in on-disk tables.
- A copy entry names its source, and replay applies it with the owner's check.
- The cleaner does not regroup chunks overwritten at random; bounding fragmentation needs a rewrite of its own, an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions). No engine separates GC output by temperature.

## Appendix: metamorphic testing spike

**Question.** How do Pebble and RocksDB test their trees, logs and recovery, which practices fit a sans-IO core under deterministic simulation, and does a metamorphic test over `internal/lsm` find bugs the property tests would need targeted scenarios for?

**Setup.** A seeded op generator driving the real `internal/lsm` under seven option sets against a map model, with a reducer and five planted bugs. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-testing](../spikes/lsm-study-testing/).

**What it decided** ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- An option set must push each internal choice to its extreme: two of five planted bugs showed only under the set that lets more runs wait than one merge takes.
- A metamorphic test and a map model check different things: the model what the tree reads, the history diff that no internal choice changes it, without a model of views, range removals or frozen maps.
- A failing seed reduces to a few ops when flushes, failed flushes and restarts are ops; a case reduced under an option's cadence is rewritten with explicit ops before it becomes a regression file.
- The simulated disk's crash model is already stricter than both engines'; what it lacked was injected write, flush and read errors and a test that crashes at every action.

## Appendix: LSM space spike

**Question.** How do Pebble and RocksDB give a compaction room, react when the disk fills, slow writers when background work falls behind, and pace deletion? Does compaction need a reserve that grows with the largest run, or can it free inputs as it goes?

**Setup.** Source study of Pebble, RocksDB, CockroachDB and ScyllaDB, and a deterministic Go model of one disk's metadata stream with whole or stepped merge output. Code and results: [spikes/lsm-study-space](../spikes/lsm-study-space/).

**What it decided** ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Install merge output in steps that bound the inputs below the step's last key, as ScyllaDB's incremental compaction: a merge then holds two segments beyond the manifests instead of up to the whole index, and runs still need no sequence numbers.
- Give each running merge's output a segment of its own, so later steps free whole segments (sharing the open segment stranded 30 to 57).
- Only work that frees space uses the reserve, sized from a checkpoint, a step per worker and a cleaner pass: 8 segments of 64 MiB.
- Slow writers at the rate the cleaner measurably frees space, and make them wait rather than fail while it can (CockroachDB's admission control).
- Pace TRIM per segment, never faster for space, since a TRIM frees nothing in the log.

## Appendix: clock slew spike

**Question.** Leases are counted on each side's own clock. How fast or slow can a time daemon run `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, what do chrony, systemd-timesyncd and ntpd do, and which clock keeps a holder's lease ending before its grantor's?

**Setup.** Every `adjtimex` adjustment applied inside User-Mode Linux v6.18 and measured against `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, the daemons' sources read, and chronyd and timesyncd run in a KVM guest. Code and results: [spikes/clock-slew](../spikes/clock-slew/).

**What it decided** ([Epoch changes and failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- `MAXFREQ` bounds only the frequency word: tick, PLL and `adjtime()` together run `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` up to 22.6% fast or slow, and chrony slews at 8.3% by default. A 1% margin would let a 4 s lease overlap its successor by 1.85 s, and covering every slew would need 37%.
- `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` ignores the daemon but keeps the boot's calibration error, which only the daemon corrects. So a lease is counted on both: the holder ends it on the faster clock, the grantor on the slower, and a holder bounds each grantor's rate from grant round trips.
- A slew lasts seconds against a clean source and minutes against internet servers, so stopping leases while the clocks disagree would stop reads for minutes.
- Neither clock measures a suspend reliably, so a holder ends its leases on resume; a VM pause is the [VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)'s.

## Appendix: sub-block writes spike

**Question.** The ChunkStore API takes byte ranges, but records, checksums and the index hold whole 4 KiB blocks. Where must a write covering part of a block be merged, and against which version, so that concurrent writers, pipelined writes and primary changes never lose or resurrect bytes? What do small files and small fsync'd appends cost against byte-granular layouts?

**Setup.** Go models with no I/O: a merge model with an oracle over failovers, an append model with `internal/disklog`'s record layout, and a space model; full write-up in the [sub-block writes study](../studies/sub-block-writes/README.md). Code and results: [spikes/sub-block-writes](../spikes/sub-block-writes/).

**What it decided** ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore), [Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [On-disk format](#on-disk-format), [Client design](#client-design), [Block frontend](#block-frontend), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A partial block is merged at the chunk primary against the newest version it has ordered, pending ones included, and replicas receive whole blocks. Merging against the committed version lost bytes, and a client merge needs compare-and-set and retries up to 1.28 times per write.
- A merged version depends on the one below it, so peering drops only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions, and a new primary merges into a chunk only once it holds every version peering kept.
- Small files and fsync'd appends keep a whole block per copy: inline data costs 12.5× the MetaStore memory per file, and byte-granular patches pay only with their bytes in header blocks or many appenders per round. Patches and short blocks stay [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

## Appendix: capacity model spike

**Question.** What can a pool promise to `statfs`, CSI and the daemons' write-back buffers when its failure domains differ in size? Is room ÷ overhead what it can write before a disk reaches `write_limit`, and if not, which figure is?

**Setup.** A Python model filling integer placement tables, built by the [placement table](#data-layer-chunkstore)'s refill and balance steps, with hashed 4 MiB chunks until the first refused write; full write-up in the [capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md). Code and results: [spikes/capacity-model](../spikes/capacity-model/).

**What it decided** ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), the [placement table](#data-layer-chunkstore), [Client design](#client-design), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- Room ÷ overhead, which Ceph's `MAX AVAIL` also computes, overstates by 1.36 to 3.3× on unequal failure domains; available from the table predicts the first refused write within 0.3%.
- Placement targets are each disk's room capped by the failure-domain rule: size-proportional targets never converged on unequal layouts, capped ones converged within 2%.
- A pool-wide space grant can land several times its share on one disk, so the grants' total must fit the gap above `write_limit` on every disk.

## Appendix: block ENOSPC spike

**Question.** When a thin-provisioned block device runs out of space, what does a filesystem on it do if the device fails new-space writes at once, fails them after a hold, or holds them until the pool grows, and is anything lost?

**Setup.** ext4 and XFS on a 1 GiB dm-thin volume over a 192 MiB pool, written past full, refused at once or held, grown after 20 s or never, then checked with `e2fsck -fn` and `xfs_repair -n`. Code, raw output and kernel logs: [spikes/block-enospc](../spikes/block-enospc/).

**What it decided** ([Block frontend](#block-frontend), [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A hold that ends when the pool grows loses nothing, metadata included, so a block volume's new-space writes are held for as long as the pool is full.
- Failing a new-space write loses its data although `fsync` reports `ENOSPC`, aborts ext4's journal so every later write gets `EROFS`, shuts XFS down at unmount, and leaves an image consistent only after a log replay that needs the space anyway. A hold that ends in failure does the same later.
- Overwrites of written blocks pass on a full pool, so letting them past `write_limit` keeps the journal alive.
- Without discard a full pool stays full after files are deleted, so the CSI driver mounts the filesystems it creates with `discard`.

## Appendix: PG epochs spike

**Question.** How fast do table epochs and per-PG intervals advance on a homelab cluster under churn and a flapping node, how many in-flight writes does a table change disturb under a cluster-wide or per-PG check, and how long does each width of a version's two fields last?

**Setup.** A Python model of a `rep3` table of 2,048 PGs on 3 and 10 nodes through a simulated homelab year and through one flapping node; full write-up in the [PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md). Code and results: [spikes/pg-epochs](../spikes/pg-epochs/).

**What it decided** ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore), [Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A version's high half counts its PG's intervals, not table epochs: an undamped flapping node fills 24 bits of table epochs in 19 hours and 32 bits in 207 days, where the busiest PG's intervals take 272 years to fill 32 bits.
- A 32-bit counter per chunk runs out at most hourly, at a million writes a second to one chunk, which a new interval of one PG absorbs.
- Per-PG checks cut disturbed writes 4.4 to 46 times on 10 nodes and keep staged moves local, but save little on 3 nodes, where a node event changes every PG.
- Tables pushed as deltas cut the bytes by up to 21 times; correctness does not need them, since every PG entry carries its interval.

## Appendix: VM pause spike

**Question.** A hypervisor that stops a virtual machine can stop every clock in it. Can a storage node in a VM learn that it was paused, snapshot-restored or live-migrated before it serves its next lease read, and what does checking cost per loop pass?

**Setup.** A guest harness printing every clock and the VMClock fields while QEMU (TCG and KVM, on `kvm-clock` and `tsc`) and Firecracker stop, snapshot, restore and migrate it, plus a model of the lease rules over pauses. Code and results: [spikes/vm-pause](../spikes/vm-pause/).

**What it decided** ([Epoch changes and failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go), [Testing strategy](#testing-strategy), [Alternatives considered](#alternatives-considered))

- A snapshot restore or live migration changes the VMClock disruption marker before the guest runs again, under QEMU and Firecracker alike, and reading it costs under 3 ns, so the loop checks it each pass and delivers `Resumed`.
- Only a QEMU guest on `kvm-clock` hides a stop in place (every clock frozen, marker unchanged); under Firecracker and under QEMU with `+invtsc` the stop shows in the holder's own clocks. Where it can hide, only the grantors' clocks show it, so every heartbeat carries the sender's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, a holder ends its leases when a grantor's clock moved further than its own could have, and it handles peer messages before reads. The model found no stale exposure with these rules, against up to 1 s with the rate bound alone.
- `ptp_kvm` tracks host time through every pause but costs 1.6 to 1.8 µs a read: a fallback, not a per-pass check.
- The rate bound is no pause detector, so a holder restarts its rate window after any detection.
- A stop on a `kvm-clock` QEMU guest whose queued messages are dropped stays an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
